arXiv:2610.05500v1 [math.CA] 04 Oct 2026
Improved dimension bounds for Falconer’s
distance problem in the plane
Alex Iosevich
Alexia Yavicoli
Abstract
Let E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} be analytic with t=\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1.
We prove that its distance set satisfies
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta E\geq\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0} for an absolute
constant \eta_{0}>0, obtained by compactness without an explicit
numerical value. We also obtain the explicit estimate
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta E\geq\frac{t^{2}-2t+5}{2t^{2}-7t+10}\quad\text{for }1<t\leq\frac{11-\sqrt{41}}{4},
which reaches \frac{7}{8} at the right endpoint.
For the pinned distance sets, we prove
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E\geq q_{*}=0.7813\ldots for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2} outside a set of Hausdorff dimension at most
one, with stronger estimates depending on t and further
bounds on the exceptional sets.
If E is compact and supports an s-Frostman measure \mu,
where s>1, then
\underline{\dim}_{\mathrm{B}}\Delta_{x}E\geq\max\left\{\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0},\min\left\{\frac{3s+1}{5},\frac{7}{8}\right\}\right\}\quad\text{for $\mu$-almost every }x.
The same lower bound holds for pinned Hausdorff dimension
under a common dyadic mass law, which may oscillate with
the scale.
The proof retains information about both endpoints in the
Kolmogorov complexity estimates of Stull and Fiedler–Stull.
Applying the partition estimates with the roles of the
endpoints reversed yields the bound \frac{4}{5} and the
rational estimate above. To pass beyond \frac{4}{5}, we
examine the constraints imposed by equality. Both information
profiles must have excess information on a common interval
of scales. Encoding a single source measure for all pins
converts this constraint into a configuration of points and
circles with many rich perpendicular bisectors. The lower
bounds supplied by Katz–Tardos and Ren–Wang contradict
the upper bound of Fu–Gan–Ren, forcing the fixed gain
\eta_{0}. This passage from rigidity to a strict improvement
has a precedent in the work of Héra, Shmerkin and Yavicoli
on Furstenberg sets, where configurations close to the
elementary bound are excluded using Bourgain’s projection
theorem. A separate selection argument at each finite
scale establishes the pinned lower box estimate for
every s-Frostman measure with s>1.
Together with recent work of Stull, Fiedler, Csörnyei
and their collaborators, these results illustrate how
effective dimension and Kolmogorov complexity, in combination
with Fourier analytic and combinatorial techniques, can yield
new bounds in classical geometric measure theory.

1 Introduction
The Falconer distance problem is one of the most intriguing problems in modern geometric measure theory. Let E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}, and define its distance set and its distance set pinned at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}, respectively, by
\Delta E=\{|y-z|:y,z\in E\},\qquad\Delta_{x}E=\{|x-y|:y\in E\},
where |\cdot| denotes the Euclidean norm.
We call x the pin and E the source set. A source point is a point
y\in E from which the distance to the pin is measured. For x\in E,
\Delta_{x}E\subseteq\Delta E.
For s\geq 0, define
\mathcal{H}^{s}(E)=\lim_{r\searrow 0}\inf\left\{\sum_{i}(\operatorname{diam}U_{i})^{s}:U_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2},\ E\subseteq\bigcup_{i}U_{i},\operatorname{diam}U_{i}\leq r\right\},
where the covers are countable and \operatorname{diam} denotes
diameter. The Hausdorff dimension is
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E=\inf\{s\in[0,\infty):\mathcal{H}^{s}(E)=0\}.
The infimum in the display before taking the limit is the
s-dimensional Hausdorff content at scale r. Omitting the
restriction \operatorname{diam}U_{i}\leq r defines the Hausdorff content
without a scale restriction.
An analytic set is a continuous image
of a complete separable metric space.
Every Borel set is analytic.
The question is how much of the size of E must survive under the
distance map. Falconer conjectured that \Delta E has positive Lebesgue
measure whenever \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1 [6]. Even the weaker
conclusion \dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta E=1 is open when
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E is only slightly larger than one.
For \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E slightly larger than one, the known lower bounds
approach \frac{3}{4}. Stull [33, Theorem 1] proved that every analytic
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} with \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1 satisfies
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Bigl\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E<\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E}{4}\Bigr\}\leq 1.
Since this exceptional set cannot contain E, also
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta E\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E}{4}. Both bounds
decrease to \frac{3}{4} as \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E decreases to one. We obtain
improvements that remain positive in this limit.
Theorem 1.1 (A universal pinned bound).
Let \alpha_{*}=\frac{10-3\mathrm{e}}{24-7\mathrm{e}}=0.371107\ldots, where \mathrm{e} is the base of the natural logarithm, and
q_{*}=\frac{3}{4}+\frac{3\alpha_{*}}{40-12\alpha_{*}}=0.78131\ldots.
Every analytic E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} with \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1
satisfies
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E<Q(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E)\right\}\leq 1,
where
Q(t)=1-\frac{(1-q_{*})(3-2t)}{1+(6-8q_{*})(t-1)}\quad\text{for }1\leq t\leq\frac{3}{2},
and Q(t)=1 for t\geq\frac{3}{2}. The function Q is increasing on
[1,\frac{3}{2}], with Q(1)=q_{*} and Q(\frac{3}{2})=1.
The exceptional set consists of the pins where the stated bound
fails.
We denote by B(z,r) the open ball
of radius r about z. A finite Borel measure \nu is s-Frostman if
\nu(B(z,r))\leq Cr^{s} for every z and 0<r\leq 1, with C
independent of z,r. Such a measure assigns mass zero to sets of
Hausdorff dimension less than s [23, Chapter 8].
Thus the conclusion holds for \nu-almost every pin whenever
s>1.
The constant \alpha_{*} comes from the estimate of Katz and Tardos
for pairwise sums within rows of a matrix whose entries are all
distinct [16, Corollary 5]. Katz and Tardos proved this estimate on the way to improved bounds for the Erdős distinct distances problem in the plane. Their explicit five-entry
and nine-entry estimates give, by the same proof, the thresholds
\frac{132}{169} and \frac{368}{471} in place of q_{*}, and
Q_{5}(t)=\frac{32t+100}{211-42t},\qquad Q_{9}(t)=\frac{88t+280}{589-118t}
in place of Q(t) for 1\leq t\leq\frac{3}{2}. The proof keeps the
threshold q_{*} independent of the source dimension, while the scale
at which its estimates apply may depend on the source and pin
measures. The source dimension enters through the estimate in
Section 6 and its iteration in
Section 8.5. The proof outline below describes which information about the two points these estimates retain.
Allowing either endpoint to serve as the pin yields a larger gain in the exponent.
Theorem 1.2 (Unpinned distance sets).
There is an absolute constant 0<\eta_{0}<\frac{3}{40} such that
every analytic E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} with t=\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1
satisfies
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta E\geq\max\left\{\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0},Y(t),R(t)\right\},
(1.1)
where S_{0}=\frac{11-\sqrt{41}}{4}=1.149218\ldots and
Y(t)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{t^{2}-2t+5}{2t^{2}-7t+10},&1\leq t\leq S_{0},\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{7}{8},&t\geq S_{0},\end{cases}\qquad R(t)=\min\left\{1,\frac{t(4-t)}{5-t}\right\}.
In particular, \dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta E\geq\frac{7}{8} for t\geq S_{0}.
The function Y is increasing on [1,S_{0}], with
Y(1)=\frac{4}{5} and Y(S_{0})=\frac{7}{8}.
For every fixed 1<t\leq S_{0}, its gain over \frac{4}{5} is explicit:
Y(t)-\frac{4}{5}=\frac{3(t-1)(5-t)}{5(2t^{2}-7t+10)}>0.
This gain tends to zero as t\searrow 1. The constant \eta_{0}
provides a uniform positive gain throughout t>1.
The bound R(t) follows from Fiedler–Stull [7, Theorem 1
and Corollary 2]; it is also recovered by letting p increase
to t in Theorem 1.5 below. It exceeds Y(t)
when t>\frac{39-\sqrt{401}}{16}=1.1859\ldots.
Table 1 records the comparison with earlier
unpinned estimates.
Two arguments enter Theorem 1.2. The reciprocal
partition estimate in Section 10, combined with
the source-dimension iteration, proves Y(t). The fixed gain
\eta_{0} requires more. At the scalar endpoint, both profiles have
a common interval of excess information. Section 13
proves this rigidity statement, and Section 29
uses the common source measure to rule out the resulting geometric
configuration. The compactness argument does not assign a numerical
value to \eta_{0}. This constant is chosen independently of the set
and its measures. The starting precision in the finite argument
may depend on the measures, as explained in
Remark 21.2.
The elementary arguments in Appendices B
and C illustrate the first interaction between the
two endpoint estimates.
There is a stronger pinned conclusion if Hausdorff dimension of
the image is replaced by lower box dimension. For a bounded set
F, let N_{r}(F) be the least number of open balls of radius r
covering F, and define
\underline{\dim}_{\mathrm{B}}F=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log_{2}N_{2^{-n}}(F)}{n}.
Put
L(s)=\min\left\{\frac{3s+1}{5},\frac{7}{8}\right\},\qquad L_{*}(s)=\max\left\{\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0},L(s)\right\}.
(1.2)
Theorem 1.3 (Pinned lower box dimension).
Let E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} be compact, and let \mu be any
nonzero finite s-Frostman measure supported on E, with s>1.
Then
\underline{\dim}_{\mathrm{B}}\Delta_{x}E\geq L_{*}(s)\quad\text{for $\mu$-almost every }x.
(1.3)
After normalizing \mu to have mass one, for every q<L_{*}(s)
and M>0 one has
\mu\{x\in E:N_{2^{-n}}(\Delta_{x}E)<2^{qn}\}\leq n^{-M}
(1.4)
for all sufficiently large n. The starting precision may depend
on E,\mu,s,q,M.
The exponent s belongs to the specified measure. No lower
mass bound or equality of Hausdorff and packing dimensions is
assumed. The explicit bound L(s) reaches \frac{7}{8} at
s=\frac{9}{8}. The proof chooses two bad pins whose complexity
profiles nearly agree at the precision under consideration.
The pair may change with the precision; Section 26
establishes the uniform estimates needed for this selection.
Corollary 1.4 (Internal lower-box exceptional sets).
For every bounded E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{x\in E:\underline{\dim}_{\mathrm{B}}\Delta_{x}E<\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0}\}\leq 1.
(1.5)
For \frac{4}{5}\leq q<\frac{7}{8}, one also has
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{x\in E:\underline{\dim}_{\mathrm{B}}\Delta_{x}E\leq q\}\leq\frac{5q-1}{3},\qquad\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{x\in E:\underline{\dim}_{\mathrm{B}}\Delta_{x}E<\frac{7}{8}\}\leq\frac{9}{8}.
No measurability assumption on E is required.
These are internal lower-box statements. Theorem 1.1
concerns Hausdorff dimension and pins anywhere in \mathbb{R}^{2}.
Under the common dyadic mass law of Theorem D.2,
matching profiles persist along all sequences of precisions, and
the bound L_{*}(s) also holds for pinned Hausdorff dimension.
Appendix D treats that case.
Fiedler and Stull combine the dimension parameters of the two endpoints
into a common minimum in part of their argument, and observe in
footnote 7 that keeping them separate could improve the bound at the
cost of more calculations [7, §4.3, footnote 7]. The next theorem
carries out this refinement, keeping the source dimension
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E and the exceptional-set parameter p separate.
Theorem 1.5 (Pinned exceptional sets).
Let E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} be analytic with \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1,
and let 1\leq p<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E. Put
B(p,t)=\min\left\{1,\frac{p+t(3-p)}{5-p}\right\}.
Then
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E<B(p,\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E)\}\leq p.
In particular, \dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E\geq\min\{1,\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E}{2}\} outside an exceptional set of dimension
at most one.
The proof of Theorem 1.1 controls the directions of the
source points as seen from each pin. Controlling also the directions of the
pins as seen from each source point leads to a second estimate, which is better
when S_{\mathrm{rel}}<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E<S_{f}. Let
\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{rel}}
\displaystyle=1+\frac{5-8\alpha_{*}-3\alpha_{*}^{2}}{6\alpha_{*}^{2}-48\alpha_{*}+30}=1.124334\ldots,
\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{switch}}
\displaystyle=1+\frac{5\alpha_{*}^{2}-\alpha_{*}-1}{10\alpha_{*}^{2}+2\alpha_{*}-6}=1.175876\ldots,
\displaystyle S_{f}
\displaystyle=1+\frac{6+10\alpha_{*}-\sqrt{(6+10\alpha_{*})^{2}-48\alpha_{*}(1+2\alpha_{*})}}{24\alpha_{*}}=1.197246\ldots.
For S_{\mathrm{rel}}\leq t\leq S_{f}, define
Q_{\mathrm{rel}}(t)=\min\Biggl\{1-\frac{5(1-\alpha_{*})(3-2t)}{6\{1+2\alpha_{*}+2(1-2\alpha_{*})t\}},\quad 1-\frac{3-2t}{4+6\alpha_{*}-4\alpha_{*}t}\Biggr\}.
(1.6)
The first expression is the smaller one up to S_{\mathrm{switch}}.
The second is the smaller one after that point.
Theorem 1.6 (A second pinned bound).
Let E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} be analytic with
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\in[S_{\mathrm{rel}},S_{f}]. Then
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E<Q_{\mathrm{rel}}(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E)\}\leq 1.
For S_{\mathrm{rel}}<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E<S_{f}, the bound Q_{\mathrm{rel}}(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E) exceeds
Q(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E), Liu’s bound \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E-\frac{1}{3} [19, Theorem 1.1], and \frac{1}{4}+\frac{\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E}{2} of Theorem 1.5.
At t=S_{\mathrm{rel}}, Q_{\mathrm{rel}}(t)=Q(t). At t=S_{f},
Q_{\mathrm{rel}}(t)=t-\frac{1}{3}, Liu’s bound.
For example, Q_{\mathrm{rel}}(1.18)=0.856983\ldots, while
Q(1.18)=0.853434\ldots. It does not change
the universal pinned constant q_{*}.
Above the threshold q_{*} we also bound the exceptional set.
Theorem 1.7 (Exceptional sets above q_{*}).
Let E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} be analytic with \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1.
(a)
For q_{*}\leq q\leq\frac{19}{24},
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E<q\}\leq 21-6\alpha_{*}-\frac{15-3\alpha_{*}}{q}.
(b)
For 0<k\leq\frac{1}{6} with k<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E-1,
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E<P(1+k)\}\leq 1+k,
where
P(t)=\frac{t^{3}+(3\alpha_{*}-2)t^{2}-(21\alpha_{*}+4)t+15\alpha_{*}+20}{9t^{2}-(6\alpha_{*}+39)t+50}.
The right side in (a) equals 1 at q=q_{*}, increases with q, and
equals \frac{39-42\alpha_{*}}{19}=1.2322\ldots at q=\frac{19}{24}.
The function P is increasing on [1,\frac{7}{6}], with P(1)=q_{*} and
P(\frac{7}{6})=0.8612\ldots. In particular, if 1<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\leq\frac{7}{6},
then \dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{x\in E:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E\geq q\}=\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E for
every q<P(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E).
Part (a) does not depend on the source dimension. Part (b) uses the source dimension
through the same iteration as Q(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E), and P(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E)>Q(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E) for
1<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\leq\frac{7}{6}.
For \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1+k part (b) improves part (a), whose
threshold at exceptional dimension 1+k is smaller than P(1+k).
At exceptional dimension 1+k, part (b) improves
Theorem 1.5 precisely when
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E<\frac{(4-k)P(1+k)-1-k}{2-k}.
For 1<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E<1.13, its last assertion improves both
R(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E) of Fiedler–Stull [7, Corollary 2]
and Liu’s bound \frac{4\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E-2}{3} for
\sup_{x\in E}\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E [19, Theorem 1.1].
Table 1 records the principal comparisons.
In its pinned rows, each estimate holds outside a set of pins in
\mathbb{R}^{2} of Hausdorff dimension at most one. For the table,
write t=\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E and
U(t)=\max\left\{\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0},Y(t),R(t)\right\}.
Table 1: Selected lower bounds obtained here and their gains over the
best previously known bounds in the corresponding settings, with
t=\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E. All gains are strictly positive throughout
the indicated ranges.

The earlier estimates are due to Stull [33, Theorem 1],
Fiedler–Stull [7, Corollary 2], and
Liu [19, Theorem 1.1].
The first two rows compare bounds that are uniform as t decreases
to one. The remaining rows compare bounds at a fixed t.
For unpinned distances, the earlier bound is the larger of
R(t) and \frac{4t-2}{3} on the displayed ranges.
These bounds cross at t=5-\sqrt{15}=1.127016\ldots.
Liu’s bound reaches \frac{7}{8} at t=\frac{37}{32},
whereas Y(t) reaches it at t=\frac{11-\sqrt{41}}{4}.
For pinned distances at exceptional dimension one, the earlier
bound is the larger of Stull’s \frac{1}{2}+\frac{t}{4} and
Liu’s t-\frac{1}{3}, which cross at t=\frac{10}{9}.
Fiedler–Stull’s stronger dimension-dependent estimate in
[7, Corollary 2] does not give this exceptional-dimension-one
conclusion.
Let
\tau=\frac{5+14\alpha_{*}-\sqrt{25-8\alpha_{*}^{2}}}{12\alpha_{*}}=1.191685897\ldots.
Q(t) exceeds Liu’s bound for 1<t<\tau.
Together, Q(t) and Q_{\mathrm{rel}}(t) extend this to 1<t<S_{f}, and beyond S_{f}
Liu’s bound is stronger and implies full dimension for
t\geq\frac{4}{3}.
For example, at t=1.1 the unpinned bound increases from
R(1.1)=0.817948\ldots to Y(1.1)=0.849576\ldots,
and at t=1.18 the pinned bound increases from
0.846666\ldots to Q_{\mathrm{rel}}(1.18)=0.856983\ldots.
1.1 Earlier results and the inputs to this paper
Falconer proved positive Lebesgue measure of the planar distance set
above dimension \frac{3}{2} [6].
Wolff lowered this
threshold to \frac{4}{3} [36]. Guth, Iosevich, Ou and Wang
proved the positive-measure conclusion above \frac{5}{4}, including
a pinned conclusion with a pin in E [11]. The present paper
concerns dimensions close to one, where the dimension problem remains
open without additional assumptions.
Bourgain’s work, following Katz–Tao, gave a uniform improvement over
the elementary exponent \frac{1}{2} at source dimension one
[2, 15]. For sources of dimension greater than one,
Keleti–Shmerkin obtained the universal unpinned bound \frac{37}{54}
and the pinned bound \frac{2}{3}\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E outside a set of
pins of dimension at most one when
1<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\leq\frac{3}{2} [17, Theorems 1.4 and 1.1].
Shmerkin improved the universal unpinned exponent to \frac{40}{57}
and obtained the pinned exponent \frac{29}{42} outside a set of
pins of dimension at most one [30, Theorems 1.1 and 1.2]. Their treatment of information across scales provides
a measure-theoretic precursor to our use of information at
intermediate scales.
The point-to-set principle of J. H. Lutz and N. Lutz expresses
Hausdorff dimension through the description lengths of individual
points [21]. Lutz–Stull developed estimates for these
description lengths under orthogonal projection, the map
z\mapsto z\cdot v for a unit vector v [22].
Stull used this approach to obtain the pinned bound quoted
above [33]. With the covering number N_{r}(F) defined above, the upper box dimension of F
is \overline{\dim}_{\mathrm{B}}F=\limsup_{r\searrow 0}\frac{\log N_{r}(F)}{\log(\frac{1}{r})}. The packing dimension is
\dim_{\mathrm{P}}E=\inf_{E\subseteq\bigcup_{i}E_{i}}\sup_{i}\overline{\dim}_{\mathrm{B}}E_{i},
where the infimum is over countable covers by bounded sets
[23, Chapter 5]. Fiedler–Stull refined the scale
partitions and retained both Hausdorff and packing dimension
parameters [7, Theorems 1 and 5]. At planar
packing dimension two, their expression becomes
\min\{1,\frac{d(4-d)}{5-d}\} for 1<d<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E, with
an exceptional-pin dimension bound d. Sections 3
and 4 specify the information estimates retained from
these proofs. References to their equation and lemma numbers use the
listed arXiv versions: [33], v2, and [7], v1.
Appendix A
contains the retained prefix calculation and its endpoint case.
Liu’s L^{2} method provides additional dimension-dependent estimates
[19]. At exceptional-pin dimension one, it yields the threshold
\min\{1,\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E-\frac{1}{3}\}. It also implies
\sup_{x\in E}\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E\geq\min\left\{\frac{4\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E-2}{3},1\right\}.
The supremum is needed because of the correction to the pinned endpoint
in [19]. The pin achieving an exponent close to the right side may
depend on that exponent. Liu states these results for compact E.
They extend to analytic E by inner approximation with compact subsets.
Regularity can lead to stronger conclusions. A compact set E of positive diameter is
s-Ahlfors–David regular if it supports a finite Borel measure
\mu with support equal to the set and
cr^{s}\leq\mu(B(z,r))\leq Cr^{s} for all points z of the set and
0<r\leq\operatorname{diam}E.
The constants are positive and
independent of z,r. Orponen proved that the distance sets of such
planar sets have packing dimension one when s\geq 1 [26]. Shmerkin obtained
full pinned Hausdorff dimension when
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E=\dim_{\mathrm{P}}E>1 [29].
Fiedler–Stull cover further ranges of these two dimensions
[7, 8]. Shmerkin–Wang proved that for every d\geq 2 and every Borel
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d} with
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E=\dim_{\mathrm{P}}E=\frac{d}{2},
\sup_{x\in E}\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E=1
[31, Theorem 1.4]. Liu recently proved that if E,F\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} are Borel, \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1, \dim_{\mathrm{H}}F=\dim_{\mathrm{P}}F and \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E+\dim_{\mathrm{H}}F>2, then \Delta_{x}E has positive Lebesgue measure for some x\in F [20, Theorem 1.1]. At source dimension at most one, Cholak, Csörnyei, N. Lutz,
P. Lutz, Mayordomo and Stull proved the pinned supremum bound
\frac{3}{4}\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E for analytic E [3, Theorem 6].
A closely related combination of algorithmic information and
incidence geometry appears in recent work of Csörnyei and
Stull [5]. They extend the Ren–Wang incidence
estimate to hyperplanes in higher dimensions and prove that,
for every Borel set E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} with
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>\frac{d}{2}, where d=3 or 4,
\sup_{x\in E}\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E\geq\frac{2}{3}.
Their proof converts incidence estimates into conditional
Kolmogorov complexity bounds and combines them over intervals
of scales. These intervals are selected by a decomposition of
complexity functions based on Jones’s traveling salesman theorem
[5, Section 5]. In dimension two, their
incidence theorem specializes to the Ren–Wang estimate used
here. Our planar improvement combines information retained
at both endpoints with a common source measure to obtain
incompatible lower and upper counts for rich perpendicular
bisectors.
Our geometric argument uses further inputs. The radial projection
about x sends y\neq x to its direction
\pi_{x}(y)=\frac{y-x}{|y-x|} on the unit circle
S^{1}=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|z|=1\}. Orponen’s theorem controls the
distribution of these directions [25]. The crossing lemma, in the circle-drawing tradition
of Székely, turns many circle incidences into repeated pairs
[34]. The restricted-sum estimate of Katz–Tardos bounds from below
the number of bisector directions produced by rows, meaning
ordered lists of source points assigned to a common circle [16], and the max-flow min-cut theorem of
Ford–Fulkerson turns this into a simultaneous choice of one
direction per row [9]. The Furstenberg estimate of Ren–Wang
bounds the number of rich bisector tubes from below [28], and
the incidence estimate of Fu–Gan–Ren for rectangularly spaced
balls bounds it from above [10]. We state the precise forms
needed when they enter the proof.
A related use of rigidity occurs in the work of Héra, Shmerkin
and Yavicoli on Furstenberg sets [12, Section 4.1].
There, a configuration close to the elementary bound has a
restricted incidence structure, which is incompatible with
Bourgain’s projection theorem. Here the starting constraint is
equality in the reciprocal information estimates. It forces a
common interval of excess information, from which we obtain
rich bisectors. The final contradiction uses the geometric
estimates specified above.
Several features of the proof also have counterparts in work on the
Erdős similarity conjecture. This conjecture asks whether, for
every infinite A\subset\mathbb{R}, there is a measurable set of
positive Lebesgue measure containing no set x+\lambda A with
x\in\mathbb{R} and \lambda\neq 0.
Shmerkin and Yavicoli [32, Section 3.2] use
probabilistic selection to construct one small set whose sums with
every member of a prescribed family cover a fixed region. The cost
of treating the family simultaneously is logarithmic in the number
of discrete tests. The same requirement of a common choice enters
Lemmas 17.1 and 17.7 below.
The source sample must serve every retained pin, and the pin sample
must preserve the witnesses for every retained source pair.
Separate choices would lose the shared incidence structure.
A second connection concerns approximate coincidences of sums.
Mora Cuellar, Iosevich, Kulkarni, Rojas Aravena and
Yavicoli [24, Section 3.2] bound their number for two
lacunary sequences, meaning positive sequences (a_{j}) satisfying
a_{j+1}\leq qa_{j} for some fixed 0<q<1.
Their estimate allows many separated sums to be extracted without
requiring the two sequences to occupy different ranges of scales.
Here sums of angular coordinates determine bisector directions,
so a related counting question enters Section 18.
Our angular rows need not be lacunary, and we use the restricted-sum
estimate of Katz–Tardos under the hypotheses available to us.
The lattice construction of Iosevich and
Yavicoli [14, Section 2] illustrates a further role for
information across scales. Sufficient branching between successive
lattice scales allows the construction of an infinite set A whose
triple sumset A+A+A=\{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}:a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in A\} lies in the
limiting set. In the present argument, excess information over a
common interval of scales produces the mass bounds from which the
rich bisectors are extracted. Both arguments use how branching is
distributed across scales. The arithmetic alignment of the lattice
construction, however, is additional structure that a general
Frostman measure need not possess.
There is also a comparison with the work of Iosevich, Kulkarni,
Mora Cuéllar, Rojas Aravena and Yavicoli on Rajchman
measures [13, Lemma 2.3 and Proposition 3.2].
A Rajchman probability measure is a Borel probability measure whose
Fourier–Stieltjes transform tends to zero at infinity. For such a
measure with compact support, that qualitative decay yields a density
statement modulo one for all sufficiently large dilates of the
support. This leads to a single set excluding all nontrivial affine
copies of that support. Our endpoint stability argument likewise
separates a qualitative limiting statement from the finite-scale
geometry, with the measure affecting the starting precision.
Here the required input is supplied by information profiles and
incidence estimates. No Rajchman hypothesis is imposed.
These comparisons describe related uses of selection, additive
structure and scale information. The estimates needed for the
distance problem are proved below.
1.2 The bisector results as separate statements
For distinct y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}, their perpendicular bisector is
\mathcal{B}(y,z)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|x-y|=|x-z|\}.
The perpendicular bisectors \mathcal{B}(y,z) defined above will
be treated as a family of lines.
Write a line as L_{n,c}=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:n\cdot z=c\}, where n\in S^{1}
is a unit normal and c\in\mathbb{R} is its offset. The parameters
(n,c) and (-n,-c) represent the same unoriented line. We give
this space the metric
\operatorname{dist}(L_{n,c},L_{n^{\prime},c^{\prime}})=\min\{|n-n^{\prime}|+|c-c^{\prime}|,|n+n^{\prime}|+|c+c^{\prime}|\}.
The dimension of a family of lines is taken in this metric. A tube is a neighborhood of a line
of specified width. Relative to a finite pin set, a tube is called
k-rich if it contains at least k pins.
Several geometric statements below are independent of the information
theory. Here the Hausdorff dimension of a family of lines is computed
in the space of affine lines equipped with the metric above,
not as the dimension of their union in \mathbb{R}^{2}. Proposition 15.1 establishes the elementary
identity
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{\mathcal{B}(y,z):z\in F\}=\dim_{\mathrm{H}}F\qquad\text{for }y\notin F.
It follows that the set of all bisectors determined by E has
Hausdorff dimension at least \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E when this dimension
is positive. The reflection map proves this identity directly.
It supplies a Hausdorff-dimension statement to accompany the finite
results.
The finite geometric estimates used below have their own hypotheses.
They concern approximate circle drawings, rows of points with rich
bisectors, the distribution of their directions, and rich tubes for
point sets with controlled spacing. These statements can also be
used independently of the description-length estimates below.
1.3 How the proofs work
A short description of a distance places constraints on both
endpoints. The estimates of Stull and Fiedler–Stull express these
constraints at each scale. We retain their dependence on the
information at both endpoints. In the unpinned problem, the resulting
inequalities can be applied in both directions. They force the
scalar bound \frac{4}{5} and identify the profiles near equality. For a prescribed pin they need not do so. They instead
force a particular distribution of pin and source mass, which we
use to construct a finite configuration of circles and points.
The geometric contradiction concerns one family of perpendicular
bisectors. Each bisector has many pins near it. The source points
on the circles provide many possible bisector directions, and
Katz–Tardos and Ren–Wang turn those choices into a lower bound
for the number of distinct bisectors. The distribution of the pins
provides an upper bound for the same number. The improvement occurs
when these bounds are incompatible. Thus information theory determines
the configuration that a small distance set would require, and
incidence geometry rules out that configuration.
There are two reasons for keeping more information than a single
dimension exponent. The first is that information may accumulate
unevenly across scales. Information missing on one interval has to
appear on another, and the projection estimates can use its location.
The second is that all pins see the same source set. A mass bound
for one source measure can be used simultaneously in the circle
drawings at different pins. Choosing a separate source measure for
each pin would lose the shared vertices needed for the counting
argument. We now explain both points and how the estimates fit
together.
Let x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2} be distinct. The line segment joining them is
[x,y]=\{(1-t^{\prime})x+t^{\prime}y:t^{\prime}\in[0,1]\},
and its length is |x-y|. We measure information by program length.
Fix a universal binary machine with prefix-free programs, meaning
that no admissible program is a proper initial segment of another.
An oracle A\subseteq\mathbb{N} supplies background information.
The machine may ask whether an integer belongs to A without
including the answer in its program. The oracle is fixed before
the points and the sequence of precisions are chosen.
For z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}, let K_{r}^{A}(z) be the length of a shortest
program, using A, that locates z within distance 2^{-r}.
The notation K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|) also supplies the complete pin x,
so the program describes only the distance. Section 2.2
specifies the encodings and the distinction between supplying a
whole point and supplying only a coarse approximation. These are
the conventions of [18, 33, 7].
To record where the information lies, choose terminal precisions
r_{j}\to\infty and write u\in[0,1] for a fraction of terminal
precision. Along a common subsequence we have
\displaystyle f(u)
\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{K_{\lfloor ur_{j}\rfloor}^{A}(y)}{r_{j}},
\displaystyle g(u)
\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{K_{\lfloor ur_{j}\rfloor}^{A}(x)}{r_{j}},
\displaystyle d_{x}(u)
\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{K_{\lfloor ur_{j}\rfloor}^{A,x}(|x-y|)}{r_{j}},
\displaystyle d_{y}(u)
\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{K_{\lfloor ur_{j}\rfloor}^{A,y}(|x-y|)}{r_{j}}.
Here \lfloor v\rfloor and \lceil v\rceil denote integer rounding
down and up, respectively, and the complexities at precision zero
are set equal to zero. These functions are the information profiles.
The source profile is f, the pin profile is g, and the distance
has one profile for each choice of pin. The points remain fixed.
Section 2.4 proves uniform convergence of all four
profiles along one subsequence.
The pairs used in the proof have directions requiring nearly one bit
per unit of precision, even after either endpoint is supplied.
Also, supplying one endpoint does not substantially shorten a
description of the other. Orponen’s radial projection theorem and
the measure-coding argument allow us to select such pairs. Under
these hypotheses, the prefix estimate reads
d_{x}(u)\geq\frac{u}{2}+\frac{f(u)}{4}.
It says how much information already accumulated at the source must
have reached the distance by scale u. At the lowest profile
f(u)=u, this is d_{x}(u)\geq\frac{3u}{4}. At the largest planar
profile f(u)=2u, it is d_{x}(u)\geq u. To improve the first bound,
we must use how the profiles change between scales.
This is the purpose of the interval transfer estimate. Suppose a
coarse location of y and its distance from x are known. Another
possible source point z must be near that location and satisfy
|x-z|=|x-y|. Thus x lies on the perpendicular bisector of
[y,z]. The points y,z determine a projection of x, at a
precision governed by their separation. The projection estimates
of Stull and Fiedler–Stull therefore put a lower bound on the
description length of every competitor z. If all competitors
require many bits, the distance and the coarse location must
together contain many bits as well.
The transfer threshold H_{f,g}(a,b) in (4.4) is the least
of these competitor costs, capped by the source information at
the terminal scale. Lemma 4.1 states the resulting bound
d_{x}(b)-d_{x}(a)\geq[H_{f,g}(a,b)-f(a)]_{+}.
The subtraction of f(a) accounts for the coarse source information
already supplied. Partitioning the projection calculation into
intervals with different patterns of information growth strengthens
H to \widehat{H} in Lemma 7.2. Both estimates
retain the two endpoint profiles.
For the unpinned problem, a short description of |x-y| remains
short after either endpoint is supplied. We may therefore assume
that both d_{x}(1) and d_{y}(1) are small and use the transfer
inequalities in both orientations. A common cutoff is a scale
after which all competitors in either orientation cost at least
a specified amount of information, called the transfer level.
Prefix and transfer then bound both endpoint profiles from below
at the cutoff. Their Lipschitz bounds carry some of this information
to earlier scales, where it strengthens the next transfer estimate.
The final contradiction uses the least possible common cutoff.
At this scale one orientation must have a competitor whose cost
equals the transfer level. Otherwise the cutoff could be moved
slightly earlier. We retain the two separate endpoint excesses,
apply the partition estimates in both directions, and return to
this equality. The resulting lower bound exceeds the value allowed
by the equality. Section 10 proves in this way that
d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1) cannot both be smaller than \frac{4}{5}.
Keeping the source dimension in the transfer level also yields
Y(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E) in Section 11. The fixed-pair
construction and the point-to-set principle in
Section 5 convert these conclusions into bounds
for \dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta E. Proposition 31.1
shows why the stated scalar system stops at \frac{4}{5}.
At equality, however, the profiles have much more structure.
They grow at the maximal rate before \frac{1}{5} and remain
above u+\frac{1}{5} until \frac{3}{5}. This interval of
excess information allows the geometric count to pass the scalar
endpoint. Compactness makes the same contradiction valid in a
fixed neighborhood of \frac{4}{5}.
For a prescribed pin, this argument has a specific failure. A
description that is short when x is supplied may be long when
only y is supplied. Thus d_{x}(1) may be small while d_{y}(1) is
large. Proposition 7.3 exhibits abstract
profiles satisfying all the scalar inequalities with d_{x}(1)
arbitrarily close to \frac{3}{4} and d_{y}(1)=1. The pinned
improvement must therefore use a relation among many pairs.
Suppose that the set of pins where the proposed bound fails has
Hausdorff dimension greater than one. We choose a source probability
measure \mu and a pin probability measure \nu, supported on
separated compact sets, both with Frostman exponent greater than
one. A relation of pairs is a Borel set G in the product of these
supports. Its fiber at a pin is G_{x}=\{y:(x,y)\in G\}. We retain
one such relation with
(\nu\times\mu)(G)=\int\mu(G_{x})d\nu(x)>0.
The radial projection theorem supplies bounded angular densities
on this relation after restriction. It is these restrictions of
the same original measures that are used throughout the geometry.
Fix a proposed distance bound q. For every pair in the retained
relation, the inequality K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\leq qn holds at infinitely
many precisions. These precisions may differ from pair to pair.
Lemma 20.4 finds infinitely many
common precisions at which the pairs satisfying this inequality
have total mass at least n^{-2}. At one such precision there are
at most 2^{qn+O(\log n)} occupied radius bins per pin. Indeed,
there are only that many short programs, and an approximation to
a radius identifies its bin up to a bounded choice. Thus the small
distance set produces many source incidences on relatively few
circles at each pin.
The profile argument supplies two further facts at these precisions.
First, g(u)-u\geq W throughout an interval [u_{-},u_{+}] of controlled
length, with W>0. This is the pin plateau. The profile need not be
constant on that interval. Coding the pin measure turns the plateau
into upper bounds for pin mass over a range of scales. Second, the
source reaches a prescribed amount of information early enough
that a lower bound for the radius complexity remains after a
coarse source location has been supplied. This is the source-parent
estimate in Section 14.
The cancellation in that second fact is worth displaying. Set
\delta=2^{-n}, and let C be a dyadic parent square containing
y. A dyadic square has the form
2^{-k}([i,i+1)\times[j,j+1)), and a parent is a containing square
at a coarser level. If J is the radius interval of length \delta
containing |x-y|, put
p_{C,J}=\mu\{z\in C:|x-z|\in J\}.
The transfer estimate, the source coding identity, and the code
for the radial distribution of \frac{\mu|_{C}}{\mu(C)} imply,
up to the selectable small errors,
\lambda n+\log_{2}\mu(C)\leq K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|\mid C)\leq-\log_{2}p_{C,J}+\log_{2}\mu(C).
The parent mass cancels, leaving p_{C,J}\leq\delta^{\lambda} at
leading exponent order. Proposition 14.2
states the precise bound with its error tolerance. It holds for
the original source measure in every occupied parent and annulus.
Thus many incidences cannot all be hidden in one small part of
a circle. If the source dimension is larger, its stronger profile
lower bound permits a larger transfer level \lambda, as proved
in Section 8.5.
We now select source squares once for all pins and use their centers
as vertices. An assigned incidence records a retained pair, its
source square, and its radius bin. The vertex lies within
O(\delta) of the associated circle. The source-parent mass bound
controls how many assigned vertices can occur in one parent on
that circle. The common-scale mass lower bound allows the selections
to lose fixed powers of n. Their logarithms are o(n), so they
do not change the limiting exponents.
Join consecutive assigned vertices along the circles. A drawing
with many edges but controlled crossings must repeat many source
pairs. A repeated pair y,z is seen from many circle centers.
Those centers lie within distance O(\frac{\delta}{|y-z|}) of
the perpendicular bisector of [y,z]. A witnessing pin is a pin
to which both source vertices were assigned on the same circle.
We have therefore constructed bisector tubes with many witnesses,
as illustrated in Figure 1.
The drawing and pruning arguments also provide short lists of
vertices on a circle, called rows, from which pairs can be chosen.
yzx_{1}x_{2}The same source pair lieson circles about different pins.The circle centers lie onthe perpendicular bisector.With incidence error O(\delta),the centers lie in a tube of widthO(\frac{\delta}{|y-z|}).One source pair, many witnessing pins
Figure 1: Why repeated circle incidences produce rich bisectors.
The two circles shown pass exactly through y,z. The finite
construction has errors of order \delta, so its witnesses lie
near the bisector. The shaded tube is enlarged for visibility.
The same source vertices are used at every pin.
The lower count uses the freedom in this choice. On an exact circle,
if two source points have angles \theta_{i},\theta_{j} about the pin,
their bisector direction is \frac{\theta_{i}+\theta_{j}}{2} modulo
\pi. Our approximate circles obey the corresponding relation
with controlled errors. After removing repeated angular entries,
the Katz–Tardos restricted-sum estimate bounds the number of
available directions for collections of rows. A large total number
of directions would not by itself suffice, since they might cluster in
a short angular interval. The matching argument in
Section 18 chooses one pair per row while
controlling the directions at every intermediate scale. Ren–Wang’s
Furstenberg theorem can then be applied to the pins and their
selected tube directions. It forces many distinct bisector tubes.
The first upper count uses the same tubes and their witnessing pins.
The pin plateau controls concentration in rectangles. The sampling
argument of Section 19 converts that
control into the rectangular occupancy required by Fu–Gan–Ren.
Their incidence theorem limits how many distinct tubes can each
contain the required number of pins. Comparing this upper count
with the Furstenberg lower count proves
Q(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E), including the universal bound q_{*}.
For the second upper count, keep the angular bound as seen from
the source endpoint as well as from the generating pin. For a
source pair separated by \ell, a length-D portion of its
bisector subtends an angle of order \ell D from that endpoint,
in our separated regions. The reverse angular bound therefore
limits how many witnesses of this pair can lie in one small
square. A rich pair must have witnesses in many such squares.
Lemma 23.2 retains the dependence on their
coarse tube occupancy and turns this additional distribution
information into a second upper count. It improves the pinned
bound to Q_{\mathrm{rel}}(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E) on the interval
specified in Theorem 1.6.
The distinction between these counts is useful. Katz–Tardos
controls the choices of directions, Ren–Wang turns distributed
directions into a lower count, and the upper incidence estimates
use the distribution of witnesses. All three refer to the same
finite configuration. Figure 2 records these
dependencies.

Keeping the pin Frostman exponent in the same lower count yields
the quantitative exceptional-set estimates of
Section 25. Appendix D
combines the matching scalar estimate with the geometric argument
when almost every pair has matching information profiles. The elementary
scalar proofs are retained in
Appendices B and C.
Section 31 identifies the limitations of the scalar
inequalities and the separate geometric comparisons.
Appendix E treats their joint
optimization.
2 Preliminaries
We collect the geometric conventions and the definitions of information
used in the proofs.
The basic object is a shortest program producing a rational point
within a prescribed error. All programs below use the same optimal
machine. The pair (x,y) and the background oracle A remain fixed
as the error tends to zero. This is what will allow a statement about
individual points to imply a Hausdorff-dimension bound.
2.1 Sets, measures, and geometric scales
The Hausdorff measure \mathcal{H}^{s}, Hausdorff and packing
dimensions, and Frostman and Ahlfors–David bounds were defined in
the introduction. See also [23, Chapters 4, 5, 8]. We will use countable stability of Hausdorff dimension. The dimension of a countable union is
the supremum of the dimensions of its members [23, Chapter 4].
The restriction \mu|_{F} is the
measure B\mapsto\mu(B\cap F), and its normalization is
\frac{\mu|_{F}}{\mu(F)} when 0<\mu(F)<\infty.
Frostman’s lemma says that every analytic set of dimension greater
than s contains a compact subset carrying an s-Frostman
probability measure [23, Chapter 8]. Its energy
I_{t}(\mu)=\iint|x-y|^{-t}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)
is finite for 0<t<s. We apply the radial projection \pi_{x} only on source regions
separated from x. For a measurable map T, its pushforward
T_{*}\mu is defined by (T_{*}\mu)(B)=\mu(T^{-1}(B)). A measure is
absolutely continuous with respect to another if it assigns zero
mass to every set of zero mass for the other. A density of \nu
with respect to \mu is a nonnegative measurable function h
such that \nu(B)=\int_{B}hd\mu for every measurable B.
This is also called the Radon–Nikodym derivative. Angular
Lebesgue measure means arclength
on S^{1}. A bound (\pi_{x})_{*}\mu\leq L\mathcal{H}^{1} means that every
arc J has preimage mass at most L|J|, where |J| is its length.
For a Borel relation G we use the fibers G_{x}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:(x,y)\in G\}
and G^{y}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:(x,y)\in G\} and the pair mass (\nu\times\mu)(G).
For a set F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m},
\operatorname{dist}(z,F)=\inf_{w\in F}|z-w| and
F^{(r)}=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\operatorname{dist}(z,F)<r\}. A set is
r-separated if distinct points have distance at least r.
A fixed enlargement of a square is its concentric dilation by a
fixed factor. We use the Hausdorff metric on nonempty compact sets,
d_{\mathrm{H}}(K,K^{\prime})=\max\left\{\sup_{z\in K}\operatorname{dist}(z,K^{\prime}),\quad\sup_{w\in K^{\prime}}\operatorname{dist}(w,K)\right\}.
A map is L-Lipschitz if it increases distances by at most a factor
L.
It is bi-Lipschitz if it is one-to-one and it and its inverse
are Lipschitz. Such maps preserve Hausdorff dimension
[23, Chapter 7]. A lower envelope for a function is
another function lying below it at every argument. We also call a
lower bound a barrier, and a constant lower bound a floor.
For functions on a fixed compact interval,
\|f-g\|_{\infty}=\sup_{u}|f(u)-g(u)| is their uniform distance.
A functional is lower semicontinuous if its value at a limit is
at most the lower limit of its values along every convergent
sequence.
Upper semicontinuity reverses this inequality and uses
the upper limit. For sets R_{n}, the set
\limsup_{n}R_{n}=\bigcap_{N}\bigcup_{n\geq N}R_{n} consists of points
belonging to infinitely many R_{n}.
We write [t]_{+}=\max\{t,0\}, \#F for cardinality, and 1_{G} for
the indicator of G, equal to one on G and zero outside it.
Logarithms are to base two. Dyadic cubes in \mathbb{R}^{m} are the
half-open products \prod_{j=1}^{m}[2^{-n}k_{j},2^{-n}(k_{j}+1)).
In the plane they are squares. We write \mathcal{D}_{r} for the grid
of dyadic squares of side r, and Q_{n}(z) for the unique
level-n cube containing z. A dyadic parent or ancestor is a
containing cube at a specified coarser level. A dyadic interval
has the analogous one-dimensional meaning. For z\neq 0,
\arg z denotes its angle modulo 2\pi.
An angular chart chooses
a real representative on an arc shorter than a full circle.
The notation A\lesssim B means A\leq CB with the stated
parameters fixed, and A\gtrsim B means B\lesssim A, and
A\asymp B or A\sim B means both inequalities. The notation
A\ll B requires a sufficiently small ratio \frac{A}{B}, or a
ratio tending to zero when a limit is specified. As usual,
O(B) denotes a quantity bounded in absolute value by CB and
o(B) one whose ratio to B tends to zero in the indicated limit.
A loss
\delta^{-\tau}, where \tau>0 is fixed and small, is called a
small power loss. It is distinguished from a fixed multiplicative
constant, which does not change a dimension exponent.
We use the line-space metric defined in the introduction.
In local coordinates, write a line as
L_{\theta,c}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\cdot x=c\},
where \theta is the angle of its unit normal and c is its
signed offset from the origin. If \theta ranges over a fixed
interval of length less than \pi and c remains bounded, then
\operatorname{dist}(L_{\theta,c},L_{\theta^{\prime},c^{\prime}})\asymp|\theta-\theta^{\prime}|+|c-c^{\prime}|.
The comparison constants depend only on these parameter ranges.
A \rho-tube is a neighborhood of width
C\rho of a line, with C fixed. Tube separation means separation
of their line parameters. A family of \rho-tubes is
parameter-separated if the defining lines of distinct members have
line-space distance at least c\rho, where c>0 is fixed.
These conventions make the covering numbers of line families precise. Dyadic slope–intercept tubes
used in the Ren–Wang estimate are introduced in
Section 18. The overlap condition for the
tubes and balls in the Fu–Gan–Ren estimate is given in
Section 19.
Several letters are used with different meanings in different parts
of the paper.
We list them here, with the places where they are defined. The terminal resolution is \delta=2^{-n}, except in
Lemma 23.2 and its proof, where \delta<\Delta
are two auxiliary dyadic scales.
In Part III, \eta denotes the side length
of the dyadic squares, called source parents, into which
the source is grouped. In Section 23 it also
denotes a small exponent loss. The dyadic scales \ell_{j} are used
to classify the angular separations of source vertices
about a pin. They determine the candidate widths
\rho_{j}=\frac{\delta}{\ell_{j}} of the tubes about perpendicular
bisectors. We write \rho for the selected width
(Sections 17 and 18).
In the proof of Lemma 4.1, \ell_{j} instead denotes a
precision. The pins are selected at resolution \delta and later thinned to
resolution \rho. The letters \beta,\beta_{0} denote deficits of
lower envelopes in Sections C,
11 and 10, while in Part III the
letter \beta is the exponent loss in the source mass cap
b=\delta^{\lambda-\beta} (Section 17). The letter
\lambda denotes the transfer level 1+\Delta of Sections 8 and 11. Written without a set
argument, \Delta is this excess or the auxiliary scale above, never a
distance set. The letters b, k, B and Q also have local
meanings, fixed where they are introduced. We use \varrho for a
distance value, such as |x-y|, in the finite-precision complexities
defined below relative to the oracle (A,x). The superscript A denotes the
fixed oracle. Scalar cutoffs such as A_{0} are local to the
statements defining them. The constant \eta_{0} is reserved for
the uniform gain in Theorem 1.2.
The plateau parameters
u_{-},u_{+},W,\varsigma,\chi,\mathfrak{e} are defined in
Proposition 8.5. The subscripts in H_{f,g} and \widehat{H}_{f,g} identify
the source profile f and the pin profile g, respectively
(Sections 4 and 7).
2.2 Complexity at finite precision
Use the fixed universal prefix-free oracle machine U of
Section 1.3, with the usual optimality
convention. Simulating another prefix-free machine adds at most a
constant depending on that machine. The following are standard
prefix-free complexity conventions [18].
Their
finite-precision versions are as in [33, 7, §2].
Write \{0,1\}^{*} for the set of finite binary strings.
For finite binary strings
\sigma,\tau, the conditional prefix-free Kolmogorov complexity of
\sigma given \tau is
K(\sigma\mid\tau)=\min\{|\pi|:\pi\in\{0,1\}^{*},\ U(\pi,\tau)=\sigma\},
the length of a shortest program that outputs \sigma when \tau is
supplied as auxiliary input, and K(\sigma) is the same minimum with
the empty string as auxiliary input. For an oracle A\subseteq\mathbb{N}, the
quantities K^{A}(\sigma) and K^{A}(\sigma\mid\tau) are defined in the
same way for a machine that may query membership in A. Omitting the
oracle superscript means that the oracle is empty. Rational
points q\in\mathbb{Q}^{m} are identified with their binary encodings
under a fixed computable bijection, so that K^{A}(q) and
K^{A}(p\mid q) are defined. Thus the finite-precision complexity defined in the introduction is
K_{r}^{A}(z)=\min\{K^{A}(q):q\in\mathbb{Q}^{m}\cap B(z,2^{-r})\}.
The convention K_{0}^{A}(z)=0 is used only at the initial profile scale.
The join A\oplus B of two oracles encodes A in the even
positions and B in the odd positions. A real point x used as
an oracle is encoded by the binary expansions of its coordinates,
with terminating expansions chosen at dyadic rationals and with
signs and integer parts included. Thus K^{A,x} uses the join of
A with this encoding and supplies arbitrarily precise rational
approximations to x. An oracle extends A if it computes A.
Its set need not contain A. For z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}, w\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
define finite conditioning by
K_{r,s}^{A}(z\mid w)=\max_{q\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}\cap B(w,2^{-s})}\min_{p\in\mathbb{Q}^{m}\cap B(z,2^{-r})}K^{A}(p\mid q).
Equal-precision conditioning is abbreviated by
K_{r}^{A}(z\mid w):=K_{r,r}^{A}(z\mid w). For conditioning points
w_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}, simultaneous finite conditioning means
K_{r}^{A}(z\mid w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n_{i}}\cap B(w_{i},2^{-r})\\
1\leq i\leq k\end{subarray}}\min_{p\in\mathbb{Q}^{m}\cap B(z,2^{-r})}K^{A}(p\mid\langle q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}\rangle),
where \langle q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}\rangle is a fixed computable encoding
of the ordered tuple as a finite string. The maximum makes this
complexity a bound for every admissible collection of conditioning
approximations. In contrast, real points in the oracle superscript
supply all their binary digits. The quantity K_{r,s}^{A}(z\mid z) is
the number of bits needed to pass from precision s to precision r
at the point z. A dyadic square Q is identified with the finite string that
encodes its level and position.
Thus K^{A}(Q) is defined, and
K_{n}^{A}(z\mid Q) denotes the minimum of K^{A}(p\mid Q) over rational
p within 2^{-n} of z. Joins of several oracles, as in
K^{A,G,x}, are formed in the same way as K^{A,x}. We use the standard
finite-precision chain rules, symmetry of information, and oracle
monotonicity (adding an oracle can only decrease a complexity, up to a
fixed simulation cost). The notation K_{r}^{A}(z,w) treats the pair as
a point in the product Euclidean space. In particular, for fixed points
and 1\leq s\leq r, the chain rules used below include
\displaystyle K_{r}^{A}(z,w)
\displaystyle=K_{r}^{A}(z)+K_{r}^{A}(w\mid z)+O(\log r),
\displaystyle K_{r,s}^{A}(z\mid z)
\displaystyle=K_{r}^{A}(z)-K_{s}^{A}(z)+O(\log r).
Interchanging z,w in the first identity is the
symmetry-of-information relation. The errors have the same order
with any fixed additional oracles.
See [33, 7, §2]. Finite conditioning on an
approximation to a point is a weaker thing than full access to that point
as an oracle, and we will need both.
The effective Hausdorff dimension of z relative to A is
\dim^{A}z=\liminf_{r\to\infty}\frac{K_{r}^{A}(z)}{r}.
Its upper effective dimension, or effective packing dimension, is
\operatorname{Dim}^{A}z=\limsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{K_{r}^{A}(z)}{r}
[7, §2].
The same definitions apply to joined oracles. In particular,
\dim^{A,x}z=\liminf_{r\to\infty}\frac{K_{r}^{A,x}(z)}{r},\qquad\operatorname{Dim}^{A,x}z=\limsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{K_{r}^{A,x}(z)}{r}.
Here the machine has access to both A and the full binary encoding
of x. The same convention applies to any finite list of additional
oracles.
A quantity is computable relative to A if an oracle algorithm approximates it to every prescribed rational
accuracy. It is lower semicomputable if such an algorithm produces
an increasing rational sequence converging to it. A collection is
computably enumerable relative to A if an oracle algorithm lists
its elements, with no requirement to decide nonmembership.
The point-to-set principle [21] states that, for every F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m},
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}F=\min_{A\subseteq\mathbb{N}}\sup_{z\in F}\dim^{A}z.
A compact set F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m} is effectively compact relative
to A if the collection of finite families of rational open balls
whose union covers F is computably enumerable relative to A.
We also use the effectively compact form of the principle. If a
compact set F is effectively compact relative to A, then
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}F=\sup_{z\in F}\dim^{A}z.
See [33, Theorem 5]. If F is
effectively compact relative to A, then \Delta_{x}F is effectively
compact relative to (A,x) [33, Proposition 7]. Compact sets and
their measures are always encoded into the oracle before the
points to which information estimates are applied are selected.
For a unit vector v, write p_{v}z=z\cdot v. Effective projection
estimates are developed in [22, 33, 7]. A nonintegral subscript
of K is rounded down to the nearest integer. A bounded change in
precision changes complexity by at most O(\log(r+2)) at terminal
precision r. The convention at precision zero is as above.
2.3 The pointwise hypotheses
For fixed distinct points x,y, put \mathbf{e}=\frac{y-x}{|y-x|}. The pointwise
hypotheses are the conditions of [7, §4] for both choices of pin.
(H1)
\dim^{A}x>1 and \dim^{A}y>1.
(H2)
K_{r}^{A,x}(\mathbf{e})\geq r-O(\log r) and K_{r}^{A,y}(\mathbf{e})\geq r-O(\log r).
(H3)
K_{r}^{A,x}(y)\geq K_{r}^{A}(y)-O(\log r) and K_{r}^{A,y}(x)\geq K_{r}^{A}(x)-O(\log r).
(H4)
K_{r}^{A}(\mathbf{e}\mid x)\geq r-o(r) and K_{r}^{A}(\mathbf{e}\mid y)\geq r-o(r). These
finite-precision bounds follow from (H2), up to logarithmic precision costs.
The constants may depend on the fixed pair and base oracle.
The directional complexity deficiency at precision r relative
to (A,x) means r-K_{r}^{A,x}(\mathbf{e}). Thus (H2) says that this
quantity is at most logarithmic in r.
This is the directional
randomness referred to below. Condition (H3) is the full-oracle
independence condition. Supplying the other endpoint saves at
most logarithmically many bits.
To obtain the bound Y(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E) in (1.1),
where Y and S_{0} are defined in Theorem 1.2, we prove
the following pointwise estimate. Let 0\leq\kappa<\kappa_{0}:=S_{0}-1.
If x,y satisfy (H1)–(H4) and \dim^{A}x,\dim^{A}y>1+\kappa, then
\liminf_{r\to\infty}\frac{K_{r}^{A}(|x-y|)}{r}\geq Y(1+\kappa).
(2.1)
Here K_{r}^{A}(|x-y|) is computed using only the background oracle A,
with neither x nor y supplied as an additional oracle.
Supplying either endpoint can only shorten a description, up to
a fixed simulation cost. Consequently
\max\{K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|),K_{r}^{A,y}(|x-y|)\}\leq K_{r}^{A}(|x-y|)+O(1).
(2.2)
Thus K_{r}^{A}(|x-y|) bounds both pin-conditioned distance complexities
from above, up to a fixed additive constant. Which of these two conditioned
complexities is larger may depend on the precision. Our unpinned
argument allows this dependence.
It does not require the same choice
of pin to give the stronger lower bound at every scale.
2.4 Information profiles and the two choices of pin
A point in a bounded part of the plane has
K_{r}^{A}(z)\leq 2r+O(\log r). More generally, increasing the precision
from m to n costs at most 2(n-m)+O(\log n) bits.
These bounds allow us to retain the information at intermediate
scales, as follows.
Fix A,x,y satisfying the pointwise hypotheses and a sequence
r_{j}\to\infty. The four normalized functions used to define
f,g,d_{x},d_{y} in Section 1.3 have a common
subsequence converging uniformly on [0,1]. The initial value is
zero, and logarithmic errors disappear after division by r_{j}.
The endpoint profiles are nondecreasing and 2-Lipschitz, and
u\leq f(u),\,g(u)\leq 2u\qquad\text{for }0\leq u\leq 1.
(2.3)
The distance profiles are nondecreasing and 1-Lipschitz.
In
particular 0\leq d_{x}(u),d_{y}(u)\leq u. When these functions are
considered only through their displayed inequalities, without
requiring a realization by points, we refer to them as scalar
profiles.
The compactness assertion follows from the preceding precision
increment bounds by linear interpolation and the usual uniform
compactness argument for functions with a common Lipschitz bound,
allowing errors tending to zero.
Lemma 2.1 (Joint extraction).
Every sequence of terminal precisions tending to infinity has one
subsequence along which the four profiles f,g,d_{x},d_{y} above
converge uniformly. Along that same subsequence, the prefix and
interval-transfer inequalities hold for both choices of pin.
Proof.
Apply the compactness argument just given to the four functions
successively, retaining each preceding convergence. Lemma 3.1 holds at every precision, and
Lemma 4.1 holds along every subsequence on which the
profiles converge, so both conclusions hold along this one.
∎
Pin

Table 2: The profiles retain their definitions when the endpoints exchange roles.
Table 2 records how the profiles are relabelled when the
endpoints exchange roles.
By (2.2), an upper bound for K_{r}^{A}(|x-y|) controls both distance
profiles. For a prescribed pin only d_{x} is controlled, so the unpinned
argument cannot be used without change. Throughout the paper the points and oracle
are chosen before the precision varies.
In the arguments below, we partition intervals of precision scales
into smaller intervals. Let F denote either a complexity function,
extended to real precision by linear interpolation between successive
precision levels, or a limiting profile. Put h(u)=F(u)-u.
Thus
h(b)-h(a)=F(b)-F(a)-(b-a)
measures the growth of F on [a,b] relative to growth at rate one.
Following the color conventions of [33, §3]
and [7, §§3–4], we call an interval [a,b]:
•
yellow if h attains its minimum at the left endpoint:
h(u)\geq h(a) for every u\in[a,b];
•
teal if h attains its minimum at the right endpoint:
h(u)\geq h(b) for every u\in[a,b];
•
green if both conditions hold:
h(a)=h(b)=\min_{u\in[a,b]}h(u).
These conditions specify where the minimum is attained. They do not
require h to be monotone. In particular, a green interval need not
be an interval on which h is constant.
A labelled yellow–teal partition assigns each interval one of the
labels yellow or teal, according to the conditions above. A green
interval may receive either label. The yellow loss is
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[a,b]\text{ in the partition}\\
[a,b]\text{ labelled yellow}\end{subarray}}\bigl(h(b)-h(a)\bigr).
Every summand is nonnegative, and green intervals contribute zero.
For the auxiliary piecewise linear constructions, we also call a
piece red when h is increasing on it and blue when
h is decreasing on it. A red–green–blue sequence consists of
pieces with these colors occurring in that order as the precision
increases. In each application, the partition intervals must also
satisfy the length restrictions stated there.
2.5 From dyadic masses to descriptions
The following elementary coding fact will be used both in the geometric
reduction and in the optional application in the appendix. A measure
is computable relative to A here when its dyadic masses can be
approximated by oracle programs with any prescribed rational error.
Lemma 2.2 (Coding a computable measure).
Let A\subseteq\mathbb{N} be an oracle, and let \mu be a
compactly supported probability measure on \mathbb{R}^{2} whose
dyadic masses are uniformly computable relative to A. That is,
a single algorithm with access to A, given a dyadic square Q
and an integer m\geq 1, approximates \mu(Q) with error at most
2^{-m}.
Then, for \mu-almost every z,
K_{n}^{A}(z)=-\log_{2}\mu(Q_{n}(z))+O_{z}(\log(n+2)).
(2.4)
Moreover, for each fixed oracle B such that A is computable
relative to B, one has
K_{n}^{B}(z)\geq-\log_{2}\mu(Q_{n}(z))-O_{B,z}(\log(n+2))
for \mu-almost every z. The exceptional null set may depend
on B.
In particular, for each fixed x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},
K_{n}^{A,x}(y)\geq K_{n}^{A}(y)-O_{x,y}(\log(n+2))
for \mu-almost every y, with an exceptional null set that may
depend on x.
Proof.
Kraft’s inequality states that a prefix-free set of binary strings
w satisfies \sum_{w}2^{-|w|}\leq 1, and conversely that lengths
\ell_{i} with \sum_{i}2^{-\ell_{i}}\leq 1 are the codeword lengths of a
prefix-free code [4, Chapter 5]. We use its effective
form, the Kraft–Chaitin theorem [18, Chapter 4], as
follows. Given n, for each level-n square Q and integer
k\geq 0, request a codeword of length k+1 for Q once a rational
mass approximation proves \mu(Q)>2^{-k}. Each request is made
once. These requests can be enumerated relative to A, and
\sum_{Q}\sum_{k:2^{-k}<\mu(Q)}2^{-(k+1)}\leq\sum_{Q}\mu(Q)=1.
For every square of positive mass the shortest requested length
is at most -\log_{2}\mu(Q)+2. The theorem therefore produces a
prefix-free code with this bound by one procedure relative to A
taking n as input. This avoids having to decide whether a
computable mass equals a dyadic threshold. Given the level n and a codeword assigned to a square Q,
the decoding algorithm, with access to A, outputs the label
of Q. Thus the codeword, together with a description of n,
provides a description of the square. Since describing n
costs O(\log(n+2)) bits, we obtain
K^{A}(Q_{n}(z))\leq-\log_{2}\mu(Q_{n}(z))+O(\log(n+2)).
For the reverse inequality, the shortest programs of distinct
level-n squares relative to B form a prefix-free set, so the
direct form of Kraft’s inequality implies \sum_{Q}2^{-K^{B}(Q)}\leq 1. Every
square in the set below has \mu(Q)<(n+2)^{-4}2^{-K^{B}(Q)}, hence,
for each fixed B,
\mu\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:K^{B}(Q_{n}(z))<-\log_{2}\mu(Q_{n}(z))-4\log_{2}(n+2)\}\leq(n+2)^{-4}.
The sum of these upper bounds is finite. The Borel–Cantelli
lemma therefore says that almost every z belongs to only
finitely many of the displayed exceptional sets, proving the
reverse bound.
A precision-n approximation to z identifies Q_{n}(z) among a
bounded number of squares. Conversely, the label of Q_{n}(z)
together with an index among its fixed-depth descendants specifies
a center within 2^{-n} of z. The index has bounded length.
It
need not be determined by the square alone. Thus point and square
complexities differ by O(\log(n+2)). This proves both assertions.
The last follows by taking B=(A,x) and using the first assertion.
The exceptional null set may depend on B. In particular, we do
not intersect the full-measure sets over all real pins. We first
fix a pin, apply the almost-everywhere assertion in the source
variable, and then use Fubini for the two fixed measures.
∎
Part I Information at the two endpoints
3 Retaining information in the projection estimates
We derive the estimates below by retaining intermediate complexity
quantities in the arguments of Stull and Fiedler–Stull.
One involves the source, and the other involves the pin. Keeping
these quantities allows the two endpoint profiles to interact.
We identify the relevant inequalities in the earlier proofs and
check uniformity in the projection direction. Appendix A
gives the source-increment summation and the modifications needed
at upper effective dimension two. Directional uniformity matters because, in
Section 4, that direction is determined by a
competitor, a point z at the same distance from the pin as y,
which a short description of the distance cannot distinguish from
y.
Lemma 3.1 (Prefix estimate).
Under (H1)–(H4), with x as the pin,
K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\geq\frac{r}{2}+\frac{1}{4}K_{r}^{A}(y)-o(r).
(3.1)
The same bound holds with x and y exchanged.
Proof.
The argument of [7, §4.3] partitions the range of precisions
[1,r] into blocks of two kinds, called yellow and teal. On a
yellow block the complexity of the distance grows at least as fast
as the precision, and on a teal block it grows at least as fast as the
complexity of the source. Let L_{r} be the total length of the
yellow blocks. Proposition A.2, proved in
Appendix A, establishes, for this L_{r}, the two
retained inequalities
K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\geq\frac{2r+L_{r}}{3}-o(r),\qquad K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\geq K_{r}^{A}(y)-L_{r}-o(r).
Their weighted average with coefficients \frac{3}{4} and
\frac{1}{4} is the asserted bound. The appendix constructs the
partition, proves the transfer on its teal blocks at D=2, and
sums the errors before removing the pin from the source complexity.
∎
The point of retaining the source complexity is visible in the
weighted average because the unknown yellow length cancels exactly.
\frac{3}{4}\frac{2r+L}{3}+\frac{1}{4}(K_{r}^{A}(y)-L)=\frac{r}{2}+\frac{K_{r}^{A}(y)}{4}.
The second borrowed estimate is Stull’s projection theorem, read so as to
keep the terminal complexity of the projected point.
Here K_{R}^{A}(x\mid p_{v}x,v) uses precision-R rational approximations
to both p_{v}x=x\cdot v and v as finite auxiliary input, taking the
worst case over these approximations as in Section 2.2.
The background oracle is A.
Lemma 3.2 (Projection estimate retaining terminal complexity).
Let x satisfy \dim^{A}x>1. Fix \tau>0. Suppose \tau R\leq t\leq R and v\in S^{1} satisfies
K_{s}^{A,x}(v)\geq s-C\log R\quad\text{ for }1\leq s\leq t
with a fixed constant C. This is the directional-randomness input in
[33, Theorem 13], in its local uniform form. Then
K_{R}^{A}(x\mid p_{v}x,v)\leq\max\left\{K_{R}^{A}(x)-R,\frac{K_{R}^{A}(x)-t}{2}\right\}+o(R).
(3.2)
The difference K_{R}^{A}(x)-K_{R}^{A}(x\mid p_{v}x,v) is the amount of
information about x that its projection determines.
We call it the
retained projection information. Equivalently, (3.2)
says that it is at least
\min\left\{R,\frac{K_{R}^{A}(x)+t}{2}\right\}-o(R).
(3.3)
More precisely, for each \xi>0 the error is at most \xi R once R\geq R_{0}(A,x,\tau,C,\xi), where C is the common directional-deficiency constant. This threshold is independent of v and t.
Proof.
We read the proof of [33, Theorem 13] before its last simplification. In the case with no red–green–blue sequence, equation (16) and the line immediately following it give
K_{R}^{A}(x\mid p_{v}x,v)\leq K_{R}^{A}(x)-R+o(R).
In the other case, the printed equation (19) yields, for a nonnegative partition parameter B,
K_{R}^{A}(x\mid p_{v}x,v)\leq\min\{K_{R}^{A}(x)-B-t,B\}+o(R).
Since \min\{c-B,B\}\leq\frac{c}{2}, this is at most \frac{K_{R}^{A}(x)-t}{2}+o(R). Combining the two cases proves the assertion. To justify the uniform threshold, the remark after [33, Lemma 14] makes its dependence on the tolerance, deficiency constant, and |x| explicit, with no direction dependence. The local lemmas use the same uniform geometric and coding estimates. The partition depends on the interpolated complexity of x,
the terminal precision R, and the length comparison t.
It does
not depend on v. Write T=t-O(\log R) for the length comparison after rounding,
chosen so that T\geq\frac{t}{2} for all sufficiently large R.
In the greedy construction of [33, Lemma 18], every two
successive pieces advance by more than T, except at the terminal
endpoint. Each complementary range therefore uses at most
2\lceil\frac{R}{T}\rceil+2 pieces. The proof of its Theorem 13
keeps one green interval, or two adjacent green intervals, and
applies this greedy construction on the two complementary ranges.
Including the green pieces leaves at most
4\lceil\frac{R}{T}\rceil+6\leq 8\lceil\frac{1}{\tau}\rceil+6.
The case without a red–green–blue sequence has no larger count.
Thus a common upper bound is
m_{\tau}=20\lceil\frac{1}{\tau}\rceil+20.
Use the fixed local tolerance \frac{\xi}{4m_{\tau}} in each estimate.
Discard partition pieces whose right endpoints are below
h_{R}=\lceil\sqrt{R}\rceil. Their total length is at most h_{R} and
their full complexity costs o(R). For every remaining piece with
right endpoint b\geq h_{R}, C\log R\leq 2C\log b. Thus the local
lemmas apply with the same deficiency constant 2C and the same
fixed tolerance, independently of v,t. Summing over the bounded
number of pieces costs at most \frac{\xi R}{4} plus
O_{C,m_{\tau}}(\log R). The remaining initial cutoff from
\dim^{A}x>1 depends only on A,x. This provides one
R_{0}(A,x,\tau,C,\xi) for all the permitted v,t.
∎
There is one endpoint issue to address before using these estimates.
In [7], the parameter D bounds the upper effective dimensions
of the two points. Some partition counts there contain
\frac{1}{2-D}. We allow D=2, so those counts do not apply.
What we actually need is a positive lower bound for the ratio of two
precisions. The next lemma supplies it, both here and in
Section 6.
Lemma 3.3 (Bounded precision ratio at the endpoint D=2).
Let 1<d<\frac{3}{2} and let [a,b] be a teal interval of the partition of
[7, §4.3] with lower parameter d and upper parameter D=2. Then
for b sufficiently large,
\frac{a}{b}\geq\frac{d}{5-d}-o(1)>0.
For a competing point z\neq y with |x-z|=|x-y|, put
t=-\log_{2}|y-z|. At terminal precision b, the non-tangent range
means t<\frac{b}{2}, and the tangent range means t\geq\frac{b}{2}.
Consequently, in every non-tangent application of the collision
estimate [7, Lemma 30] in the proof of [7, Lemma 31]
on such an interval, where t<\frac{b}{2},
the directional precision t
satisfies t\geq a-O(1) and the projection precision is R=b-t, so that
\frac{t}{R} is bounded below by a positive constant depending only on d.
The
projection partition of Lemma 3.2 has uniformly bounded
size.
Lemma 3.2 applies with the information gain
\min\{R,\frac{dR+t}{2}\}-o(R). No division by 2-D occurs.
Proof.
The lower bound on \frac{a}{b} is the first estimate of [7, Lemma 26],
which remains valid throughout the stated range. The second estimate
of that lemma is not needed here. The
bound S<\frac{2}{d(2-D)} on the number of red–green–blue sequences
in the proof of [7, Theorem 16] is not used at D=2.
Instead the bounded ratio \frac{t}{R} bounds the size of the
projection partition, and the retained projection estimate
Lemma 3.2 supplies exactly the input required in the
proof of [7, Lemma 31]. Thus that proof applies with uniform errors
in the actual applications. Appendix A
writes out the selected teal transfer and lists each replacement
of a bound involving 2-D.
∎
4 The interval transfer lemma
Suppose the distance from y to x is known, as is a coarse
approximation to y. How many further bits are needed to locate
y accurately? A competitor z must lie near that coarse location
and on the same circle about x. If every such competitor requires
a long description, a short description of the distance cannot hide
much source information. This is the transfer principle used by
Stull and Fiedler–Stull.
We retain the dependence on |z-y|. This separation determines the
precision at which the competitor reveals a projection of the pin,
and hence the part of the pin profile that enters the estimate.
Suppose |x-z|=|x-y| and s=-\log_{2}|y-z|. We consider
0<s\leq r, the separation range required by the transfer lemma
below. The exact identity
\left|\mathbf{e}\cdot\frac{y-z}{|y-z|}\right|=\frac{|y-z|}{2|x-y|}
shows that the unit normal v=\frac{y-z}{|y-z|} to the perpendicular bisector lies within C_{x,y}2^{-s} of one of the two perpendiculars to \mathbf{e}. The sign costs at most one bit. Thus (H2) supplies a common logarithmic directional-deficiency bound through precision s-c_{0}, where c_{0} depends only on the fixed pair.
The equal-distance condition also places x on the perpendicular
bisector of [y,z]. Consequently,
p_{v}x=p_{v}\left(\frac{y+z}{2}\right)=\frac{|y|^{2}-|z|^{2}}{2|y-z|}.
Thus the two source points determine a projection of the pin.
Since |y-z|=2^{-s}, precision-r approximations to y and z
determine both v and p_{v}x at precision r-s-O(1). Indeed,
normalizing the difference y-z loses approximately s bits
of precision.
The complexity bound below combines two contributions.
The term K_{s}^{A}(y) accounts for the coarse position information
shared by y and z. The minimum term accounts for the additional
information about the pin revealed by the perpendicular-bisector
constraint, using Lemma 3.2 at precision r-s
and the directional bound established above.
For every fixed \theta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}), uniformly for competitors with
\theta r\leq s<\frac{r}{2}-O(\log r), combine the
complexity bound for a competing point in [7, Lemma 30]
with Lemma 3.2 to obtain
K_{r}^{A}(z)\geq K_{s}^{A}(y)+\min\left\{r-s,\frac{K_{r-s}^{A}(x)+s}{2}\right\}-o(r).
(4.1)
For s\geq\frac{r}{2}-O(\log r), put r^{\prime}=r-O(\log r)
so that the identity
|p_{\mathbf{e}}z-p_{\mathbf{e}}y|=\frac{|z-y|^{2}}{2|x-y|}
makes the two projections indistinguishable at precision r^{\prime}.
Lemma 13 of [7] and the directional information in (H2)–(H4)
then give
\displaystyle K_{r}^{A}(z)
\displaystyle\geq K_{s}^{A}(y)+K_{r^{\prime}-s,r^{\prime}}^{A}(\mathbf{e}\mid y)-O(\log r)
\displaystyle\geq K_{s}^{A}(y)+K_{r^{\prime}-s}^{A,y}(\mathbf{e})-O(\log r)
\displaystyle\geq K_{s}^{A}(y)+r-s-o(r).
Here finite conditioning supplies no more information than the
full oracle y. If r-s=O(\log r), closeness to y alone
implies the same bound with logarithmic error. Thus the terminal
range does not require a direction-dependent threshold.
The transfer argument uses an auxiliary device from
[7, Lemma 14]. For a rational \eta with
\eta r\leq K_{r}^{A}(y), that lemma constructs from
y, r, and \eta an oracle G=D(r,y,\eta), which we call the
lowering oracle, with the following properties, all up to
O(\log r) errors. Relative to (A,G) the complexity of y at
precision r equals \eta r, its complexity at each precision
s\leq r is at least \min\{\eta r,K_{s}^{A}(y)\}, and complexities
conditioned on y are unchanged. Thus G caps the
source complexity at \eta r. This lets us test whether a proposed
amount of source information must pass to the distance. With this
oracle the positive source term becomes
\min\{\eta r,K_{s}^{A}(y)\}. Item (ii) of that lemma preserves the pin information in the
complexity bound for a competing point.
K_{r-s,r}^{A,G}(x\mid y)=K_{r-s,r}^{A}(x\mid y)+O(\log r),
whereas
K_{r-s}^{A,G}(x\mid p_{v}x,v)\leq K_{r-s}^{A}(x\mid p_{v}x,v)+O(1).
More explicitly, with R=r-s, item (ii), finite versus full
conditioning, and (H3) give
K_{R,r}^{A,G}(x\mid y)=K_{R,r}^{A}(x\mid y)+O(\log r)\geq K_{R}^{A,y}(x)-O(\log r)\geq K_{R}^{A}(x)-O(\log r).
The unconditional pin information is preserved as well.
K_{R}^{A,G}(x)\geq K_{R}^{A,G,y}(x)-O(1)\geq K_{R}^{A,y}(x)-O(\log r)\geq K_{R}^{A}(x)-O(\log r).
These are preservation properties of the lowering oracle.
No
algorithm computing a shortest-description oracle from y is
needed. For the tangent bound, the conditioning precision in Lemma 13
of [7] is r^{\prime}, whereas the lowering oracle was constructed at
precision r. We therefore use the full-oracle assertion of
Lemma 14(ii), rather than identify these two finite conditioning
precisions. When r^{\prime}-s\geq 1, it implies
\displaystyle K_{r^{\prime}-s,r^{\prime}}^{A,G}(\mathbf{e}\mid y)
\displaystyle\geq K_{r^{\prime}-s}^{A,G,y}(\mathbf{e})-O(\log r)
\displaystyle=K_{r^{\prime}-s}^{A,y}(\mathbf{e})+O(\log r)
\displaystyle\geq r^{\prime}-s-O(\log r).
Together with the capped value of K_{s}^{A,G}(y), this proves
K_{r}^{A,G}(z)\geq\min\{\eta r,K_{s}^{A}(y)\}+r-s-O(\log r)
through the tangent range. If s\geq r^{\prime}, then r-s=O(\log r),
and the same conclusion follows by using the closeness of z to
y at precision s. Thus the terminal end of this range is
covered as well.
The negative projection residual can only decrease after G
is supplied. Consequently the capped complexity bound for a competing point uses
the projection theorem relative to the original oracle A,
without assuming new directional randomness relative to G.
We now use the joint limiting profiles defined in
Section 2. All interval endpoints below are
fixed proportions of the terminal precision.
No error estimate is
optimized over a growing collection of microscopic intervals.
By Lemma 3.1,
d_{x}(u)\geq\frac{u}{2}+\frac{f(u)}{4},\qquad d_{y}(u)\geq\frac{u}{2}+\frac{g(u)}{4}.
(4.2)
Put
\displaystyle p_{g}(s;b)
\displaystyle=\min\left\{b-s,\,\frac{g(b-s)+s}{2}\right\},
(4.3)
\displaystyle H_{f,g}(a,b)
\displaystyle=\min\left\{f(b),\,\inf_{a\leq s\leq b}\big(f(s)+p_{g}(s;b)\big)\right\}.
(4.4)
Because of (2.3), p_{g}(s;b)\geq\min\{\frac{b}{2},b-s\}.
For s\geq\frac{b}{2} it equals b-s. We call p_{g} the closed projection gain, referring to its
