explicit formula, and H_{f,g} the transfer threshold. For a
separation parameter s, the quantity f(s)+p_{g}(s;b) is the
normalized competitor cost. The other entry f(b) is the terminal
cap. Thus H_{f,g}(a,b) is the least of these costs on [a,b].
Lemma 4.1 (Transfer in the limit).
For every fixed 0<a<b\leq 1,
d_{x}(b)-d_{x}(a)\geq\left[H_{f,g}(a,b)-f(a)\right]_{+}.
(4.5)
With y as the pin and x as the source, the corresponding inequality is
d_{y}(b)-d_{y}(a)\geq[H_{g,f}(a,b)-g(a)]_{+}.
Proof.
If H_{f,g}(a,b)\leq f(a), monotonicity proves the claim.
Otherwise choose
f(a)<c<H=H_{f,g}(a,b),\qquad h=H-c>0,
and put
n_{j}=\lfloor br_{j}\rfloor,\qquad\ell_{j}=\lceil ar_{j}\rceil,\qquad m_{j}=\lfloor cr_{j}\rfloor.
We cap the source complexity at m_{j} using
G_{j}=D(n_{j},y,\frac{m_{j}}{n_{j}}) relative to A.
The strict margin h will make the competitor estimates uniform.
The rational cap parameter costs O(\log r_{j}) bits to describe.
it is specified by the two integers m_{j},n_{j}=O(r_{j}).
All uses of the lowering lemma and the chain rule are uniform in
the auxiliary oracle. The machine constructions relativize to
the same universal oracle machine, and the cap parameters are
included in the description. Thus the error bounds remain valid
although G_{j} changes with j.
We suppress integer roundings in the following estimates.
For competitors at separations sr_{j} with a\leq s<\frac{b}{2}, the capped complexity bound for a competing point is
K_{br_{j}}^{A,G}(z)\geq r_{j}\min\{c,f(s)\}+r_{j}p_{g}(s;b)-o(r_{j}).
Together with the tangent estimate for s\geq\frac{b}{2}, this is bounded below uniformly by
cr_{j}+\min\{p_{g}(s;b),h\}r_{j}-o(r_{j}).
Choose any fixed rational 0<\varepsilon<\min\{\frac{1}{4},\frac{h}{4b}\}, and then take j large enough that the total error is at most \frac{\varepsilon br_{j}}{2}. For s<\frac{b}{2}, p_{g}(s;b)\geq\frac{b}{2} ensures the required \varepsilon br_{j} surplus. For s\geq\frac{b}{2}, p_{g}(s;b)=b-s, and
\min\{b-s,\,h\}-\frac{\varepsilon b}{2}\geq\min\{\varepsilon b,\,b-s-\varepsilon b\}.
This is exactly the terminal allowance in [7, Lemma 15], including s\nearrow b. Its hypothesis only requires separations S=-\log_{2}|y-z|\leq n_{j}.
Points closer than terminal precision need not be considered. The O(\log r_{j}) transition about \frac{b}{2} is absorbed by the fixed margin. Uniform convergence and the fixed ratio \frac{a}{b}>0 supply common error bounds over all required competitors. Its other hypothesis holds since K_{n_{j}}^{A,G_{j}}(y)=m_{j}+O(\log r_{j})\leq m_{j}+\frac{\varepsilon n_{j}}{2}.
Explicitly, [7, Lemma 14(iii)], (H3), and the chain rule give
\displaystyle K_{n_{j}}^{A,G_{j},x}(y)
\displaystyle\geq m_{j}-O(\log r_{j}),
\displaystyle K_{\ell_{j}}^{A,G_{j},x}(y)
\displaystyle\leq K_{\ell_{j}}^{A}(y)+O(1),
\displaystyle K_{n_{j},\ell_{j}}^{A,G_{j},x}(y\mid y)
\displaystyle\geq m_{j}-K_{\ell_{j}}^{A}(y)-O(\log r_{j}).
Lemma 15 of [7] transfers this increment with loss 3\varepsilon n_{j}+O(\log r_{j}), including the description of its rational parameters. Removing G_{j} only increases the conditional distance complexity up to a fixed simulation cost. With \varrho=|x-y| and a fixed integer c_{1}, the worst-case definition of finite conditioning implies
K_{n,\ell}^{A,x}(\varrho\mid y)\leq K_{n,\ell-c_{1}}^{A,x}(\varrho\mid\varrho)+O(\log n):
every precision-\ell approximation to y, with oracle x, computes a precision-(\ell-c_{1}) approximation to \varrho. The chain rule and the bounded scalar precision shift identify the expression on the right with
K_{br_{j}}^{A,x}(|x-y|)-K_{ar_{j}}^{A,x}(|x-y|)+O(\log r_{j}).
Let j\to\infty to obtain d_{x}(b)-d_{x}(a)\geq c-f(a)-3\varepsilon b. This holds for every sufficiently small fixed \varepsilon>0.
Let \varepsilon\searrow 0, and then c\nearrow H. This proves (4.5). The partition count is bounded because \frac{a}{b} is fixed.
No precision-dependent collection of intervals is summed.
∎
The geometric and information-theoretic input is now contained in
three groups of inequalities: the profile bounds
(2.3), the prefix estimates (4.2),
and the interval transfers (4.5). The next section shows how to choose a fixed pair satisfying these
hypotheses. The elementary consequences in
Appendices B and C give a
short introduction to the scalar argument. The stronger estimates
are proved in Sections 11 and 10.
5 From a pair of points to a set
The scalar inequalities concern one fixed pair of points. To use
it for a distance set, we must choose a pair that has enough
complexity and enough independence in both directions.
The point-to-set principle requires this for every initial oracle.
We first enlarge that oracle to include the fixed geometric data,
and only then choose the pair. All precisions will use this same
choice.
For a compact set, we can encode a sequence of finite rational sets
whose Hausdorff distances from it are at most 2^{-j} at stage j.
This supplies approximations with a known error, and makes the compact
set effectively compact relative to the encoding oracle. For a finite
measure, enumerate the dyadic cubes and encode rational approximations
to every cube mass, each with error at most 2^{-j} at stage j.
Include the total mass and a bound for the support. These are countably
many finite strings, so a fixed coding of finite strings and indices
places all the data in one oracle A_{0}. Positive total masses can
then be divided out to compute the normalized measures. The coordinate
boundaries have measure zero for the measures used below, since their
Frostman exponents exceed one.
Given an arbitrary oracle B, use the join
A=B\oplus A_{0}: even positions encode B and odd positions encode
A_{0}. Thus a computation with oracle A has access to all the fixed
geometric data and can simulate a computation with oracle B.
No effective procedure for constructing A_{0} from an arbitrary
analytic set is required. The point-to-set principle permits every
oracle, and the data are fixed before any point is selected.
Proposition 5.1 (A fixed pair for every oracle).
Let E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} be analytic with \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1, and let
1<s<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E. For every oracle B there are an oracle
A=B\oplus A_{0} and points x,y\in E satisfying (H1)–(H4) relative
to A, with \dim^{A}x\geq s and \dim^{A}y\geq s.
Proof.
Choose separated compact pieces E_{1},E_{2}\subset E with positive finite s-dimensional Hausdorff measure.
By the Hausdorff density theorem [23, Theorem 6.2],
the upper s-density of \mathcal{H}^{s}|_{E_{i}} is finite almost
everywhere. A countable restriction followed by inner regularity
provides compact positive-measure subsets E_{i}^{\prime}\subset E_{i} and
common constants C,r_{0}>0 such that
\mathcal{H}^{s}(E_{i}\cap B(z,r))\leq Cr^{s} for z\in E_{i}^{\prime} and 0<r<r_{0}.
If B(w,r) meets E_{i}^{\prime} at z, it lies in B(z,2r).
Enlarging C for r\geq\frac{r_{0}}{2} proves that
\mathcal{H}^{s}|_{E_{i}^{\prime}} is s-Frostman. Let \mu_{i} be its
normalization and fix 1<t<s.
Both measures have finite t-energy.
Start with an arbitrary oracle B, and use the preceding construction
to form A=B\oplus A_{0}, encoding both compact pieces, their finite
Hausdorff restrictions, the total masses, and all dyadic cell masses.
This supplies the hypotheses of [33, Lemma 36(2)], also after
normalization. For each fixed endpoint x, that lemma establishes
full-oracle independence for almost every opposite endpoint y.
Apply it for both choices of pin and then use Fubini to obtain (H3)
for product-almost every pair. For every fixed A, the set
\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim^{A}z<s\} has zero \mathcal{H}^{s} measure by the usual
low-complexity covers. Thus (H1) also holds almost everywhere, even
though the background oracle contains the measure data.
Orponen’s radial absolute-continuity theorem [25, Theorem 1.11] has an exceptional pin set of dimension at most 2-t<1. The opposite s-dimensional measure assigns this set zero mass. For each fixed pin, the angular complexity deficiency set relative to that pin and A has zero arclength by [33, Lemma 36(1)]. Radial absolute continuity and Fubini give (H2) for product-almost every pair, with either endpoint as the pin. The bad-pair relations here are Borel because the finite-precision oracle computation conditions are countable combinations of finite-query conditions. The finite conditional directional conditions follow directly from the full-oracle bounds. Fix one pair in the intersection of these full-measure sets.
The pair satisfies \dim^{A}x,\dim^{A}y\geq s, since the set
\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim^{A}z<s\} is \mathcal{H}^{s}-null. Thus the quantifier order is
\displaystyle\forall B\,\exists A=B\oplus A_{0}\,\exists(x,y)\in E_{1}\times E_{2}
\displaystyle\forall\text{ sufficiently large precisions, with (H1)--(H4) satisfied by this same pair. }
Choosing different pairs
at different scales would not suffice. The scalar bounds for this pair,
Y and the scalar bound \frac{4}{5}, are proved in Sections 11
and 10, after the partition estimates of
Part II. Together with the point-to-set
principle they prove Theorem 1.2. The term
R(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E) follows from Theorem 1.5.
∎
There is no pinned conclusion from this argument alone. A low
unconditioned distance complexity forces both endpoint-conditioned
complexities to be low simultaneously. A low complexity conditioned on the prescribed pin x alone does
not provide (B.2), whose derivation used the upper
bound for the distance complexity conditioned on y. Part II identifies the resulting scalar obstruction
and the critical profile structure used in the geometric proof.
6 The asymmetric pinned refinement
What can the information estimates prove when the pin is prescribed?
We first separate the lower dimension of the pin from that of the
source. Fiedler and Stull use one parameter for both, and note in
their footnote 7 that this restriction can be relaxed. Keeping the
two parameters separate yields an explicit exceptional-set estimate.
The proof uses the partition argument alone.
Proposition 6.1 (Separate pin and source parameters).
Let 1<p\leq s<\frac{3}{2}, let A be an oracle, and let x\neq y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.
Suppose \dim^{A}x>p, \dim^{A}y>s, and, with
\mathbf{e}=\frac{y-x}{|y-x|}, suppose the Fiedler–Stull conditions with x as the pin hold:
K_{r}^{A,x}(\mathbf{e})\geq r-O(\log r),\qquad K_{r}^{A,x}(y)\geq K_{r}^{A}(y)-O(\log r),\qquad K_{r}^{A}(\mathbf{e}\mid y)\geq r-o(r).
Then
\dim^{A,x}|x-y|\geq B(p,s):=\min\left\{1,\,\frac{p+s(3-p)}{5-p}\right\}.
(6.1)
Proof.
Use the partition of [7, §4.3] with its auxiliary lower parameter
d=p and upper parameter D=2. These parameters satisfy all the lower and
upper dimension assumptions used in that construction. Let f be its
piecewise linear interpolation of K_{u}^{A}(y), and let L be the total
yellow length in the partition of [1,r].
Consider a teal interval [a,b]. In the green case in the construction,
f(b)-f(a)=b-a.
Otherwise, a is the root selected by equations (10)–(11) of [7].
With d=p,D=2, its exact defining identity is
f(b)-f(a)=\frac{p}{2}b-\frac{p-1}{2}a.
(6.2)
Retain the stronger source bound f(b)\geq sb-o(b), rather than replacing
it by pb-o(b). The planar upper bound f(a)\leq 2a+O(\log b) implies
2a+O(\log b)\geq\left(s-\frac{p}{2}\right)b+\frac{p-1}{2}a-o(b),
and hence
\frac{a}{b}\geq\frac{2s-p}{5-p}-o(1).
(6.3)
For k=\frac{a}{b}, the slope in (6.2) is
C_{p}(k)=\frac{p-(p-1)k}{2(1-k)},\qquad C_{p}^{\prime}(k)=\frac{1}{2(1-k)^{2}}>0.
Consequently, a non-green teal has source growth at least
\left(c(p,s)-o(1)\right)(b-a),\qquad c(p,s)=\frac{p(2-s)+s}{5-2s}.
A green interval has slope one. If c(p,s)>1, a sufficiently large
non-green teal would contradict its teal property.
If c(p,s)=1, the
same calculation yields slope at least 1-o(1). Thus every teal satisfies
K_{b,a}^{A}(y\mid y)\geq\min\{1,\,c(p,s)\}(b-a)-o(b).
(6.4)
Independence and the finite-precision chain rule allow the same estimate
relative to (A,x), at logarithmic cost per interval.
The transfer on each teal remains exactly [7, Lemma 31].
The
partition itself has not been changed. At the endpoint D=2 the bound
S<\frac{2}{d(2-D)} on the number of red–green–blue sequences
in the proof of [7, Theorem 16] is not usable.
Lemma 3.3 supplies the replacement, with
d=p, and shows that the proof of Lemma 31 applies with uniform errors.
Write Q_{r}=K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|) and c_{0}=\min\{1,c(p,s)\}.
The first summation in [7, §4.4], using
(6.4), reads
Q_{r}\geq L+c_{0}(r-L)-o(r).
For its second summation retain the source lower bound sr:
the yellow source increments total at most 2L+o(r), whereas the
yellow distance increments total at least L-o(r) and all teal
source increments transfer to distance increments. Therefore
sr\leq K_{r}^{A,x}(y)+o(r)\leq L+Q_{r}+o(r).
Combining,
Q_{r}\geq\max\{L+c_{0}(r-L),sr-L\}-o(r).
(6.5)
If c_{0}=1, this proves full dimension. Otherwise the two affine functions
of L meet in [0,r], and their common value is
\frac{s+c(p,s)(1-s)}{2-c(p,s)}r=\frac{p+s(3-p)}{5-p}r.
The condition c(p,s)\geq 1 is equivalent to the last fraction being at
least one, proving the stated cap.
Fix an arbitrarily small error tolerance before taking
r large. Successive teal intervals at least halve the precision, and
yellow blocks are maximal, so there are O(\log r) blocks and the sum
of their upper endpoints is O(r). The interval errors therefore total
O(\varepsilon r)+O((\log r)^{2}). A fixed initial range has negligible
cost. The positive lower precision ratio of Lemma 3.3
ensures the projection estimates are uniform. Letting r\to\infty and then
\varepsilon\searrow 0 proves (6.1).
∎
The two inequalities (6.5) contain more
than the cap B(p,s). Keeping the terminal source complexity in the
second one, instead of the lower bound sr, yields a prefix estimate
that improves Lemma 3.1 whenever an endpoint has
dimension larger than one.
Corollary 6.2 (Prefix estimate with the retained teal rate).
Under the hypotheses of Proposition 6.1,
suppose c=c(p,s)<1. Then
K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\geq\frac{cr+(1-c)K_{r}^{A}(y)}{2-c}-o(r),
(6.6)
and the limiting profiles f,d_{x} of Section 2,
along any common sequence of precisions, satisfy
d_{x}(u)\geq(1-2b)u+bf(u)\quad\text{for }0\leq u\leq 1,\qquad b=b(p,s)=\frac{1-c(p,s)}{2-c(p,s)}\in\Bigl(0,\frac{1}{4}\Bigr].
(6.7)
Proof.
In the proof of the proposition, Q_{r}\geq L+c(r-L)-o(r), and the
second summation shows that K_{r}^{A,x}(y)\leq L+Q_{r}+o(r) before the source
lower bound sr is inserted.
The independence hypothesis
K_{r}^{A,x}(y)\geq K_{r}^{A}(y)-O(\log r) then implies
K_{r}^{A}(y)\leq L+Q_{r}+o(r). Multiply the first inequality by
\frac{1}{2-c} and the second by \frac{1-c}{2-c} and add.
The
terms in L cancel, which is (6.6). For
u>0 apply (6.6) at the precision
\lfloor ur_{j}\rfloor, divide by r_{j}, and let j\to\infty.
The
identity \frac{c}{2-c}=1-2b proves (6.7), and at
u=0 both sides vanish. Since c(p,s) increases in p and in s,
c\geq c(1,1)=\frac{2}{3}, so b\leq\frac{1}{4}, and b>0 because
c<1.
∎
At p=s=1, (6.7) is the prefix estimate
(4.2).
Since b\leq\frac{1}{4} and f(u)\leq 2u,
(6.7) implies (4.2) in every case.
With p=1+v and s=1+\kappa, 0\leq v\leq\kappa,
b(1+v,1+\kappa)=\frac{1-2\kappa-v+\kappa v}{(1-\kappa)(4-v)},
(6.8)
which is positive exactly when c<1. We will use the following two cases.
\begin{gathered}b_{\mathrm{pin}}(\kappa)=\frac{1-2\kappa}{4(1-\kappa)}\quad\text{for }0\leq\kappa<\frac{1}{2},\\
b_{\mathrm{sym}}(\kappa)=b(1+\kappa,1+\kappa)=\frac{1-3\kappa+\kappa^{2}}{(1-\kappa)(4-\kappa)}\quad\text{for }0\leq\kappa<\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}.\end{gathered}
(6.9)
The first is b(1,1+\kappa). For \kappa>0, if the pin has
dimension larger than one and the source has dimension larger than
1+\kappa, choose p>1 sufficiently close to one and
s<1+\kappa sufficiently close to 1+\kappa, with
p\leq s and p<\dim^{A}x. The corollary applies, and continuity
establishes (6.7) with b=b_{\mathrm{pin}}(\kappa)
after letting p\searrow 1 and s\nearrow 1+\kappa.
For \kappa=0, choose p=s>1 below both endpoint dimensions
and let p=s\searrow 1, obtaining b=\frac{1}{4}.
The second coefficient is obtained, for \kappa>0, by taking
p=s\nearrow 1+\kappa when both endpoint dimensions are larger
than 1+\kappa. Its case \kappa=0 follows from the same
p=s\searrow 1 argument. It holds for either choice of pin.
Proof of Theorem 1.5.
First suppose 1<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\leq\frac{3}{2} and
1\leq p<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E. Write t=\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E.
Suppose, to obtain a contradiction, that the exceptional set in the
statement has dimension greater than p. Since
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E<B(p,t)\}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathbb{Q}\\
0<q<B(p,t)\end{subarray}}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E<q\},
countable stability supplies q<B(p,t) for which the latter set
has dimension greater than p.
Choose p^{\prime}>p, with p^{\prime}>1, smaller than both this exceptional
dimension and t. Since B is nondecreasing in its first parameter,
B(p^{\prime},t)\geq B(p,t)>q. By continuity in its second parameter,
choose p^{\prime}<s<t such that q<B(p^{\prime},s). Here s<\frac{3}{2}.
Choose a compact source subset K\subset E with
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}K>s. Every selected bad pin for E is also a
bad pin for K, so the Borel set
B_{q}(K)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}K<q\}
has dimension greater than p^{\prime}. Its Borel measurability follows
by expressing small Hausdorff content through finite rational
open covers of the compact set \Delta_{x}K.
Take a source Frostman measure \mu of exponent greater than s
on a compact subset of K, and a pin Frostman measure \nu of
exponent greater than p^{\prime} on a compact subset of B_{q}(K).
Positive-mass restrictions arrange separated compact supports.
Fix one oracle A encoding the supports and both measures,
including their dyadic masses, as in Section 5.
Lemma 2.2 and Fubini’s theorem imply full-oracle
independence for product-almost every pair in both orientations.
The Frostman bounds also imply
\dim^{A}x>p^{\prime} and \dim^{A}y>s for product-almost every pair.
Apply Orponen’s radial absolute-continuity theorem [25] to
each measure. In either orientation its exceptional set has
dimension less than one, and hence has zero mass for the opposite
measure. The angular complexity exceptional sets have zero
arclength. Radial absolute continuity and Fubini therefore imply
K_{r}^{A,x}(\mathbf{e})\geq r-O(\log r),\qquad K_{r}^{A,y}(\mathbf{e})\geq r-O(\log r)
for product-almost every pair, where \mathbf{e}=\frac{y-x}{|y-x|}.
Changing the sign of the direction in the second orientation
costs only a constant. The second inequality implies the finite
conditional bound required in
Proposition 6.1.
Select one pair satisfying these conclusions. That proposition yields
\dim^{A,x}|x-y|\geq B(p^{\prime},s)>q.
On the other hand, the compact source support is effectively
compact relative to A. Its distance image is effectively compact
relative to (A,x), so the restricted point-to-set principle implies
\dim^{A,x}|x-y|\leq\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}K<q.
This is a contradiction. Notice that the two directional estimates
were obtained separately. Randomness relative to x alone would
not supply the estimate conditioned on y.
If \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\geq\frac{3}{2}, choose compact source subsets
with dimensions below \frac{3}{2} and increasing to it. The
p=1 conclusion just proved supplies lower bounds increasing to
one, outside a countable union of exceptional sets of dimension at
most one. Thus the pinned distance sets have full dimension there.
This also proves the assertion for every 1\leq p<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E,
since B(p,\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E)=1 in this range.
∎
Part II Partition estimates and the two endpoint profiles
7 Why the scalar method stops at three quarters
The estimates just proved still approach \frac{3}{4} as the
source dimension decreases to one. Is this only a weakness in how
we optimized them? The following construction shows that it is a
limitation of the pointwise scalar system without a common-source
mass bound. The later annular cap records information about one
measure across many pairs, which is an additional kind of constraint.
We allow every finite yellow–teal partition from Stull’s projection
argument, so the obstruction persists even after the projection
gain is strengthened. The resulting profiles satisfy all the
displayed inequalities, have endpoint dimensions greater than one,
and have d_{x}(1) arbitrarily close to \frac{3}{4}. The opposite
distance profile has d_{y}(1)=1, which explains why the unpinned
argument leads to no contradiction.
The stronger partition functional has a second use. Near the
barrier, it forces the pin profile to contain a plateau.
That plateau will provide the pin spacing estimates in
Part III.
For a profile g, put h_{g}(u)=g(u)-u. For a labelled
yellow–teal partition \mathcal{P}, define its loss by
\operatorname{loss}_{g}(\mathcal{P})=\sum_{[v,w]\in\mathcal{P}\text{ yellow}}\bigl(h_{g}(w)-h_{g}(v)\bigr).
Let \mathcal{L}_{t}(g;R) be the infimum of this loss over finite
partitions of [0,R] with piece lengths at most t. The colors
have the endpoint-minimum meanings given in
Section 2.4. A partition template records its
ordered endpoints and color labels.
It can be transported to a
nearby interval as in the next lemma. Define
P_{g}(t;R)=g(R)-\mathcal{L}_{t}(g;R),\qquad\widehat{p}_{g}(t;b)=\max\left\{\min\left\{b-t,\frac{g(b-t)+t}{2}\right\},P_{g}(t;b-t)\right\},
and set P_{g}(t;0)=0, so \widehat{p}_{g}(b;b)=0.
For 0<a\leq b\leq 1, define
\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,b)=\min\left\{f(b),\inf_{a\leq t\leq b}\bigl(f(t)+\widehat{p}_{g}(t;b)\bigr)\right\}.
Thus \widehat{H}_{f,g} is H_{f,g} with p_{g} replaced by
\widehat{p}_{g}. Telescoping the increments of h_{g} shows
that \mathcal{L}_{t}(g;R)\geq h_{g}(R), because teal increments are
nonpositive. Consequently
\widehat{p}_{g}(t;b)\leq b-t.
(7.1)
Lemma 8.3 below shows that
P_{g}(t;b-t)\geq p_{g}(t;b), so in fact \widehat{p}_{g}(t;b)=P_{g}(t;b-t)
for t<b. We keep the closed gain in the notation because p_{g},
unlike P_{g} (Remark 7.4), is
monotone in g, which the envelope arguments of
Sections B, C,
and 11 use.
Lemma 7.1 (Uniform finite witnesses for the partition gain).
The map (g,t,R)\mapsto P_{g}(t;R) is lower semicontinuous for the
uniform topology on profiles and for t,R>0.
Fix 0<a<\frac{b}{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}, profiles f,g, and
c<\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,b). There exist \xi>0, an integer m,
and finitely many finite partition templates with the following
property. If \|f^{\prime}-f\|_{\infty}+\|g^{\prime}-g\|_{\infty}<\xi, then
f^{\prime}(b)>c+\xi and, for every t\in[a,\frac{b}{2}], either the
closed projection gain or a partition obtained from one of the
templates yields
f^{\prime}(t)+\max\left\{\min\left\{b-t,\frac{g^{\prime}(b-t)+t}{2}\right\},g^{\prime}(b-t)-\operatorname{loss}_{g^{\prime}}(\mathcal{P}_{t})\right\}>c+\xi.
The partition \mathcal{P}_{t} has at most m pieces, covers
[0,b-t], and has all piece lengths at most t.
Templates are transported by linear rescaling, trimming, and
splitting at minima. Thus the number of pieces and the strict
margin are uniform in the competitor separation.
Proof.
Put h=g-u. Take a witnessing partition of [0,R] with k
pieces and loss B. If (R^{\prime},t^{\prime},g^{\prime}) is close to (R,t,g), rescale
its endpoints by \frac{R^{\prime}}{R}. The transported function and
h^{\prime}=g^{\prime}-u differ by at most
\|g^{\prime}-g\|_{\infty}+2|R^{\prime}-R|
on corresponding points. Split each transported piece at a minimum
of h^{\prime}. For an originally yellow piece, its new loss is at most
its old loss plus twice this error.
For an originally teal piece
the new loss is at most twice this error. Indeed, if v is the
new minimum on [u,w], the repaired loss is h^{\prime}(w)-h^{\prime}(v).
A transported piece exceeds t^{\prime} by at most
e=\frac{t}{R}|R^{\prime}-R|+|t^{\prime}-t|. In a sufficiently small
neighborhood e<t^{\prime}. For a yellow piece, split off its terminal
excess and repair that short piece at a minimum. For a teal
piece, keep its terminal interval of length t^{\prime} and repair the
short initial piece. The 1-Lipschitz bound charges at most
2e per piece. Hence a repaired partition has at most 6k
pieces and loss
B+O_{k,R,t}\bigl(\|g^{\prime}-g\|_{\infty}+|R^{\prime}-R|+|t^{\prime}-t|\bigr).
Taking a partition within any positive tolerance of the infimum
proves upper semicontinuity of \mathcal{L}_{t}(g;R), and therefore
lower semicontinuity of P_{g}(t;R).
Now put \eta_{\mathrm{gap}}=\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,b)-c>0. At every
t\in[a,\frac{b}{2}], the closed gain or one finite partition
witnesses a sum greater than c+\frac{3\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}}{4}.
The terminal cap
satisfies f(b)\geq c+\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}. The preceding repair argument and
continuity of the closed gain preserve a margin greater than
\frac{\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}}{2} in a neighborhood of that separation and of the
two profiles. A finite subcover of the compact separation interval
provides finitely many templates. Take the maximum of their repaired
piece counts and the minimum of their neighborhood radii, and
decrease \xi below \frac{\eta_{\mathrm{gap}}}{2}. These choices prove the claim.
∎
Lemma 7.2 (The full partition transfer).
Let x,y satisfy (H1)–(H4) relative to A. Along every sequence
of precisions on which the four profiles converge uniformly,
the limiting profiles satisfy
d_{x}(b)-d_{x}(a)\geq[\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,b)-f(a)]_{+}\quad\text{for }0<a<b\leq 1,
and, with y as the pin,
d_{y}(b)-d_{y}(a)\geq[\widehat{H}_{g,f}(a,b)-g(a)]_{+}.
Proof.
Fix a finite yellow–teal partition of [0,R] with interval lengths at
most t. Uniform convergence of the normalized pin complexity makes
its intervals approximately yellow or teal, with errors tending to zero.
The hypotheses in [33, Lemmas 14–15] already allow an additive
error proportional to the terminal precision of the interval. If the
uniform profile error is \varepsilon_{N}\to 0, the yellow or teal
condition has slack at most 2\varepsilon_{N}N+O(\log N) after
rounding and applying the chain rule. On pieces whose terminal
precision is at least \kappa N, this slack is o(1) times that
precision, for each fixed \kappa>0. Thus those lemmas apply.
A yellow interval costs its increment of g-u, and a teal interval costs
o(1) after normalization. The chain rule sums these costs over the
fixed finite partition, as in [33, Lemma 17]. Directional randomness
through tN-O(\log N) suffices.
At a length boundary, trim an O(\log N) precision segment from
the right endpoint of a yellow interval or the left endpoint of a
teal interval, preserving its color condition, and charge the
removed segment by its full complexity.
Thus this partition retains g(R) minus its yellow loss. Take the
infimum over finite partitions after the large-precision limit.
No effective procedure for recovering a limiting profile or an
optimal partition is needed. Once the cap and its strict margin
are fixed, the next paragraph selects finitely many templates
with a fixed bound on their numbers of pieces. At precision N,
encode their rounded integer endpoints and color labels as part
of the description. This costs O(m\log(N+2)) bits for at most
m pieces, and hence disappears after division by N.
The template count is fixed before N tends to infinity.
Lemma 7.1 supplies finitely many
partition templates and a uniform strict margin for a cap below
\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,b) on the early separation interval
[a,\frac{b}{2}]. If that interval is empty, only the tangent
estimate is needed. To avoid
any threshold issue from tiny pieces, discard an initial precision
range [0,\kappa N], costing at most 2\kappa N+o(N), and charge newly
trimmed tiny pieces by their full complexity. Hold \kappa>0 fixed as
N\to\infty, then let \kappa\searrow 0. Every remaining local
projection estimate is applied at a uniformly positive macroscopic
precision. The direction-uniform bounds in [33, Lemmas 14–15]
therefore apply to all competitors.
For t\geq\frac{b}{2}, the tangent estimate yields b-t as before. Substituting
these uniform projection bounds into the capped collision argument of
Lemma 4.1 completes the proof. The positive pin information
is preserved by the lowering oracle and its negative projection
residual can only decrease.
No randomness relative to that extra oracle
is assumed.
∎
Proposition 7.3 (A scalar obstruction for a prescribed pin).
For every 0<\theta<\frac{1}{4} there are nondecreasing, 2-Lipschitz
profiles f_{\theta},g_{\theta} vanishing at zero, with
(1+\theta)u\leq f_{\theta}(u),g_{\theta}(u)\leq 2u,
and nondecreasing, 1-Lipschitz functions d_{x},d_{y} vanishing at zero,
such that both prefix inequalities, the refined prefix inequalities
(6.7) for every coefficient b(p,s) with
1<p\leq s\leq 1+\theta, and both strengthened transfer
inequalities with \widehat{H} hold, but
d_{x}(1)=\frac{3}{4}+\theta,\qquad d_{y}(1)=1.
Thus these scalar constraints alone cannot force
d_{x}(1)\geq\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon for any fixed universal
\varepsilon>0, when x is the prescribed pin.
Proof.
Put F=1+\theta, Q=\frac{3}{4}+\theta, and define
h_{0}(u)=\begin{cases}u,&0\leq u\leq\frac{1}{4},\\
\frac{1}{4},&\frac{1}{4}\leq u\leq\frac{1}{2},\\
\frac{3}{4}-u,&\frac{1}{2}\leq u\leq\frac{3}{4},\\
0,&\frac{3}{4}\leq u\leq 1,\end{cases}g_{0}(u)=u+h_{0}(u).
Take
f_{\theta}(u)=\min\{2u,F\},\qquad g_{\theta}(u)=(1-\theta)g_{0}(u)+2\theta u,\qquad d_{x}(u)=\min\{u,Q\},\qquad d_{y}(u)=u.
The stated monotonicity, Lipschitz and dimension bounds are immediate.
The prefix inequality for d_{x} holds when u\leq Q since f_{\theta}(u)\leq 2u,
and when u\geq Q since
\frac{u}{2}+\frac{f_{\theta}(u)}{4}\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{F}{4}=\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\theta}{4}\leq Q.
The prefix inequality for d_{y} follows from g_{\theta}(u)\leq 2u.
For the refined prefixes, c(p,s) increases in both parameters,
so b(p,s)\geq b_{\mathrm{sym}}(\theta)>0. Their right side is
u-b(2u-f_{\theta}(u))\leq u when u\leq Q. When u\geq Q,
f_{\theta}(u)=1+\theta, and that right side is at most
1-b_{\mathrm{sym}}(\theta)(1-\theta)=\frac{(1+\theta)(3-\theta)}{4-\theta}<Q,\qquad Q-\frac{(1+\theta)(3-\theta)}{4-\theta}=\frac{5\theta}{4(4-\theta)}>0.
For d_{y}, the refined prefixes again follow from g_{\theta}(u)\leq 2u.
We first record the relevant obstruction to projection partitions. Write
h_{\theta}=g_{\theta}-u. It equals u on [0,\frac{1}{4}] and is
nondecreasing on [0,\frac{1}{2}]. If 0<t<\frac{1}{4} and R>\frac{1}{2}, every
partition interval whose interior meets [0,\frac{1}{4}] ends before \frac{1}{2}.
It cannot be teal while containing a positive part of that initial
increase. Therefore its yellow costs, summed over the initial rise,
are at least \frac{1}{4}. Hence
\mathcal{L}_{t}(g_{\theta};R)\geq\frac{1}{4}\quad\text{for }0<t<\frac{1}{4},R>\frac{1}{2}.
(7.2)
Also g_{\theta}(R)\leq g_{0}(R)+\theta for 0\leq R\leq 1.
Consider the transfer inequality for d_{x} on 0<a<b\leq 1. If b\leq Q, the
distance increment is b-a, and (7.1) proves the
required inequality by evaluating the infimum at a.
Suppose b>Q. If a\geq\frac{1}{4}, the terminal cap implies
\widehat{H}_{f_{\theta},g_{\theta}}(a,b)\leq F. When a<\frac{F}{2},
F-f_{\theta}(a)=F-2a\leq Q-a;
when a\geq\frac{F}{2}, this source increment is zero. These statements
give the required transfer, including the case a\geq Q.
It remains to consider a<\frac{1}{4} and b>Q. Set R=b-a>\frac{1}{2}.
By (7.2),
P_{g_{\theta}}(a;R)\leq g_{0}(R)+\theta-\frac{1}{4}\leq Q-a.
For the last inequality, use g_{0}(R)=R when R\geq\frac{3}{4}, and
g_{0}(R)=\frac{3}{4} when \frac{1}{2}<R<\frac{3}{4}.
The other gain in the maximum defining \widehat{p} is also at most
Q-a: when R\geq\frac{3}{4} it is at most \frac{b+\theta}{2}, and otherwise
it is at most \frac{\frac{3}{4}+\theta+a}{2}.
Both are bounded by Q-a for
a<\frac{1}{4} and b\leq 1.
Evaluating the competitor infimum at a now yields
\widehat{H}_{f_{\theta},g_{\theta}}(a,b)\leq f_{\theta}(a)+Q-a.
This is the required transfer inequality for d_{x}, because d_{x}(a)=a and d_{x}(b)=Q.
Finally, the transfer inequality for d_{y}(u)=u always holds.
\widehat{H}_{g_{\theta},f_{\theta}}(a,b)\leq g_{\theta}(a)+\widehat{p}_{f_{\theta}}(a;b)\leq g_{\theta}(a)+b-a.
This completes the verification of every interval for both choices of pin.
∎
Remark 7.4 (The partition gain is not monotone).
Let g_{1}(u)=u and g_{2}(u)=u+h_{0}(u), with h_{0} as in the preceding
proof. Then g_{1}\leq g_{2}, but
P_{g_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{4};\frac{3}{4}\right)=\frac{3}{4},\qquad P_{g_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{4};\frac{3}{4}\right)=\frac{1}{2}.
For g_{1}, every partition has zero loss. For g_{2}, a partition
piece of length at most \frac{1}{4} meeting the initial increase in a
positive-length interval ends before \frac{1}{2} and cannot be teal.
The yellow costs therefore sum to at least \frac{1}{4}. The partition
with endpoints 0,\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4} attains that loss.
Thus a pointwise pin lower envelope cannot be substituted inside
P_{g}. The envelope updates in Sections B
and C use only the closed gain p_{g}.
The plateau
argument below and the proof of Theorem 1.2 in
Section 11 work directly with partition losses.
An affine pin profile g(u)=\sigma u, 1\leq\sigma\leq 2, illustrates
the information recovered by the full partition gain. Its loss is
(\sigma-1)R for every partition, so P_{g}(t;R)=R.
The full transfer then implies \widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,1)\geq 1, since
f(u)\geq u. Consequently d_{x}(1)\geq 1-f(a)\geq 1-2a for every
a>0, and d_{x}(1)=1 follows by letting a tend to zero.
The countermodel is a statement about the displayed pointwise
inequalities, without the common-source mass bound of
Proposition 14.2.
It does not assert that a Euclidean pair realizes all four profiles.
The next section derives the common structure of nearly critical
profiles.
The geometric argument will use information shared by
several actual pairs.
8 The plateau forced by a small pinned distance profile
The scalar obstruction does more than explain the limitation of the
previous argument. It suggests what must happen if a pinned distance
profile is nearly as small as \frac{3}{4}. We now prove that the
source must acquire information quickly at the early scales, and
that the pin must retain an excess of nearly \frac{1}{4} over the
trivial profile on an interval of length nearly \frac{1}{4}. Both
statements are proved at a general transfer level, because a source
of dimension larger than one admits a larger level
(Section 8.5), and the level enters the final
exponent comparison.
8.1 The certificate and the first hitting scale
Throughout this section f,g,d_{x} satisfy the profile bounds
(2.3), the prefix estimate (4.2)
with x as the pin, and the transfer estimate for d_{x} with
\widehat{H} (Lemma 7.2). Fix
0\leq\Delta<\frac{1}{2} and put
\lambda=1+\Delta,\qquad F=\frac{1}{2}+\Delta,\qquad a_{c}=\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\Delta}{2},\qquad W_{0}=\frac{1-2\Delta}{4},\qquad q=\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\Delta}{2}+\omega,
(8.1)
where \omega\geq 0. Let a_{*} be the first point
with f(a_{*})=F.
It exists when f(1)\geq F. We say that the pair
has the certificate at level \lambda if
H_{f,g}(a_{*},1)\geq\lambda,
(8.2)
where H_{f,g} is the closed transfer functional (4.4). In
particular f(1)\geq\lambda. For \Delta=0 the certificate holds
for every admissible pair. For a_{*}\leq s\leq\frac{1}{2} one has
f(s)\geq\frac{1}{2} and p_{g}(s;1)\geq\frac{1}{2}, for
s\geq\frac{1}{2} one has f(s)\geq s and p_{g}(s;1)=1-s, and
f(1)\geq 1. Section 8.5 obtains \Delta>0 from a
source dimension larger than one.
Lemma 8.1 (The first hitting scale).
If the pair has the certificate at level \lambda and
d_{x}(1)\leq q, then
a_{c}\leq a_{*}\leq a_{c}+2\omega.
Proof.
The bound f(u)\leq 2u implies a_{*}\geq\frac{F}{2}=a_{c}. The prefix
estimate at a_{*}, the transfer estimate on [a_{*},1], and
(8.2) give
q\geq d_{x}(1)\geq\frac{a_{*}}{2}+\frac{F}{4}+\lambda-F=\frac{a_{*}}{2}+\frac{F}{4}+\frac{1}{2},
which is a_{*}\leq 2q-\frac{F}{2}-1=a_{c}+2\omega.
∎
This first-hitting estimate holds for every \omega\geq 0. In
particular, suppose both orientations satisfy the prefix and closed
transfer estimates, and d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1)\leq Q with
\frac{3}{4}\leq Q<1. At level \lambda=1, both closed transfer
thresholds then have height at least one at
A_{\mathrm{init}}=\frac{1}{4}+2\left(Q-\frac{3}{4}\right)=2Q-\frac{5}{4}.
Indeed, apply the lemma with either endpoint as the pin and use
the monotonicity of H in its lower endpoint. This is the form
needed in Section 10.
For the plateau arguments that follow, we impose the additional
restriction
0<\omega<\frac{1-2\Delta}{24}.
8.2 Partition bounds
For a continuous function h on a compact interval I=[u,v]
and t>0, define
L_{t}(h;I)=\inf_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[a,b]\in\mathcal{P}\\
[a,b]\text{ labelled yellow}\end{subarray}}\bigl(h(b)-h(a)\bigr),
where \mathcal{P} ranges over finite yellow–teal partitions of I
with piece lengths at most t, as in
Section 7. A yellow piece has its minimum at
its left endpoint, and a teal piece has its minimum at its right
endpoint. Either label is allowed when both endpoints minimize h.
Set L_{t}(h;[u,u])=0. Thus
\mathcal{L}_{t}(g;R)=L_{t}(h_{g};[0,R]). The next lemma is the
elementary partition bound behind Stull’s projection argument.
Lemma 8.2 (A terminal-minimum partition bound).
Let h:[0,L]\to[0,\infty) be 1-Lipschitz, with h(L)=0 and
h(0)=p. For every t>0,
L_{t}(h;[0,L])\leq\max\{0,\frac{L-t-p}{2}\}.
(8.3)
The right side is attained as an upper bound by a partition with at most
\lceil\frac{2L}{t}\rceil+8 intervals.
Proof.
When L\leq t the whole interval is teal. First suppose that h is
piecewise linear with slopes 1 and -1. Apply the red–green–blue
construction preceding [33, Observation 20] to u+h(u).
In terms of h, red means increasing, blue means decreasing, and green
means an interval of length at most t whose two endpoints are equal
and minimize h. This construction uses only the finite polygonal
profile.
The point-dimension assumption used later in
[33, Lemma 21] is not involved here.
If there is a red–green–blue sequence, the construction supplies one
or two adjacent green intervals of combined length at least t.
Keep these intervals and partition the two complementary intervals by
the ordinary greedy yellow–teal rule. This rule
chooses the furthest admissible next endpoint at distance at most t,
labelling the resulting interval teal whenever possible. A non-teal
yellow interval has length t, unless it reaches the terminal endpoint.
otherwise continuity permits a further yellow extension. Two successive
teal intervals can be merged whenever their combined length is at most
t. Consequently successive greedy endpoints obey
a_{i+2}>a_{i}+t, except at the last endpoint, and a subinterval of length
\ell needs at most \lceil\frac{2\ell}{t}\rceil+2 pieces. The partition
retaining the green interval or pair therefore has at most
\lceil\frac{2L}{t}\rceil+8 pieces.
Let U be its yellow loss and let V be minus the sum of its teal
increments, including the green intervals with increment zero. Then
U-V=-p. The 1-Lipschitz bound, and the green length of at least
t, give U+V\leq L-t. Thus U\leq\frac{L-t-p}{2}.
If there is no red–green–blue sequence, there can be no red interval
in the auxiliary partition. Indeed, after a red interval a later blue
interval would give a red–green–blue sequence by choosing the last
red preceding the first subsequent blue. The absence of later blue
intervals would make the final height strictly greater than a preceding
height, since green intervals have zero endpoint increment. This
contradicts h(L)=\min h. The auxiliary partition thus consists only
of blue and green intervals. It can be coarsened to an all-teal
partition. Greedily choose the furthest teal endpoint at distance at
most t lying outside the interiors of the auxiliary green intervals.
Such a next endpoint always exists, because each green interval has
length at most t and the heights at all eligible endpoints are
nonincreasing. Two consecutive resulting intervals could be merged
if their combined length were at most t. The same endpoint spacing
therefore bounds their number by \lceil\frac{2L}{t}\rceil+2. Their loss is zero.
Finally approximate a general h uniformly by nonnegative finite
polygonal functions h_{j} with slopes 1 and -1, preserving both
endpoint values. One construction replaces each linear interpolation
segment by a rising-then-falling roof with the same endpoints. The
1-Lipschitz assumption makes that roof possible.
Its height above the
linear segment is at most the mesh size. The preceding partitions
have a uniform cardinality bound. After padding by zero-length
intervals, pass to a subsequence on which every endpoint and every
colour converges. The endpoint-minimum properties are closed under
uniform convergence, as are the length constraints, and the total
yellow loss converges. Remove zero-length intervals. The resulting
finite partition proves both assertions.
∎
Lemma 8.3 (Endpoint estimates for the partition loss).
Let h:[0,L]\to[0,\infty) be 1-Lipschitz with h(0)=p_{0} and
h(L)=p_{1}. For every t>0,
L_{t}(h;[0,L])\leq p_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\left[L-t-p_{0}-p_{1}\right]_{+}.
(8.4)
If moreover p_{0}=0 and
L_{t}(h;[0,L])\geq p_{1}+W,\qquad W>\frac{L-p_{1}-2t}{2},
then, for every u\in[0,L],
h(u)\geq\min\{W,u+2W-L+p_{1}+t,2W+t-u\}.
(8.5)
Proof.
Reversal u\mapsto L-u exchanges yellow and teal intervals and is a
bijection between admissible partitions. For any partition, the
forward loss minus the reversed loss is the sum of all signed
increments, namely p_{1}-p_{0}.
Hence the same identity holds for the
two infima. Partition losses are also subadditive under
concatenation, since partitions with losses arbitrarily close to
their infima can be concatenated.
Choose z where h attains its minimum m. Apply
(8.3) to h-m on [0,z] and to the
reversal of h-m on [z,L]. Combining this with the reversal identity, we obtain
L_{t}(h;[0,L])\leq p_{1}-m+\frac{1}{2}[z-t-p_{0}+m]_{+}+\frac{1}{2}[L-z-t-p_{1}+m]_{+}.
If both positive parts vanish, the right side is at most p_{1}.
If both are positive, it equals \frac{1}{2}(L-2t-p_{0}+p_{1}).
If only the first is positive, it equals
\frac{1}{2}(z-t-p_{0}+2p_{1}-m)\leq\frac{1}{2}(L-t-p_{0}+p_{1}), since
p_{1}-m\leq L-z. If only the second is positive, it equals
\frac{1}{2}(L-z-t+p_{1}-m)\leq\frac{1}{2}(L-t-p_{0}+p_{1}), since
p_{0}-m\leq z. Thus the loss is at most
\max\{p_{1},\frac{1}{2}(L-t-p_{0}+p_{1})\}, which is
(8.4).
For the second assertion fix u and put p=h(u). The reversal
identity and (8.3) on [0,u], and
(8.4) on [u,L], give
\displaystyle L_{t}(h;[0,L])-p_{1}
\displaystyle\leq p+\frac{1}{2}[u-t-p]_{+}+\frac{1}{2}[L-p_{1}-u-t-p]_{+}
\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{p,\frac{p+u-t}{2},\frac{p+L-p_{1}-u-t}{2},\frac{L-p_{1}-2t}{2}\right\}.
The last entry is strictly below W, so one of the first three is
at least W. Solving each for p results in
(8.5).
∎
Lemma 8.4 (A long interval above a prescribed height).
Let h:[0,L]\to[0,\infty) be 1-Lipschitz with h(0)=0 and
h(L)=p_{1}. Suppose t>0 and
L_{t}(h;[0,L])\geq p_{1}+W,\qquad W>\max\left\{p_{1},\frac{L-p_{1}-2t}{2}\right\}.
Then there is a u_{-} with
W\leq u_{-}\leq L-p_{1}-t-W,\qquad h(u)\geq W\quad\text{for }u_{-}\leq u\leq u_{-}+t.
(8.6)
Proof.
Put c=\frac{L-p_{1}}{2}. The last two terms in
(8.5) coincide at u=c and equal
2W+t-c\geq W, so h(c)\geq W. For p_{1}<m<W, the same estimate
shows that
(L-p_{1}-t-2W+m,2W+t-m)\cap[0,L]\subseteq\{h>m\}.
This interval contains c, and its endpoints lie in [0,L], since
otherwise the estimate at 0 or at L would contradict
h(0)=0<m or h(L)=p_{1}<m. Let (a_{m},b_{m}) be the component of
\{h>m\} containing c. Then h(a_{m})=h(b_{m})=m and
a_{m}\leq L-p_{1}-t-2W+m,\qquad b_{m}\geq 2W+t-m.
Suppose b_{m}-a_{m}\leq t. Then [a_{m},b_{m}] is an admissible teal
interval with loss zero. On the two complementary intervals,
(8.4) implies
L_{t}(h;[0,a_{m}])\leq m+\frac{1}{2}[a_{m}-t-m]_{+}=m,\qquad L_{t}(h;[b_{m},L])\leq p_{1}+\frac{1}{2}[L-b_{m}-t-m-p_{1}]_{+}=p_{1},
because both positive-part arguments are at most
L-p_{1}-2t-2W<0. Concatenation would imply
L_{t}(h;[0,L])\leq m+p_{1}<W+p_{1}, a contradiction. Thus b_{m}-a_{m}>t.
As m increases to W, the components (a_{m},b_{m}) are nested,
since all contain c. Their closures intersect in an interval
[u_{-},u_{+}] with u_{+}-u_{-}\geq t, and h\geq W on it by continuity. The
bound for a_{m} implies u_{-}\leq L-p_{1}-t-W, and h(0)=0 with the
Lipschitz bound implies u_{-}\geq W. The interval [u_{-},u_{-}+t] has the
asserted properties.
∎
8.3 The pin plateau and its excess
The plateau proposition records, besides the plateau, the amount
\mathfrak{e}\omega by which the full transfer threshold
\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,1) falls short of \lambda for lower
endpoints a after the critical competitor. This excess enters
the finite comparison in two ways, and Lemma 8.7
turns it into a lower envelope for the source profile.
Proposition 8.5 (The pin plateau).
Suppose the pair has the certificate at level \lambda and
d_{x}(1)\leq q. Then there are numbers
\chi,\varsigma,\mathfrak{e} and u_{-},u_{+},W with
\begin{gathered}-1\leq\chi\leq 2,\qquad\varsigma\geq\max\{0,-2\chi\},\qquad 3\varsigma+2\chi\leq 4,\qquad 0\leq\mathfrak{e}\leq\varsigma,\\
u_{+}-u_{-}=a_{c}+\chi\omega\leq a_{*},\qquad f(u_{+}-u_{-})=F-\varsigma\omega,\\
W=W_{0}-(\varsigma+\chi-\mathfrak{e})\omega,\qquad 0\leq u_{-}-W\leq(2\varsigma-2\mathfrak{e})\omega,\end{gathered}
(8.7)
such that
g(u)-u\geq W\quad\text{for }u_{-}\leq u\leq u_{+},
(8.8)
and such that, for every a>u_{+}-u_{-},
\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,1)\geq\lambda-\mathfrak{e}\omega.
(8.9)
In particular
W\geq W_{0}-2\omega,\qquad(u_{+}-u_{-})+2W=\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\Delta}{2}-(2\varsigma+\chi-2\mathfrak{e})\omega,\qquad u_{+}\leq\frac{1}{2}+(\varsigma-\mathfrak{e})\omega.
(8.10)
Proof.
Write h=g-u. By Lemma 8.1,
a_{c}\leq a_{*}\leq a_{c}+2\omega, and by (8.2) every
competitor s\geq a_{*} satisfies f(s)+\widehat{p}_{g}(s;1)\geq\lambda.
For every s and R=1-s, the partition gain
is at least the closed gain. Indeed
(8.4) with p_{0}=0 implies
L_{s}(h;[0,R])\leq h(R)+\frac{1}{2}[R-s-h(R)]_{+}, hence
P_{g}(s;R)=g(R)-L_{s}(h;[0,R])\geq\min\left\{R,\frac{g(R)+s}{2}\right\}=p_{g}(s;1),
so that \widehat{p}_{g}(s;1)=P_{g}(s;1-s).
Fix 0<\vartheta\leq\frac{1}{8} and put
\Phi(u)=\frac{3f(u)}{4}-\frac{u}{2} and
\ell=a_{c}-(1+\vartheta)\omega. Since \Phi(0)=0<\ell and
\Phi(a_{*})=\frac{3F}{4}-\frac{a_{*}}{2}\geq a_{c}-\omega>\ell, there
is a last point a_{0}<a_{*} with \Phi(a_{0})=\ell, and \Phi\geq\ell
on [a_{0},a_{*}].
Since \Phi(u)\leq u, also a_{0}\geq\ell>0. The
prefix estimate implies f(a_{0})-d_{x}(a_{0})\leq\Phi(a_{0})=\ell, so the
transfer estimate yields
\Lambda_{\vartheta}:=\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a_{0},1)\leq d_{x}(1)+f(a_{0})-d_{x}(a_{0})\leq q+\ell=\lambda-\vartheta\omega.
As f(1)\geq\lambda and every competitor at or after a_{*} costs at
least \lambda, there is a competitor s\in[a_{0},a_{*}) with
f(s)+P_{g}(s;1-s)=\Lambda_{\vartheta}+e,\qquad 0\leq e\leq\vartheta^{2}\omega.
Write
s=a_{c}+\chi\omega,\qquad f(s)=F-\varsigma\omega,\qquad R=1-s,\qquad h(R)=\varphi\omega,\qquad\mathfrak{e}=\frac{\lambda-\Lambda_{\vartheta}}{\omega}\geq\vartheta,
where \chi,\varsigma,\varphi,\mathfrak{e} depend on \vartheta.
The bounds a_{0}\leq s<a_{*}, f(s)\leq F, f(s)\leq 2s, and
\Phi(s)\geq\ell give
-1-\vartheta\leq\chi\leq 2,\qquad\varsigma\geq\max\{0,-2\chi\},\qquad 3\varsigma+2\chi\leq 4+4\vartheta,\qquad\varsigma\leq 2+2\vartheta.
(8.11)
Consequently \varsigma+\chi\leq\frac{7}{3},
\varsigma-\frac{\chi}{2}\leq\frac{5}{2}(1+\vartheta)\leq\frac{45}{16},
3\varsigma+\chi\leq 5(1+\vartheta)\leq\frac{45}{8}, and
\widehat{p}_{g}(s;1)=\Lambda_{\vartheta}+e-f(s)=\frac{1}{2}+(\varsigma-\mathfrak{e})\omega+e is smaller than the
length cap R=\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\Delta}{2}-\chi\omega, because
(\varsigma+\chi)\omega<W_{0} and
e\leq\vartheta^{2}\omega<\mathfrak{e}\omega. Hence the closed gain
p_{g}(s;1) equals \frac{g(R)+s}{2}=\frac{1+\varphi\omega}{2}, and
p_{g}\leq\widehat{p}_{g} implies
\mathfrak{e}\leq\varsigma-\frac{\varphi}{2}+\frac{e}{\omega}.
The identity \widehat{p}_{g}(s;1)=P_{g}(s;R)=R+h(R)-L_{s}(h;[0,R]) now
reads
L_{s}(h;[0,R])=h(R)+W_{\vartheta},\qquad W_{\vartheta}=W_{0}-(\varsigma+\chi-\mathfrak{e})\omega-e.
The hypotheses of Lemma 8.4 hold for h on
[0,R] with t=s and p_{1}=h(R):
\displaystyle W_{\vartheta}-\frac{R-h(R)-2s}{2}
\displaystyle=\frac{1+2\Delta}{8}-\left(\varsigma-\frac{\chi}{2}-\frac{\varphi}{2}-\mathfrak{e}\right)\omega-e\geq\frac{1}{8}-\frac{45}{16}\omega-e>0,
\displaystyle W_{\vartheta}-h(R)
\displaystyle=W_{0}-(\varsigma+\chi+\varphi-\mathfrak{e})\omega-e\geq W_{0}-\frac{45}{8}\omega-3e>0,
using \varphi\geq 0 in the first line and
\varphi\leq 2\varsigma-2\mathfrak{e}+\frac{2e}{\omega} in the second,
together with \omega<\frac{W_{0}}{6}\leq\frac{1}{24} and
e\leq\vartheta^{2}\omega\leq\frac{\omega}{64}. The lemma supplies
h\geq W_{\vartheta} on [u_{-,\vartheta},u_{-,\vartheta}+s] with
W_{\vartheta}\leq u_{-,\vartheta}\leq R-h(R)-s-W_{\vartheta}=W_{\vartheta}+(2\varsigma-\varphi-2\mathfrak{e})\omega+2e.
Moreover, for every a>s we have a>a_{0}, so
\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,1)\geq\Lambda_{\vartheta}=\lambda-\mathfrak{e}\omega.
Let \vartheta\searrow 0 along a sequence on which
(\chi,\varsigma,\varphi,\mathfrak{e},u_{-,\vartheta}) converges, and
denote the limits by the same letters. Put t=a_{c}+\chi\omega,
W=W_{0}-(\varsigma+\chi-\mathfrak{e})\omega, u_{-}=\lim u_{-,\vartheta},
and u_{+}=u_{-}+t. Continuity of f and h ensures f(t)=F-\varsigma\omega,
t\leq a_{*}, and h\geq W on [u_{-},u_{+}].
The limiting bounds give
(8.7), with
0\leq\mathfrak{e}\leq\varsigma-\frac{\varphi}{2}\leq\varsigma.
The monotonicity of \widehat{H}_{f,g} in its lower endpoint implies
(8.9). Finally
\varsigma+\chi\leq\frac{4+\chi}{3}\leq 2 gives W\geq W_{0}-2\omega, the
formula for (u_{+}-u_{-})+2W is a substitution, and
u_{+}\leq W+(2\varsigma-2\mathfrak{e})\omega+a_{c}+\chi\omega=\frac{1}{2}+(\varsigma-\mathfrak{e})\omega.
∎
We write
u_{0}=\varsigma+2\chi\geq 0,\qquad\varpi=\min\{2,1+u_{0}\},\qquad e_{0}=\mathfrak{e}+[u_{0}-1]_{+}
(8.12)
for the combinations of the plateau parameters that enter the
geometry. The inequalities defining the polytope (8.7) imply
\varsigma\leq 2-\frac{u_{0}}{2},\qquad 2\varsigma+\chi\leq 3-\frac{u_{0}}{4},\qquad\varsigma+\chi\leq 2,\qquad\varpi+\varsigma\leq\frac{7}{2},\qquad 2\varsigma+\varpi\leq 5.
(8.13)
Indeed 3\varsigma+2\chi=2\varsigma+u_{0}\leq 4 proves the first two.
For the last two use \varpi=1+u_{0} when u_{0}\leq 1 and
2\varsigma\leq 4-u_{0}\leq 3 when u_{0}\geq 1.
8.4 The source profile near the first hitting scale
The source parents of the finite argument will have side
\delta^{a} with a slightly larger than a_{*}. The next two
lemmas locate a_{*} in terms of the plateau parameters and bound the
source profile at the scales between a_{c}-\omega and a_{*}.
Lemma 8.6 (The first hitting scale and the plateau).
Under the hypotheses of Proposition 8.5,
a_{*}\leq a_{c}+\varpi\omega.
Proof.
Write s=u_{+}-u_{-}=a_{c}+\chi\omega and D_{0}=2s-f(s)=u_{0}\omega, and put
b_{0}=\frac{a_{*}}{2}. Since s\geq a_{c}-\omega and
b_{0}\leq\frac{a_{c}}{2}+\omega, we have b_{0}<s. For b_{0}\leq t\leq s
the 2-Lipschitz bound implies f(t)-t\geq f(s)-2s+t=t-D_{0}\geq b_{0}-D_{0}.
For s\leq t\leq a_{*}, monotonicity and the Lipschitz bound give
f(t)\geq\max\{F-\varsigma\omega,F-2(a_{*}-t)\}, and the mean of the
two affine bounds yields
f(t)-t\geq F-a_{*}-\frac{\varsigma\omega}{2}. On [b_{0},a_{*}] every
closed gain is saturated because t\geq\frac{a_{*}}{2} and g(R)\geq R give
p_{g}(t;a_{*})=a_{*}-t. Hence
H_{f,g}(b_{0},a_{*})\geq\min\left\{a_{*}+b_{0}-D_{0},F-\frac{\varsigma\omega}{2}\right\}.
Prefix at b_{0}, the transfer on [b_{0},a_{*}], and f(b_{0})\leq 2b_{0}
give
d_{x}(a_{*})\geq H_{f,g}(b_{0},a_{*})-b_{0}\geq\min\left\{a_{*}-D_{0},F-\frac{a_{*}}{2}-\frac{\varsigma\omega}{2}\right\}.
The second branch exceeds a_{c}+\omega, because a_{*}\leq a_{c}+2\omega,
\varsigma\leq 2, and \omega<\frac{a_{c}}{6} give
F-\frac{a_{*}}{2}-\frac{\varsigma\omega}{2}-a_{c}-\omega\geq\frac{a_{c}}{2}-3\omega>0. On the other hand the transfer on
[a_{*},1] and (8.2) give
d_{x}(a_{*})\leq d_{x}(1)-(\lambda-F)\leq q-\frac{1}{2}=a_{c}+\omega. Hence
the first branch is at most a_{c}+\omega, that is,
a_{*}\leq a_{c}+(1+u_{0})\omega. Together with
Lemma 8.1 this is the claim.
∎
Lemma 8.7 (A source envelope at the row scales).
Under the hypotheses of Proposition 8.5, if
\mathfrak{e}\leq\frac{1}{8}, then
f(a)\geq a+a_{c}-(1+u_{0}+\mathfrak{e})\omega\quad\text{for }a_{c}-\omega\leq a\leq a_{c}+\varpi\omega.
(8.14)
Proof.
Let s=u_{+}-u_{-} and D_{0}=u_{0}\omega as above. For a\leq s the
Lipschitz bound implies f(a)\geq 2a-D_{0}, and
(2a-D_{0})-[a+a_{c}-(1+u_{0}+\mathfrak{e})\omega]=a-a_{c}+(1+\mathfrak{e})\omega\geq\mathfrak{e}\omega\geq 0. For
a\geq a_{*} we have f(a)\geq F, while the right side of
(8.14) is at most F because
a\leq a_{c}+\varpi\omega and \varpi\leq 1+u_{0}.
Let s<a<a_{*} and put b=\frac{a}{2}\leq s. As in the preceding
proof, f(t)-t\geq b-D_{0} on [b,s],
f(t)-t\geq\frac{f(s)+f(a)}{2}-a on [s,a], and the closed gains
on [b,a] are saturated, so
H_{f,g}(b,a)\geq\min\left\{a+b-D_{0},\frac{f(s)+f(a)}{2}\right\},\qquad d_{x}(a)\geq\min\left\{a-D_{0},\frac{f(s)+f(a)-a}{2}\right\}.
On the other hand (8.9) and the transfer on
[a,1] give d_{x}(a)\leq q-\lambda+\mathfrak{e}\omega+f(a)=f(a)-a_{c}+(1+\mathfrak{e})\omega. If the second branch of the lower
bound were at most this upper bound, then
f(a)\geq f(s)+2a_{c}-a-2(1+\mathfrak{e})\omega\geq 3a_{c}-(\varpi+\varsigma+2+2\mathfrak{e})\omega, so
f(a)-F\geq a_{c}-\frac{23}{4}\omega>0 by
(8.13) and \mathfrak{e}\leq\frac{1}{8},
contradicting f(a)\leq F. Hence the first branch is at most the
upper bound, which is (8.14).
∎
8.5 Larger certificates from the source dimension
If the source profile satisfies f(u)\geq(1+\kappa)u, which is the
case for a source measure with Frostman exponent 1+\kappa, the
certificate holds at a level above one, and the level can be
improved by iterating the prefix and transfer estimates. The
iteration is carried out with the prefix estimate in the form
d_{x}(u)\geq(1-2b)u+bf(u)\quad\text{for }0\leq u\leq 1,
(8.15)
where 0<b\leq\frac{1}{4}: the case b=\frac{1}{4} is
(4.2), and Corollary 6.2 yields
smaller coefficients when the endpoints have dimension larger than
one.
Lemma 8.8 (Bootstrapped certificate).
Suppose f(u)\geq(1+\kappa)u on [0,1] with 0<\kappa<1, that
(8.15) holds for some 0<b\leq\frac{1}{4}, and
that d_{x}(1)\leq q with
1-b(1-\kappa)\leq q<1-\frac{b(1-\kappa)}{2}.
(8.16)
Put
\mathcal{D}=\frac{\kappa}{b(1-\kappa)},\qquad\lambda_{*}=1+\mathcal{D}(1-q),\qquad\Delta_{*}=\lambda_{*}-1.
Then the first point a_{*} with f(a_{*})=\lambda_{*}-\frac{1}{2}
exists, and
H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq\lambda_{*}\quad\text{for }a_{*}\leq a\leq 1,\qquad a_{*}\leq\frac{q-\frac{1}{2}-b(\lambda_{*}-\frac{1}{2})}{1-2b},\qquad a_{*}\leq\frac{1}{4}+2\Bigl(q-\frac{3}{4}\Bigr)-\frac{\Delta_{*}}{2}.
Proof.
Put \lambda_{0}=1+\frac{\kappa}{2} and let a_{0} be the first point
with f(a_{0})=\lambda_{0}-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1+\kappa}{2}.
It lies at
or before \frac{1}{2}. For a_{0}\leq s\leq\frac{1}{2},
p_{g}(s;1)\geq\frac{1}{2} implies f(s)+p_{g}(s;1)\geq\lambda_{0}.
For
s\geq\frac{1}{2}, f(s)+1-s\geq 1+\kappa s\geq\lambda_{0}, and f(1)\geq 1+\kappa\geq\lambda_{0}. Thus H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq\lambda_{0} for
a\geq a_{0}.
Suppose H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq\lambda_{j} for a\geq a_{j}, where a_{j} is
the first point with f(a_{j})=\lambda_{j}-\frac{1}{2}, a_{j}\leq\frac{1}{2},
and \lambda_{0}\leq\lambda_{j}\leq\lambda_{*}. The prefix estimate
(8.15) at s\geq a_{j} and the transfer on [s,1]
give q\geq(1-2b)s+bf(s)+\lambda_{j}-f(s), that is,
(1-b)f(s)\geq\lambda_{j}-q+(1-2b)s. Hence, for
\frac{1}{2}\leq s\leq 1,
f(s)+1-s\geq 1+\max\left\{\kappa s,\frac{\lambda_{j}-q-bs}{1-b}\right\}\geq\lambda_{j+1}:=1+\frac{\kappa}{b+(1-b)\kappa}(\lambda_{j}-q),
the minimum over s of the maximum being attained where the two
affine functions meet, at s_{j}=\frac{\lambda_{j}-q}{b+(1-b)\kappa}.
Here s_{j}\geq\frac{1}{2} is the upper inequality in
(8.16) at \lambda_{j}=\lambda_{0}, and
s_{j}\leq 1 follows from \lambda_{j}\leq\lambda_{*}\leq 1+\kappa, which is
the lower inequality. At s=\frac{1}{2} this shows that the first
point a_{j+1} with f(a_{j+1})=\lambda_{j+1}-\frac{1}{2} is at
or before \frac{1}{2}.
For a_{j+1}\leq s\leq\frac{1}{2} the gain
p_{g}(s;1)\geq\frac{1}{2} gives f(s)+p_{g}(s;1)\geq\lambda_{j+1}, and f(1)\geq 1+\kappa\geq\lambda_{j+1}. Thus H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq\lambda_{j+1}
for a\geq a_{j+1}.
The map \lambda\mapsto 1+\frac{\kappa}{b+(1-b)\kappa}(\lambda-q) has
slope less than one and fixed point \lambda_{*}, and
\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda_{*} is the upper inequality in
(8.16). Hence
\lambda_{j}\nearrow\lambda_{*}, the points a_{j} increase to some
a_{*}\leq\frac{1}{2} with f(a_{*})=\lambda_{*}-\frac{1}{2} by
continuity, and the inequalities H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq\lambda_{j} for
a\geq a_{*} pass to the limit. The prefix estimate
(8.15) and the transfer at a_{*} give
q\geq(1-2b)a_{*}+b(\lambda_{*}-\frac{1}{2})+\frac{1}{2}, which is the
first bound for a_{*}.
The second follows in the same way from
(4.2), which is implied by
(8.15) because b\leq\frac{1}{4} and f(u)\leq 2u.
∎
At b=\frac{1}{4} the lemma says that if \frac{\kappa}{4}\leq\varepsilon<\frac{1+\kappa}{8}
and q=\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon, then \lambda_{*}=\frac{1-4\kappa\varepsilon}{1-\kappa}.
With b=b_{\mathrm{pin}}(\kappa) from (6.9), the
range is \frac{\kappa}{2}\leq\varepsilon<\frac{1+2\kappa}{8} and
\mathcal{D}=\frac{4\kappa}{1-2\kappa},\qquad\Delta_{*}=\frac{\kappa(1-4\varepsilon)}{1-2\kappa};\text{with }b=b_{\mathrm{sym}}(\kappa),\qquad\mathcal{D}=\frac{\kappa(4-\kappa)}{1-3\kappa+\kappa^{2}}.
(8.17)
With \lambda=\lambda_{*} and \Delta=\Delta_{*}, the pair has the
certificate (8.2), and the target
q=\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon has the form (8.1)
with \omega=\varepsilon-\frac{\Delta_{*}}{2}. The two bounds for
a_{*} imply \Delta_{*}\leq 2\varepsilon. Since q<1, we obtain
0\leq\Delta_{*}<\frac{1}{2} and \omega\geq 0. This is how the source
dimension enters Theorems 1.1
and 1.2: through the prefix coefficient b and the
lower bound 1+\kappa, it raises the level of the certificate, and
the arguments of Section 11 and of
Part III are carried out at that level.
9 Barriers for the reciprocal estimates
A small distance profile forces both endpoints to carry information
before a common cutoff. We first follow that information to later
scales. The two barriers proved here will allow us to move the cutoff
backwards. The reciprocal argument in the next section retains the
separate endpoint values when this common lower bound is no longer enough.
Throughout this section f,g,d_{x},d_{y} satisfy (2.3),
the prefix estimate (4.2), the transfer estimate of
Lemma 4.1, and the full partition transfer of
Lemma 7.2, for both choices of pin, and
d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1)\leq Q. We write h_{g}=g-u as in
Section 7, and \ell=\min\{f,g\}.
9.1 The correlated prefix–transfer estimate
For 0<a\leq 1 and z\geq 0 put
\phi_{a}(z)=z-\frac{3}{4}\min\{z,2a\}=\max\Bigl\{\frac{z}{4},z-\frac{3a}{2}\Bigr\},
(9.1)
and, for a continuous nondecreasing \ell_{0}:[0,1]\to[0,\infty),
G_{\ell_{0}}(a,b)=\frac{a}{2}+\inf_{a\leq t\leq b}\bigl\{\phi_{a}(\ell_{0}(t))+p_{\ell_{0}}(t;b)\bigr\}\quad\text{for }0<a\leq b\leq 1;
(9.2)
the endpoint t=b contributes \frac{a}{2}+\phi_{a}(\ell_{0}(b)), since
p_{\ell_{0}}(b;b)=0. The right side depends only on \ell_{0} on
[0,b] and is nondecreasing in \ell_{0}. The condition \ell_{0}\leq f,g
is used in the following lemma.
Lemma 9.1 (Correlated prefix and transfer).
If \ell_{0}\leq f and \ell_{0}\leq g, then d_{x}(b),d_{y}(b)\geq G_{\ell_{0}}(a,b)
for 0<a\leq b\leq 1.
Proof.
For a<b, the transfer estimate without its positive part and the
prefix estimate give
d_{x}(b)\geq\frac{a}{2}+\frac{f(a)}{4}+H_{f,g}(a,b)-f(a)=\frac{a}{2}+\inf_{a\leq t\leq b}\bigl\{f(t)-\frac{3}{4}f(a)+p_{g}(t;b)\bigr\},
where the endpoint t=b accounts for the term f(b) in
(4.4). Since f is nondecreasing and f(a)\leq 2a,
f(t)-\frac{3}{4}f(a)\geq f(t)-\frac{3}{4}\min\{f(t),2a\}=\phi_{a}(f(t))\geq\phi_{a}(\ell_{0}(t)),
because \phi_{a} is nondecreasing, and p_{g}(t;b)\geq p_{\ell_{0}}(t;b).
For a=b the claim is the prefix estimate, as \ell_{0}(b)\leq 2b.
Exchanging the roles of x and y proves the bound for d_{y}.
∎
9.2 Two barriers
Fix \lambda\geq 1 and put k=\lambda-Q. We say that the tails have
height \lambda at A if H_{f,g}(A,1)\geq\lambda and
H_{g,f}(A,1)\geq\lambda. If the tails have height \lambda at A,
then for b\geq A the transfer estimate on [b,1] implies
Q\geq d_{x}(1)\geq d_{x}(b)+\lambda-f(b), and likewise for g, so
Lemma 9.1 with \ell_{0}=\ell implies
\ell(b)\geq k+G_{\ell}(a,b)\quad\text{for }b\geq A,0<a\leq b.
(9.3)
Moreover, if H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq\lambda, then
Q\geq\frac{a}{2}+\frac{f(a)}{4}+\lambda-f(a), that is,
f(a)\geq\frac{4}{3}\Bigl(k+\frac{a}{2}\Bigr),\qquad\text{hence}\quad a\geq k\quad\text{and}\quad f(u)\geq 2u-\frac{4}{3}(a-k)\quad\text{for }0\leq u\leq a,
(9.4)
by f(a)\leq 2a and the 2-Lipschitz bound. We call \frac{4}{3}(a-k)
the defect of the cutoff a.
Lemma 9.2 (Two barriers).
Let k>0, 0\leq\beta<k, 4k\leq 1, and A=k+\frac{3}{4}\beta.
Suppose that \ell(t)\geq 2t-\beta for 0\leq t\leq A and that
(9.3) holds. Then
\displaystyle\ell(t)
\displaystyle\geq t+k-\frac{\beta}{2}
\displaystyle\text{for }A\leq t\leq 3k,
(9.5)
\displaystyle\ell(t)
\displaystyle\geq\frac{t}{2}+\frac{5k}{2}-\frac{\beta}{2}
\displaystyle\text{for }3k\leq t\leq 4k.
(9.6)
Proof.
Put m=k-\frac{\beta}{2}.
We first prove the lower bound on the middle interval. For 0\leq\mathfrak{d}<k put a=k-\mathfrak{d}
and
L(t)=\max\bigl\{t,\min\{2t-\beta,t+a-\frac{\beta}{2}\}\bigr\}.
We claim that
G_{L}(a,b)\geq b-\frac{\beta}{2}\quad\text{if }\beta\leq 2a\text{ and }a\leq b\leq 3a.
(9.7)
The two affine pieces of L meet at t_{0}=a+\frac{\beta}{2}, and
L(t)=t+a-\frac{\beta}{2}\geq 2a for t\geq t_{0}. Put
F(t)=\frac{a}{2}+\phi_{a}(L(t)). For t\geq t_{0},
\phi_{a}(L(t))=L(t)-\frac{3a}{2}, so F(t)=t-\frac{\beta}{2}. For
a\leq t\leq t_{0}, L(t)\geq 2t-\beta implies
F(t)\geq\frac{a+t}{2}-\frac{\beta}{4}. The branch b-t of
p_{L}(t;b) therefore yields F(t)+b-t\geq b-\frac{\beta}{2} in both
cases, using t\leq t_{0} in the second. For the other branch put
u=b-t and consider
F(t)+\frac{L(u)+t}{2}-(b-\frac{\beta}{2}). If t\leq t_{0} this is at
least \frac{a}{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}-u+\frac{L(u)}{2}, which is
nonnegative for u\leq t_{0} by L(u)\geq 2u-\beta and \beta\leq 2a, and
for u\geq t_{0} by L(u)=u+a-\frac{\beta}{2} and u\leq b-a\leq 2a. If
t\geq t_{0}, twice the difference is b-3u+L(u), which is at least
t-\beta\geq 0 for u\leq t_{0} and at least 3a+\frac{\beta}{2}-b\geq 0
for u\geq t_{0}, by u\leq b-t_{0}. This proves the claim.
For A\leq B<3k let
\mathfrak{d}(B)=\max_{A\leq t\leq B}[t+m-\ell(t)]_{+}. This is
continuous in B, and \mathfrak{d}(A)=0 because
\ell(A)\geq 2A-\beta=A+m+\frac{\beta}{4}. Suppose
\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{d}(B)>0 is so small that B<3(k-\mathfrak{d})
and \beta\leq 2(k-\mathfrak{d}), and put a=k-\mathfrak{d}. Then
\ell\geq L on [0,B]: on [0,A] by hypothesis, and on [A,B]
because \ell(t)\geq t+m-\mathfrak{d}=t+a-\frac{\beta}{2}. At a point
b\in[A,B] where the maximum \mathfrak{d} is attained,
(9.3), the monotonicity of G in its envelope, and
(9.7) give \ell(b)\geq k+b-\frac{\beta}{2}=b+m,
contradicting \ell(b)=b+m-\mathfrak{d}. Hence the set of
B\in[A,3k) with \mathfrak{d}(B)=0 is open and closed in [A,3k),
so it is all of [A,3k), and continuity covers the endpoint t=3k.
We now extend the lower bound to the next interval. Put
L_{0}(t)=\max\{t,\min\{2t-\beta,t+m\}\} and
P(t)=\frac{t}{2}+\frac{5k}{2}-\frac{\beta}{2}. By the hypothesis and
the first barrier, \ell\geq L_{0} on [0,3k], and
\ell(3k)\geq 3k+m=P(3k). Fix 3k\leq B<4k, let
\mathfrak{D}=\max_{3k\leq t\leq B}[P(t)-\ell(t)]_{+}, and suppose
\mathfrak{D}>0 is so small that a=k-\frac{\mathfrak{D}}{2} satisfies
a>\beta and B-a<3k. Let b\in[3k,B] attain the maximum. We claim
G_{\ell}(a,b)\geq\frac{b}{2}+\frac{3k}{2}-\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{\mathfrak{D}}{2}.
(9.8)
Write e=\frac{\mathfrak{D}}{2} and t_{0}=k+\frac{\beta}{2}, where the
two affine pieces of L_{0} meet. For a\leq t\leq 3k we use
\ell\geq L_{0}.
With F(t)=\frac{a}{2}+\phi_{a}(L_{0}(t)),
F(t)=\begin{cases}\frac{a+t}{2}-\frac{\beta}{4},&a\leq t\leq a+\frac{\beta}{2},\\[2.0pt]
2t-\beta-a,&a+\frac{\beta}{2}\leq t\leq t_{0},\\[2.0pt]
t+m-a,&t_{0}\leq t\leq 3k,\end{cases}
because L_{0}(t)=2t-\beta on [a,t_{0}], L_{0}(t)=t+m\geq 2a on
[t_{0},3k], and 2t-\beta\geq 2a exactly when t\geq a+\frac{\beta}{2}.
In each case F(t)\geq t-\frac{\beta}{2}, so the branch b-t gives at
least b-\frac{\beta}{2}, which suffices since b\geq 3k. For the
other branch put u=b-t<3k.
After subtracting \frac{b}{2} the
quantity to bound is F(t)+\frac{L_{0}(u)-u}{2}. If t\leq t_{0}, then
u\geq 3k-t_{0}\geq t_{0}, so L_{0}(u)=u+m and the quantity is at least
F(a)+\frac{m}{2}=\frac{3k}{2}-\frac{\beta}{2}-e. If t\geq t_{0} and
u\geq t_{0}, it is at least t_{0}+m-a+\frac{m}{2}=\frac{3k}{2}-\frac{\beta}{4}+e.
If t\geq t_{0} and \beta\leq u\leq t_{0}, then L_{0}(u)=2u-\beta and the
quantity equals b-\frac{u}{2}+m-a-\frac{\beta}{2}\geq b-\frac{k}{2}-\frac{5\beta}{4}+e,
which suffices since b\geq 3k and \beta<k. If u\leq\beta, then
L_{0}(u)\geq u and the quantity is at least
b-\beta+m-a=b-\frac{3\beta}{2}+e, which suffices as well. Finally let
3k\leq t\leq b. Then \ell(t)\geq P(t)-\mathfrak{D}\geq 2a, so
\frac{a}{2}+\phi_{a}(\ell(t))=\ell(t)-a\geq\frac{t}{2}+\frac{3k}{2}-\frac{\beta}{2}-e.
Adding b-t\geq\frac{b}{2}-\frac{t}{2} for the first branch, or
\frac{\ell(u)+t}{2}\geq\frac{u+t}{2}=\frac{b}{2} for the other, yields
at least \frac{b}{2}+\frac{3k}{2}-\frac{\beta}{2}-e. This proves the
claim. Now (9.3) implies
\ell(b)\geq P(b)-\frac{\mathfrak{D}}{2}, contradicting
\ell(b)=P(b)-\mathfrak{D}. The same open-closed argument propagates
\mathfrak{D}(B)=0 from B=3k to every B<4k, and continuity covers the endpoint
t=4k.
∎
Lemma 9.3 (Updating a common cutoff).
Suppose 1\leq\lambda<\frac{9}{8}, \frac{1}{5}<k<\frac{1}{4},
k<\frac{4\lambda-1}{14}, and 0\leq v<k.
If the tails have height \lambda at
A^{\prime}=k+\frac{3v}{4}<\frac{1}{2}, they also have that height at
a(v)=\max\left\{\lambda-1+v,\frac{2\lambda-1-k}{4}+\frac{5v}{8},\frac{4\lambda-1-5k}{9}+\frac{5v}{9}\right\}.
The new defect is
\frac{4}{3}(a(v)-k)=\max\left\{\frac{4v}{3}-\frac{4(k-\lambda+1)}{3},\frac{5v}{6}+\frac{2\lambda-1-5k}{3},\frac{20v}{27}+\frac{4(4\lambda-1-14k)}{27}\right\}.
(9.9)
Proof.
The cutoff defect (9.4) and
Lemma 9.2 give the early bound and both barriers,
with v in place of \beta. The third entry of a(v) implies
a(v)-k\geq\frac{4\lambda-1-14k+5v}{9}>0.
If a(v)\geq A^{\prime} there is nothing to prove, so
let a(v)<A^{\prime}. Competitors t\geq A^{\prime} and the terminal cap
are covered by the tails at A^{\prime}. For a(v)\leq t\leq A^{\prime}, the branch
1-t of p_{g}(t;1) gives f(t)+1-t\geq 1+t-v\geq\lambda, since
t\geq\lambda-1+v. For the other branch, 1-t\in[A^{\prime},4k], because
A^{\prime}<\frac{1}{2} and a(v)\geq k>1-4k.
So the two barriers for g
and the early bound for f give
f(t)+\frac{g(1-t)+t}{2}\geq\min\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}+2t+\frac{k}{2}-\frac{5v}{4},\frac{1}{4}+\frac{9t}{4}+\frac{5k}{4}-\frac{5v}{4}\Bigr\}\geq\lambda,
the last inequality being t\geq a(v) for the second and third
entries of a(v). Thus H_{f,g}(a(v),1)\geq\lambda, and
H_{g,f}(a(v),1)\geq\lambda by symmetry.
The formula for the new defect follows by subtracting k from
each entry and multiplying by \frac{4}{3}.
∎
10 Reciprocal estimates at a general transfer level
We now pass the estimates from one endpoint to the other and back
again. The level-one conclusion is the scalar bound \frac{4}{5}.
Keeping the transfer level as a parameter will also account for the
source dimension. The essential point is to retain the separate
excesses at the early cutoff until they return to the same contact
equation at the end of the proof.
In this and the following scalar arguments, a scalar system means
four profiles satisfying (2.3), the prefix bounds
(4.2), and the closed and full partition transfers
of Lemmas 4.1 and 7.2 in both
orientations. In particular, the endpoint profiles are nondecreasing
and 2-Lipschitz; the distance profiles are nondecreasing and
1-Lipschitz; all four vanish at zero. The notation
d_{x}(u)\geq\frac{u}{2}+\frac{f(u)}{4},\qquad d_{x}(b)-d_{x}(a)\geq[\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,b)-f(a)]_{+}
(10.1)
will refer to these inequalities together with their reverse orientation.
We write \mathcal{L}_{v}(g;[a,b])=L_{v}(g-u;[a,b]) for the
partition loss of Section 7 and abbreviate
\mathcal{L}_{v}(g;R)=\mathcal{L}_{v}(g;[0,R]).
The full transfer implies the closed transfer. Lemma 7.1
allows strict caps to pass to uniformly converging systems; no
continuity of the partition infimum is needed.
Restriction to [0,r] followed by division of both axes by r
preserves this system. At level one its certificate is automatic.
At the first hit f(a)=\frac{1}{2} we have a\leq\frac{1}{2}.
For competitors below \frac{1}{2}, use f\geq\frac{1}{2} and
p_{g}\geq\frac{1}{2}; for later competitors, use
f(u)+1-u\geq 1. Thus H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq 1, and likewise
with the endpoints exchanged.
Proposition 10.1 (Reciprocal estimate at level \lambda).
Assume the scalar system (10.1) in both orientations. Let \lambda=1+\Delta with
0\leq\Delta<\frac{1}{8}. Suppose there are cutoffs a_{f},a_{g}\leq\frac{1}{2} such that
\displaystyle f(a_{f})
\displaystyle=\lambda-\frac{1}{2},
\displaystyle H_{f,g}(a_{f},1)
\displaystyle\geq\lambda,
\displaystyle g(a_{g})
\displaystyle=\lambda-\frac{1}{2},
\displaystyle H_{g,f}(a_{g},1)
\displaystyle\geq\lambda.
Then
\max\{d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1)\}\geq\frac{4}{5}+\frac{3}{5}\Delta.
(10.2)
Proof.
Suppose the conclusion fails. Choose
\max\left\{d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1),\frac{11+10\Delta}{14}\right\}<Q<\frac{4}{5}+\frac{3}{5}\Delta.
Such a choice is possible because \Delta<\frac{1}{8}. Put k=\lambda-Q.
Then
\frac{1+2\Delta}{5}<k<\frac{3+4\Delta}{14}<\frac{1}{4}.
(10.3)
We say that a cutoff is common if both closed tails have height
at least \lambda there.
We begin by locating the least common cutoff.
The prefixes and transfer at a_{f},a_{g} show that both cutoffs are
at most
A_{\rm init}=2Q-\frac{\lambda}{2}-\frac{3}{4}=\frac{3}{4}+\frac{3\Delta}{2}-2k.
The initial defect is v_{0}=1+2\Delta-4k. Lemma 9.3 has defect maps
\frac{4}{3}v-\frac{4}{3}(k-\Delta),\quad\frac{5}{6}v+\frac{1+2\Delta-5k}{3},\quad\frac{20}{27}v+\frac{4(3+4\Delta-14k)}{27}.
Their fixed points are 4(k-\Delta), 2+4\Delta-10k<0, and
\beta_{\max}=\frac{12+16\Delta}{7}-8k.
The range (10.3) ensures
0<\beta_{\max}<v_{0}<k and v_{0}<4(k-\Delta).
Iteration therefore yields a common cutoff
k+\frac{3\beta_{\max}}{4}.
The common cutoffs form a nonempty closed interval. Its least element has the form
A_{*}=k+\frac{3\beta}{4},\qquad 0\leq\beta\leq\beta_{\max}.
(10.4)
(9.4) and Lemma 9.2 give,
with \ell=\min(f,g), x_{0}=k+\frac{\beta}{2}, and c=k-\frac{\beta}{2},
\displaystyle\ell(u)
\displaystyle\geq 2u-\beta
\displaystyle(0\leq u\leq A_{*}),
(10.5)
\displaystyle\ell(u)
\displaystyle\geq u+c
\displaystyle(x_{0}\leq u\leq 3k),
(10.6)
\displaystyle\ell(u)
\displaystyle\geq\frac{u}{2}+\frac{5k}{2}-\frac{\beta}{2}
\displaystyle(3k\leq u\leq 4k).
(10.7)
The orderings implied by (10.3) are
k\leq x_{0}\leq A_{*}<2k<T:=1-2k<3k<1-A_{*}\leq b_{*}:=1-k<4k<1.
In addition \beta<k and k-\beta>\Delta.
We next improve the bounds on [T,b_{*}].
For b\geq A_{*}, the oriented form of Lemma 9.1
and the transfer from b to 1 imply
f(b)\geq k+\frac{a}{2}+\inf_{a\leq u\leq b}\{\phi_{a}(f(u))+p_{g}(u;b)\}.
(10.8)
Only k=\lambda-Q enters this estimate. We use it to prove the
following bounds without replacing the two endpoint excesses by their minimum.
Put
P=f(x_{0})-2k,\qquad p=g(x_{0})-2k.
Then 0\leq P,p\leq\beta, and the 2-Lipschitz bound implies
f(t)\geq 2t-\beta+P\quad\text{for }t\leq x_{0},\qquad g(t)\geq 2t-\beta+p\quad\text{for }t\leq x_{0}.
(10.9)
Set F=f(T) and G=g(T), and define
\begin{split}C_{f}&=\min\{c,a_{0}+\frac{P}{4}+\frac{G}{2}\},\\
C_{g}&=\min\{c,a_{0}+\frac{p}{4}+\frac{F}{2}\},\qquad a_{0}=\frac{7k}{2}-1-\frac{\beta}{4}.\end{split}
(10.10)
f(u)\geq u+C_{f},\quad g(u)\geq u+C_{g}\quad(T\leq u\leq b_{*}).
(10.11)
We prove the first assertion. Monotonicity and Lipschitz continuity
give g(u)\geq G-2[T-u]_{+}. Let d_{f}=a_{0}+\frac{P}{4}+\frac{G}{2}, and put
M_{f}=b_{*}+d_{f}.
For T\leq b\leq b_{*} and k\leq t\leq T we claim
k+\frac{k}{2}+\phi_{k}(f(t))+p_{g}(t;b)\geq b+C_{f}.
(10.12)
On [k,x_{0}] use (10.9) and split at
y_{0}=k+\frac{\beta-P}{2}. The term before the projection gain is bounded below by
\frac{3k}{2}+\frac{t}{2}-\frac{\beta-P}{4} for t\leq y_{0}, and by
2t-\beta+P for t\geq y_{0}.
The branch b-t contributes at least b+c.
For the other branch, the cone for g yields at least
\min\{M_{f},b+d_{f}\} on the first interval. On the second interval
it yields at least
\min\{M_{f}+\frac{\beta-P}{2},b+d_{f}\}.
Since b+d_{f}\leq M_{f}, both are at least b+d_{f}.
For x_{0}\leq t\leq T we have
f(t)\geq\max\{t+c,2k+P\}. The first gain branch again contributes
b+c. The other gain branch uses b-t\leq T and is at least
b+\frac{G}{2}-T+f(t)-\frac{t}{2}\geq b+\frac{G}{2}-T+\frac{3k}{2}-\frac{\beta}{4}+\frac{P}{2}\geq b+d_{f}.
This proves (10.12).
Replace k in the cutoff by a=k-\epsilon>0. The cost on
[a,T] is at least b+C_{f}-3\epsilon. On [k,T], changing a
loses at most \frac{\epsilon}{2}, since \phi_{a} increases as a
decreases. On [a,k], compare with t=k. The 2-Lipschitz source
profile loses at most 2\epsilon inside the 1-Lipschitz function
\phi_{k}, the additive cutoff loses \frac{\epsilon}{2}, and monotonicity
of g implies p_{g}(t;b)\geq p_{g}(k;b)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}.
Let B_{f}(u)=\max\{F,u+C_{f}\} on [T,b_{*}]. It is nondecreasing
and 1-Lipschitz. The middle barrier (10.6) implies
F\geq T+c>2k, and C_{f}\leq c, so B_{f}(T)=F.
If D_{0}=\max[B_{f}-f]_{+}>0, choose a maximizer b and
0<\epsilon\leq\min\{\frac{D_{0}}{4},\frac{k}{2}\}. At that maximizer
B_{f}(b)=b+C_{f}. Early competitors have cost at least
B_{f}(b)-3\epsilon>f(b).
For T\leq t\leq b, we have t\geq\frac{b}{2}, so p_{g}(t;b)=b-t.
Also f(t)\geq F>2k>2a. Their cost in (10.8) is
f(t)+\epsilon+b-t\geq B_{f}(b)-D_{0}+\epsilon>f(b).
This contradicts (10.8). Hence f\geq B_{f}, proving
the assertion. Exchange the orientations for g.
The full partition estimate in the reverse orientation improves
the cone bounds. Each of the two possible losses requires a
correction by \Delta.
The endpoint values satisfy
\begin{split}G\geq\min\{&2C_{g}+3k+\frac{\beta}{2}-p+\frac{P}{2}-2\Delta,\\
&C_{g}+1-k-\frac{\beta}{2}-p+\frac{P}{2}-\Delta\},\end{split}
(10.13)
and the symmetric inequality for F.
Let a<k tend to k, put \epsilon=k-a, and
e_{t}=2t-f(t). Prefix and full transfer give a competitor
s\in[a,A_{*}), with R=1-s, for which
L_{s}(h_{g};[0,R])\geq h_{g}(R)+s+(k-a)-e_{s}+\frac{3}{4}e_{a}-\Delta-o(1).
(10.14)
Indeed their threshold is at most
Q+a-\frac{3e_{a}}{4}<\lambda.
Competitors at or above A_{*} and the terminal
cap have cost at least \lambda. The full partition gain is
g(R)-L_{s}(h_{g};[0,R]), which proves (10.14).
On [0,x_{0}], (8.4) bounds the loss by h_{g}(x_{0}), whose
positive part vanishes because s>\beta for a close to k.
On [x_{0},T], subtract the floor c from (10.6) and
use (8.4). Adding results in
L_{s}(h_{g};[0,x_{0}])+L_{s}(h_{g};[x_{0},T])\leq\max\left\{G-T+p,\frac{G-s-\beta+p}{2}\right\}.
(10.15)
On [T,R] use h_{g}\geq C_{g} from (10.11).
If R>b_{*}, monotonicity extends this to
h_{g}\geq C_{g}-\epsilon throughout [T,R].
After subtracting that floor, the positive-part argument in
(8.4) is at most 2\epsilon, because
R-T-s=2k-2s\leq 2\epsilon. Consequently
L_{s}(h_{g};[T,R])\leq h_{g}(R)-C_{g}+2\epsilon.
(10.16)
Thus passing slightly beyond b_{*} produces a vanishing error only.
Write \mathfrak{v}=s-a. The source excess sharpens both elementary
optimizations needed to combine the losses. We claim
3\mathfrak{v}-2e_{s}+\frac{3}{2}e_{a}\geq-\frac{\beta-P}{2},\qquad\mathfrak{v}-e_{s}+\frac{3}{4}e_{a}\geq-\frac{\beta-P}{2}.
(10.17)
For s\leq x_{0}, put B=\beta-P. Then
0\leq e_{a}\leq B and e_{s}\leq\min\{B,e_{a}+2\mathfrak{v}\}.
Each expression is minimized at the intersection
\mathfrak{v}=\frac{B-e_{a}}{2}. The resulting values are
-\frac{B}{2} and -\frac{B}{2}+\frac{e_{a}}{4}, respectively.
For s\geq x_{0}, use
e_{s}\leq\min\{\beta,2\mathfrak{v}-2\epsilon-P\} and e_{a}\geq 0.
The minimum over \mathfrak{v}\geq 0 is therefore bounded below by
-\frac{\beta}{2}+3\epsilon+\frac{3P}{2} and
-\frac{\beta}{2}+\epsilon+\frac{P}{2}.
These are at least -\frac{\beta-P}{2}, proving both bounds.
If the second entry of (10.15) is used, combining
(10.14)–(10.16) yields
a+2k+3\mathfrak{v}-2e_{s}+\frac{3}{2}e_{a}-2\Delta\leq G-\beta+p-2C_{g}+o(1).
The first bound in (10.17) and a\nearrow k
give the first entry of (10.13).
If the first entry of (10.15) is used, the second bound
in (10.17) proves the second entry of
(10.13). One case occurs along a sequence
a\nearrow k. This proves the feedback estimate.
Put \xi=P-p, A=5k-1-\Delta, and
B=3k-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{\Delta}{2}. The cone formulas and
(10.13) imply
C_{f}\geq\min\{c,C_{g}+A+\frac{\xi}{2},\frac{C_{g}}{2}+B+\frac{\xi}{2}\},
and its symmetric version with \xi replaced by -\xi.
To eliminate C_{g}, define the nondecreasing function
\Phi_{\xi}(u)=\min\left\{c,u+A+\frac{\xi}{2},\frac{u}{2}+B+\frac{\xi}{2}\right\}.
The two inequalities are C_{f}\geq\Phi_{\xi}(C_{g}) and
C_{g}\geq\Phi_{-\xi}(C_{f}). Monotonicity implies
C_{f}\geq\Phi_{\xi}(\Phi_{-\xi}(C_{f})). Expanding this composition results in
\displaystyle\Phi_{\xi}(\Phi_{-\xi}(u))=\min\Bigl\{
\displaystyle c,c+A+\frac{\xi}{2},\frac{c}{2}+B+\frac{\xi}{2},u+2A,
\displaystyle\frac{u}{2}+A+B,\frac{u}{2}+\frac{A}{2}+B+\frac{\xi}{4},\frac{u}{4}+\frac{3B}{2}+\frac{\xi}{4}\Bigr\}.
Since A>0, the entry u+2A is strictly larger than u.
The remaining three nonconstant entries are strictly larger than
u precisely when, respectively,
u<2(A+B),\qquad u<2B+A+\frac{\xi}{2},\qquad u<2B+\frac{\xi}{3}.
Thus, if C_{f} were below the three constant entries and these
three thresholds, every entry in the composition would exceed
C_{f}, contradicting C_{f}\geq\Phi_{\xi}(\Phi_{-\xi}(C_{f})). We obtain
\begin{split}C_{f}\geq\min\Bigl\{&c,c+A+\frac{\xi}{2},\frac{c}{2}+B+\frac{\xi}{2},\\
&2(A+B),2B+A+\frac{\xi}{2},2B+\frac{\xi}{3}\Bigr\}.\end{split}
(10.18)
Exchanging f and g replaces \xi by -\xi and proves the six
bounds for C_{g}. No sign assumption on \xi is used in this elimination.
We now return to the contact at the least cutoff.
Minimality of A_{*} forces a contact in at least one orientation;
label it so that
f(A_{*})+p_{g}(A_{*};1)=1+\Delta.
Set K=1+2\Delta-4k and J=K-\beta. The cone for g at
1-A_{*} implies
C_{g}\leq\min\{J,K-2P\}.
(10.19)
Indeed, p_{g}(A_{*};1)\geq\frac{1+C_{g}}{2} and
f(A_{*})\geq\max\{2k+\frac{\beta}{2},2k+P\}.
Let
a_{1}=5k-1-2\Delta>0,
so A=a_{1}+\Delta. The six entries in (10.18), minus J,
are
\begin{gathered}a_{1}+\frac{\beta}{2},\qquad 2a_{1}+\Delta+\frac{\beta+\xi}{2},\qquad\frac{3a_{1}}{2}+\frac{\Delta}{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}+\frac{\xi}{2},\\
4a_{1}+3\Delta,\qquad 3a_{1}+2\Delta+\frac{\xi}{2},\qquad 2a_{1}+\Delta+\frac{\xi}{3}.\end{gathered}
(10.20)
If \xi\leq 0, the six differences for C_{g} are all positive,
contrary to (10.19). Thus 0<\xi\leq\beta.
For the weaker floor C_{g}, its first, second, and fourth entries
are positive. At least one of the other three must be nonpositive.
The third requires \xi\geq 3a_{1}+\Delta+\frac{\beta}{2} and hence
\xi\geq 6a_{1}+2\Delta. The fifth and sixth give still stronger
bounds. Therefore
\beta\geq\xi\geq 6a_{1}+2\Delta.
Substitute this into (10.20). Every entry is at
least 4a_{1}+\Delta, so
C_{f}\geq J+4a_{1}+\Delta.
(10.21)
The branch 1-A_{*} cannot attain the contact, because
f(A_{*})+1-A_{*}\geq 1+k-\frac{\beta}{4}>1+\Delta.
Consequently
g(1-A_{*})=2+2\Delta-2f(A_{*})-A_{*}.
