Monotonicity and the two lower bounds for f(A_{*}) yield
G\leq\min\{2+2\Delta-5k-\frac{7\beta}{4},2+2\Delta-5k-\frac{3\beta}{4}-2P\}.
In the formula for C_{f}, these give
\displaystyle C_{f}
\displaystyle\leq\min\{k+\Delta-\frac{9\beta}{8}+\frac{P}{4},k+\Delta-\frac{5\beta}{8}-\frac{3P}{4}\}
\displaystyle\leq k+\Delta-\beta.
(10.22)
Use the first entry when P\leq\frac{\beta}{2} and the second when
P\geq\frac{\beta}{2}. Finally,
(J+4a_{1}+\Delta)-(k+\Delta-\beta)=3a_{1}>0.
This contradicts (10.21)–(10.22)
and proves the proposition.
∎
Corollary 10.2 (The level-one scalar bound).
Every scalar system satisfies
\max\{d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1)\}\geq\frac{4}{5}.
Proof.
The level-one certificates were verified before
Proposition 10.1. Apply that proposition with \Delta=0.
∎
11 The rational Hausdorff bound
The source dimension raises the certificate above level one. The
reciprocal estimate converts this higher certificate into an explicit
distance bound by one linear inequality.
The bound Y in Theorem 1.2.
Fix 0<\kappa<S_{0}-1. Consider the information profiles of a pair satisfying (H1)–(H4), with both effective dimensions greater than 1+\kappa. Corollary 6.2, in its symmetric case, has the coefficient
b=\frac{1-3\kappa+\kappa^{2}}{(1-\kappa)(4-\kappa)},\qquad d_{x}(u)\geq(1-2b)u+bf(u),
and the analogous bound for d_{y}. Lemma 8.8 raises the tail level to
\lambda=1+\Delta,\qquad\Delta=D(1-Q),\qquad D=\frac{\kappa(4-\kappa)}{1-3\kappa+\kappa^{2}},
when both terminal distance profiles are at most Q in the
permitted prefix range. Proposition 10.1 then forces
Q\geq\frac{4}{5}+\frac{3}{5}D(1-Q),\qquad Q\geq 1-\frac{1}{5+3D}=\frac{4+\kappa^{2}}{5-3\kappa+2\kappa^{2}}=Y(1+\kappa).
Here is the parameter check needed for this argument by
contradiction. If the proposed scalar bound fails, choose Q just
below Y(1+\kappa) and above both terminal values. The lower end of
the bootstrap range is
R(1+\kappa)=1-b(1-\kappa)<Y(1+\kappa), as follows from
the positive difference computed below. The upper end
1-\frac{b(1-\kappa)}{2} is at least \frac{7}{8}, while
Y(1+\kappa)<\frac{7}{8}. At the limiting value Q=Y(1+\kappa),
\Delta=\frac{\kappa(4-\kappa)}{5-3\kappa+2\kappa^{2}}<\frac{1}{8}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\kappa<\frac{7-\sqrt{41}}{4}.
The inequalities remain valid when Q is sufficiently close from
below. Thus the certificate and Proposition 10.1 apply.
This proves the scalar improvement. Apply Proposition 5.1 and the point-to-set argument of Section 5, using an auxiliary Frostman exponent strictly below t=\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E. Let 1+\kappa increase to \min\{t,S_{0}\}. Continuity proves the endpoint and the constant continuation; the independent bound R remains available.
For the comparison with the bootstrap range, write t=1+\kappa.
Direct subtraction yields
Y(t)-R(t)=\frac{1-5\kappa+9\kappa^{2}-8\kappa^{3}+2\kappa^{4}}{(5-3\kappa+2\kappa^{2})(4-\kappa)}>0.
Indeed 0\leq\kappa\leq S_{0}-1<\frac{3}{20}, and the numerator
is at least 1-5\kappa-8\kappa^{3}>1-\frac{3}{4}-\frac{27}{1000}>0.
∎
12 Matching profiles and the minimum-ratio argument
When the endpoint profiles agree, a single conditioned distance
profile satisfies the scalar inequalities in both orientations.
We can then choose a scale at which its information per unit
precision is nearly smallest. At that scale, every earlier
distance prefix has a useful linear lower bound. This additional
bound lets the tail iteration reach the full source floor.
Proposition 12.1 (A stronger bound for a matching pair).
Suppose the scalar system has f=g and
f(u)\geq(1+\kappa)u, with 0<\kappa<\frac{1}{8}. Then, separately for
each orientation,
d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1)\geq\frac{4}{5}+\frac{3}{5}\kappa.
(12.1)
Proof.
Use one of the two distance profiles and call it d. Because
f=g, the quadruple (f,f,d,d) satisfies every scalar inequality.
The system is invariant under restriction to [0,r] and the
rescaling F(u)=\frac{f(ru)}{r}, D(u)=\frac{d(ru)}{r}.
Let q=\inf_{0<r\leq 1}\frac{d(r)}{r}. The level-one reciprocal bound,
applied after each rescaling, implies q\geq\frac{4}{5}. If d(1) were
below the claimed bound, so would q. Choose r_{j} with
\frac{d(r_{j})}{r_{j}}\to q and extract a uniformly convergent subsequence
of the rescaled profiles. The limiting scalar system still
satisfies all the transfers. For the full transfer, use Lemma 7.1 to pass every strict cap to the approximating
profiles. Rename the limits f,d. They satisfy
d(1)=q,\qquad d(u)\geq qu,\qquad f(u)\geq(1+\kappa)u.
(12.2)
We now raise the tail height to 1+\kappa.
Initially take \lambda_{0}=1+\frac{\kappa}{2} and let a_{0} be the first
point with f(a_{0})=\lambda_{0}-\frac{1}{2}. The floor implies a_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}.
For a_{0}\leq r\leq\frac{1}{2}, use p_{f}(r;1)\geq\frac{1}{2}; for r\geq\frac{1}{2},
use f(r)+1-r\geq 1+\kappa r. Thus
H_{f,f}(a,1)\geq\lambda_{0} for a\geq a_{0}.
Suppose the tail has height \lambda_{j} from its first hitting
point a_{j}\leq\frac{1}{2}, where
\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda_{j}\leq 1+\kappa. The transfer and
(12.2) imply
f(r)\geq\lambda_{j}-q+qr\qquad(r\geq a_{j}).
For \frac{1}{2}\leq r\leq 1 it follows that
\displaystyle f(r)+1-r
\displaystyle\geq 1+\max\{\kappa r,\lambda_{j}-q-(1-q)r\}
\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{j+1}:=1+\frac{\kappa}{\kappa+1-q}(\lambda_{j}-q).
The two affine functions intersect inside [\frac{1}{2},1] because
\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda_{j}\leq 1+\kappa and q<1.
At r=\frac{1}{2}, the inequality puts the next first hitting point at
or before \frac{1}{2}. Earlier competitors are handled by
p_{f}(r;1)\geq\frac{1}{2}, and the terminal cap by f(1)\geq 1+\kappa.
Hence the induction continues.
The affine update has slope less than one and fixed point
1+\kappa. Therefore \lambda_{j}\uparrow 1+\kappa. The first
hitting points converge, giving the certificate in
Proposition 10.1 at \Delta=\kappa for (f,f,d,d).
That proposition forces q\geq\frac{4}{5}+\frac{3\kappa}{5}, the contradiction.
∎
The linear lower bound in (12.2) is not an additional
assumption on the original distance profile. It is obtained by
choosing a scale where the normalized distance complexity is
nearly minimal and taking a limit. That choice of scale is the reason the tail height reaches the full source floor.
13 Rigidity at the reciprocal endpoint
The scalar inequalities alone admit endpoint models at four fifths
(Proposition 31.1). What matters for the
geometric argument is that every such model has a long interval
of excess information. We prove this for two possibly different
endpoint profiles. Figure 3 shows the forced lower envelope. In this section put
k=\frac{1}{5},\qquad T=\frac{3}{5},\qquad b_{*}=\frac{4}{5}.
Proposition 13.1 (Two-profile endpoint rigidity).
Every system (10.1) with d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1)\leq\frac{4}{5} satisfies
\displaystyle f(u)=g(u)
\displaystyle=2u
\displaystyle(0\leq u\leq\frac{1}{5}),
(13.1)
\displaystyle f(u),g(u)
\displaystyle\geq u+\frac{1}{5}
\displaystyle(\frac{1}{5}\leq u\leq\frac{3}{5}).
(13.2)
Consequently
H_{f,g}(\frac{3}{10},1),\ H_{g,f}(\frac{3}{10},1)\geq 1.
(13.3)
uprofile\frac{1}{5}\frac{3}{5}\frac{4}{5}1f,g\geq u+\frac{1}{5}f,g\geq u
Figure 3: A common lower envelope forced at the endpoint. The shaded
strip has height \frac{1}{5}. This envelope is a lower bound, not an assertion
that the profiles coincide or that the displayed function is an extremizer.
13.1 Endpoint cutoff data and the exact skew identity
We first verify the cutoff, cone and feedback estimates at k=\frac{1}{5}.
The strict reciprocal proof must be adjusted at this endpoint.
The least common closed-tail cutoff is
A=k+\frac{3\beta}{4},\qquad 0\leq\beta\leq\frac{4}{35}.
(13.4)
Set
\displaystyle x_{0}=k+\frac{\beta}{2},\quad c=k-\frac{\beta}{2},\quad J=k-\beta,
\displaystyle P=f(x_{0})-2k,\quad p=g(x_{0})-2k,\quad F=f(T),\quad G=g(T),\quad\xi=P-p,
\displaystyle C_{f}=\min\{c,-\frac{3}{10}-\frac{\beta}{4}+\frac{P}{4}+\frac{G}{2}\},\quad C_{g}=\min\{c,-\frac{3}{10}-\frac{\beta}{4}+\frac{p}{4}+\frac{F}{2}\}.
Here 0\leq P,p\leq\beta. The barriers and cones are
\displaystyle f(u),g(u)
\displaystyle\geq 2u-\beta
\displaystyle(0\leq u\leq A),
(13.5)
\displaystyle f(u),g(u)
\displaystyle\geq u+c
\displaystyle(x_{0}\leq u\leq T),
(13.6)
\displaystyle f(u)
\displaystyle\geq u+C_{f},\quad g(u)\geq u+C_{g}
\displaystyle(T\leq u\leq b_{*}).
(13.7)
The reciprocal feedback reads
\displaystyle C_{f}
\displaystyle\geq\min\{c,C_{g}+\frac{\xi}{2},\frac{C_{g}+J}{2}+\frac{\xi}{2}\},
(13.8)
\displaystyle C_{g}
\displaystyle\geq\min\{c,C_{f}-\frac{\xi}{2},\frac{C_{f}+J}{2}-\frac{\xi}{2}\}.
To justify (13.4) at the endpoint, the first-hit argument
starts at A_{0}=\frac{7}{20} with defect \beta_{0}=k. The first barrier’s
proof still works at that defect. Its requirements are
\beta_{0}\leq 2(k-\mathfrak{d}) and b<3(k-\mathfrak{d}) for
sufficiently small positive maximum defect \mathfrak{d}.
It yields \min(f(T),g(T))\geq\frac{7}{10}. For
\frac{17}{50}\leq u\leq\frac{7}{20}, the early bound and this last estimate give
f(u)+\frac{g(1-u)+u}{2}\geq\frac{5}{2}u+\frac{3}{20}\geq 1,\qquad f(u)+1-u\geq 1+u-k\geq 1,
and similarly in the opposite orientation. Thus \frac{17}{50} is a
common cutoff with defect \frac{14}{75}<k. The usual update now applies;
its defect maps at k=\frac{1}{5} are
v\mapsto\frac{4v}{3}-\frac{4}{15},\qquad v\mapsto\frac{5v}{6},\qquad v\mapsto\frac{20v}{27}+\frac{4}{135}.
Their maximum decreases to \frac{4}{35}. In the proof of the update,
the strict inequality formerly written k>1-4k is replaced by
a(v)>k=1-4k, which follows from its third entry. The other
endpoint equalities, T=3k and b_{*}=4k, cause no change to the
barrier, cone, or feedback proofs.
Minimality of A forces a contact in at least one orientation;
label it so that f(A)+p_{g}(A;1)=1. The length branch cannot
attain the contact, because f(A)-A\geq k-\frac{\beta}{4}>0. Hence
g(1-A)=2-2f(A)-A.
The cone for g at 1-A and
f(A)\geq\max\{2k+\frac{\beta}{2},2k+P\} give
\displaystyle C_{g}
\displaystyle\leq\min\{J,k-2P\},
(13.9)
\displaystyle G
\displaystyle\leq\min\{1-\frac{7\beta}{4},1-\frac{3\beta}{4}-2P\}.
Substituting the latter two bounds in the formula for C_{f} gives
C_{f}\leq\min\{k-\frac{9\beta}{8}+\frac{P}{4},k-\frac{5\beta}{8}-\frac{3P}{4}\}\leq J.
(13.10)
For the last inequality use the first entry if P\leq\frac{\beta}{2} and
the second otherwise.
If \beta=0, (13.5) and (13.6) already prove
the proposition. Suppose henceforth \beta>0. Since
C_{f},C_{g}\leq J<c, the half-slope branch in the first inequality
of (13.8) is no smaller than C_{g}+\frac{\xi}{2}.
The ceiling c exceeds C_{f}. Thus C_{f}\geq C_{g}+\frac{\xi}{2}.
The second inequality implies the reverse bound. We obtain
the identity
C_{f}-C_{g}=\frac{P-p}{2}.
(13.11)
Both cone minima are on their second branches, so also
G=2C_{f}+3k+\frac{\beta-P}{2},\qquad F=2C_{g}+3k+\frac{\beta-p}{2}.
(13.12)
We have not substituted k=\frac{1}{5} in the
six-branch lower bound from the strict reciprocal argument. Its derivation discards a term
u+2(5k-1) using strict positivity, which fails at this endpoint.
The direct identity (13.11) is what is needed instead.
13.2 A cutoff inequality that can be used twice
Write e_{u}=2u-f(u). This information deficit is nonnegative and
nondecreasing. Euler’s number is written \mathrm{e}, and
\mathfrak{e} denotes the plateau parameter of
Proposition 8.5.
The same arguments will be used for g.
Lemma 13.2 (Cutoff inequality).
Let k<a<A and \sigma\in\{\frac{3}{4},1\}. If
d_{x}(a)\geq a-(1-\sigma)e_{a},
then a-k\geq\sigma e_{a}.
Proof.
Suppose instead that \theta=a-k<\sigma e_{a}. The transfer and
the upper bound on d_{x}(1) give
\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,1)\leq\frac{4}{5}+a-\sigma e_{a}<1.
Choose competitors v\in[a,A) with full cost below this upper
bound plus an arbitrarily small error. Competitors at or beyond
A and the terminal cap have cost at least one. Put R=1-v,
w=v-a, and B=\beta-P. The partition loss must satisfy
\mathcal{L}_{v}(g;R)\geq h_{g}(R)+v+k-a-e_{v}+\sigma e_{a}-o(1).
(13.13)
The endpoint partition bound, using (13.6)–(13.7),
gives
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{v}(g;[0,x_{0}])+\mathcal{L}_{v}(g;[x_{0},T])
\displaystyle\leq\max\{G-T+p,\frac{G-v-\beta+p}{2}\},
(13.14)
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{v}(g;[T,R])
\displaystyle\leq h_{g}(R)-C_{g}.
Here v>k>\beta, T<R<b_{*}, and R-T-v=2k-2v<0.
Concatenating these finite partitions bounds the full loss.
Consequently at least one of the inequalities
\displaystyle G
\displaystyle\geq C_{g}+1-k+U_{1}-p-o(1),
(13.15)
\displaystyle G
\displaystyle\geq 2C_{g}+a+2k+U_{2}+\beta-p-o(1),
holds, where U_{1}=w-e_{v}+\sigma e_{a} and
U_{2}=3w-2e_{v}+2\sigma e_{a}.
For v\leq x_{0} use e_{v}\leq\min\{B,e_{a}+2w\}, which gives
U_{1}\geq-\frac{B}{2}+(\sigma-\frac{1}{2})e_{a},\qquad U_{2}\geq-\frac{B}{2}+(2\sigma-\frac{3}{2})e_{a}.
For v\geq x_{0} use e_{v}\leq\min\{\beta,2w+2\theta-P\},
which gives
U_{1}\geq-\frac{\beta}{2}+\frac{P}{2}-\theta+\sigma e_{a},\quad U_{2}\geq-\frac{\beta}{2}+\frac{3P}{2}-3\theta+2\sigma e_{a}.
These bounds are obtained at the meeting points of the two
affine bounds for e_{v}; allowing all w\geq 0 only weakens them.
Subtract G from the two right sides of (13.15), and
use (13.11)–(13.12). The lower bounds are
\begin{array}[]{c|cc}&\text{first branch}&\text{second branch}\\
\hline\cr v\leq x_{0}&J-C_{g}+(\sigma-\frac{1}{2})e_{a}&\theta+(2\sigma-\frac{3}{2})e_{a}\\
v\geq x_{0}&J-C_{g}-\theta+\sigma e_{a}&P-2\theta+2\sigma e_{a}.\end{array}
All four are strictly positive because C_{g}\leq J, P\geq 0,
\theta>0, and \sigma e_{a}>\theta. The errors can be taken
smaller than their minimum, a contradiction. The proof applies
also when a\geq x_{0}, in which case the first row is unnecessary.
∎
13.3 Recovering the plateau
The ordinary prefix is d_{x}(a)\geq a-\frac{e_{a}}{4}. Apply
Lemma 13.2 with \sigma=\frac{3}{4} for every k<a<A:
e_{a}\leq\frac{4}{3}(a-k),\qquad h_{f}(a)\geq\frac{4k-a}{3}.
(13.16)
By continuity e_{k}=0, and monotonicity of e forces f(u)=2u
for 0\leq u\leq k. The same holds for g.
Let a_{0}=\frac{A}{2}<k. On [a_{0},k] one has h_{f}(u)=u\geq a_{0}.
On [k,A], (13.16) implies
h_{f}(u)\geq k-\frac{\beta}{4},\qquad k-\frac{\beta}{4}-a_{0}=\frac{k}{2}-\frac{5\beta}{8}\geq\frac{1}{35}>0.
For a_{0}\leq u\leq A, the projection gain is saturated because
p_{g}(u;A)=A-u, since u\geq\frac{A}{2} and g\geq u.
It follows that
H_{f,g}(a_{0},A)=A+a_{0}.
Indeed, the cost is A+h_{f}(u)\geq A+a_{0}, with equality at
u=a_{0}; this also includes the terminal cap. The prefix forces
d_{x}(a_{0})=a_{0}. The closed transfer then implies
d_{x}(A)\geq a_{0}+(A+a_{0})-f(a_{0})=A.
The 1-Lipschitz upper bound forces equality and then
d_{x}(u)=u\quad(0\leq u\leq A).
(13.17)
The same reasoning applies to d_{y}.
Now use Lemma 13.2 a second time, with \sigma=1 and
(13.17). We obtain e_{a}\leq a-k, hence
h_{f}(u),h_{g}(u)\geq k\quad(k\leq u\leq A).
(13.18)
The first application forced exact information growth before k.
That exact growth made the distance profile linear up to A, so the
second application can retain the entire source defect.
The plateau propagates to 3k as follows.
The oriented version of Lemma 9.1, combined with the transfer from b to 1, at b\geq A
and a=k-\varepsilon, reduces to
f(b)\geq\varepsilon+\inf_{a\leq u\leq b}\{f(u)+p_{g}(u;b)\},
(13.19)
because f(a)=2a and monotonicity makes the linear branch of
\phi_{a}(f(u)) active. If the barrier f(u)\geq u+k fails on
[k,2k], let D>0 be its maximum defect, attained at b>A.
For u\geq k\geq\frac{b}{2}, the gain is b-u and the cost is at least
b+k-D=f(b). For a\leq u\leq k, comparison with u=k loses
at most 3\varepsilon and leaves a cost of at least b+k-3\varepsilon.
Choose 0<2\varepsilon<D in (13.19) to get a contradiction.
Exchange the two profiles to establish both barriers on [k,2k].
Next suppose there is a positive maximum defect on [2k,3k].
Late competitors u\geq\frac{b}{2} are handled in the same way.
For k\leq u\leq\frac{b}{2}, both u and b-u belong to [k,2k],
where both barriers now hold. The two gain branches give costs
at least b+k and \frac{b}{2}+\frac{5k}{2}\geq b+k, respectively.
The interval [a,k] again loses at most 3\varepsilon.
This proves (13.2) for both profiles.
Finally, if \frac{3}{10}\leq u\leq\frac{1}{2}, then f(u)\geq\frac{1}{2} and
p_{g}(u;1)\geq\frac{1}{2}. If u\geq\frac{1}{2}, then p_{g}(u;1)=1-u
and f(u)\geq u. The terminal cap is at least one.
This proves (13.3) and Proposition 13.1.
Lemma 13.3 (Qualitative stability).
For every \varepsilon>0 there is \eta(\varepsilon)>0 such that
every scalar system (10.1) with
d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1)\leq\frac{4}{5}+\eta(\varepsilon) satisfies
\displaystyle f(u),g(u)
\displaystyle\geq 2u-\varepsilon
\displaystyle(0\leq u\leq\frac{1}{5}),
\displaystyle f(u),g(u)
\displaystyle\geq u+\frac{1}{5}-\varepsilon
\displaystyle(\frac{1}{5}\leq u\leq\frac{3}{5}),
and
H_{f,g}(\frac{3}{10},1),H_{g,f}(\frac{3}{10},1)\geq 1-\varepsilon.
The constant depends only on \varepsilon.
Proof.
If this failed, choose systems with terminal bounds decreasing
to \frac{4}{5} and violating one of the conclusions at the fixed
tolerance \varepsilon. The uniform Lipschitz bounds give a
jointly uniformly convergent subsequence. The prefix and closed
transfers pass to the limit directly, and
Lemma 7.1 passes the full transfer.
The limit is an endpoint system. Proposition 13.1
satisfies all the asserted bounds with zero error. Uniform convergence,
and continuity of the closed functional H, give a contradiction.
∎
14 The source parent and the annular cap
The finite geometry will require a bound on the source mass that
can lie both in a small parent square and near one circle. The
interval-transfer proof already contains the needed information.
Before its last chain rule, the coarse source location is still
available as conditioning data. We keep it.
A lower bound for this conditional complexity becomes an upper bound
for annular mass by measure coding. The cancellation of the parent
mass leaves a bound for every occupied
parent and radius bin with respect to the same original source
measure.
Lemma 14.1 (Conditional source-parent transfer).
Let (x,y) satisfy the pointwise hypotheses of the interval-transfer
lemma, relative to A, and put \varrho=|x-y|. Along any common limiting
profile sequence r_{j}, for fixed 0<a<b\leq 1, write
C_{j}=Q_{\lceil ar_{j}\rceil}(y). Then
\liminf_{j\to\infty}\frac{K_{\lfloor br_{j}\rfloor}^{A,x}(\varrho\mid C_{j})}{r_{j}}\geq[H_{f,g}(a,b)-f(a)]_{+}.
The notation on the left means ordinary finite-string conditioning on
the encoded dyadic square, while retaining the full oracle x.
Proof.
Put H=H_{f,g}(a,b). If H\leq f(a), the assertion follows
from nonnegativity. Otherwise
choose f(a)<c<H and put
n_{j}=\lfloor br_{j}\rfloor,\qquad\ell_{j}=\lceil ar_{j}\rceil,\qquad m_{j}=\lfloor cr_{j}\rfloor.
Use the source-lowering oracle G_{j}=D(n_{j},y,\frac{m_{j}}{n_{j}}).
For each sufficiently small fixed rational \varepsilon>0, the
competitor bounds in the proof of Lemma 4.1 verify
both hypotheses of [7, Lemma 15], uniformly for all large j.
The two finite-precision inequalities being combined are
\displaystyle m_{j}-K_{\ell_{j}}^{A}(y)-O(\log r_{j})
\displaystyle\leq K_{n_{j},\ell_{j}}^{A,G_{j},x}(y\mid y),
\displaystyle K_{n_{j},\ell_{j}}^{A,G_{j},x}(y\mid y)
\displaystyle\leq K_{n_{j},\ell_{j}}^{A,G_{j},x}(\varrho\mid y)+3\varepsilon n_{j}+O_{\varepsilon}(\log r_{j}).
The first follows from Lemma 14(iii) of [7], (H3), and the
chain rule. The second is Lemma 15, with the rational cap parameter
included in its description cost. Dropping G_{j} from the last
conditional complexity increases it up to an absolute simulation
constant. Hence
K_{n_{j},\ell_{j}}^{A,x}(\varrho\mid y)\geq m_{j}-K_{\ell_{j}}^{A}(y)-3\varepsilon n_{j}-O_{\varepsilon}(\log r_{j}).
Every allowed precision-\ell_{j} rational approximation
to y leaves only a bounded number of possibilities for C_{j}.
For each such approximation, specify the correct square by an
index of bounded length. The index may depend on the approximation.
Its length does not. A program supplied with that index and the
precision parameters can therefore simulate a program conditioned
on C_{j}. Consequently
K_{\lfloor br_{j}\rfloor,\lceil ar_{j}\rceil}^{A,x}(\varrho\mid y)\leq K_{\lfloor br_{j}\rfloor}^{A,x}(\varrho\mid C_{j})+O(\log r_{j}).
This is the required direction for the worst-case coarse conditioning.
No maximum is replaced by a minimum. Take the profile limit, then
\varepsilon\searrow 0 and c\nearrow H.
∎
Proposition 14.2 (An occupied-parent annular mass cap).
Let q>0, \lambda\geq 1, and 0<a<1.
Let \mu be a compactly supported s-Frostman probability measure,
s>1, whose dyadic masses are computable relative to A.
Suppose the source–pin pairs under consideration satisfy the
pointwise prefix and transfer hypotheses and the almost-sure source
coding identity
K_{k}^{A}(y)=-\log_{2}\mu(Q_{k}(y))+O_{y}(\log(k+2)),
and that every limiting profile of the pair along precisions n
with K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\leq qn satisfies H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq\lambda.
Then for each such fixed pair (x,y) and every \zeta>0, all
sufficiently large n with K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\leq qn satisfy
K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|\mid Q_{\lfloor an\rfloor}(y))\geq\lambda n-K_{\lfloor an\rfloor}^{A}(y)-\zeta n.
(14.1)
In particular, if C=Q_{\lfloor an\rfloor}(y) and J is the
half-open dyadic radius interval of length 2^{-n} containing |x-y|, then
\mu\{z\in C:|x-z|\in J\}\leq 2^{-(\lambda-\zeta)n}
(14.2)
for all sufficiently large such scales, after decreasing the error
tolerance in (14.1) if necessary.
If the original relation is Borel, then for any fixed pin
probability measure \nu, the thresholds can
be made uniform on a fixed Borel subrelation, that is, a Borel
subset of the original set of pairs, losing arbitrarily little
(\nu\times\mu)-measure. Therefore, on that subrelation,
(14.2) holds simultaneously for every
source parent and every terminal radius bin occupied by a retained
low-complexity pair. The mass on its left is the full source mass,
not the pruned mass.
Proof.
Suppose (14.1) fails along an infinite
sequence of low-distance scales. Extract uniformly convergent
endpoint and distance profiles on a subsequence. By hypothesis
H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq\lambda, so
Lemma 14.1 contradicts the assumed
failure after division by n. Fixed rounding changes do not affect
this argument.
For the mass conclusion, write
p_{C,J}=\mu\{z\in C:|x-z|\in J\}.
Since s>1, dyadic coordinate lines and circles have zero source
mass. For \mu(C)>0, its computability allows division by \mu(C)
uniformly from the label of C. The normalized restriction
\mu_{C}=\frac{\mu|_{C}}{\mu(C)} has a radial pushforward about
x whose dyadic interval masses are lower semicomputable relative
to (A,x,C): inner
approximations by dyadic squares converge from below because the
boundaries have zero mass, although no rate of convergence is
available. This is enough to construct the required code. For each
fixed input (C,n) and every level-n radius interval J, request
a codeword of length k+1 as soon as the enumeration proves
\frac{p_{C,J}}{\mu(C)}>2^{-k}. Make each request once. The total
requested weight satisfies
\sum_{J}\sum_{k:\,2^{-k}<\frac{p_{C,J}}{\mu(C)}}2^{-(k+1)}\leq\sum_{J}\frac{p_{C,J}}{\mu(C)}=1.
The Kraft–Chaitin theorem [18, Chapter 4] supplies a
prefix-free code by one algorithm taking (C,n) as input.
The shortest requested length for J is at most
-\log_{2}\frac{p_{C,J}}{\mu(C)}+2. Its decoder outputs the midpoint
of J, which is within 2^{-n} of |x-y|. Describing n costs
O(\log(n+2)) bits. Thus, when p_{C,J}>0,
K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|\mid C)\leq-\log_{2}\frac{p_{C,J}}{\mu(C)}+O(\log(n+2)).
The coding algorithm takes the square label C as conditional
input, so its program index and its additive constant are uniform
over all occupied parents.
There is no additional K^{A}(C) term. The zero-mass case is immediate.
Combining this with
(14.1) and
K_{\lfloor an\rfloor}^{A}(y)=-\log_{2}\mu(C)+O_{y}(\log n)
leaves the two inequalities
\lambda n+\log_{2}\mu(C)-\zeta_{0}n-O_{y}(\log n)\leq K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|\mid C)\leq-\log_{2}p_{C,J}+\log_{2}\mu(C)+O(\log n),
where \zeta_{0}>0 is the tolerance used in the conditional bound.
Subtracting \log_{2}\mu(C) from both sides proves
-\log_{2}p_{C,J}\geq\lambda n-\zeta_{0}n-O_{y}(\log n) along the
low-distance scales. Choose \zeta_{0}<\zeta and then increase the
threshold to absorb the logarithmic error.
This proves the stated error-tolerance formulation.
Finally, the conditions are Borel. For each fixed error tolerance,
the increasing union of the sets on which the required inequalities
hold for every n\geq N is the full admissible relation. Choose one
N retaining all but any prescribed amount of its product measure.
Every occupied parent and radial-bin then contains a pair to which the uniform
bound applies, proving the simultaneous assertion. This restriction
changes only the relation of pairs. The oracle A and the original
measures \mu,\nu remain fixed. The pin oracle is never truncated.
∎
The hypothesis on H_{f,g} is supplied by Section 8.
If the pair has the certificate (8.2) at level
\lambda and d_{x}(1)\leq q, then H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq\lambda for every
a\geq a_{*}, because H_{f,g}(a,1) is nondecreasing in a, and
Lemma 8.6 shows that a_{*}\leq a_{c}+\varpi\omega. For
\lambda=1 the certificate holds for every admissible pair, and
Lemma 8.1 implies a_{*}\leq\frac{1}{4}+2\omega.
So the
cap holds at every parent scale a>\frac{1}{4}+2\omega.
Corollary 14.3 (Keeping all parents).
Let \nu be the pin probability measure, let G be one of the
relations with uniform thresholds in
Proposition 14.2, and put
R_{n}=\{(x,y)\in G:K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\leq qn\},\qquad\mu_{x,n}=\mu|_{R_{n,x}},\qquad P_{n}=\int\mu_{x,n}(\mathbb{R}^{2})d\nu(x).
For all sufficiently large n, with \ell=\lfloor an\rfloor,
every level-\ell dyadic parent C and terminal
radius bin J satisfy
\mu_{x,n}\{z\in C:|x-z|\in J\}\leq 2^{-(\lambda-\zeta)n}.
This uses the original common source measure in all parents
simultaneously.
Proof.
An unoccupied cell and radial-bin has zero restricted mass.
An occupied one is
bounded by the full source mass in
(14.2).
∎
At the critical scales, the cap bounds each occupied parent–radius
mass by \delta^{\lambda}, up to the stated power loss. Finite selection will
retain only source cells carrying a specified fraction of their
original mass. Dividing the cap by the least retained cell mass then
bounds the number of assigned vertices in each parent on a circle.
This is the parent-incidence hypothesis used in
Section 17. Figure 4
shows the intersection whose mass is controlled.
xysource parent C\operatorname{side}(C)=2^{-\lfloor an\rfloor}J: radius intervalof length \delta=2^{-n}Shaded intersection:C\cap\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|x-z|\in J\}Fine source cellshave side \delta.
Figure 4: The occupied-parent annular cap. Supplying the coarse square
C leaves a lower bound for the information in the radius. Coding
the radial distribution of \frac{\mu|_{C}}{\mu(C)} cancels the
parent mass and bounds the full mass of the shaded intersection.
The annulus and fine cells are enlarged for visibility.
Their
drawn widths are not a scale comparison. In the finite selection
the parent side is \eta=2^{-\lfloor a_{\mathrm{par}}n\rfloor}, with
a_{\mathrm{par}} slightly larger than the first hitting scale a_{*}
of the source profile.
Part III Circle graphs, bisectors, and the pinned improvement
15 Perpendicular bisectors and their size
This section separates two notions of size that occur in the proof.
Hausdorff dimension measures a fixed family of lines through all
small scales. A finite count measures a line family at one
specified scale. The proposition below concerns Hausdorff
dimension.
The finite counts are the subject of
Sections 17–19.
The identity after the proposition explains how repeated circle
incidences produce rich tubes. Both follow directly from
elementary geometry.
Proposition 15.1 (Bisectors with one endpoint fixed).
Let y\in\mathbb{R}^{2} and F\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{y\}.
In the line-space metric of Section 2,
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{\mathcal{B}(y,z):z\in F\}=\dim_{\mathrm{H}}F.
Consequently, if E has positive Hausdorff dimension and
\mathcal{B}(E)=\{\mathcal{B}(y,z):y,z\in E,y\neq z\}, then
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\mathcal{B}(E)\geq\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E.
Proof.
Write z-y=rn, with r>0 and n\in S^{1}. Its bisector has
normal n and offset
c=n\cdot y+\frac{r}{2}.
Conversely, reflecting y across L_{n,c} recovers the other
endpoint:
z=y+2(c-n\cdot y)n.
The second formula is independent of the choice between (n,c)
and (-n,-c). The first map and its inverse are locally Lipschitz
away from r=0. Cover the punctured plane by countably many
bounded angular charts with r bounded above and bounded away
from zero. On sufficiently small closed subcharts the maps are
bi-Lipschitz. Hausdorff dimension is preserved there, and countable
stability proves the equality for F.
For the final assertion fix y\in E and apply the equality to
F=E\setminus\{y\}. Removing one point does not change a
positive Hausdorff dimension, and this pinned bisector family is
contained in \mathcal{B}(E).
∎
The approximate bisector relation follows from the identity
|x-y|^{2}-|x-z|^{2}=|y|^{2}-|z|^{2}-2x\cdot(y-z).
If the two distances agree to error O(\delta) and remain bounded,
then x is within O(\frac{\delta}{|y-z|}) of
\mathcal{B}(y,z). Source separation |y-z|\geq c\eta makes this a
tube of width O(\frac{\delta}{\eta}), and separation at a scale
\delta^{c} with c\leq a_{\mathrm{par}} results in the width
O(\delta^{1-c}) used in Sections 17
and 18.
This calculation explains why the circle graph must produce
separated pairs rather than just distinct vertices.
yzxx^{\prime}The same pair occurs oncircles about several pins.Their centers lie near theperpendicular bisector.Tube width: O(\frac{\delta}{|y-z|})For |y-z|\geq c\eta: O(\frac{\delta}{\eta}).
Figure 5: Repeated circle incidences produce a rich bisector tube.
The disks represent terminal source cells.
Their radii and the tube
width are enlarged for visibility. Actual incidences have error
O(\delta), so the centers lie in a tube rather than necessarily on
the exact bisector.
16 Drawing approximate circle incidences
In a multigraph, distinct edges may join the same pair of vertices.
These are parallel edges, and their number is the multiplicity of
the pair. A loop joins a vertex to itself. A simple graph has
neither loops nor parallel edges. A complete graph joins every
pair of distinct vertices. An independent set of vertices has no
edge between any two of its members. These are the graph
conventions used below.
Join consecutive assigned source vertices along each circle.
This produces a graph with many edges and few crossings, because
two distinct circles meet at most twice. The approximate incidences
require some care. A circle can enter a source disk without being
assigned to its vertex.
Inside each disk we route assigned strands through the vertex and
other strands past it. The latter create no new graph incidences.
The extra crossings are controlled by the number of circle strands
entering the disks. At our cardinalities, this cost is smaller than
the main circle-pair count.
A drawing below represents each edge by a simple arc joining
its two vertices. Its interior avoids every graph vertex.
Overlapping arcs are separated by arbitrarily small perturbations.
All interior intersections are transverse, and no three edge
interiors meet at one point. A proper intersection is a crossing.
Edges may share an endpoint, and that shared endpoint is not counted.
The crossing number \operatorname{cr}(G) is the least number of
crossings over these drawings of G. We write V(G) and E(G)
for its numbers of vertices and edges when a graph argument uses
these as numerical quantities.
Lemma 16.1 (Drawing prescribed circle incidences).
Fix R\geq 1 and 0<d_{0}<D_{0}. Let 0<\delta\ll\frac{d_{0}}{R}, and let
V source vertices p_{v} have mutual distances greater than 5R\delta.
Let \mathcal{C} be a finite set of distinct circles, all with radii
in [d_{0},D_{0}], and write C=\#\mathcal{C}.
Choose pairwise disjoint vertex disks
D_{v}=B(p_{v},r_{v}), with R\delta<r_{v}<2R\delta.
Specify an arbitrary set of incidences (v,\gamma) such that
\gamma meets D_{v}. Let I denote their number.
There is a loop-free multigraph G on the source vertices, with at least
I-C edges, obtained by joining consecutive prescribed visits on
each circle, and a drawing satisfying
\operatorname{cr}(G)\leq 2\binom{C}{2}+4\sum_{v}\binom{k_{v}}{2},\qquad k_{v}=\#\{\gamma\in\mathcal{C}:\gamma\cap D_{v}\neq\varnothing\}.
(16.1)
The disk radii may be chosen generically inside the indicated ranges.
No prescribed visit is lost if its circle already meets the smaller
disk of radius R\delta.
If the circles are centered at M distinct pins and, for each pin,
their radii are \delta-separated, then
\operatorname{cr}(G)\leq C_{R}(C^{2}+VM^{2}).
(16.2)
One may take
C_{R}=2(\lfloor 4R\rfloor+2)^{2}.
Every graph edge retains its original circle label and has both
endpoints among that circle’s prescribed incidences.
Proof.
Perturb each disk radius sufficiently slightly inside the indicated
range, preserving exactly the same circle visits, and avoid the
finitely many values that produce a circle tangency to a disk boundary
or put a circle–circle intersection on such a boundary. Since r_{v}<d_{0}, a circle meeting
D_{v} has a single proper arc inside it. This follows also directly
by writing the disk condition on a circle as one inequality for the
cosine of its angular parameter. There is therefore one local
strand for each of the k_{v} circles.
Inside D_{v}, replace an incident strand by the two straight spokes
from its boundary points to p_{v}. Replace a nonincident strand by
a straight chord, or by a generic two-segment polygonal path if a
chord would pass through p_{v}. All these paths stay in the convex
disk. A nonincident path is chosen to avoid p_{v}, overlaps, and
nonrequired multiple intersections. Each pair of local strands
has at most four proper intersections, since each uses at most two
straight segments. In particular, incident strands meeting at the
common graph vertex do not contribute proper crossings.
Outside the disks retain the original circle arcs. Distinct circles
have at most two intersection points, so these portions contribute
at most 2\binom{C}{2} crossings. If necessary, an arbitrarily small
generic perturbation of the circles, preserving all disk visits,
puts their exterior intersections in general position.
The same
bound holds. Such a perturbation exists because the finite list of
boundary incidences was made transverse. The local paths can then
be chosen in general position as above.
A circle with k\geq 2 prescribed visits now contributes k graph edges
between successive prescribed vertices in cyclic order. When k=2
these are two parallel edges, which are allowed. Discard circles
with zero or one prescribed visit. The total number of retained
edges is at least I-C. A nonprescribed disk passage never creates
a graph endpoint. The preceding crossing count proves
(16.1).
For a pin x, a circle of radius r can meet D_{v} only if
||x-p_{v}|-r|\leq r_{v}<2R\delta. A \delta-separated set of radii
has at most \lfloor 4R\rfloor+2 members in this interval. Thus
k_{v}\leq(\lfloor 4R\rfloor+2)M, proving
(16.2) with the stated constant:
the two terms in (16.1) are at most
C^{2} and 2V(\lfloor 4R\rfloor+2)^{2}M^{2}.
∎
The angular span of a row about a pin x is the smallest interval
in its chosen angular chart containing the angles \arg(v-x) of
its entries v. Its length is the angular diameter. An angular
window is an interval in that chart.
Corollary 16.2 (Clique edges in disjoint circle rows).
Assume the hypotheses of
Lemma 16.1, including that the circles
are centered at M distinct pins and have \delta-separated radii
at each pin. On each circle choose disjoint rows of at most s
prescribed source vertices, in circular order, such that their angular
spans have disjoint interiors. Draw all edges of the complete graph on
each row, retaining parallel edges from different rows or circles. For each fixed s this multigraph
has a drawing satisfying
\operatorname{cr}(G_{s})\leq C_{R,s}(C^{2}+VM^{2}).
(16.3)
For example, the construction below permits
C_{R,s}=32\binom{s}{2}^{2}(\lfloor 4R\rfloor+2)^{2}.
No incidence with an unselected vertex is added.
Proof.
Within a row, label an edge by the ordered pair (i,j) of the
positions of its endpoints. For each circle there are at most
L=\binom{s}{2} such labels. For a fixed label, the supporting arcs
in different row spans have disjoint interiors.
Use L arbitrarily close concentric circle tracks for each original
circle, one per label. The tracks can be chosen to preserve all
disk visits, as in the proof of
Lemma 16.1. Support each selected edge
on its track between its two endpoint disks.
It may pass other disks
without being incident to their vertices. Distinct tracks are
circles, hence have at most two exterior intersections, and at most
one selected edge occupies any given track arc. Their total
exterior crossing count is therefore O_{s}(C^{2}).
Fix one disk visit of one track. Its exterior support is a union
of arcs with disjoint interiors: the edge label is fixed and the
row spans on this circle have disjoint interiors. At each of
the two boundary points of the visit, at most one supported edge
arc can enter the disk. Thus the visit carries at most two
incident ends, or one passage, or a combination bounded by these
two boundary ports. Route each incident end to p_{v} by a spoke,
and each passage by a chord or a generic two-segment path avoiding
p_{v}. Figure 6 illustrates these rules.
With L tracks for each of the k_{v} original circle visits,
there are at most 2Lk_{v} local paths. The more generous bound
4Lk_{v} permits endpoint splitting and generic perturbations. The same two-segment construction produces
at most
4\binom{4Lk_{v}}{2}=2(4Lk_{v})(4Lk_{v}-1)\leq 32L^{2}k_{v}^{2}
local crossings.
The exterior crossing count is at most 2\binom{LC}{2}\leq L^{2}C^{2}.
Since k_{v}\leq(\lfloor 4R\rfloor+2)M, summation proves
(16.3) with the displayed choice
of C_{R,s}.
All local replacements stay inside their respective disks.
If a replacement creates a self-intersection of an edge,
erase the loop.
This removes intersections and adds no vertex.
A final arbitrarily small perturbation produces simple edge arcs
in general position without increasing the displayed bound.
All graph endpoints were prescribed row vertices throughout.
∎
p_{v}Before local routingPrescribed disk visitsp_{v}After local routingSpokes and passages
Figure 6: Routing inside a source disk. Each solid spoke ends at the
graph vertex.
A dashed path passes through the disk while avoiding
the vertex. The boundary ports are unchanged. Two assigned visits
are routed through the vertex, while the unassigned visit is
routed past it. In the
clique construction there are \binom{s}{2} tracks per circle,
one for each pair of row positions. Each local path uses at most
two straight segments, so two paths have at most four proper
intersections. A shared graph endpoint is not a crossing.
Lemma 16.3 (Parallel edges give approximate bisector richness).
In the first lemma, if vertices p\neq q have graph multiplicity
m_{G}(p,q), the number of edges joining them, then at least c_{R}m_{G}(p,q) distinct pins lie within
distance
C_{R,D_{0}}\frac{\delta}{|p-q|}
of the perpendicular bisector of p,q.
For the row-clique graph the same statement holds with constants
also depending on s.
Proof.
A circle contributes at most two edges to a fixed pair in the
cyclic graph. Each pin has at most C_{R} circle radii meeting the
disk of p, so at least c_{R}m_{G}(p,q) distinct pins support the
parallel edges. The row-clique graph has a similarly bounded
number of contributions per circle and pair, with a constant
depending only on s.
If a circle centered at x meets both disks, then
\big||x-p|-|x-q|\big|\leq r_{p}+r_{q}\leq 4R\delta.
Both distances are at most D_{0}+2R\delta. Consequently
\big||x-p|^{2}-|x-q|^{2}\big|\leq C_{R,D_{0}}\delta.
The expression on the left equals twice |p-q| times the distance
of x from the bisector. This proves the claim. The labels in the
drawing refer to the original circles, so auxiliary track
perturbations introduce no change in this distance calculation.
∎
Lemma 16.4 (Localized drawing).
In the setting of Corollary 16.2,
suppose in addition that every row has angular diameter at most
Cw about its pin, where \delta\ll w\ll 1, and that each source
vertex belongs to at most C rows at any one pin. For a square Q
of a grid of mesh w, let V_{Q} be the number of source vertices in
a fixed enlargement of Q. Then the drawing can be chosen with
\operatorname{cr}(G_{s})\leq C_{R,s}M^{2}\Bigl(\sum_{Q}V_{Q}^{2}+V\Bigr)\leq C_{R,s}M^{2}V\Bigl(1+\max_{Q}V_{Q}\Bigr).
(16.4)
Proof.
Use the drawing of Corollary 16.2.
Every exterior edge is the short track arc between its endpoint
disks inside its row span.
Since the radii are bounded and the span
is O(w), the arc lies within O(w) of either endpoint, and the
local routings change this by O(\delta). Charge each exterior
crossing to the mesh square containing it.
Both crossing edges then
have an endpoint in a fixed enlargement of that square. At most
CMV_{Q} edge occurrences have an endpoint there, because a vertex
belongs to O(1) rows at each of the M pins and a row has
\binom{s}{2} edges. Distinct tracks meet at most twice and arcs
of different rows on one track have disjoint interiors, so the
crossings charged to Q number at most C(MV_{Q})^{2}. Inside a
source disk the local routing costs O_{R,s}(M^{2}) crossings, as in
the corollary. Summing proves the first bound in
(16.4), and \sum_{Q}V_{Q}\lesssim V implies the
second.
∎
For source representatives in distinct terminal \delta-squares,
the required disk separation can be arranged by a fixed finite
coloring of the grid. A color retaining a fixed fraction of the
prescribed incidences is sufficient. In the configuration of
Section 17, V\lesssim\frac{p}{b},
M\lesssim\upsilon_{f}^{-1}, and C\lesssim\upsilon_{f}^{-1}\delta^{-q},
so the two terms in (16.3) are
O(\upsilon_{f}^{-2}\delta^{-2q}) and O(p\upsilon_{f}^{-2}b^{-1}).
The
second is the smaller one because p\delta^{2q}\leq b.
Thus local detours do not introduce a new loss.
17 Circle graphs produce rows with rich bisectors
The reason for drawing the graph can be seen from the counts. After
a thinning of the source described below, the source vertices number
about \frac{p}{b}, the circles about \upsilon_{f}^{-1}\delta^{-q},
and the rows about \frac{p^{2}}{nb\upsilon_{f}}, where the symbols are
introduced next. A simple graph with these many vertices and edges
would have more crossings than the drawing of
Section 16 allows. Hence many edges must be
repeated.
For a source pair, repetition means that many circles from different
pins visit both endpoints. Those pins lie near the perpendicular
bisector. We need this conclusion for every pair in a row of fixed
length, so we draw all pairs within each row and discard rows
containing a pair of low multiplicity. Two further steps sharpen the
conclusion. An ordered selection inside each row extracts shorter
rows whose pair separations lie between two consecutive scales.
The
drawing of these rows can then be localized, which increases the
multiplicity of their pairs. Finally the pins are thinned to the
resolution of the resulting bisector tubes.
17.1 The finite inputs
The finite configuration is produced from the measures in
Section 20. We list here the properties that
the circle argument uses, so that this section and the next two are
finite statements. The hypotheses below govern the pinned applications
through Section 25.
Proposition 29.1 states a separate
set of finite hypotheses and verifies the relevant counting steps
directly for the rigid endpoint configuration. In the present application,
\Delta,\omega,\lambda,F,a_{c},W_{0},q are as in
(8.1), and \theta is a fixed template in the
sense of Section 20: a point
(u_{-},W,\varsigma,\chi,\mathfrak{e}) of the polytope
(8.7), with u_{+}=u_{-}+a_{c}+\chi\omega and with
u_{0},\varpi,e_{0} given by (8.12), together with a branch
index, where branch 1 requires \mathfrak{e}\leq\frac{1}{8} and
branch 2 requires \mathfrak{e}\geq\frac{1}{8}. Fixed
tolerances \beta,\xi,\xi_{\mathrm{par}},\tau_{0}>0 are chosen in
Section 21. Put
a_{\mathrm{par}}=a_{c}+\varpi\omega+\xi_{\mathrm{par}},\qquad\delta=2^{-n},\qquad\eta=2^{-\lfloor a_{\mathrm{par}}n\rfloor},\qquad b=\delta^{\lambda-\beta},
(17.1)
where \xi_{\mathrm{par}} is a selectable positive tolerance in the
parent exponent. The integer rounding in \eta changes its side
length by at most a factor two. Fix candidate exponents
\begin{gathered}a_{0}=a_{c}-\omega-\beta-\xi<a_{1}<\cdots<a_{\mathfrak{n}}=a_{\mathrm{par}},\\
0<a_{j}-a_{j-1}\leq\xi\quad\text{for }1\leq j\leq\mathfrak{n},\end{gathered}
(17.2)
with \mathfrak{n} fixed, together with dyadic lengths
\ell_{j}\asymp\delta^{a_{j}} such that \ell_{0}\geq 4\delta^{a_{0}} and
\ell_{\mathfrak{n}}<c_{0}\eta, where c_{0} is the source-parent
separation constant of Proposition 17.3.
The candidate tube widths are \rho_{j}=\frac{\delta}{\ell_{j}}\asymp\delta^{\zeta_{j}},
\zeta_{j}=1-a_{j}. All source and pin sets lie in fixed bounded regions
separated by a fixed positive distance, and all circle radii lie in a
fixed interval [d_{0},D_{0}]\subset(0,\infty). The numbers
m,\upsilon_{f},\upsilon_{1},\ldots,\upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}}\in(0,1] are
cell masses of the source and pin measures \mu,\nu, and
p\in(0,1] is a relation mass with p\geq n^{-C} for a fixed C.
The notation X\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}Y means
X\leq Cn^{C}p^{-C}Y with C fixed. We use
X\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}Y for Y\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}X, and
X\asymp_{\mathrm{pol}}Y when both inequalities hold.
A quantity denoted by O_{\mathrm{pol}}(Y) has absolute value
\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}Y.
Such factors are
\delta^{-o(1)}.
Lemma 20.1, applied in
Corollary 20.3, supplies a common
\delta-separated source set S of cell centers, a
\delta-separated set X of actual pins, with at most one pin in each
dyadic \delta-cell and N=\#X, and marked
incidences (x,v,J) between pins, source vertices, and radial bins
of length \delta, called assigned incidences in that lemma, with
the following properties.
(F1)
\#S\leq m^{-1},
\frac{cp}{\upsilon_{f}}\leq N\leq\upsilon_{f}^{-1}, and the number I
of marked incidences is at least \frac{cp}{m\upsilon_{f}}. At each
pin at most C\delta^{-q} radial bins are marked and their radii
are \delta-separated.
Every marked vertex is within C\delta of
its circle, and a vertex has O(1) marked radial labels at any one
pin.
(F2)
The number A_{x}(J) of source vertices marked
at x with direction in an angular interval J, and the number
A_{x}(C_{\eta},J_{\delta}) marked at x in a source square C_{\eta} of
side \eta with radial bin J_{\delta}, satisfy
A_{x}(J)\leq\frac{C(|J|+\delta)}{pm},\qquad A_{x}(C_{\eta},J_{\delta})\leq\frac{Cb}{pm};
the second bound holds also for annuli of width O(\delta) about
x.
(F3)
Every source \delta-cell containing a
vertex of S has mass in [m,2m), with m\leq b. Every source
square of side \eta containing a vertex of S has mass at most
\delta^{F-\tau_{0}}. If \theta has branch 1, then for every
a\in[a_{c}-\omega,a_{\mathrm{par}}] every dyadic square of side
\asymp\delta^{a} containing a vertex of S has mass at most
\delta^{a+a_{c}-(1+u_{0}+\mathfrak{e})\omega-\tau_{0}}.
(F4)
Every \delta-cell containing a pin of X
has mass in [\upsilon_{f},2\upsilon_{f}), and for each j its
ancestor of side \rho_{j} has mass in [\upsilon_{j},2\upsilon_{j}).
Every ancestor of side 2^{-k}, 0\leq k\leq n, of a pin of X has
mass at most \delta^{\frac{k}{n}+T_{\theta}(\frac{k}{n})-\tau_{0}}, where
T_{\theta}(u)=\max\{0,W-\operatorname{dist}(u,[u_{-},u_{+}])\}. Moreover
\nu(B(z,r))\leq Cr^{1+\varkappa} for all balls, with a fixed
\varkappa>0.
The angular density bound L of
Lemma 20.1 is a fixed constant
and has been absorbed into C. All cardinalities are polynomial in
\delta^{-1}. These bounds refer to the original measures.
No source
law is renormalized at different pins.
Fix also an angular cutoff
\rho_{\min}=\delta^{1-a_{c}+\omega+\beta+2\xi}.
(17.3)
We assume
2\beta+2\xi<a_{c}-\omega,\qquad\xi_{\mathrm{par}}+3\tau_{0}\leq\beta,\qquad 8\xi_{\mathrm{par}}\leq\tau_{0},\qquad a_{\mathrm{par}}<\frac{1}{2},
(17.4)
which hold once the tolerances are small, since
a_{\mathrm{par}}\leq\frac{1}{2}-W_{0}+2\omega+\xi_{\mathrm{par}}. Then
\rho_{\min}\leq\rho_{j}\leq\delta^{\frac{1}{2}} for every j, \frac{\delta}{\rho_{\min}}\to 0,
and, with
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ang}}=a_{c}+\Delta-\omega-2\beta-2\xi>0,
we have \frac{\rho_{\min}}{b}=2^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ang}}n}.
The positivity follows from 2\beta+2\xi<a_{c}-\omega and
\Delta\geq 0. Every tolerance is fixed before n tends to infinity.
17.2 One thinning of the source for all pins
A thinning retains each point independently with a specified
probability. We write \mathbb{P} for probability, \mathbb{E}Z
for expectation, and \operatorname{Var}(Z)=\mathbb{E}(Z-\mathbb{E}Z)^{2}
for variance. We use Markov’s inequality
\mathbb{P}\{Z\geq t\}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}Z}{t} for Z\geq 0, and
Chebyshev’s inequality
\mathbb{P}\{|Z-\mathbb{E}Z|\geq t\}\leq\frac{\operatorname{Var}(Z)}{t^{2}}.
For a sum Z of independent zero-one variables with mean m, the
Chernoff bounds used below are
\mathbb{P}\{Z<\frac{1}{2}m\}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{m}{8}},\qquad\mathbb{P}\{Z\geq m+t\}\leq\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2(m+\frac{t}{3})}\right)\quad\text{for }t>0.
See [1, Chapters 2, 4 and Appendix A].
The union bound adds the failure probabilities over all events.
With at most 2^{Cn} events, the upper-tail estimate ensures
Z\leq C^{\prime}(m+n) for all of them with probability tending to one,
by taking C^{\prime} sufficiently large. These are the simultaneous
sampling bounds used in this and the subsequent sections.
The simultaneous requirement is essential. A related probabilistic
selection appears in Shmerkin–Yavicoli [32, Lemmas 3.4 and 3.6],
where one small set must work for a whole family of sumsets.
Their discrete argument is adapted from Ruzsa, as explained there.
Here the selected vertices must preserve both the total incidence
count and the upper occupancy bounds at every retained pin. The
following proof establishes these properties for one source sample.
Lemma 17.1 (One simultaneous source restriction).
For every sufficiently large n, there is a subset S^{\prime}\subset S
such that, retaining the marked incidences with vertices in S^{\prime},
the following hold.
\displaystyle\#S^{\prime}
\displaystyle\leq\frac{Cp}{b},
\displaystyle I^{\prime}
\displaystyle\geq\frac{cp^{2}}{b\upsilon_{f}},
(17.5)
\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{x}(C_{\eta},J_{\delta})
\displaystyle\leq Cn,
\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{x}(J)
\displaystyle\leq\frac{C|J|}{b}\quad\text{for }|J|\geq\rho_{\min}.
(17.6)
Every square Q of side \asymp\delta^{a} with
a\in[a_{0},a_{\mathrm{par}}], and every fixed enlargement of it,
satisfies
\#(S^{\prime}\cap Q)\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}\frac{\mu_{a}}{b},\qquad\mu_{a}=\delta^{F-\tau_{0}}\Bigl(\frac{\delta^{a}}{\eta}\Bigr)^{2},
(17.7)
and, if \theta has branch 1 and a\geq a_{c}-\omega, also with
\mu_{a}=\delta^{a+a_{c}-(1+u_{0}+\mathfrak{e})\omega-\tau_{0}}.
The parent bound in (17.6) holds for annuli of
width O(\delta) as well.
Proof.
Keep each source vertex independently with probability
t=\frac{c_{0}pm}{b}\leq 1, with one choice serving every pin. Each
parent and radial-bin occupancy has expectation at most Cc_{0}. The binomial
tail bound and a union bound over the polynomially many parent and radial-bin
pairs and pins show that all these occupancies are at most Cn
with probability tending to one. For a dyadic angular interval of
length at least a fixed multiple of \rho_{\min}, the expected
count is at most \frac{C|J|}{b}, since
\frac{\delta}{\rho_{\min}}\to 0. Chernoff’s inequality and a
second union bound give A^{\prime}_{x}(J)\leq C(\frac{|J|}{b}+n)
simultaneously. A cover by a bounded number of dyadic intervals
of comparable length, in the fixed angular charts, extends this
bound to every |J|\geq\rho_{\min}. The additive term is absorbed
uniformly over all such intervals, because
\sup_{|J|\geq\rho_{\min}}\frac{bn}{|J|}\leq\frac{bn}{\rho_{\min}}=n2^{-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ang}}n}\longrightarrow 0.
This proves the angular bound for every fixed choice of tolerances
satisfying (17.4). The threshold for n
may depend on these tolerances, but is uniform in the pins and intervals.
A square Q of side \asymp\delta^{a} meets O((\frac{\delta^{a}}{\eta})^{2})
squares of side \eta, each of mass at most \delta^{F-\tau_{0}} if
it contains a vertex of S, and in branch 1 with a\geq a_{c}-\omega
it is covered by O(1) dyadic squares of side \asymp\delta^{a}, each of
mass at most \delta^{a+a_{c}-(1+u_{0}+\mathfrak{e})\omega-\tau_{0}} if it
contains a vertex of S, both by (F3). Since the
\delta-cells of S have mass at least m, Q contains at most
\frac{C\mu_{a}}{m} vertices of S, and the expected number retained
is at most \frac{C\mu_{a}}{b}. Chernoff’s inequality and a union bound
over the dyadic scales in the stated range and their polynomially many
squares, followed by a bounded dyadic cover of each Q, give
(17.7) with an additive
O(n), which is absorbed because \frac{\mu_{a}}{b} grows faster than
every power of n: the exponent of \delta in \frac{\mu_{a}}{b} is
at most
F+\xi_{\mathrm{par}}-\lambda+\beta=-\frac{1}{2}+\xi_{\mathrm{par}}+\beta<-\frac{a_{c}}{2}\leq-\frac{1}{8}.
Indeed \xi_{\mathrm{par}}<\frac{1}{2}-a_{c} follows from
a_{\mathrm{par}}<\frac{1}{2} and \varpi\geq 0, while
\beta<\frac{a_{c}}{2} follows from
(17.4). For the covering bound,
a\leq a_{\mathrm{par}} implies \delta^{a}\geq\frac{\eta}{2}.
Dyadic rounding therefore changes only a fixed factor. For the
branch 1 bound, use
a+a_{c}-(1+u_{0}+\mathfrak{e})\omega\leq F+\xi_{\mathrm{par}},
which follows from a\leq a_{\mathrm{par}} and \varpi\leq 1+u_{0}.
Let d_{v} be the number of marked incidences at the vertex v.
Then d_{v}\leq CN. Hence
\mathbb{E}I^{\prime}=tI\geq\frac{cp^{2}}{b\upsilon_{f}},\qquad\operatorname{Var}(I^{\prime})\leq CN\mathbb{E}I^{\prime},\qquad\frac{\operatorname{Var}(I^{\prime})}{(\mathbb{E}I^{\prime})^{2}}\leq\frac{Cb}{p^{2}}\longrightarrow 0,
so the incidence bound holds with probability tending to one.
Finally \mathbb{E}\#S^{\prime}\leq\frac{c_{0}p}{b}, and Markov’s inequality
bounds the probability of violating the vertex bound by
\frac{1}{10} after choosing its constant. All requirements hold
simultaneously with positive probability. An annulus of width
O(\delta) meets O(1) radial bins, which proves the last
assertion.
∎
17.3 Rows with rich bisectors
The degree of a pin in a collection of rows means the number of
rows assigned to it. To regularize degrees dyadically, divide
positive degrees into intervals [2^{j},2^{j+1}) and retain one
class. When there are O(n) classes, one carries at least an
O(n)^{-1} fraction of the total count being divided.
Lemma 17.2 (Multigraph edge bound).
A loop-free multigraph with V vertices, E edges, maximum edge
multiplicity at most k_{1}\geq 1, and a drawing with X
crossings satisfies
E\leq C\bigl(k_{1}V+k_{1}^{\frac{1}{3}}V^{\frac{2}{3}}X^{\frac{1}{3}}\bigr).
(17.8)
Proof.
Independently for each bundle of k\leq k_{1} parallel edges,
retain the bundle with probability \frac{k}{k_{1}} and then
choose one of its edges uniformly. The resulting random graph is
simple, with expected edge count \frac{E}{k_{1}} and expected
crossing count at most \frac{X}{k_{1}^{2}}: each edge survives
with probability \frac{1}{k_{1}}, edges from distinct bundles
are independent, and two edges of one bundle cannot both survive.
Apply the crossing lemma [34, Theorem 1] in its global form
\operatorname{cr}(G_{\mathrm{samp}})\geq c\frac{E(G_{\mathrm{samp}})^{3}}{V^{2}}-CV,
then Jensen’s inequality. Multiplication by k_{1}^{2} and
rearrangement give (17.8).
∎
Proposition 17.3 (Circle graphs force rows with rich bisectors).
Fix an integer s_{0}\geq 3. Under the hypotheses of this section there
are rows of s_{0} marked source vertices of S^{\prime}, each row lying on
one original circle, with the following properties.
1.
At least \frac{cp}{n^{2}\upsilon_{f}} pins carry rows, and each
of them carries between \mathfrak{d} and 2\mathfrak{d} rows, where
\frac{cp^{2}}{nb}\leq\mathfrak{d}\leq\frac{Cp}{b}.
2.
On each circle the angular spans of its rows have disjoint
interiors, and every row lies in one member of a fixed angular
partition into windows of width
w_{0}=\frac{C_{s_{0}}nb}{p^{2}\delta^{q}}. The source angles in a
row are pairwise separated by at least c_{0}\eta, with c_{0}>0
fixed. Each source vertex belongs to at most C_{s_{0}} rows at any
one pin.
At a retained pin, any angular interval of length
r\geq\rho_{\min} contains at most \frac{Cr}{b} row entries, and
each window contains at most \frac{Cw_{0}}{b} rows.
3.
Every pair of vertices in every row has at least
k_{1}=\frac{cp^{4}\delta^{2q}}{n^{3}b\upsilon_{f}}
(17.9)
distinct pins x\in X with \bigl||x-v|-|x-z|\bigr|\leq C\delta.
The constants depend only on s_{0} and the fixed geometric bounds.
Proof.
Apply Lemma 17.1 and retain its marked
incidences. First color the source points in boundedly many colors
so that points of one color have pairwise distance greater than
100(C_{0}+1)\delta, where C_{0}\delta bounds the incidence error.
This is possible by the bounded degree of the corresponding
proximity graph. Independently color the dyadic side-\eta
parents according to their two indices modulo 4. Distinct parents
of one color have distance at least \eta. Keep a combined color
carrying a fixed fraction of the marked incidences. Thus all
retained source disks of radius O(\delta) can be made disjoint,
and the bounds of Lemma 17.1 persist.
On each circle keep one marked vertex from each occupied parent.
Since the parent occupancy is at most Cn, the number of
representatives is at least
T_{0}=\frac{cp^{2}}{nb\upsilon_{f}}.
Representatives on a common circle are at distance at least
\eta, their radii about the pin differ by O(\delta), and the
radii are bounded above and below.
So their angles are separated
by at least c_{0}\eta. A vertex has O(1) marked radial labels at
a pin, hence occurs in O_{s_{0}}(1) rows there.
The number of circles is at most
\mathcal{C}_{0}=C\upsilon_{f}^{-1}\delta^{-q}. Partition each circle into
angular windows of width w_{0}, order the representatives in each
window, and group consecutive representatives into disjoint rows
of s_{0}, discarding fewer than s_{0} at the end of each window. The
discarded count is at most C_{s_{0}}\mathcal{C}_{0}(1+w_{0}^{-1}). Since
\frac{nb}{p^{2}\delta^{q}}=\frac{n}{p^{2}}\delta^{a_{c}-\omega-\beta}\to 0,
the term C_{s_{0}}\mathcal{C}_{0} is negligible against T_{0} and
w_{0}\to 0.
The term C_{s_{0}}\mathcal{C}_{0}w_{0}^{-1} is a small fraction of
T_{0} once the constant in w_{0} is large. We obtain at least cT_{0}
rows.
Form the multigraph on the retained vertices with all
\binom{s_{0}}{2} pairs of every row, one edge per row occurrence.
Within a circle, the angular spans of its rows have disjoint
interiors. Corollary 16.2 provides a
drawing with
\operatorname{cr}(G)\leq C_{s_{0}}\bigl(\mathcal{C}_{0}^{2}+\#S^{\prime}N^{2}\bigr)\leq C_{s_{0}}X_{0},\qquad X_{0}=\upsilon_{f}^{-2}\delta^{-2q},
where the last inequality uses \#S^{\prime}\leq\frac{Cp}{b} and
\frac{\delta^{2q}}{b}=\delta^{\frac{1}{2}+2\omega+\beta}\to 0. Let
G_{\mathrm{lo}} consist of the edge occurrences of pairs whose
total multiplicity is less than k_{1}. Its crossing number is at
most that of G, so Lemma 17.2 implies
E(G_{\mathrm{lo}})\leq C_{s_{0}}\bigl(k_{1}\#S^{\prime}+k_{1}^{\frac{1}{3}}(\#S^{\prime})^{\frac{2}{3}}X_{0}^{\frac{1}{3}}\bigr).
Since (\#S^{\prime})^{2}X_{0}\leq\frac{Cp^{2}}{b^{2}\upsilon_{f}^{2}\delta^{2q}},
the second term is at most (Cc)^{\frac{1}{3}}T_{0}, where c is the
constant in (17.9), and k_{1}\#S^{\prime}\leq CT_{0}\frac{p^{3}\delta^{2q}}{n^{2}b}=o(T_{0}).
Choosing c small makes E(G_{\mathrm{lo}}) a small fraction of
the row count. Every row containing a low-multiplicity pair
contributes a distinct edge occurrence to G_{\mathrm{lo}}: the
rows are disjoint on each circle, and occurrences from different
circles retain their separate circle labels. Consequently at least
cT_{0} rows have every pair of multiplicity at least k_{1}. A fixed
unordered source pair contributes O_{s_{0}}(1) edge copies at any one
pin, since only O(1) of its \delta-separated radius bins can
meet the two source disks and each vertex is used at most once in a
row per circle. Thus every pair in a surviving row is nearly
equidistant, to error C\delta, from at least c_{s_{0}}k_{1} distinct
pins.
Renaming c proves item 3.
Keep exactly a fixed multiple of T_{0} surviving rows. A pin has
at most C_{s_{0}}\#S^{\prime}\leq\frac{C_{s_{0}}p}{b} rows. Delete the pins with
fewer than \frac{cp^{2}}{nb} rows.
For a small constant they
account for fewer than half the rows, since N\leq\upsilon_{f}^{-1}.
Among the O(n) remaining dyadic degree classes, one carries at
least \frac{cT_{0}}{n} rows.
Its pins have degree between
\mathfrak{d} and 2\mathfrak{d} and number at least
\frac{cT_{0}}{n\mathfrak{d}}\geq\frac{cp}{n^{2}\upsilon_{f}}. The sampled
angular bound (17.6), with the bounded row
participation of each vertex, bounds the number of row entries in
an interval of length r\geq\rho_{\min} by \frac{Cr}{b} and the
window population by \frac{Cw_{0}}{b}.
∎
A witness of a source pair is a pin supporting an assigned
occurrence of both vertices on one circle. The witnesses in
item 3 can be chosen among the assigned occurrences
of the pair on the original circles: this is how their multiplicity
was counted. We make that choice throughout, and retain the same
assignment when rows are restricted or pins are coarsened.
The witnesses of item 3 lie within O(\frac{\delta}{|v-z|}) of the
perpendicular bisector of the pair, by the squared-distance identity
of Section 15.
The width of this tube
depends on the separation of the pair. The next step selects rows
whose pair separations all lie between two consecutive candidate
scales.
17.4 Rows at one pair-separation scale
Lemma 17.4 (Ordered selection between consecutive scales).
Let s_{1}\geq 2 and let \ell_{0}>\ell_{1}>\cdots>\ell_{\mathfrak{n}}>0 be
lengths whose consecutive ratios are powers of two. Consider the
nested partitions of one interval of length \ell_{0} into cells of
length \ell_{j} at depth j. Let F_{0} be a finite subset of that
interval with at most one point in each cell of depth \mathfrak{n}.
If \#F_{0}>(2s_{1}-2)^{\mathfrak{n}}, then for some
j\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\} there are s_{1} points of F_{0} in one
cell of depth j-1 whose pairwise distances all lie between
\ell_{j} and \ell_{j-1}.
Proof.
If every occupied cell had at most 2s_{1}-2 occupied children, there
would be at most (2s_{1}-2)^{\mathfrak{n}} occupied cells of depth
\mathfrak{n}. Thus some cell of depth j-1 has at least 2s_{1}-1
occupied children. Order these children from left to right and
choose one point from the first, third, fifth, and so on, through
the (2s_{1}-1)st. Between two chosen children lies a whole child
cell, so the chosen points are at least \ell_{j} apart, and their
common parent bounds their separation by \ell_{j-1}.
∎
Proposition 17.5 (Rows with a common pair-separation scale).
Fix s_{1}\geq 3 and apply Proposition 17.3
with s_{0}=(2s_{1}-2)^{\mathfrak{n}}+1. Then there is an index
1\leq j\leq\mathfrak{n}, and there are rows of s_{1} vertices of S^{\prime}
on one original circle each, with the following properties.
Write
a=a_{j-1}, c=a_{j}, \zeta=\zeta_{j}=1-c, \rho=\rho_{j}, and
w=\ell_{j-1}\asymp\delta^{a}.
1.
At least \gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\upsilon_{f}^{-1} pins carry
rows, each with degree between \mathfrak{d}^{\prime} and 2\mathfrak{d}^{\prime},
where \mathfrak{d}^{\prime}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}b^{-1}.
2.
Every row lies in one cell of a fixed angular partition into
windows of width w.
Every pair in a row has angular separation
between \ell_{j} and \ell_{j-1}.
On each circle the spans of the
rows have disjoint interiors.
A vertex belongs to O_{s_{1}}(1) rows at
any one pin.
At a retained pin an angular interval of length
r\geq\rho contains at most \frac{Cr}{b} row entries and a window at
most \frac{Cw}{b} rows.
3.
Every pair v,z in a row has the k_{1} witnesses of
Proposition 17.3, and each witness lies
within C\rho of the perpendicular bisector of v,z, whose direction
differs from the half-sum of the two source angles, modulo \pi and
up to a fixed rotation, by O(\rho).
Proof.
An s_{0}-row lies in a window of width
w_{0}=\frac{C_{s_{0}}nb}{p^{2}\delta^{q}}=C_{s_{0}}\frac{n}{p^{2}}\delta^{a_{c}-\omega-\beta}\leq\frac{1}{4}\delta^{a_{0}} for small \delta, and its entries are
separated by at least c_{0}\eta>\ell_{\mathfrak{n}}. Use the three root
grids of mesh \ell_{0} with origins 0,\frac{\ell_{0}}{3},\frac{2\ell_{0}}{3},
each with its nested dyadic descendants. An interval of length at
most \frac{\ell_{0}}{4} meets the boundary of at most one of the
three grids, so every row lies in one root cell of some grid, and
each terminal cell contains at most one of its entries.
Lemma 17.4 provides an s_{1}-entry subrow, a
grid, and an index j such that all pair separations lie in
[\ell_{j},\ell_{j-1}] and the subrow lies in one cell of length
\ell_{j-1}. Among the 3\mathfrak{n} choices of grid and index,
retain one carrying a fixed fraction of the rows. The depth-(j-1)
cells of the chosen grid are the asserted angular partition. Remove
the pins carrying fewer than a small fixed multiple of
\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{n} subrows and regularize the remaining degrees
dyadically.
Item 1 follows from item 1 of
Proposition 17.3. Subrow spans lie inside
the original row spans, so their disjoint interiors, the bounded
participation of each vertex, and the angular counts persist.
For a pair v,z in a subrow and one of its witnesses x,
\bigl||x-v|^{2}-|x-z|^{2}\bigr|\leq C\delta and |v-z|\geq c\ell_{j},
so x lies within \frac{C\delta}{\ell_{j}}=C\rho of the bisector.
Replacing the two radii by their mean changes the chord by
O(\delta) and its direction by O(\rho), which proves the half-sum
formula.
∎
17.5 Localized drawing and a second pruning
The rows of Proposition 17.5 have angular
diameter at most w\asymp\delta^{a}, so their drawing can be
localized. This increases the multiplicity of their pairs when
a is close to a_{\mathrm{par}}. Put
\mathfrak{h}=a_{\mathrm{par}}-a\geq 0,\qquad\psi(\mathfrak{h})=\begin{cases}\min\{2\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}+e_{0}\omega\},&\theta\text{ of branch 1 and }a\geq a_{c}-\omega,\\
2\mathfrak{h},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}
(17.10)
Proposition 17.6 (Richness from the shorter rows).
A fraction \gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}1 of the rows of
Proposition 17.5 can be retained, and the
pin degrees regularized, so that items 1 and 2 there persist and
every pair v,z in every retained row has at least
k_{f}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}N\max\left\{\frac{\delta^{2q}}{b},b\delta^{-F+\tau_{0}+\psi(\mathfrak{h})}\right\}
(17.11)
distinct pins x\in X with \bigl||x-v|-|x-z|\bigr|\leq C\delta.
Proof.
The retained rows number T\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\frac{N}{b}, by
item 1 of Proposition 17.5 and
N\asymp_{\mathrm{pol}}\upsilon_{f}^{-1}, and their vertices number
V\leq\#S^{\prime}\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}b^{-1}. Lemma 16.4
with mesh w, together with (17.7) at the
exponent a, provides a drawing with
X\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}N^{2}V\max_{Q}V_{Q}\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}\frac{N^{2}\mu_{a}}{b^{2}},
and \mu_{a}\leq\delta^{F-\tau_{0}-\psi(\mathfrak{h})}:
indeed \delta^{a}\leq\eta\delta^{-\mathfrak{h}} implies
\mu_{a}\leq\delta^{F-\tau_{0}-2\mathfrak{h}}, and in branch 1 with
a\geq a_{c}-\omega,
a+a_{c}-(1+u_{0}+\mathfrak{e})\omega=F-\mathfrak{h}-(1+u_{0}+\mathfrak{e}-\varpi)\omega+\xi_{\mathrm{par}}\geq F-\mathfrak{h}-e_{0}\omega,
by (17.1), F=2a_{c}, and (8.12).
In the remaining cases \psi(\mathfrak{h})=2\mathfrak{h}. The
same drawing also obeys the bound X\lesssim N^{2}\delta^{-2q} of
Proposition 17.3. Let k_{f} be the right
side of (17.11) with a small constant. Then
\frac{T^{3}}{V^{2}X}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}N\max\left\{\frac{\delta^{2q}}{b},b\delta^{-F+\tau_{0}+\psi(\mathfrak{h})}\right\},\qquad k_{f}V\ll T,
the last because \frac{\delta^{2q}}{b}\to 0 and
b\delta^{-F+\psi}=\delta^{\frac{1}{2}+\psi-\beta}\to 0. As in the
proof of Proposition 17.3,
Lemma 17.2 bounds the edge occurrences of
pairs of multiplicity below k_{f} by a small fraction of T.
Deleting every row containing such a pair, converting multiplicity
into distinct pins by the bounded participation at each pin, and
regularizing degrees proves the claim.
∎
At leading exponent order the two candidate thresholds in
(17.11) cross at \psi(\mathfrak{h})=2\omega.
With the tolerances retained, equality occurs at
\psi(\mathfrak{h})=2\omega+2\beta-\tau_{0}. The first is the multiplicity available
at every scale.
The second uses the source occupancy of
(17.7) and is better when the row scale is
close to the parent scale. When a<a_{c}-\omega, the gap satisfies
\mathfrak{h}>a_{\mathrm{par}}-a_{c}+\omega=(\varpi+1)\omega+\xi_{\mathrm{par}}\geq 2\omega,
so \min\{2\omega,\psi(\mathfrak{h})\}=2\omega whichever branch the
template has.
Only this minimum enters the exponents of
Section 21.
17.6 Pins at the resolution of the tubes
The witnesses lie within C\rho of the bisectors, but they are only
\delta-separated. We thin them to one pin per dyadic \rho-cell,
keeping the original pin measure, so that the resulting set is
spread like a set of dimension one at all scales between \rho and
one.
Lemma 17.7 (Pin selection at the tube resolution).
Let X^{\prime}\subset X be the pins carrying rows in
Proposition 17.6, so that
\#X^{\prime}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\upsilon_{f}^{-1}, and let \mathcal{E} be
the set of source pairs in the retained rows, each e\in\mathcal{E}
with its witness set \mathcal{W}(e)\subset X, \#\mathcal{W}(e)\geq k_{f}.
Write \upsilon_{\zeta}=\upsilon_{j} for the pin ancestor mass at the
scale \rho=\rho_{j}. If
W_{0}-4\omega-\beta-\xi_{\mathrm{par}}>0,
(17.12)
then for all sufficiently small \delta there is a set P\subset X
with at most one point in each dyadic \rho-cell such that
\displaystyle\#P
\displaystyle\leq\upsilon_{\zeta}^{-1},\qquad\#(P\cap X^{\prime})\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\upsilon_{\zeta}^{-1},
(17.13)
\displaystyle\#(P\cap\mathcal{W}(e))
\displaystyle\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\frac{k_{f}\upsilon_{f}}{\upsilon_{\zeta}}\quad\text{for }e\in\mathcal{E},
(17.14)
and, for every z and r\geq\rho,
\#(P\cap B(z,r))\leq\frac{\nu(B(z,r+\sqrt{2}\rho))}{\upsilon_{\zeta}}.
(17.15)
The rows at the pins of P\cap X^{\prime} are unchanged. Choose one of the nine
classes of \rho-cells with fixed indices modulo three which contains
at least a ninth of P\cap X^{\prime}, and let P^{\prime} be the points of
P\cap X^{\prime} in that class. Then
\frac{\#(P^{\prime}\cap B(z,r))}{\#P^{\prime}}\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}r^{1+\varkappa}\leq r\quad\text{for }\rho\leq r\leq 1.
(17.16)
Proof.
By (F4), every occupied \rho-cell has mass below
2\upsilon_{\zeta} and each of its occupied \delta-cells has mass at
least \upsilon_{f}, so it contains at most L=\lceil\frac{2\upsilon_{\zeta}}{\upsilon_{f}}\rceil
pins of X, and 2\upsilon_{\zeta}>\upsilon_{f} by nesting, so
L\asymp\frac{\upsilon_{\zeta}}{\upsilon_{f}}. Label the pins of each
\rho-cell by distinct slots in \{1,\ldots,L\}, choose one uniform
slot in every occupied cell independently, and retain its pin if the
slot is occupied. Each pin survives with probability \frac{1}{L}.
For a fixed pair e, the number of retained witnesses is a sum of
independent indicators, one per occupied \rho-cell, with mean
\frac{\#\mathcal{W}(e)}{L}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\frac{k_{f}\upsilon_{f}}{\upsilon_{\zeta}}.
This mean is a positive power of \delta^{-1}: by
(17.11) and N\upsilon_{f}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}1 it is
at least \delta^{o(1)}\frac{\delta^{2q}}{b\upsilon_{\zeta}}, and
\upsilon_{\zeta}\leq C\rho^{1+\varkappa}\leq C\delta^{\zeta} with
\zeta=1-c\geq 1-a_{\mathrm{par}} gives the exponent
\zeta-(2q-\lambda+\beta)\geq 1-a_{c}-\varpi\omega-\xi_{\mathrm{par}}-\frac{1}{2}-2\omega-\beta\geq W_{0}-4\omega-\beta-\xi_{\mathrm{par}}>0.
Chernoff’s lower-tail bound
\mathbb{P}\{Z<\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}Z\}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathbb{E}Z}{8}} and a
union bound over the polynomially many pairs show that all the
witness counts are at least half their means with probability
tending to one. The number of retained pins of X^{\prime} is a sum of the
same kind with mean \frac{\#X^{\prime}}{L}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\upsilon_{\zeta}^{-1},
again a positive power of \delta^{-1}, and is at least half its
mean with probability tending to one. Fix a sample realizing these
events. There are at most \upsilon_{\zeta}^{-1} occupied
\rho-cells, each having mass at least \upsilon_{\zeta}, which implies
\#P\leq\upsilon_{\zeta}^{-1}.
The points stay at their original locations. The \rho-cells of
the points of P\cap B(z,r) lie in B(z,r+\sqrt{2}\rho) and have
mass at least \upsilon_{\zeta}, which proves
(17.15). Each of the nine classes modulo three
is \rho-separated.
Choose such a class containing at least a ninth of P\cap X^{\prime},
and let P^{\prime} be its intersection with P\cap X^{\prime}. Then
\#P^{\prime}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\upsilon_{\zeta}^{-1}.
Dividing (17.15) by this lower bound and using
\nu(B(z,3r))\leq Cr^{1+\varkappa} proves
(17.16).
∎
Only the pins of P^{\prime} are used to generate tubes.
The whole set P
remains the witness set. The pin ancestor masses at scales between
\rho and one are those of (F4).
Dividing them by
\upsilon_{\zeta} bounds the number of points of P in the
corresponding squares. This is the input to the rectangular bound of
Section 19, while
(17.16) is the input to the Furstenberg bound of
Section 18.
18 Counting the bisector directions
The circle argument produces, at each pin of P^{\prime}, rows in which every
pair has a rich bisector. We now choose one pair from every row so
that the chosen directions are spread at every scale between
\rho and one. The restricted-sum estimate controls the number of
distinct directions that any set of rows can produce, and a
max-flow argument turns this into a simultaneous choice. The
Furstenberg estimate of Ren and Wang then provides a lower bound for
the number of distinct tubes. All results in this section are finite
and make no information-theoretic assumptions.
18.1 The restricted-sum estimate
For an n\times s real matrix A=(a_{ij}) let
S(A)=\{a_{ij}+a_{ik}:i,j,k\in\mathbb{N},\ 1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j<k\leq s\} be the set of
pairwise sums of entries from a common row, and let f_{s}(n) be the
minimum of |S(A)| over all such matrices whose sn entries are
pairwise distinct. The defining requirement in [16, §1]
is “all its sn entries being pairwise distinct.” In
this statement n is the number of rows.
Theorem 18.1 (Katz–Tardos, [16, Corollary 5]).
For any \epsilon>0 there exists an s>0 such that for all n>0 we
have f_{s}(n)\geq n^{\frac{10-3\mathrm{e}}{24-7\mathrm{e}}-\epsilon}.
Write
\alpha_{*}=\frac{10-3\mathrm{e}}{24-7\mathrm{e}}=0.371107\ldots.
Distinctness is required across the whole matrix. Katz and Tardos
point out that the bounds of [35] hold whenever no two rows
share more than one entry, whereas their argument uses that all entries
are distinct [16, §4]. The explicit cases stated after
[16, Theorem 4] are
f_{5}(n)\geq n^{\frac{7}{19}},\qquad f_{7}(n)\geq n^{\frac{33}{89}},\qquad f_{9}(n)\geq n^{\frac{59}{159}}.
Fix \frac{1}{3}<\alpha<\alpha_{*} and choose an integer s\geq 3 such
that f_{s}(n)\geq c_{\alpha}n^{\alpha} for all n.
Katz–Tardos supply
one, and s=5 permits \alpha=\frac{7}{19}, s=9 permits
\alpha=\frac{59}{159}. All constants below can depend on \alpha,s
and fixed geometric bounds, but not on \delta.
18.2 A simultaneous choice of one direction per row
At each retained pin discard surplus rows so that exactly
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime} rows remain. All upper bounds and pair-witness
lower bounds persist, and the row count decreases by at most a
factor two. Fix a pin x\in P^{\prime} and these rows of
Proposition 17.6.
Recall a\leq c\leq a+\xi,
\zeta=1-c, \rho\asymp\delta^{\zeta}, w\asymp\delta^{a}, and
2c<1. The direction of the bisector of a pair is determined, up to
O(\rho) and a fixed rotation, by the half-sum of the two source
angles. A direction bin is an interval of length \rho in the
angular chart. The bins form a fixed partition of that chart.
When defining a measure on the outputs, we identify each bin with
its midpoint.
The output of a pair is the bin containing its half-sum.
This description also explains the connection with the near-additive-energy
estimate of [24, Lemma 19], which counts pairs of sums
that differ by at most a prescribed error. Such an estimate for the
angular rows could control clustering of their directions. The
lacunarity used in that lemma is unavailable here. Moreover, the
selection below requires bounds for every subfamily of rows and
at each relevant scale. A replacement would have to provide that
uniform control while retaining the number of rows needed by the
incidence argument.
Lemma 18.2 (A simultaneous choice of directions).
Fix small \xi_{\mathrm{cut}}>0 and let s_{1} be the
\mathfrak{c}-color Ramsey number of the complete graph on s
vertices, where \mathfrak{c}=\lceil\frac{1}{\xi_{\mathrm{cut}}}\rceil+4.
This is the least integer such that every coloring of the pairs
of that many vertices with \mathfrak{c} colors has s vertices
all of whose pairs have one color.
Ramsey’s theorem [27] makes it finite. Then one can choose one pair from every row at x so that the
probability measure \sigma_{x} giving mass \frac{1}{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}} to
the output of each chosen pair satisfies
\sigma_{x}(J)\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}\begin{cases}|J|,&w\leq|J|\leq 1,\\
\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}}|J|^{\alpha}w^{1-\alpha},&\rho\leq|J|\leq w,\end{cases}\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}=\alpha\max\{\xi,\xi_{\mathrm{cut}}\},
(18.1)
for every angular interval J. Consequently, with
s_{\mathrm{act}}=\alpha+(1-\alpha)\frac{a}{\zeta}<1,
(18.2)
one has \sigma_{x}(J)\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}}|J|^{s_{\mathrm{act}}}
for \rho\leq|J|\leq 1.
Proof.
Work in one window, containing R\leq\frac{Cw}{b} rows. Unwrap the
angular chart, subtract the fixed rotation, and double the
directions, so that each pair (i,j) of a row with angles
\vartheta_{i},\vartheta_{j} has a real output o_{ij} with
|o_{ij}-(\vartheta_{i}+\vartheta_{j})|\leq C\rho.
Rounding the outputs to
a mesh comparable to \rho changes this by a constant. All outputs
lie in an interval of length O(w). Choose a dyadic root interval of
comparable length, using one of three translated grids, and the
finite dyadic tree below it, whose nodes are intervals and
whose children are their two halves. Its leaves are the terminal
intervals, of length comparable to \rho.
The root intervals of different windows have bounded overlap.
For a tree interval I prescribe the capacity
c(I)=C_{0}\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}}|I|^{\alpha}w^{1-\alpha}b^{-1}.
We first verify the cut inequality needed for the flow. We claim that if U is a set of rows and
\mathcal{A} is an antichain, a collection of tree intervals none
containing another, covering every output
of every row of U, then
\sum_{I\in\mathcal{A}}c(I)\geq\#U, provided C_{0} is large. Put
r_{0}=\frac{\ell_{j}}{100}, so that entries of one row are separated
by at least 100r_{0}. If \mathcal{A} contains an interval of length
at least r_{0}, its capacity is at least
c_{1}C_{0}\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}}\delta^{\alpha(c-a)}\frac{w}{b}\geq c_{1}C_{0}\frac{w}{b}\geq R\geq\#U,
with an absolute constant c_{1}>0, because c-a\leq\xi and \tau_{\mathrm{dir}}\geq\alpha\xi. Otherwise
every interval of \mathcal{A} is shorter than r_{0}. Put
r_{i}=r_{0}\delta^{i\xi_{\mathrm{cut}}} and sort the intervals of
\mathcal{A} into bins according to the i with
|I|\in(r_{i+1},r_{i}].
Since the leaves have length comparable to
