\rho=\delta^{\zeta}, r_{0}\asymp\delta^{c}, and \zeta-c<1, at
most \mathfrak{c} bins occur. Color every pair of every row of U
by the bin of the interval of \mathcal{A} containing its output.
By Ramsey’s theorem each row contains s entries all of whose pairs
have one color, and one color is used by a subfamily U^{\prime} of at
least \frac{\#U}{\mathfrak{c}} rows.
Let r=r_{i} be its scale.
Round the entries of these s-subrows to multiples of r.
Entries
within a subrow remain distinct because r\leq r_{0}. Every rounded
value belongs to at most \frac{Cr}{b} subrows, by item 2 of
Proposition 17.5, so the conflict graph on
U^{\prime}, joining two subrows when they share a rounded entry, has an
independent set of at least \frac{c\#U^{\prime}b}{r} subrows
with globally distinct rounded entries, or a single subrow if this
number is below one. Katz–Tardos give at least
c(\frac{\#Ub}{r})^{\alpha} distinct pair sums of rounded entries
in this independent set. A rounded pair sum differs by O(r)
from its original output, since every bin contains a tree interval
of length comparable to or greater than \rho, so \rho\lesssim r.
Thus only
O(1) distinct rounded sums can correspond to any one output
bin at scale r. There are consequently at least a fixed
multiple of this many occupied output bins. An interval of the
color has length at most r and meets O(1) rounded outputs, so
at least c(\frac{\#Ub}{r})^{\alpha} intervals of \mathcal{A} carry
this color, each of length greater than r\delta^{\xi_{\mathrm{cut}}}.
Their capacities sum to at least
cC_{0}\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}+\alpha\xi_{\mathrm{cut}}}(\#U)^{\alpha}\Bigl(\frac{w}{b}\Bigr)^{1-\alpha}\geq cC_{0}\#U,
by \#U\leq\frac{Cw}{b} and \tau_{\mathrm{dir}}\geq\alpha\xi_{\mathrm{cut}}.
This proves the claim.
A flow on a directed network assigns a nonnegative amount to each
arc, no larger than its capacity, and conserves total inflow and
outflow except at the source and sink. Its value is the net flow
out of the source. A cut separates the source from the sink.
Its
capacity is the sum of capacities of arcs leaving the source side.
The original max-flow min-cut theorem appears in [9].
For the directed networks with integer capacities used here, start
with zero flow. Each arc has a forward residual arc of capacity
equal to its unused capacity and a reverse residual arc of capacity
equal to its flow. Retain only arcs of positive residual capacity.
Augment along a residual source–sink path by
its minimum residual capacity, increasing flow on forward arcs and
decreasing it on reverse arcs. Flows remain integral, and every
augmentation increases the value by at least one. The value is
bounded by the total capacity leaving the source, so the process
terminates. When no residual path remains, the vertices reachable
from the source determine a cut whose outgoing arcs are saturated
and whose incoming arcs carry zero flow. The flow value equals
this cut capacity, and every flow value is at most any cut capacity.
This proves maximality and integrality.
Build a network with a source joined to every row
by an arc of capacity one, every row joined to each of its output
leaves by an arc of capacity R, every tree interval I joined to
its parent by an arc of capacity \lceil c(I)\rceil, and the root
joined to the sink by an arc of capacity R. Every cut of capacity
less than R leaves some rows U joined to the source and must
separate all their leaves from the root by tree arcs.
The highest
such arcs on the leaf–root paths form a covering antichain, so the
cut has capacity at least R-\#U+\sum_{\mathcal{A}}\lceil c(I)\rceil\geq R.
By the integral-flow argument above there is an
integral flow of value R, that is, one output per row, with at
most \lceil c(I)\rceil chosen outputs in each tree interval I.
Since w\gtrsim\rho\gg b, every c(I) is at least a fixed
constant, so the ceilings change only constants, and an arbitrary
interval is covered by O(1) tree intervals of comparable length.
Dividing by \mathfrak{d}^{\prime}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}b^{-1} proves the
second line of (18.1), after accounting for the
bounded overlap of the windows. For |J|\geq w, every row with
chosen output in J has its entries in a fixed enlargement of J,
so there are O(\frac{|J|}{b}) such rows, which proves the first
line.
To obtain the stated exponent, for \rho\leq|J|\leq w write |J|=\delta^{u}
with a\leq u\leq\zeta.
Then
\alpha u+(1-\alpha)a-s_{\mathrm{act}}u=(1-\alpha)a(1-\frac{u}{\zeta})\geq 0,
so |J|^{\alpha}w^{1-\alpha}\leq|J|^{s_{\mathrm{act}}}.
For |J|\geq w,
|J|\leq|J|^{s_{\mathrm{act}}}.
∎
18.3 The Furstenberg estimate and the tube family
We use the discretized Furstenberg estimate of Ren and Wang.
For a dyadic square Q of side \rho in slope–intercept parameter
space, the corresponding dyadic \rho-tube is
\bigcup_{(a,b)\in Q}\{(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:v=au+b\},
restricted to the fixed bounded spatial region under consideration.
Its central line has the parameters of the midpoint of Q.
We work in bounded-slope charts. In the Ren–Wang chart the slopes
lie in [-1,1]. These tubes have width comparable to \rho on
the bounded regions used here [28, Definition 2.3].
For dyadic \rho, a finite family \mathcal{F} of dyadic
\rho-squares or dyadic \rho-tubes is a (\rho,s,C)-set if
\#\{\text{members of }\mathcal{F}\text{ meeting }B\}\leq Cr^{s}\#\mathcal{F} for every ball B of radius
r\in[\rho,1] in the respective parameter space
[28, Definitions 1.3 and 2.3].
For tubes, this count is taken over their defining squares in
slope–intercept parameter space. A pair (\mathcal{P},\mathcal{T}) is
a (\rho,s,C,M)-nice configuration if every p\in\mathcal{P} is
assigned a (\rho,s,C)-set \mathcal{T}(p)\subset\mathcal{T} with
\#\mathcal{T}(p)\sim M and T\cap p\neq\varnothing for all
T\in\mathcal{T}(p) [28, Definition 3.1].
Theorem 18.3 (Ren–Wang, [28, Theorem 4.1]).
Let s\in(0,1], t\in(0,2], and \epsilon>0. There is
\eta_{\mathrm{RW}}>0 such that, for all sufficiently small \rho,
every (\rho,s,\rho^{-\eta_{\mathrm{RW}}},M)-nice configuration
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{T}) in which \mathcal{P} is a
(\rho,t,\rho^{-\eta_{\mathrm{RW}}})-set satisfies
\#\mathcal{T}\gtrsim_{\epsilon}\rho^{-\min\{t,\frac{s+t}{2},1\}+\epsilon}M.
We apply this with t=1 in the proof of
Theorem 1.1 and with t=1+\varkappa in the
proof of Theorem 1.7.
For t=1 the
exponent is \frac{1+s}{2}.
Proposition 18.4 (The tube family).
Fix \vartheta>0 and let s^{\prime} be a point of a fixed finite grid in
(0,1) with \vartheta\leq s_{\mathrm{act}}-s^{\prime}\leq 2\vartheta.
Suppose
\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}<\frac{\vartheta\zeta}{2}. For all sufficiently
large n there exist a family
\mathcal{T} of \rho^{\prime}-tubes, \rho^{\prime}=2^{j_{0}}\rho with j_{0} fixed,
and subfamilies \mathcal{T}(x), x\in P^{\prime\prime}, for a subset
P^{\prime\prime}\subset P^{\prime} with \#P^{\prime\prime}\geq c\#P^{\prime}, such that (P^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{T})
is a (\rho^{\prime},s^{\prime},C_{n}\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}},M)-nice
configuration with M=\lceil\rho^{-s^{\prime}}\rceil and
C_{n}\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}1, after the
identification of P^{\prime\prime} with the set of dyadic \rho^{\prime}-squares
containing its points.
Every tube of \mathcal{T} contains a fixed
C\rho-neighborhood of the bisector of one of the chosen pairs at
one of its pins, and hence at least k points of P, where
k\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\frac{k_{f}\upsilon_{f}}{\upsilon_{\zeta}};
(18.3)
and \mathcal{T} is contained in a parameter-separated family of
width-C\rho tubes of the same cardinality up to a constant.
Proof.
In this proof C_{n}\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}1 denotes a common
upper bound for the polynomial factors in Lemma 18.2.
These factors cannot be treated as fixed constants. At each
x\in P^{\prime} consider the measure M\sigma_{x}, of total mass M. By
(18.1), a single direction bin then carries mass at
most C_{n}\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}}\rho^{s_{\mathrm{act}}-s^{\prime}}<1, and an
interval of length r\geq\rho carries mass at most
C_{n}\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}}(\frac{r}{\rho})^{s^{\prime}}, since
r^{s_{\mathrm{act}}}\leq r^{s^{\prime}}. On the dyadic tree of direction bins,
join a source to every leaf by an arc of capacity one, every nonroot
tree interval I to its parent by an arc of capacity
\lceil M\sigma_{x}(I)\rceil, and the root to a sink by an arc of
capacity M. The flow sending M\sigma_{x}(\text{leaf})\leq 1 through
every leaf is feasible and has value M. By integrality there is an
integral flow of value M. It selects exactly M supported distinct
direction bins, and every tree interval I contains at most
\lceil M\sigma_{x}(I)\rceil of them. An interval of length r\geq\rho
meets at most two tree intervals of length less than 2r, so it
contains at most 6C_{n}\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}}(\frac{r}{\rho})^{s^{\prime}}
of them. For each selected bin keep one chosen pair with output in
it and the line through x in the bisector direction of that pair.
It lies within C\rho of the bisector and of x.
The leaf bound is strictly smaller than one for large n:
the positive power margin
\zeta(s_{\mathrm{act}}-s^{\prime})-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}>\frac{\vartheta\zeta}{2} absorbs C_{n}.
Restrict to one of finitely many angular charts, in which direction
and slope are bi-Lipschitz.
At each pin one chart carries a fixed
fraction of the selected bins, and one chart is chosen by a fixed
fraction of the pins. Write lines as y=ax+b in a fixed bounded
region. For a sufficiently large fixed j_{0}, in one of finitely
many translated dyadic grids in the (a,b)-plane, the dyadic
\rho^{\prime}-square containing the parameter of a selected line contains
a C\rho-neighborhood of it, so the corresponding dyadic tube
contains the original width-C\rho tube within the region: keep the
slope and vary the intercept over the error interval. The tube
therefore contains the pin, the bisector neighborhood, and all
witnesses. Choose a translation for each selected line for which this
containment holds. At each pin retain a translation carrying a
fixed fraction of its lines, then retain a fixed fraction of pins
with the same translation. Translating the slope and intercept
grid amounts to one fixed shear and translation in the plane,
which preserve the required incidence and spacing bounds up to
constants.
A dyadic \rho^{\prime}-square in the parameter space absorbs O(1)
selected bins at a fixed pin, so the selected tubes at each pin
still number \sim M and satisfy the (\rho^{\prime},s^{\prime},C_{n}\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}})
condition with a new constant. A dyadic \rho^{\prime}-square of the plane
contains O(1) points of P^{\prime}.
Keep one of them per square. This
is the nice configuration.
(18.3) is
(17.14). Finally, associate to each selected dyadic parameter square
its central line and a tube of sufficiently large fixed width
C\rho. In the bounded region this tube contains the entire
dyadic tube, hence all its witnesses. Distinct parameter squares
have separated centers. A bounded coloring accommodates any
additional fixed dilation. This constructs a family of comparable
cardinality while preserving the richness of every member.
∎
The lower bound for \#\mathcal{T} now follows from
Theorem 18.3 with \mathcal{P}=P^{\prime\prime}, which is a
(\rho^{\prime},1,\delta^{-o(1)})-set by (17.16), and
s=s^{\prime}:
\#\mathcal{T}\gtrsim\rho^{-\frac{1+s^{\prime}}{2}+\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}}M\geq\rho^{-\frac{1+3s^{\prime}}{2}+\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}}\geq\delta^{-\frac{\zeta+3s_{\mathrm{act}}\zeta}{2}+3\vartheta+\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}},
(18.4)
where \epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}>0 is fixed and the enlargement
\rho^{\prime}=2^{j_{0}}\rho and the constants \delta^{-o(1)} change no
exponent. The tolerance \eta_{\mathrm{RW}} depends on s^{\prime} and
\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}.
Since s^{\prime} ranges
over a fixed finite grid, the minimum of these tolerances is a fixed
positive number, and the losses \delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}} and
\delta^{-o(1)} are below \rho^{-\eta_{\mathrm{RW}}} once
\tau_{\mathrm{dir}} is small and \delta is small.
19 Rectangular sampling and rich incidences
The upper bound for the rich tubes comes from an incidence theorem
of Fu, Gan and Ren for balls that are spread on a rectangular grid.
In this section a tube is a fixed-length neighborhood of width
C\rho of a line in a bounded region, and separation refers to
the line parameters of Section 2 in a fixed
finite collection of charts.
For \rho>0, a family of \rho-balls, or of \rho-tubes, is
essentially distinct if the intersection of two members has at most
half the area of either member.
T_{k}(\mathcal{B}) denotes the set of
essentially distinct \rho-tubes meeting more than k balls of
\mathcal{B}.
Theorem 19.1 (Fu–Gan–Ren, [10, Theorem 3]).
Let 1\leq U\leq V\leq\rho^{-1}, and divide [0,1]^{2} into
U^{-1}\times V^{-1} rectangles. Let \mathcal{B} be a family of
\rho-balls with at most one ball in each rectangle. For every
\sigma>0 and every k>\max\{\rho^{1-2\sigma}UV,1\},
\#T_{k}(\mathcal{B})\lesssim_{\sigma}\rho^{-\sigma}\#\mathcal{B}UVk^{-2}\bigl(k^{-1}+U^{-1}\bigr).
(19.1)
A parameter-separated family splits
into a bounded number of essentially distinct families, since overlap
exceeding half the area forces parameter distance O(\rho), and
separation bounds the degree of this conflict graph. The same holds
for balls about a separated set. These bounded colorings, fixed
dilations, and fixed similarities affect only constants, and allow
(19.1) to be used for the separated pins and tubes of
the circle construction.
Lemma 19.2 (Rectangular sampling).
Let X be a \rho-separated set of N points in a fixed bounded
region. Suppose \rho\leq c_{0}h\leq c_{0}R\leq c_{0}, with c_{0}>0
sufficiently small, and
\sum_{x^{\prime}\in X,|x-x^{\prime}|\leq 3R}\min\left\{1,\frac{h}{|x-x^{\prime}|}\right\}\leq H\quad\text{for }x\in X,
(19.2)
where the diagonal term is one. Fix 0<\sigma<\frac{1}{4}, and let
\mathcal{T} be a parameter-separated family of \rho-tubes, each
containing at least k points of X. If
k\geq CH\max\left\{\frac{\rho^{1-2\sigma}}{Rh},1\right\}
(19.3)
for a sufficiently large fixed C, then
\displaystyle\#\mathcal{T}
\displaystyle\lesssim_{\sigma}\frac{\rho^{-\sigma}N}{Rh}\left(H^{2}k^{-3}+RHk^{-2}\right),
(19.4)
\displaystyle I(X,\mathcal{T})
\displaystyle\lesssim_{\sigma}\frac{\rho^{-\sigma}N}{Rh}\left(H^{2}k^{-2}+RHk^{-1}\right),
(19.5)
where I(X,\mathcal{T}) is the number of pairs (x,T) with
x\in X\cap T.
Proof.
It suffices to prove (19.4) for a family whose
members contain between k and 2k points: the classes with
richness in [2^{j}k,2^{j+1}k) satisfy the hypothesis at every
larger richness, summing their bounds proves
(19.4), and multiplying each bound by 2^{j+1}k
before summing yields (19.5). The grid and the
sample below may depend on the class.
Round R and h to comparable dyadic lengths. Rotate the
R\times h rectangular grid uniformly and translate it uniformly
modulo one cell. Two points x,x^{\prime} lie in one cell only if
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq 3R and their displacement perpendicular to the long
direction is at most h, which has probability
O(\min\{1,\frac{h}{|x-x^{\prime}|}\}). By (19.2)
the expected occupancy of the cell containing a given point is at
most CH. Delete every point within C_{1}\rho of a grid boundary.
This has probability O(\frac{\rho}{R}+\frac{\rho}{h})\leq\frac{1}{64}
after reducing c_{0}. Delete also all points in cells of occupancy
greater than L=\lceil C_{2}H\rceil.
Markov’s inequality bounds this
probability by \frac{1}{64} for C_{2} large. The expected number
of deleted incidences is at most
\frac{1}{32}I(X,\mathcal{T})\leq\frac{1}{16}k\#\mathcal{T}. Fix a grid
attaining this bound. Fewer than \frac{1}{8}\#\mathcal{T} tubes lose
more than \frac{k}{2} points, so a subfamily \mathcal{T}_{0} with
\#\mathcal{T}_{0}\geq\frac{7}{8}\#\mathcal{T} retains at least
\frac{k}{2} points per tube, and the \rho-balls about the
retained points lie inside their cells.
Label the retained points of each cell by distinct slots in
\{1,\ldots,L\}. Independently in every cell select a uniform
slot and keep the point occupying it, if any. The sample X^{\prime} has
at most one point per cell and \mathbb{E}\#X^{\prime}\leq\frac{N}{L}. For a
tube T\in\mathcal{T}_{0}, its number of sampled points is a sum of
independent indicators, one per cell, with mean m_{T}\geq\frac{k}{2L}
and variance at most m_{T}.
Hence
\mathbb{P}\left\{\#(X^{\prime}\cap T)<\frac{k}{4L}\right\}\leq\frac{4}{m_{T}}\leq\frac{8L}{k}\leq\frac{1}{16},
by (19.3). Also
\mathbb{P}\{\#X^{\prime}>\frac{16N}{L}\}\leq\frac{1}{16}. By the union
bound, the expected number of tubes of \mathcal{T}_{0} with at least
\frac{k}{4L} sampled points on a sample with
\#X^{\prime}\leq\frac{16N}{L} is at least
\frac{7}{8}\#\mathcal{T}_{0}>\frac{1}{2}\#\mathcal{T}. Fix such a
sample.
In coordinates parallel to the grid, a grid-aligned square of a
fixed dyadic side contains the configuration, and a fixed
similarity sends it to [0,1]^{2}.
Restriction to finitely many
charts and a bounded coloring preserve a fixed fraction of the
relevant incidence counts. Thus (19.1) applies to the
balls about X^{\prime} with U\asymp R^{-1}, V\asymp h^{-1}, and
threshold \frac{k}{8L}. In the rescaled coordinates the balls are
contained in their U^{-1}\times V^{-1} rectangles, with at most
one ball per rectangle and 1\leq U\leq V\leq\rho^{-1}, where \rho
is also rescaled by the same fixed similarity. The last inequality
uses \rho\leq c_{0}h. Its hypothesis follows from
(19.3). With \#X^{\prime}\lesssim\frac{N}{L} and
L\asymp H\geq 1 it gives
\#\mathcal{T}\lesssim_{\sigma}\frac{\rho^{-\sigma}N}{Rh}\left(H^{2}k^{-3}+RHk^{-2}\right),
which is (19.4) for one richness class.
∎
20 From measures to finite configurations
We now return to the measures and construct the finite sets needed
by the incidence theorem. The main requirement is that all pins
see vertices from one common source set. Separate discretizations
for separate pins would not give a graph on which the crossing
argument could be used.
We keep the original measures and the original oracle throughout.
The pins are actual points, with their full oracles available.
Source vertices are centers of small dyadic squares, so their
incidences with circles have error O(\delta). An assigned incidence
records a relation retained by the selection.
A circle merely
passing close to a source vertex need not be assigned to it.
Lemma 20.1 (Measure-to-finite selection with occupied-cell bounds).
Let \mu,\nu be probability measures on fixed bounded source and pin
regions at positive distance from one another. Let G be a Borel
relation, and suppose that for every relevant pin x the angular
pushforward of \mu|_{G_{x}} is bounded by L times angular Lebesgue
measure. Fix dyadic scales \delta\leq\rho\leq\eta and a Borel
subrelation R\subset G of pair mass P>0.
Assume the following bounds whenever the indicated cells or bins
are occupied by R with positive source-fiber mass.
1.
Every occupied source \delta-square Q satisfies
m_{-}\leq\mu(Q)\leq m_{+}.
2.
Every occupied pin \rho-square S satisfies
v_{-}\leq\nu(S)\leq v_{+}.
For every dyadic scale r=2^{-k}\geq\rho, each ancestor containing
a relevant pin satisfies \nu(Q_{k}(x))\leq U_{k}.
3.
For every source \eta-square C and terminal dyadic radial
interval J of length \delta which contains a pair from R_{x},
\mu(R_{x}\cap C\cap\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|x-y|\in J\})\leq b.
4.
At each pin, at most M terminal radial intervals are occupied.
Then there are a \rho-separated finite set X of actual pins, a
common set V of source \delta-square centers, and assigned
incidences (x,v,J), with circles centered at x and radii equal
to the midpoints of J, such that, writing N=\#X, I for the
number of assigned incidences, and \mathcal{C} for the circle family,
\displaystyle\#V
\displaystyle\leq m_{-}^{-1},
\displaystyle N
\displaystyle\leq v_{-}^{-1},
\displaystyle I
\displaystyle\geq\frac{cP}{m_{+}v_{+}},
\displaystyle N
\displaystyle\geq\frac{I}{C\#V},
(20.1)
\displaystyle\#\mathcal{C}
\displaystyle\leq NM,
\displaystyle\bigl||x-v|-\operatorname{mid}(J)\bigr|
\displaystyle\leq C\delta.
(20.2)
Each source vertex has at most C assigned radial labels at one pin.
At every selected pin, assigned source vertices in an angular
interval A obey
\#\{v\in V:\arg(v-x)\in A,v\text{ assigned at }x\}\leq\frac{CL(|A|+\delta)}{Pm_{-}}.
(20.3)
For every source \eta-parent and every assigned radial bin, the
number of assigned vertices in that parent is at most
\frac{Cb}{Pm_{-}}.
(20.4)
The same conclusion holds, with another constant, for vertices in
any annulus of width C_{0}\delta about the pin. Finally,
\#(X\cap B(z,2^{-k}))\leq\frac{CU_{k}}{v_{-}}\quad\text{for }2^{-k}\geq\rho.
(20.5)
The constants depend only on the fixed geometric bounds, including
the source–pin separation and C_{0}.
Proof.
Write
\lambda_{x,j}(Q)=\mu\{y\in R_{x}\cap Q:|x-y|\in J_{j}\}.
One source \delta-square meets at most a fixed number B_{0} of
terminal radial bins about any pin, since distance is 1-Lipschitz.
Choose \tau=\frac{P}{4B_{0}} and retain a cell and radial-bin assignment only when
\lambda_{x,j}(Q)\geq\tau\mu(Q)>0.
The total discarded source mass at each pin is at most
B_{0}\tau\sum_{Q}\mu(Q)=B_{0}\tau. Hence the retained relation has
pair mass at least \frac{P}{2}.
Let F(x) be the number of retained cell and radial-bin assignments at x.
Each assignment has mass at most m_{+}, so
\int F(x)d\nu(x)\geq\frac{P}{2m_{+}}.
For each pin \rho-square S with positive integral, choose an
actual relevant pin x_{S}\in S such that
F(x_{S})\geq\frac{1}{2\nu(S)}\int_{S}Fd\nu.
Such a choice exists because the integral is positive. The pin
has a positive retained fiber in the already uniformized relation:
its assigned cells and bins satisfy the cell-mass and parent
bounds, its occupied ancestors satisfy the pin-mass bounds, and
the angular bound holds on the containing fiber G_{x_{S}}.
These properties refer to the same original measures \mu,\nu,
which are not replaced when x_{S} is selected. Its occupied square has
mass between v_{-} and v_{+}. Therefore
\sum_{S}F(x_{S})\geq\frac{P}{4m_{+}v_{+}},\qquad\#\{S\}\leq v_{-}^{-1}.
Colour the \rho-grid by its two coordinate indices modulo three.
One of the nine colours carries at least one ninth of the displayed
incidence sum. Keep that colour. The selected actual pins are
\rho-separated. Take as V the centers of all source squares
used by their retained assignments. Disjointness and the lower
source-cell mass give \#V\leq m_{-}^{-1}. There are at most B_{0}
assignments per source vertex and pin, proving the last bound in
(20.1). The radial count and approximate
circle incidence follow directly from the construction.
For the angular bound, every good point in a source square whose
center has direction in A has direction in the C\delta-enlargement
of A. This uses the fixed positive source–pin separation.
The active cell and radial-bin sets are disjoint and each has mass at least
\tau m_{-}. Their total mass is at most CL(|A|+\delta) by the
original angular bound on \mu|_{G_{x}}. Division proves
(20.3).
For one parent and bin, sum the disjoint active masses and use the
restricted parent and radial-bin bound b. Division by \tau m_{-} proves
(20.4).
An annulus of width C_{0}\delta for the source centers can correspond
to only a bounded number of their actual radial bins, so the same
bound applies after changing the constant.
For (20.5), a ball of radius 2^{-k}
meets only a bounded number of dyadic squares of that side length.
Keep only those squares containing selected pins. Each such square
has full \nu-mass at most U_{k}, witnessed by one of its selected
relevant pins. Its selected \rho-squares are disjoint, nested
inside it, and each has mass at least v_{-}. This proves the count.
No global mass bound on unoccupied ancestors is required.
∎
A common template for the plateau and the source parent
Let \Omega be the compact set of
\theta=(u_{-},W,\varsigma,\chi,\mathfrak{e}) satisfying the constraints
of (8.7) that do not involve f:
\displaystyle-1\leq\chi\leq 2,\qquad\varsigma\geq\max\{0,-2\chi\},\qquad 3\varsigma+2\chi\leq 4,\qquad 0\leq\mathfrak{e}\leq\varsigma,
\displaystyle W=W_{0}-(\varsigma+\chi-\mathfrak{e})\omega,\qquad 0\leq u_{-}-W\leq(2\varsigma-2\mathfrak{e})\omega.
Put u_{+}=u_{-}+a_{c}+\chi\omega, define the tent
T_{\theta}(u)=\max\{0,W-\operatorname{dist}(u,[u_{-},u_{+}])\}\quad\text{for }0\leq u\leq 1,
and let u_{0},\varpi,e_{0} be given by (8.12).
All of
these depend continuously on \theta. A template is a point of
\Omega together with a branch index: branch 1 requires
\mathfrak{e}\leq\frac{1}{8} and branch 2 requires
\mathfrak{e}\geq\frac{1}{8}. By Proposition 8.5,
Lemma 8.6, and the 1-Lipschitz property of
g(u)-u\geq 0, every limiting profile of a pair with the certificate
at level \lambda and d_{x}(1)\leq q satisfies
g(u)\geq u+T_{\theta}(u)\quad\text{for }0\leq u\leq 1,\qquad a_{*}\leq a_{c}+\varpi\omega,
for some \theta\in\Omega, and if \mathfrak{e}\leq\frac{1}{8} also
the envelope (8.14). The next lemma replaces
these limiting statements by finitely many tests at one precision.
Lemma 20.2 (Finite selection of a common template).
Fix \tau_{0}>0 and 0<\xi_{\mathrm{par}}\leq\frac{\tau_{0}}{4}. There
is a finite set \Theta of templates, independent of the pair, with
the following property. Let (x,y) satisfy the profile hypotheses
relative to the oracle A, and suppose that every limiting profile
of the pair along precisions n with K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\leq qn has
the certificate (8.2) at level \lambda. Then all
sufficiently large n with K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\leq qn admit a template
\theta\in\Theta such that, writing
a_{\mathrm{par}}=a_{c}+\varpi\omega+\xi_{\mathrm{par}} and
C_{n}=Q_{\lfloor a_{\mathrm{par}}n\rfloor}(y),
\displaystyle K_{n}^{A}(y)
\displaystyle\geq(\lambda-\tau_{0})n,
(20.6)
\displaystyle\frac{K_{k}^{A}(x)}{n}
\displaystyle\geq\frac{k}{n}+T_{\theta}\left(\frac{k}{n}\right)-\tau_{0}\quad\text{for }0\leq k\leq n,
(20.7)
\displaystyle K_{\lfloor a_{\mathrm{par}}n\rfloor}^{A}(y)
\displaystyle\geq(F-\tau_{0})n,
(20.8)
\displaystyle K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|\mid C_{n})
\displaystyle\geq\lambda n-K_{\lfloor a_{\mathrm{par}}n\rfloor}^{A}(y)-\tau_{0}n,
(20.9)
and, if \theta has branch 1,
\frac{K_{k}^{A}(y)}{n}\geq\frac{k}{n}+a_{c}-(1+u_{0}+\mathfrak{e})\omega-\tau_{0}\quad\text{for }a_{c}-\omega\leq\frac{k}{n}\leq a_{\mathrm{par}}.
(20.10)
On any fixed positive-measure Borel relation of such pairs, the
threshold and the coding errors of Lemma 2.2
can be made uniform on a fixed positive-measure subrelation, and at
each such n one common template is valid on at least
\frac{1}{\#\Theta} of the low-distance pair mass.
Proof.
The tents, the number \varpi, and the coefficient
(1+u_{0}+\mathfrak{e})\omega are continuous on the compact branches
\Omega\cap\{\mathfrak{e}\leq\frac{1}{8}\} and
\Omega\cap\{\mathfrak{e}\geq\frac{1}{8}\}. Choose a finite net in
each branch such that every point of the branch has a net point with
tent and coefficient within \frac{\tau_{0}}{3} and with
\varpi\omega within \frac{\xi_{\mathrm{par}}}{3}.
Let \Theta be
the union of the two nets.
Suppose a fixed pair failed all templates at infinitely many
low-distance precisions. Extract uniformly convergent source, pin,
and distance profiles along them, as in
Lemma 2.1. The limiting profiles satisfy the
prefix and transfer estimates, d_{x}(1)\leq q, and the certificate.
So they have the plateau, the bound a_{*}\leq a_{c}+\varpi_{\theta_{0}}\omega,
and, if \mathfrak{e}_{\theta_{0}}\leq\frac{1}{8}, the envelope
(8.14), for some \theta_{0}\in\Omega. Let
\theta be a net point of the branch containing \theta_{0} within
the prescribed errors. Its parent scale satisfies
a_{\mathrm{par}}\geq a_{c}+\varpi_{\theta_{0}}\omega+\frac{2}{3}\xi_{\mathrm{par}}>a_{*},
hence f(a_{\mathrm{par}})\geq F, and
H_{f,g}(a_{\mathrm{par}},1)\geq H_{f,g}(a_{*},1)\geq\lambda because
H_{f,g}(a,1) is nondecreasing in a. Also f(1)\geq\lambda.
Uniform convergence implies
(20.6)–(20.8) at all
large precisions of the sequence, and
Lemma 14.1, with
a=a_{\mathrm{par}} and b=1, establishes (20.9).
Indeed, from the finer square Q_{\lceil a_{\mathrm{par}}n\rceil}(y)
and one extra bit one computes C_{n}=Q_{\lfloor a_{\mathrm{par}}n\rfloor}(y).
Thus the complexity conditioned on C_{n} is at least that conditioned
on the finer square minus O(1). The source complexities at these
two precisions differ by O(\log n), absorbed by the fixed tolerance.
For a template of branch 1, the envelope (8.14)
holds on [a_{c}-\omega,a_{c}+\varpi_{\theta_{0}}\omega].
On the remaining
part of the tested interval, of length at most
\frac{4}{3}\xi_{\mathrm{par}}\leq\frac{\tau_{0}}{3}, monotonicity of f
loses at most this length. Including the net error proves
(20.10) at all large precisions of the sequence.
This contradicts the assumed failures.
The eventual conditions are Borel, so the threshold and the
pair-dependent coding constants can be bounded on a
positive-measure subrelation, losing an arbitrarily small prescribed
amount of mass. Assign each pair to its first valid template in a
fixed ordering and retain a largest class at each precision.
∎
Corollary 20.3 (The finite inputs).
In the setting of Lemma 20.4, let
\lambda,F,a_{c},W_{0},q be as in (8.1), suppose
that every limiting profile of every pair of G along
low-distance precisions has the certificate (8.2)
at level \lambda, and let the tolerances satisfy
(17.4). After one fixed restriction of G
of positive pair mass, every sufficiently large low-distance
precision n with mass P_{n} admits a template \theta\in\Theta and
masses m,\upsilon_{f},\upsilon_{1},\ldots,\upsilon_{\mathfrak{n}} such
that the subrelation of R_{n} on which \theta is valid, the source
\delta-cell mass lies in [m,2m), the pin \delta-cell mass lies
in [\upsilon_{f},2\upsilon_{f}), and the pin ancestor mass at side
\rho_{j} lies in [\upsilon_{j},2\upsilon_{j}) for every j, has mass
p\geq\frac{cP_{n}}{n^{\mathfrak{n}+2}}.
Lemma 20.1, applied to this
subrelation with \rho=\delta, m_{-}=m, m_{+}=2m, v_{-}=\upsilon_{f},
v_{+}=2\upsilon_{f}, the cap b=\delta^{\lambda-\beta},
M\leq C\delta^{-q}, and
U_{k}=\delta^{\frac{k}{n}+T_{\theta}(\frac{k}{n})-\tau_{0}}, supplies the finite
inputs (F1)–(F4) of
Section 17 with this p.
Proof.
Apply Lemma 20.2 with tolerance
\frac{\tau_{0}}{2}. Its uniformization, together with the fixed
restrictions of Lemma 20.4, produces
the relation G on which the tests hold for all n\geq N. At each
low-distance precision retain a largest template class.
The coding identities and the bound K_{k}^{A}(z)\leq 2k+O(\log k)
give cell masses at least \delta^{2+\tau_{0}} at every occupied
cell and ancestor for large n, and all masses are at most one, so
each of the \mathfrak{n}+2 dyadic mass classes has O(n) values.
One product class carries mass at least \frac{cP_{n}}{n^{\mathfrak{n}+2}}.
The tests of Lemma 20.2 and the coding
identities give the mass bounds of (F3)
and (F4): at an occupied source cell, parent, or
tested ancestor, and at an occupied pin ancestor, one retained pair
witnesses the bound for the original measure, and the coding error
O(\log n) is below \frac{\tau_{0}}{2}n. In particular
m\leq\delta^{\lambda-\tau_{0}}\leq b by
(20.6) and 3\tau_{0}\leq\beta, and the parents
have mass at most \delta^{F-\tau_{0}} by (20.8), and the ancestor bound is (20.7). The Frostman
bound in (F4) is the exponent of \nu.
The conditional test (20.9) implies the
parent–annulus cap: the coding argument in the proof of
Proposition 14.2 uses only the
conditional bound and the source coding identity, and yields
\mu\{z\in C_{n}:|x-z|\in J\}\leq\delta^{\lambda-2\tau_{0}}\leq b for every
occupied parent and terminal radius bin, as in
Corollary 14.3. At most 2^{qn+O(1)}
programs have length at most qn, and each output determines a
bounded number of terminal radial bins, so at most C\delta^{-q}
bins are occupied at each pin.
Throughout this count the entire
original pin remains available as an oracle. Every pin selected by
Lemma 20.1 belongs to the
retained subrelation and therefore inherits the template and all
mass classes. The remaining assertions are the conclusions of that
lemma.
The angular bound L is fixed and is absorbed into the
constants. The selections are finite measure-theoretic operations
and introduce no new oracle.
∎
Lemma 20.4 (Classical bad pins give one recurrent product relation).
Let K\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} be compact with \dim_{\mathrm{H}}K>1, and let
q>0. If
B_{q}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}K<q\}
has Hausdorff dimension greater than one, then there are separated
compact source and pin supports, Frostman probability measures
\mu,\nu on them whose exponents 1+\kappa<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}K and
1+\varkappa<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}B_{q} may be prescribed, with
\kappa,\varkappa>0, one
oracle A, and a positive-measure Borel relation G with all the
fixed-pair hypotheses, such that
G\subseteq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}:K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\leq qn\}.
The angular-density and fixed-threshold restrictions used above can
be imposed on G while preserving positive measure. For the resulting
relations R_{n} and masses P_{n}, one has \sum_{n}P_{n}=\infty and
P_{n}\geq n^{-2} for infinitely many n. Every limiting source
profile of a pair of G satisfies f(u)\geq(1+\kappa)u, every pin x
of a pair of G satisfies \dim^{A}x\geq 1+\varkappa, and
\nu(B(z,r))\leq Cr^{1+\varkappa} for every ball.
Proof.
The map x\mapsto\Delta_{x}K is continuous into the space of compact
sets with the Hausdorff metric. The condition that a compact set
has dimension less than q is Borel. Express it using a rational
exponent below q and, for every integer m, the existence of a
finite rational open cover with diameters and total exponent cost
less than \frac{1}{m}. Thus B_{q} is Borel.
Frostman’s lemma and compact extraction give probability measures
with the prescribed exponents on compact subsets of K and of
B_{q}. By restricting to two disjoint small balls carrying positive
mass, and then to compact positive-mass subsets, arrange separated
supports.
The exponents are unchanged. Denote the compact source
support by K^{\prime}\subset K.
Use the encoding described in Section 5 to choose
one oracle A making the fixed compact supports effectively compact
and both measures computable, including their dyadic masses. The same
oracle is used for every pin and every precision. Lemma 2.2, first with
B=(A,x) and then with the endpoints exchanged, establishes both
full-oracle independence estimates on a product-full Borel relation,
by Fubini. It also proves the base-oracle dyadic mass identities.
The Frostman bounds give both effective dimensions
greater than one.
More precisely, the source coding identity and
\mu(Q_{k}(y))\leq C2^{-(1+\kappa)k} give
K_{k}^{A}(y)\geq(1+\kappa)k-O_{y}(\log(k+2)), so every limiting source
profile satisfies f(u)\geq(1+\kappa)u. The same argument with \nu
gives K_{k}^{A}(x)\geq(1+\varkappa)k-O_{x}(\log(k+2)), hence
\dim^{A}x\geq 1+\varkappa, for \nu-almost every pin. Include this
condition in the product-full relation. Radial absolute continuity and the arclength-null
angular complexity deficiency sets give the directional hypotheses
for both choices of pin, as in Proposition 5.1.
This establishes the fixed-pair hypotheses for arbitrary computable
Frostman measures, not only for normalized Hausdorff restrictions.
For each pin in the chosen support, \Delta_{x}K^{\prime} is effectively
compact relative to (A,x). The restricted point-to-set principle
therefore implies, for every y\in K^{\prime},
\dim^{A,x}|x-y|\leq\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}K^{\prime}\leq\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}K<q.
Consequently every such pair has infinitely many scales with
K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\leq qn. This uses one common oracle A.
No family of separately chosen pin-dependent point-to-set oracles
is being encoded.
Intersect the full-measure good-pair relation with the bounded radial
density restriction and then the fixed-threshold restrictions
described above. The resulting fixed relation G has positive pair
measure, and every pair still has infinitely many low-distance scales.
Tonelli’s theorem implies
\sum_{n}P_{n}=\int_{G}\sum_{n}1_{R_{n}}d(\nu\times\mu)=\infty.
Since \sum_{n}n^{-2}<\infty, infinitely many P_{n} are at least
n^{-2}.
∎
We have obtained a common finite source set, separated actual pins,
and assigned circle incidences with the required angular and parent
bounds. The preceding circle and bisector estimates now apply to this configuration.
We complete the parameter calculation in the next section.
21 The universal pinned improvement
All parts of the pinned argument are now available. We first
record the exponents of the configuration produced by
Corollary 20.3 and the selections of
Sections 17 and 18, then
show that they are incompatible with the two tube counts. The
parameters are chosen before the set and its measures. The measures
determine how far out in the precision sequence we must go, but do
not change the threshold.
21.1 The exponents of the configuration
Fix \frac{1}{3}<\alpha<\alpha_{*} with a row length s as in
Section 18, and put
c_{\alpha}=3\alpha-1\in\left(0,\frac{1}{4}\right),\qquad r_{\alpha}=\frac{3\alpha}{40-12\alpha}<\frac{1}{24}.
(21.1)
Let 0\leq\Delta<\frac{1}{2} and 0<\omega<\frac{1-2\Delta}{24}.
Then Section 8 applies,
and \lambda,F,a_{c},W_{0},q are given by (8.1).
Let \theta be the template, a\leq c\leq a+\xi the row exponents,
\zeta=1-c, \rho\asymp\delta^{\zeta}, and
\mathfrak{h}=a_{\mathrm{par}}-a, as in
Propositions 17.5
and 17.6.
Put t=u_{+}-u_{-} and write the pin ancestor
mass at the scale \rho as \upsilon_{\zeta}=\delta^{\gamma}. Let
\tau>0 absorb the template, mass-class, coding, and polynomial
losses: we take \tau_{0}\leq\frac{\tau}{2} and
\xi,\xi_{\mathrm{par}}\leq\tau, and use that every factor
\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}} is at most \delta^{-\tau} for large n
along the recurrent precisions. Let P be the pin set of
Lemma 17.7, \mathcal{T} the tube family of
Proposition 18.4, and k the number of points of
P in each tube. For all sufficiently large recurrent n,
\displaystyle\#P
\displaystyle\leq\delta^{-\gamma},
\displaystyle\gamma
\displaystyle\geq\max\{\zeta,u_{+}+W\}-\tau,
(21.2)
\displaystyle H
\displaystyle\leq\delta^{-\iota_{H}},
\displaystyle\iota_{H}
\displaystyle=\gamma-u_{+}-W+\tau\geq 0,
(21.3)
\displaystyle k
\displaystyle\geq\delta^{-\iota_{k}},
\displaystyle\iota_{k}
\displaystyle=\gamma-\mathfrak{r}-\beta-\tau,\qquad\mathfrak{r}=\frac{1}{2}+\min\{2\omega,\psi(\mathfrak{h})\},
(21.4)
\displaystyle 1-a_{\mathrm{par}}
\displaystyle\leq\zeta\leq 1-a_{0},
(21.5)
\displaystyle\#\mathcal{T}
\displaystyle\gtrsim\delta^{-\Lambda},
\displaystyle\Lambda
\displaystyle=\frac{(1+3s_{\mathrm{act}})\zeta}{2}-3\vartheta-\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}},
(21.6)
where H is the reciprocal sum (19.2) for
P at R=\delta^{u_{-}} and h=\delta^{u_{+}}, and s_{\mathrm{act}} is
given by (18.2).
For (21.2), \#P\leq\upsilon_{\zeta}^{-1} is
(17.13). The \rho-cell of a pin has mass at least
\upsilon_{\zeta}.
It lies in a ball of radius 2\rho, so
\upsilon_{\zeta}\leq C\rho^{1+\varkappa}\leq C\delta^{\zeta} by
(F4), and it is nested in the ancestor of side
2^{-\lfloor u_{+}n\rfloor}, because
1-a_{\mathrm{par}}-u_{+}\geq W_{0}-(\varpi+\varsigma-\mathfrak{e})\omega-\xi_{\mathrm{par}}>0
by (8.10) and (8.13),
and that ancestor has mass at most \delta^{u_{+}+W-\tau_{0}-o(1)} by
(F4). For (21.3), let z\in P and
\rho\leq r\leq 1. The ball B(z,r+\sqrt{2}\rho) is covered by O(1)
dyadic squares of side comparable to r, and those containing a
point of P are ancestors of pins of X.
The \rho-cells of the counted points are disjoint, each has mass
at least \upsilon_{\zeta}=\delta^{\gamma}, and each lies in one of
these occupied ancestor squares, whose masses are at most
C\delta^{u+T_{\theta}(u)-\tau_{0}} by (F4). Summing only
their masses, we obtain
\#(P\cap B(z,r))\leq C\delta^{u+T_{\theta}(u)-\tau_{0}-\gamma} for
r=\delta^{u}, which is at most Cr\delta^{W-\tau_{0}-\gamma} for
h\leq r\leq 3R. Summing over the dyadic annuli
2^{i}h\leq|z-z^{\prime}|<2^{i+1}h, 0\leq i\leq n, and the ball B(z,h),
the reciprocal sum is at most Cnh\delta^{W-\tau_{0}-\gamma},
which is (21.3).
\iota_{H}\geq 0 by
(21.2). For (21.4),
(18.3), (17.11), and
N\upsilon_{f}\geq cp from (F1) give
k\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\frac{1}{\upsilon_{\zeta}}\max\Bigl\{\frac{\delta^{2q}}{b},b\delta^{-F+\tau_{0}+\psi(\mathfrak{h})}\Bigr\}=\delta^{-\gamma}\max\bigl\{\delta^{\frac{1}{2}+2\omega+\beta},\delta^{\frac{1}{2}+\psi(\mathfrak{h})-\beta+\tau_{0}}\bigr\},
since 2q-\lambda=\frac{1}{2}+2\omega and \lambda-F=\frac{1}{2}.
For 0<\delta<1, the map u\mapsto\delta^{u} is decreasing, so
\max\{\delta^{u_{1}},\delta^{u_{2}}\}=\delta^{\min\{u_{1},u_{2}\}}.
Thus the maximum in the richness bound corresponds to the minimum in
\mathfrak{r}. Since \tau_{0}\leq\frac{\beta}{3} by
(17.4), we have
\min\{2\omega+\beta,\psi(\mathfrak{h})-\beta+\tau_{0}\}\leq\min\{2\omega,\psi(\mathfrak{h})\}+\beta.
Consequently k\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\delta^{-\gamma+\mathfrak{r}+\beta}.
Absorbing the polynomial loss into \delta^{\tau} yields
(21.4).
Estimate (21.5) is a_{0}<c\leq a_{\mathrm{par}}, and
(21.6) is (18.4).
Proposition 21.1 (Strict exponent comparison).
The finite configuration is impossible for all sufficiently small
\delta whenever the fixed positive parameters satisfy
\displaystyle\frac{3\alpha}{4}(1-2\Delta)-(10-3\alpha)\omega
\displaystyle>3\beta+7\tau+\sigma+3\vartheta+\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}},
(21.7)
\displaystyle W_{0}-6\omega
\displaystyle>\beta+4\tau+2\sigma.
(21.8)
Both hold for every \omega<r_{\alpha}(1-2\Delta) once
\sigma,\beta,\tau,\vartheta,\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}>0 are
sufficiently small.
Proof.
We begin with the upper count. Split P into the nine classes of
\rho-cells with fixed indices modulo three. Each class is
\rho-separated, every tube of \mathcal{T} contains at least
\frac{k}{9} points of one class, and one class does so for at least
a ninth of the tubes. Apply Lemma 19.2 to
that class, with \rho, R=\delta^{u_{-}}, h=\delta^{u_{+}}, richness
\frac{k}{9}, and the bound H, which dominates the reciprocal sum
of the class.
The parameter-separated family of width-C\rho tubes
containing \mathcal{T} has cardinality \gtrsim\#\mathcal{T} by
Proposition 18.4. The two exponents of
\delta^{-1} in (19.4) are
\gamma+u_{-}+u_{+}+2\iota_{H}-3\iota_{k}=U_{1}+3\beta+5\tau,\qquad\gamma+u_{+}+\iota_{H}-2\iota_{k}=U_{2}+2\beta+3\tau,
where
U_{1}=3\mathfrak{r}-t-2W,\qquad U_{2}=2\mathfrak{r}-W;
the exponent \gamma cancels from both. Moreover
U_{1}-U_{2}=\mathfrak{r}-t-W=\min\{2\omega,\psi(\mathfrak{h})\}+(\varsigma-\mathfrak{e})\omega\geq 0
by (8.7) and a_{c}+W_{0}=\frac{1}{2}. Therefore
\#\mathcal{T}\lesssim_{\sigma}\delta^{-U_{1}-3\beta-5\tau-\sigma}.
(21.9)
The hypotheses of the lemma have positive power margins. By
(8.7), (8.10),
(8.13), (21.5), and
\mathfrak{r}\leq\frac{1}{2}+2\min\{\omega,\mathfrak{h}\},
\displaystyle\zeta-u_{+}
\displaystyle\geq W_{0}-(\varpi+\varsigma-\mathfrak{e})\omega+\mathfrak{h}-\xi_{\mathrm{par}}-\xi\geq W_{0}-4\omega-2\tau,
\displaystyle\iota_{k}-\iota_{H}
\displaystyle=u_{+}+W-\mathfrak{r}-\beta-2\tau\geq W_{0}-5\omega-\beta-2\tau,
\displaystyle\iota_{k}-\iota_{H}-(u_{-}+u_{+}-\zeta)
\displaystyle=W-u_{-}+\zeta-\mathfrak{r}-\beta-2\tau\geq W_{0}-6\omega-\beta-4\tau,
using u_{+}+W\geq t+2W, 2\varsigma+\chi\leq 3, \varpi+\varsigma\leq\frac{7}{2},
2\varsigma+\varpi\leq 5, and \mathfrak{h}-2\min\{\omega,\mathfrak{h}\}\geq-\omega.
The first implies \frac{\rho}{h}\to 0, the second
\frac{k}{H}\to\infty, and the third with
(21.8) implies
\frac{kRh}{H\rho^{1-2\sigma}}\to\infty, which is
(19.3) with room for the constants, the
factor \frac{1}{9}, and dyadic rounding.
For the lower count, by (21.6), (18.2),
\zeta\geq 1-a-\xi, and a=a_{c}+\varpi\omega+\xi_{\mathrm{par}}-\mathfrak{h},
\Lambda\geq\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}+(1-3\alpha)a-\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}\xi-3\vartheta-\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}\geq L-2\tau-3\vartheta-\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}},
where
L=\frac{3}{4}(1+\alpha)+\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3\alpha}{2}\Bigr)\Delta-c_{\alpha}\varpi\omega+c_{\alpha}\mathfrak{h}.
(21.10)
We compare the two exponents. Put \mathfrak{u}=\frac{\mathfrak{h}}{\omega}\geq 0 and
f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})=\frac{1}{\omega}\min\{2\omega,\psi(\mathfrak{h})\}, so that
f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})=\min\{2,2\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{u}+e_{0}\} in branch 1 and f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})=\min\{2,2\mathfrak{u}\} in
branch 2.
In branch 1 with a<a_{c}-\omega both expressions equal 2,
since then \mathfrak{u}>2, as noted after Proposition 17.6.
By (8.10),
U_{1}=\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\Delta}{2}+[3f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})+2\varsigma+\chi-2\mathfrak{e}]\omega,\qquad L-U_{1}=\frac{3\alpha}{4}(1-2\Delta)-C_{1}(\mathfrak{u})\omega,
with
C_{1}(\mathfrak{u})=3f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})+2\varsigma+\chi-2\mathfrak{e}+c_{\alpha}(\varpi-\mathfrak{u}).
We claim that C_{1}(\mathfrak{u})\leq 9-c_{\alpha}=10-3\alpha for all \mathfrak{u}\geq 0. The
function 3f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})-c_{\alpha}\mathfrak{u} increases until f_{0} reaches 2 and
decreases afterwards, so its maximum is at the first point \mathfrak{u}_{*} with
f_{0}(\mathfrak{u}_{*})=2, and C_{1}(\mathfrak{u})\leq 6+2\varsigma+\chi-2\mathfrak{e}+c_{\alpha}(\varpi-\mathfrak{u}_{*}).
In branch 1, \mathfrak{u}_{*}=\max\{1,2-e_{0}\}. If e_{0}\leq 1, then
\varpi-\mathfrak{u}_{*}=\varpi-2+e_{0}=u_{0}+\mathfrak{e}-1 by (8.12),
and 2\varsigma+\chi\leq 3-\frac{u_{0}}{4} yields
C_{1}(\mathfrak{u})\leq 9-c_{\alpha}+\Bigl(c_{\alpha}-\frac{1}{4}\Bigr)u_{0}+(c_{\alpha}-2)\mathfrak{e}\leq 9-c_{\alpha}.
If e_{0}>1, then u_{0}>1, \varpi=2, \mathfrak{u}_{*}=1, and
u_{0}=e_{0}+1-\mathfrak{e}\geq 2-\mathfrak{e}, so
C_{1}(\mathfrak{u})\leq 9-\frac{u_{0}}{4}-2\mathfrak{e}+c_{\alpha}\leq 9-\frac{1}{2}+c_{\alpha}\leq 9-c_{\alpha},
because 2c_{\alpha}<\frac{1}{2}. In branch 2, \mathfrak{u}_{*}=1 and
\varpi-1=\min\{1,u_{0}\}, so
C_{1}(\mathfrak{u})\leq 9-\frac{u_{0}}{4}-2\mathfrak{e}+c_{\alpha}\min\{1,u_{0}\}\leq 9-2\mathfrak{e}\leq 9-\frac{1}{4}\leq 9-c_{\alpha},
because c_{\alpha}\min\{1,u_{0}\}\leq\frac{u_{0}}{4} and
\mathfrak{e}\geq\frac{1}{8}. This proves the claim, hence
L-U_{1}\geq\frac{3\alpha}{4}(1-2\Delta)-(10-3\alpha)\omega.
(21.11)
Comparing (21.6) with (21.9), the
exponent of \delta^{-1} in the lower bound exceeds that in the
upper bound by at least the left side of (21.7)
minus its right side. This is impossible as \delta\to 0. Finally,
if \omega<r_{\alpha}(1-2\Delta), the left side of
(21.7) is positive, and \omega<\frac{W_{0}}{6}
makes W_{0}-6\omega positive.
Choosing the loss parameters
successively small proves the last assertion.
∎
The coefficient 10-3\alpha is sharp for these scalar inequalities:
\varsigma=2, \chi=-1, u_{0}=\mathfrak{e}=0, \varpi=1, and \mathfrak{u}=2
give C_{1}(\mathfrak{u})=9-c_{\alpha}. Whether a configuration with these
parameters exists is a different question, which we do not address.
21.2 Proof of the theorem
Proof of Theorem 1.1.
We first prove the bound q_{*}. Fix \frac{1}{3}<\alpha<\alpha_{*}
with a row length s such that f_{s}(n)\geq cn^{\alpha}, and
0<\omega<r_{\alpha}.
Put \Delta=0, so that \lambda=1,
F=\frac{1}{2}, a_{c}=W_{0}=\frac{1}{4}, and q=\frac{3}{4}+\omega.
Choose \sigma,\vartheta,\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}>0 small, then the
fixed finite direction grid of Proposition 18.4
covering all s_{\mathrm{act}}\in[\alpha,1), and let \eta_{*}>0 be
the minimum tolerance of Theorem 18.3 over this grid at t=1
and \epsilon=\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}. Choose \beta,\tau>0 satisfying
(21.7)–(21.8), then
\tau_{0}\leq\frac{\tau}{2} and \xi,\xi_{\mathrm{par}},\xi_{\mathrm{cut}}\leq\tau
satisfying (17.4) and
\alpha\max\{\xi,\xi_{\mathrm{cut}}\}<\min\{\frac{\vartheta}{4},\frac{\eta_{*}}{4}\}, the
candidate exponents (17.2) with their number
\mathfrak{n}, the net \Theta of Lemma 20.2,
the row lengths s_{1} of Lemma 18.2 and s_{0} of
Proposition 17.5. All these choices precede
the set and its measures.
Let K be compact with \dim_{\mathrm{H}}K>1, and suppose that its
bad-pin set at threshold q has dimension greater than one.
Lemma 20.4 supplies separated Frostman
measures \mu,\nu with exponents greater than one, one oracle A,
and one positive-measure relation G whose low-distance relations
have masses P_{n}\geq n^{-2} at arbitrarily large precisions. Every
pair of G has the certificate at level 1
(Section 8). Corollary 20.3
supplies, at each such precision, a template, mass classes with
p\geq cn^{-\mathfrak{n}-4}, and the finite inputs
(F1)–(F4). The scale conditions of
Section 17 hold: \frac{\rho_{\min}}{bn}\to\infty
and \frac{\delta^{2q}}{b}\to 0 by (17.4),
and (17.12) follows from
(21.8). Propositions 17.3,
17.5, and 17.6,
Lemma 17.7, Lemma 18.2, and
Proposition 18.4 produce the pins P and the tubes
\mathcal{T}, and Proposition 21.1 excludes the
configuration for all sufficiently small \delta. Since the
recurrent precisions are unbounded, this is a contradiction, and the
bad-pin set of K has dimension at most one. All finite choices are
made after the oracle is fixed.
They need not be computable or
consistent at different precisions.
For analytic E with \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1, choose a compact
K\subset E of dimension greater than one. Since
\Delta_{x}K\subset\Delta_{x}E for every pin, the bad-pin set of E at
threshold q is contained in that of K. Choose
\alpha_{j}\nearrow\alpha_{*} in (\frac{1}{3},\alpha_{*}), each with a fixed
row length, and 0<\omega_{j}<r_{\alpha_{j}} with
\omega_{j}\nearrow r_{\alpha_{*}}. The bad-pin set of E at threshold
q_{*} is the countable union over j of its bad-pin sets at the
thresholds \frac{3}{4}+\omega_{j}, the jth treated with \alpha=\alpha_{j}.
Countable stability of Hausdorff dimension implies
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E<q_{*}\}\leq 1. Each
threshold uses one fixed row length.
No estimate at zero margin is
used. With \alpha=\frac{7}{19} and s=5 the same argument proves
the threshold \frac{3}{4}+r_{\alpha}=\frac{132}{169}, and
\alpha=\frac{59}{159}, s=9, yields \frac{368}{471}.
Now suppose \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1. For 0<r<\frac{1}{8} and
0\leq\kappa\leq\frac{1}{2} put
E_{r}(\kappa)=\frac{r+(\frac{1}{2}-4r)\kappa}{1-8r\kappa},
(21.12)
so that Q(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E)=\frac{3}{4}+E_{r}(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E-1) for 1\leq\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\leq\frac{3}{2}, with
r=q_{*}-\frac{3}{4}=r_{\alpha_{*}}. Fix \alpha as above, put
r=r_{\alpha}, fix 0<\kappa<\min\{\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E-1,\frac{1}{2}\}, and let
\varepsilon<E_{r}(\kappa) be close to E_{r}(\kappa). Put
q=\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon,\qquad b=b_{\mathrm{pin}}(\kappa)=\frac{1-2\kappa}{4(1-\kappa)},\qquad\Delta_{*}=\frac{\kappa(1-4\varepsilon)}{1-2\kappa},\qquad\omega=\varepsilon-\frac{\Delta_{*}}{2},
so that \Delta_{*} is the level of Lemma 8.8 with
this b, by (8.17). Multiplying by 1-2\kappa shows
that \omega<r_{\alpha}(1-2\Delta_{*}) is equivalent to
\varepsilon<E_{r}(\kappa). At \varepsilon=E_{r}(\kappa), with
D=1-8r\kappa,
\displaystyle\Delta_{*}=\frac{(1-4r)\kappa}{D}<\frac{1}{2},\qquad\omega=\frac{r(1-2\kappa)}{D}>0,
\displaystyle\varepsilon-\frac{\kappa}{2}=\frac{r(1-2\kappa)^{2}}{D}>0,\qquad\frac{1+2\kappa}{8}-\varepsilon=\frac{(1-2\kappa)(1-8r+8r\kappa)}{8D}>0,
and the last two inequalities are the range
(8.16) for b=b_{\mathrm{pin}}(\kappa), which
reads \frac{\kappa}{2}\leq\varepsilon<\frac{1+2\kappa}{8}. So for
\varepsilon close to E_{r}(\kappa) we have 0\leq\Delta_{*}<\frac{1}{2},
0<\omega<r_{\alpha}(1-2\Delta_{*}), and the range. Choose the
parameters as above with \Delta=\Delta_{*} and this \omega. Apply
Lemma 20.4 to a compact K\subset E
with \dim_{\mathrm{H}}K>1+\kappa, with a source measure
of Frostman exponent 1+\kappa.
Then every limiting source profile
satisfies f(u)\geq(1+\kappa)u. The pins of G have effective
dimension larger than one, so Corollary 6.2,
applied with 1<p\leq s<1+\kappa and then p\searrow 1,
s\nearrow 1+\kappa, yields the prefix estimate (8.15)
with b=b_{\mathrm{pin}}(\kappa) for every limiting profile, and
Lemma 8.8 supplies the certificate at level
\lambda_{*}=1+\Delta_{*} for every limiting profile along low-distance
precisions. The finite argument applies verbatim and proves
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E<q\}\leq 1. Since
\frac{\partial E_{r}}{\partial\kappa}=\frac{(1-4r)^{2}}{2D^{2}}>0,\qquad\frac{\partial E_{r}}{\partial r}=\frac{(1-2\kappa)^{2}}{D^{2}}>0,
letting \varepsilon\nearrow E_{r}(\kappa), then
\kappa\nearrow\min\{\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E-1,\frac{1}{2}\}, and then \alpha\nearrow\alpha_{*}
through countably many thresholds, countable stability proves the
bound \frac{3}{4}+E_{r_{\alpha_{*}}}(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E-1)=Q(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E) for \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\leq\frac{3}{2}.
For \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\geq\frac{3}{2} the thresholds increase to
\frac{3}{4}+E_{r}(\frac{1}{2})=1. The monotonicity of E_{r} in
\kappa shows that Q is increasing, and Q(1)=q_{*}. With
r=\frac{21}{676} and r=\frac{59}{1884} the same argument yields
the fixed-row formulas stated after the theorem.
∎
Remark 21.2 (Dependence of the threshold on the measures).
The constants suppressed in \lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}, the angular
density bound L, the uniformization thresholds of
Lemma 20.2, and the coding constants of
Lemma 20.4 depend on the fixed
measures \mu,\nu and on the relation G: through the Frostman
constants and support diameters, the source–pin separation, and
the fixed constants of the drawing lemmas. The proof uses only that
they are independent of the precision n. They are not bounded in
terms of the Frostman exponents or of \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E alone: the
uniformization thresholds come from Borel–Cantelli arguments
without a rate, and L is chosen so that a restriction retains
positive mass. Consequently the scale below which the finite
configuration is excluded depends on the measures, whereas the
incidence parameters and the threshold are fixed before the set and
the measures are chosen. The source dimension enters only through
the level of the certificate. The theorem is a dimension bound, for
which the existence of arbitrarily large admissible scales is all
that is needed.
No effective threshold in terms of the dimension is
claimed. This dependence already enters the information-to-measure
step, through the almost-sure coding lemma, before the finite
geometric argument is applied.
22 Two-sided angular bounds and bisector witnesses
We retain the notation of Sections 17 and
18. The source and pin sets are related by
assigned incidences. Previously we controlled the directions of the
source vertices from each pin. We now also control the directions
of the assigned pins from each source vertex. Near a perpendicular
bisector, this second angular estimate becomes a bound for the
number of witnesses in a small square.
All source and pin locations are in fixed bounded sets at positive distance
from each other. Constants may depend on this separation, on the diameter
bounds, and on the fixed incidence error. Directions are in S^{1}.
Using finitely many angular charts changes constants only.
Let P be a pin set and let I\subset P\times V be the assigned
incidence relation with a common finite source set V. Assume, for each v\in V and every angular interval J of length at least \rho, that
\#\{x\in P:(x,v)\in I,\arg(x-v)\in J\}\leq C_{0}N_{0}(|J|+\rho).
(22.1)
Here N_{0} is a cardinality normalization, comparable to \#P up to
the allowed polynomial factors. The rest of this section explains how to supply
(22.1) in the construction of Section 20.
For distinct v,z\in V put \ell=|v-z| and
\rho_{\ell}=\frac{\delta}{\ell}. Let \mathcal{W}(v,z) consist of assigned
common-incidence witnesses satisfying
(x,v),(x,z)\in I,\qquad\bigl||x-v|-|x-z|\bigr|\leq C\delta.
Any subcollection, in particular the witnesses from actual row occurrences,
may be used. It is essential to keep this assigned-incidence condition.
Whenever the grid \mathcal{D}_{D} is used in the following count,
D is a dyadic side length.
Theorem 22.1.
Suppose (22.1) holds with \rho\leq C\rho_{\ell}, and
\rho_{\ell}\leq c\ell. For every square Q of side D\geq\rho_{\ell},
\#(\mathcal{W}(v,z)\cap Q)\leq CC_{0}N_{0}(\ell D+\rho_{\ell}).
(22.2)
Consequently, if \#\mathcal{W}(v,z)\geq k, then
\#\{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{D}:Q\cap\mathcal{W}(v,z)\neq\varnothing\}\geq\frac{k}{CC_{0}N_{0}(\ell D+\rho_{\ell})}.
(22.3)
Without the comparison \rho\leq C\rho_{\ell}, the same conclusions
hold with \ell D+\rho_{\ell}+\rho in place of \ell D+\rho_{\ell}.
Proof.
The squared-distance identity places the witnesses in the
C\rho_{\ell}-neighborhood of the perpendicular bisector L of v,z.
The distance from v to L is \frac{\ell}{2}. A segment of L of length
O(D), at distance bounded above and below from v, has radial image
as viewed from v of angular length O(\ell D): the derivative of
its angle with respect to arclength is
\frac{\operatorname{dist}(v,L)}{|x-v|^{2}}=O(\ell).
Thickening the segment by O(\rho_{\ell}) enlarges its radial image by
O(\rho_{\ell}). Intersecting with the pin region, and splitting into a
bounded number of segments if necessary, shows that the radial image of
Q\cap L^{(C\rho_{\ell})} is covered by boundedly many arcs of total
length O(\ell D+\rho_{\ell}). Apply (22.1) to these arcs,
enlarging short arcs to the resolution \rho. This proves
(22.2).
Summing over occupied D-squares proves (22.3).
∎
22.1 Preserving both angular bounds
The additional condition is available from the common relation, but it
requires changing the deterministic choice of finite pin representatives.
Lemma 22.2 (Two-sided angular restriction).
Let \mu,\nu be separated compactly supported planar Frostman probability
measures, both of exponent strictly greater than one. Any Borel relation
G of positive \nu\times\mu measure has a positive-measure restriction
G^{\prime} and a finite L such that
(\pi_{x})_{*}(\mu|_{G^{\prime}_{x}})\leq L\mathcal{H}^{1},\qquad(\pi_{y})_{*}(\nu|_{(G^{\prime})^{y}})\leq L\mathcal{H}^{1}
for almost every relevant x,y, respectively.
Proof.
Orponen’s radial absolute continuity theorem [25], applied in both
orientations, establishes absolute continuity for almost every center with
respect to the other measure: the exceptional dimension is strictly less
than one, so the other Frostman measure assigns it mass zero.
Choose measurable versions of the two radial densities. Restrict G to
pairs where both densities evaluated at the pair’s direction are at most
L. These restrictions increase, as L\to\infty, to a product-full
subset of G. Choose finite L retaining positive mass. Each restricted
fiber has angular pushforward bounded by its truncated original density.
∎
The restriction is fixed before the recurrent low-distance precisions
are selected. Every subsequent subrelation inherits these bounds.
Lemma 22.3 (Random representatives retain both angular bounds).
Use Lemma 20.1, with source
\delta-cell masses in [m,2m), fine pin-cell masses in [v,2v),
and relation mass p\geq n^{-C}, where \delta=2^{-n}.
If the relation has both angular bounds of Lemma 22.2, its
finite representatives can be chosen so that all original forward
estimates hold and, simultaneously for every source-cell center v_{Q},
\#\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:(x,v_{Q})\text{ is assigned},\arg(x-v_{Q})\in J\}\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}v^{-1}(|J|+\delta)+O(n)
(22.4)
at every dyadic angular scale. Polynomial factors depend on n,p^{-1}
and the fixed measure constants.
Proof.
First perform the active cell and radial-bin selection in the proof of Lemma 20.1.
An assigned source cell Q has active fiber mass at least
\tau\mu(Q), with \tau\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}1, and the surviving active
relation has mass \gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}p. Choose one independent point
x_{B} from each relevant fine pin cell B, with law
\frac{\nu|_{B}}{\nu(B)}, and discard samples with no active assignments.
For a source cell Q and an angular interval J, the indicator that
x assigns Q and \arg(x-v_{Q})\in J is bounded by
\frac{1}{\tau\mu(Q)}\int_{Q}1_{G^{\prime}}(x,y)1_{\{\arg(x-y)\in J^{(C\delta)}\}}d\mu(y).
This uses the active-mass lower bound and the fixed source–pin separation.
Integrate in x over the pin cells and use \nu(B)\geq v.
Fubini and the reverse angular bound give the expectation at most
\frac{CL(|J|+\delta)}{\tau v}.
The count is a sum of independent indicators. A Chernoff upper tail,
with an additive sufficiently large multiple of n, holds simultaneously
for every source cell and every dyadic angular interval: there are only
polynomially many in \delta^{-1}. Arbitrary intervals follow by a
bounded dyadic cover at comparable scale.
The original incidence lower bound also survives this
random choice. If F(x) counts active assignments, then
F(x)\lesssim m^{-1}, while the expected total is
\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\frac{p}{mv}. Independence implies
\operatorname{Var}\Big(\sum_{B}F(x_{B})\Big)\lesssim m^{-1}\mathbb{E}\sum_{B}F(x_{B}).
The ratio of variance to squared expectation tends to zero, since it is
at most a polynomial factor times \frac{v}{p}, and v has a positive-power
upper bound from the pin Frostman estimate. All forward angular and
parent and radial-bin upper bounds hold pointwise for any chosen relevant pin.
Thus a realization has the required incidence lower bound and all reverse
upper bounds. Fixed residue-class selections and subsequent deletions
preserve them, at the permitted polynomial cost.
∎
22.2 Coarsening the pins
At the chosen tube scale \rho=\rho_{j}, for the application in
Section 23, first use
Lemma 23.1. Every occupied \rho-cell
then contains exactly
L_{0}=L_{j}\asymp_{\mathrm{pol}}\frac{\upsilon_{\zeta}}{v} fine pins,
where \upsilon_{\zeta} is the common ancestor mass. Use exactly
L_{0} occupied slots in the selection of
Lemma 17.7, so every occupied \rho-cell
retains exactly one actual pin. Each fine pin has inclusion
probability L_{0}^{-1}, independently across different coarse cells. For each fixed source
vertex and angular interval, the new count is again a sum of independent
indicators, with expectation bounded by (22.4) divided
by L_{0}. Chernoff and the same union bound give
\#\{x\in P:(x,v_{Q})\text{ assigned},\arg(x-v_{Q})\in J\}\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}\upsilon_{\zeta}^{-1}(|J|+\rho),\qquad|J|\geq\rho.
The additive logarithmic errors are absorbed because
\upsilon_{\zeta}\lesssim\rho^{1+\varkappa} with fixed
\varkappa>0. The same random experiment preserves every rich-pair
witness lower bound, by the positive lower-tail margin in Lemma 17.7 and a union bound over its polynomially many source pairs.
Apply that lower-tail argument to the assigned row-occurrence
witnesses. Their cardinality has already been established, and each
retained witness still assigns both source vertices. Hence (22.1) is obtained with
N_{0}=\upsilon_{\zeta}^{-1} and C_{0}\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}1.
For the pair range in Proposition 17.5 \ell_{j}\lesssim\ell\lesssim w
and common tube width \rho\asymp\frac{\delta}{\ell_{j}}, use the last
variant of Theorem 22.1. The resulting uniform bound is
\#(\mathcal{W}(v,z)\cap Q_{D})\lesssim_{\mathrm{pol}}N_{0}(wD+\rho).
23 A second upper bound for rich bisectors
We use the witnesses supplied by the graph: each one records an
assigned occurrence of the source pair on a circle. The bounds of
Section 22 apply to these witnesses because
both source vertices remain assigned to the pin.
The common finite construction determines the following parameters.
Write w=\delta^{a}, \rho=\delta^{\zeta}, and
N_{0}=\upsilon_{\zeta}^{-1}, allowing fixed comparison constants.
By Proposition 17.5,
\zeta=1-a+O(\xi). By (17.11) and
(18.3), we may use the lower richness bound
k\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}N_{0}\delta^{\mathfrak{r}+\beta},\qquad\mathfrak{r}=\frac{1}{2}+\min\{2\omega,2\mathfrak{h}\},\qquad\mathfrak{h}=a_{\mathrm{par}}-a.
This follows from the two-dimensional covering of source parents
in (17.7), so it holds for both template branches.
The minimum and the tolerance \beta have the same origin as in
(21.4). The additional branch 1 source bound can
increase the richness.
We use the covering bound common to both branches.
The parameter \beta can be chosen arbitrarily small after the
strict exponent margins are fixed.
Put \mathfrak{u}=\frac{\mathfrak{h}}{\omega}.
The finite scales in (17.1) give the exact bounds
a=a_{c}+(\varpi-\mathfrak{u})\omega+\xi_{\mathrm{par}},\qquad a\geq a_{c}-\omega-\beta-\xi.
Here 0\leq\varpi\leq 2 and \mathfrak{u}\geq 0. In particular,
(\varpi-\mathfrak{u})\omega\geq-\omega-\beta-\xi-\xi_{\mathrm{par}}.
In the leading exponent calculations below we set these tolerances
to zero, obtaining \varpi-\mathfrak{u}\geq-1. Every expression used
in the comparisons is Lipschitz in these parameters on the fixed
range. The error is therefore O(\beta+\xi+\xi_{\mathrm{par}}) and
is chosen smaller than the strict margins. This also covers the
first row scale, which may lie slightly below a_{c}-\omega.
The angular probability at each generating pin satisfies
(18.1). Therefore (18.4) yields, after
making its fixed tolerances as small as required,
\#\mathcal{T}\gtrsim\delta^{-\Lambda+o(1)},\qquad\Lambda=\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}+(1-3\alpha)a.
(23.1)
Here and below the exponent notation permits any prescribed small
fixed loss.
All strict margins are fixed before that loss is chosen.
Put N_{0}=\upsilon_{\zeta}^{-1}, so that \#P\asymp_{\rm pol}N_{0}.
The reverse angular estimate reads
\#(\mathcal{W}(e)\cap Q_{D})\lesssim_{\rm pol}N_{0}(wD+\rho),\qquad D\geq\rho.
We shall use a candidate parent side comparable to
D=\min\{w,\frac{\rho}{w}\}. These finitely many candidate parent scales
are frozen before source thinning and row construction.
Lemma 23.1 (Preparing uniform pin parents).
Fix finitely many candidate parent widths D_{j} and tube widths \rho_{j},
and fix a positive integer L_{\mathrm{reg}}, with
\epsilon=\frac{1}{L_{\mathrm{reg}}}. Before the source thinning and the construction
of rows, the finite pin set can be restricted at inverse-polynomial cost
so that the following hold for every candidate j:
1.
distinct occupied D_{j}-parents are at distance at least D_{j}.
2.
the numbers of retained fine pins in occupied cells are exactly
constant at every scale in
\{D_{j}^{\epsilon},D_{j}^{2\epsilon},\ldots,D_{j},\rho_{j}\}.
3.
after choosing one actual pin independently and uniformly in each
occupied \rho_{j}-cell, the numbers of selected pins in occupied cells
are exactly constant at the displayed coarser scales.
The finite mass bounds and both angular bounds persist with adjusted
polynomial constants. The resulting marked incidence count and pin
cardinality satisfy the original lower bounds with adjusted polynomial
losses. Consequently the row construction and its richness conclusions
can be performed on this already restricted pin set.
Proof.
First retain a dyadic class of marked pin degrees carrying at least an
inverse-polynomial fraction of the marked incidences. The degrees are
then within a factor two, and the class contains an inverse-polynomial
fraction of \upsilon_{f}^{-1} pins: each degree is at most \frac{C}{m}, while
the initial incidence count is at least an inverse-polynomial multiple
of (m\upsilon_{f})^{-1}. Any subsequent inverse-polynomial fraction of
this class preserves an inverse-polynomial fraction of its incidences.
At each candidate D_{j}, retain one of the nine residue classes of
parent indices modulo three. This loses only a fixed factor at each
of finitely many scales and ensures the asserted separation. Apply the
usual branching regularization to the finite ancestor tree at the
union of the displayed levels. Working from the finest frozen level towards the root,
pigeonhole the number of occupied children into dyadic ranges and
retain a range carrying an inverse-polynomial fraction of the surviving
leaves. The already regularized descendant subtrees have equal size,
so this weighted pigeonhole preserves that fraction of fine pins. There are O(n) possible ranges. Prune each
surviving parent to exactly the same power-of-two number of children,
and continue recursively. This loses at most an inverse polynomial
in n and leaves an exactly uniform tree at every frozen level.
In particular every \rho_{j}-cell has the same number L_{j} of fine
pins, and every coarser frozen parent has the same number of occupied
\rho_{j}-cells. The latter count is unchanged by selecting the actual
representative within each cell.
Every retained fine pin has original fine-cell mass at least
\upsilon_{f}, and every occupied \rho_{j}-cell has original mass in
[\upsilon_{j},2\upsilon_{j}). Thus
L_{j}\lesssim\frac{\upsilon_{j}}{\upsilon_{f}}.
Conversely, there are at most \upsilon_{j}^{-1} occupied \rho_{j}-cells,
whereas the number of surviving fine pins is
\gtrsim_{\rm pol}\upsilon_{f}^{-1}. Therefore
L_{j}\asymp_{\rm pol}\frac{\upsilon_{j}}{\upsilon_{f}}.
The random representatives consequently have the same inclusion
probability, to polynomial factors, as in the original pin-coarsening
lemma. Its lower-tail argument preserves all rich-pair witness counts
and an inverse-polynomial fraction of the generating pins. The
reverse angular upper-tail argument is valid in the same experiment.
Its expectation is the fine angular count divided by L_{j}.
All mass bounds refer to the original measure and survive restriction.
The forward angular and source-parent estimates hold pointwise, and
reverse angular counts can only decrease before the random selection.
This proves all claims. Each candidate parent side is replaced by a comparable dyadic side
whose logarithmic exponent is divisible by L_{\mathrm{reg}}. The comparison
factor depends only on L_{\mathrm{reg}}.
Its logarithmic error is O(\frac{L_{\mathrm{reg}}}{n}),
and the displayed L_{\mathrm{reg}}-th roots are then dyadic. This is absorbed
in the already selectable scale tolerances.
∎
23.1 A linear dependence on coarse tube occupancy
Lemma 23.2 (Quantitative coarse occupancy).
Let 0<\delta<\frac{\Delta}{32}, 0<\Delta\leq 1 be dyadic scales and let \epsilon>0
be sufficiently small with \epsilon^{-1}\in\mathbb{N}.
Let \mathcal{P} be a multiset of dyadic \delta-squares in [0,1)^{2}, meaning
that each square may occur with a positive integer multiplicity.
Its total multiplicity is N.
All counts involving \mathcal{P}
include these repetitions. Suppose each occupied square of side \Delta^{j\epsilon} contains
the same number of fine squares, counted with multiplicity, for
1\leq j\leq\epsilon^{-1}. Assume these scales are dyadic and that
distinct occupied \Delta-squares have distance at least \Delta.
Write \mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathcal{P}) for the occupied dyadic r-squares.
Here we use dyadic tubes in one bounded-slope chart. A dyadic tube
is the union of the lines y=ux+v whose parameters (u,v) belong
to one dyadic square in a fixed nested parameter grid. For r\geq\delta,
T^{r} is the tube corresponding to the dyadic r-ancestor of that
parameter square. If U is a dyadic r-tube, 4U and 6U denote
its 4r- and 6r-neighborhoods, respectively. In particular,
(T^{s})^{r}=T^{r} whenever \delta\leq s\leq r. This ancestor convention
will be needed in the induction.
These are the uniformity and separation conditions in
[28, Lemma 4.8]. Suppose also
that, for all dyadic r\in[\Delta,1] and all r-squares Q,
\#(\mathcal{P}\cap Q)\leq KrN,\qquad K\leq\Delta^{-\epsilon}.
Fix C\geq 1 and b\geq 1. Let \mathcal{T}_{A,b} be a family of
distinct dyadic \delta-tubes. Suppose that, for each T\in\mathcal{T}_{A,b},
there are at least A\geq 2 occupied \Delta-parents Q such that
at least b members of \mathcal{P}, counted with multiplicity,
are contained in Q\cap 4T. Assume
\#\{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathcal{P}):Q\subset 6T^{r}\}\leq Cr\#\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathcal{P})\quad\text{for }r=\Delta^{\epsilon},\ldots,\Delta,\qquad Ab>C\delta^{1-2\epsilon}N.
Then, with an absolute fixed constant c_{0} (for example c_{0}=10),
\#\mathcal{T}_{A,b}\lesssim_{\epsilon}C\delta^{-c_{0}\epsilon}\frac{N^{2}}{A^{3}b^{2}}.
(23.2)
The parameter grid may have a fixed translation. It must be the
same nested grid at every scale. All implicit constants are independent
of C.
Proof.
We track the constant in [28, Lemma 5.1], using
[28, Lemma 5.2 and Proposition 2.6] unchanged. Their proof is an induction on
the fine width \delta, for all multisets satisfying the hypotheses,
with \Delta,C,K frozen. The bound on K is expressed in terms of the frozen scale \Delta
and therefore persists through the induction.
The global Frostman hypothesis and Lemma 5.2 give
\#\mathcal{T}_{A,b}\lesssim K\log(\frac{2}{\Delta})\frac{N^{2}}{(Ab)^{2}}.
Thus if A\lesssim C\Delta^{-2\epsilon}, this already proves
(23.2), after absorbing a logarithm. If \Delta is
bounded below in terms of \epsilon, the same weak estimate suffices,
as A\lesssim\Delta^{-1}.
The first induction base case is \delta>\Delta^{1+\epsilon}.
Uniformity and the coarse occupancy bound at \Delta imply
Ab\lesssim C\Delta N. This contradicts
Ab>C\delta^{1-2\epsilon}N when \Delta is sufficiently small:
the fixed geometric constants are absorbed by the positive power gap
\frac{\Delta^{1-\epsilon-2\epsilon^{2}}}{\Delta}\to\infty.
In the remaining case A is larger than a sufficiently large constant
times C\Delta^{-2\epsilon}. Put
n_{r}=\#\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathcal{P}) and choose the smallest frozen scale
r=\Delta^{j\epsilon}, equivalently the largest j, for which
A>c_{1}C\Delta^{-\epsilon}rn_{r}.
Such a scale exists by the planar packing bound at \Delta^{\epsilon},
and it is strictly larger than \Delta by the coarse occupancy
hypothesis. The next level and planar child packing imply
c_{1}C\Delta^{-\epsilon}rn_{r}<A\lesssim C\Delta^{-2\epsilon}rn_{r}.
(23.3)
For every finer frozen scale t<r, failure of the cut inequality implies
rn_{r}<tn_{t}.
The constant C cancels. Uniformity therefore
shows that, for Q\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathcal{P}) and an occupied frozen
t-square R\subset Q,
\#(\mathcal{P}\cap R)=\frac{N}{n_{t}}\leq\frac{t}{r}\frac{N}{n_{r}}=\frac{t}{r}\#(\mathcal{P}\cap Q).
Rounding an intermediate dyadic side up to the next frozen side
costs at most \Delta^{-\epsilon}. Thus the rescaled configuration
inside Q has local Frostman constant \Delta^{-\epsilon},
independent of C, at every scale between \frac{\Delta}{r} and 1.
A tubelet is the part of a fine tube in an r-square. We use the
essentially distinct tubelets \mathbb{U}_{Q} and the covering tubes
of width comparable to \frac{\delta}{r} from
[28, Lemma 5.1, Step 4]. For a tubelet u based in Q, let
u^{+} be the fixed enlargement in that construction, restricted
to Q, and give it weight
w(u)=\#\{R\in\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{P}):R\subset Q,\ \#(\mathcal{P}\cap R\cap u^{+})\geq b\}.
Assigning a \Delta-parent only to its containing r-square
keeps every counted fine square in the configuration to which the
local Frostman estimate applies. The tubelet cover ensures that
the sum of the weights incident to each original tube is at least
a fixed multiple of A. At most a bounded number of tubelets
in any one Q are incident to that tube. Since every such Q
lies in 6T^{r}, tubelets of weight one contribute at most
a constant times Crn_{r} per tube. Choose c_{1} in
(23.3) larger than this absolute constant. After
discarding these tubelets, the weighted incidence count is still
bounded below by a fixed multiple of A for each tube. Only
tubelets with w(u)\geq 2 remain.
For a covering tube \mathbf{T}, write \mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{T}}
for its associated tubelets and \mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{T}} for its
fine tubes. Each fine tube and each tubelet belongs to only
boundedly many such families. Straighten \mathbf{T} and dilate
its transverse coordinate by a factor comparable to
\frac{r}{\delta}. Its tubelets then have both dimensions
comparable to r, and the fine tubes have width comparable to
r. This is the rescaling in Step 4 of the cited proof. The
high–low estimate is therefore applied at width r.
Let S be the least power of two not smaller than
\Delta^{-\frac{\epsilon}{100}}. Because
\Delta\leq r\leq\Delta^{\epsilon}, for sufficiently small \Delta
we have
r^{-\frac{\epsilon}{100}}\leq S\leq r^{-1}.
These are exactly the restrictions on the enlargement parameter
in [28, Proposition 2.6] after rescaling. Its enlarged
fine tubes have width comparable to S\delta in the original
coordinates.
Put
W_{2}=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathcal{P})}\sum_{u\in\mathbb{U}_{Q}:\,w(u)\geq 2}w(u)^{2}.
Inside each Q, apply Lemma 5.2 of [28] after scaling Q
to the unit square. The two relevant widths are
\frac{\delta}{r}<\frac{\frac{\Delta}{r}}{32}, the parent separation is
\frac{\Delta}{r}, and the local Frostman constant is at most
\Delta^{-\epsilon}. Since Q contains exactly \frac{N}{n_{r}}
fine squares, counted with multiplicity, this proves
W_{2}\lesssim n_{r}\Delta^{-\epsilon}\log(\frac{2r}{\Delta})(\frac{N}{bn_{r}})^{2}\lesssim_{\epsilon}n_{r}\Delta^{-2\epsilon}(\frac{N}{bn_{r}})^{2}.
(23.4)
The fixed enlargements and the bounded overlap of the tubelet
cover change only absolute constants. In particular, C does
not occur in this estimate.
For a fine tube T, define its enlarged weighted count by
F(T)=\sum_{\mathbf{T}:\,T\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{T}}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathbb{U}_{\mathbf{T}}\\
u\subset 6(T^{S\delta})\end{subarray}}w(u).
The rescaled high–low estimate and Cauchy–Schwarz imply, for
every subfamily \mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{A,b},
A\#\mathcal{V}\lesssim_{\epsilon}(\frac{S}{r}\#\mathcal{V}\,W_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}+S^{-1+\epsilon}\sum_{T\in\mathcal{V}}F(T).
(23.5)
The same tubelets, weights, and covering tubes may be used for
every \mathcal{V}. The lower bound per tube survives restriction,
and the sum of squared weights can only decrease. This explains
why the estimate is available for subfamilies as well as for the
whole family.
If the first term controls the left side of
(23.5) for at least half the tubes,
(23.4) implies
\#\mathcal{T}_{A,b}\lesssim_{\epsilon}\frac{S\Delta^{-2\epsilon}}{rn_{r}}\frac{N^{2}}{A^{2}b^{2}}\lesssim_{\epsilon}C\Delta^{-5\epsilon}\frac{N^{2}}{A^{3}b^{2}},
where the last step uses the right side of (23.3).
This is the sole nontrivial occurrence of C in this part of the proof.
To pass to the other case, call T good if
F(T)\geq cS^{1-\epsilon}A, where c>0 is a sufficiently small
constant depending only on \epsilon. Apply
(23.5) to the bad tubes. Their second
term is at most a constant times cA times their number, and is
absorbed into the left side. Thus, if at least half the tubes are
bad, the preceding bound proves the lemma. Otherwise at least
half are good, and we retain them. This argument justifies the
pointwise lower bound needed below.
We describe the parent count explicitly. In the tubelet decomposition
of [28, Lemma 5.1, Step 4], the tubelets associated to one
covering tube have directions within O(\frac{\delta}{r}) of
one another. Their fixed enlargements have bounded overlap. Each
fine tube belongs to only boundedly many covering tubes. Thus for
each good T we can choose one covering tube whose contribution
is at least a fixed fraction of F(T).
For a \Delta-parent Q, let g_{T}(Q) count the tubelets in this
chosen family that contribute to the enlarged incidence of T and
for which Q is one of the parents counted by w(u). Counting the same
tubelet–parent pairs in two orders gives
\sum_{Q}g_{T}(Q)\gtrsim S^{1-\epsilon}A.
The tubelets are nearly parallel and essentially distinct. A
width-O(S\delta) strip can contain only O(S) of them through
one \Delta-parent, since r\geq\Delta. Hence g_{T}(Q)\lesssim S.
The overlap bound also holds for the fixed enlargements u^{+}:
within one covering tube, their slopes vary by O(\frac{\delta}{r})
and their transverse positions are separated at scale \delta,
up to a bounded number of classes. If M_{0} is the resulting fixed
bound, their incidences in
Q account for at least \frac{g_{T}(Q)b}{M_{0}} fine-square copies.
This remains true for a multiset, since each copy is counted at most
M_{0} times.
Choose a dyadic value k_{T}\geq 1 for which the parents with
k_{T}\leq g_{T}(Q)<2k_{T} carry at least a fraction
c(\log S)^{-1} of the last displayed sum. There are at least
c\frac{S^{1-\epsilon}A}{k_{T}\log S}
such parents. Pigeonhole once more over the tubes to fix one
