value k=k_{T}, with 1\leq k\lesssim S, losing a further factor
O(\log S) in the number of tubes. Set \delta^{\prime}=8S\delta and
replace each retained tube by its dyadic \delta^{\prime}-ancestor.
The incidence containment checked below shows that every such
ancestor is rich in the selected parents, with
A^{\prime}\gtrsim\frac{S^{1-\epsilon}A}{k\log S},\qquad b^{\prime}=\max\{1,\lfloor\frac{kb}{M_{0}}\rfloor\}\asymp kb.
If the expression inside the floor is less than one, a selected
parent still contains at least one fine-square copy, which explains
the maximum with one. Each ancestor has at most 64S^{2} dyadic
fine descendants. After deleting duplicate ancestors we therefore
obtain a family \mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime},b^{\prime}} with
\#\mathcal{T}_{A,b}\lesssim S^{2}\log S\,\#\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime},b^{\prime}}.
The two logarithmic losses have different roles. One enters A^{\prime},
and the other enters this cardinality bound. All overlap constants
are absolute and independent of C.
The fixed dilation 8 accommodates our neighborhood convention:
the tubelets counted in the low-frequency term lie in
6(T^{S\delta}), their contributing fine squares lie within a further
c_{2}\delta for an absolute constant c_{2}, and replacing a fine
square by its \delta^{\prime}-ancestor adds at most \sqrt{2}\delta^{\prime}.
Since T^{S\delta}\subset T^{\delta^{\prime}}, the total distance from
T^{\delta^{\prime}} is at most
6S\delta+c_{2}\delta+\sqrt{2}\delta^{\prime}=\bigl(\frac{3}{4}+\frac{c_{2}}{8S}+\sqrt{2}\bigr)\delta^{\prime}<4\delta^{\prime}
for sufficiently large S. Thus these ancestors lie in
4T^{\delta^{\prime}}. Once \delta^{\prime}<\Delta, their dyadic ancestors
also remain in the same \Delta-parent as the original fine
squares.
Thus the enlarged incidences satisfy the same 4T condition as the
original ones. The dilation changes no exponent or dependence on C.
For sufficiently small \Delta, we have \delta^{\prime}<\frac{\Delta}{32},
since \delta\leq\Delta^{1+\epsilon}. Let \mathcal{P}^{\delta^{\prime}}
be the multiset obtained by replacing every fine square of
\mathcal{P} by its \delta^{\prime}-ancestor, retaining multiplicity. The total count N,
all frozen parent counts, their uniformity and separation, and the
global Frostman bound are unchanged. For every
frozen t\geq\Delta,
(T^{\delta^{\prime}})^{t}=T^{t},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{t}(\mathcal{P}^{\delta^{\prime}})=\mathcal{D}_{t}(\mathcal{P}).
The coarse occupancy inequality therefore holds with exactly the
same C. A comparison of widths alone would not establish this.
The richness condition is also preserved. More explicitly,
\frac{A^{\prime}b^{\prime}}{C(\delta^{\prime})^{1-2\epsilon}N}\gtrsim_{\epsilon}\frac{S^{\epsilon}}{\log S}\frac{Ab}{C\delta^{1-2\epsilon}N}>1
for sufficiently small \Delta, because \epsilon is fixed and
S\to\infty as \Delta\to 0. Also, k\lesssim S and the original
small-A exclusion imply
A^{\prime}\gtrsim_{\epsilon}\frac{S^{-\epsilon}}{\log S}A\gtrsim_{\epsilon}\frac{C\Delta^{-2\epsilon+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{100}}}{\log(\frac{2}{\Delta})}\geq 2
after decreasing the same threshold for \Delta. Thus all the
inductive hypotheses hold. Applying the induction hypothesis retains the same single
factor C. The powers in the recurrence can be checked before absorbing
constants. Substitution of the lower bound for A^{\prime} and of b^{\prime}\asymp kb
produces
\frac{S^{2}\log S}{(A^{\prime})^{3}(b^{\prime})^{2}}\lesssim\frac{kS^{-1+3\epsilon}(\log S)^{4}}{A^{3}b^{2}}\lesssim\frac{S^{3\epsilon}(\log S)^{4}}{A^{3}b^{2}},
since k\lesssim S. The replacement of
\delta^{-c_{0}\epsilon} by (8S\delta)^{-c_{0}\epsilon} contributes
(8S)^{-c_{0}\epsilon}. With c_{0}=10, the recurrence coefficient
is at most a constant depending only on \epsilon times
S^{-7\epsilon}(\log S)^{4}. Choose \Delta small enough that this
coefficient is less than \frac{1}{2}. This choice is independent
of C. Choose the implicit constant in the asserted bound larger
than those in the weak estimate and the high-frequency estimate.
The low-frequency step then uses at most half this constant at
the larger width \delta^{\prime}. Induction on the dyadic fine width
closes, with \Delta,C,K unchanged and with one factor of C.
∎
23.2 Parents below the row width
Let w=\delta^{a}, \rho=\delta^{\zeta}, \zeta=1-a up to the selectable
row-scale tolerance. At each pin x generating rows, retain the
direction probability \sigma_{x} of Lemma 18.2,
with its two angular bounds. Write T^{r}(x,\theta) for the
width-r tube through x in direction \theta, restricted to
the fixed bounded region containing the pins. For any chosen
parent side D\in[\rho,w], averaging
against the global pin Frostman bound yields
\int\#(P\cap T^{Cr}(x,\theta))d\sigma_{x}(\theta)\lesssim_{\rm pol}N_{0}\{r+\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}}w^{1-\alpha}r^{\alpha}\}\lesssim_{\rm pol}\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}}N_{0}r(\frac{w}{D})^{1-\alpha},\qquad r\geq D.
Indeed the angular interval associated to a point at distance t is
O(\frac{r}{t}).
Use the linear direction bound when \frac{r}{t}\geq w, the
\delta^{-\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}}w^{1-\alpha}(\frac{r}{t})^{\alpha} bound otherwise.
Points within distance r contribute at most CN_{0}r^{1+\varkappa}.
For the remaining points, sum over distance annuli with t>r\geq\rho.
The truncated energies at exponents 1 and \alpha are bounded by
the pin Frostman estimate. Uniformity at the finitely many frozen parent scales,
Markov’s inequality, and restriction of the directional probabilities
therefore supply the coarse occupancy hypothesis of
Lemma 23.2 with
C\leq\delta^{-o(1)-\eta_{\mathrm{occ}}}(\frac{w}{D})^{1-\alpha},
where \eta_{\mathrm{occ}}>0 is an arbitrarily small preset tolerance. Choose
\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}<\frac{\eta_{\mathrm{occ}}}{2} and all remaining exponent losses
with sum less than \frac{\eta_{\mathrm{occ}}}{2}. This deletes
a fixed fraction of directional mass, not witnesses. Renormalize the
retained directional probabilities and apply
Proposition 18.4 to them. Uniformity,
separation, and the forward and reverse angular estimates are prepared
before rows are constructed as above. The normalized global pin
Frostman constant is at most \delta^{-\tau} times polynomial
factors, with \tau>0 selectable. The candidate exponent
\frac{\log D}{\log\delta} stays bounded away from zero. Choosing the
input losses sufficiently small therefore ensures K\leq D^{-\epsilon},
as required by Lemma 23.2.
This is a bound in terms
of the frozen parent scale, and is not replaced by a bound in terms
of the fine width.
Choose D=\min\{w,\frac{\rho}{w}\}, up to the fixed dyadic comparison
factor from the preceding lemma. At leading exponent order this is
D=\frac{\rho}{w} when a<\frac{1}{3}, and D=w otherwise.
At this scale the assigned-witness cap is O_{\rm pol}(N_{0}\rho).
The exponent paid for C is
c_{C}=(1-\alpha)[1-3a]_{+}. Consequently put
B_{\rm eff}=\zeta-c_{C}=\min\{\zeta,\alpha+(2-3\alpha)a\}.
If B_{\rm eff}>\mathfrak{r} with a fixed positive margin, then the
richness condition of Lemma 23.2 holds once
\beta and the other tolerances are chosen smaller than this margin,
as does the requirement of at least two occupied parents.
For each tube choose one original source pair and a subset of exactly
\lfloor k\rfloor of its assigned witnesses, with
k\gtrsim_{\rm pol}N_{0}\delta^{\mathfrak{r}+\beta}. Dyadic pigeonholing
by the witness count in a parent supplies A parents with between
b and 2b witnesses each, where
Ab\gtrsim\frac{k}{\log(\frac{1}{\delta})},\qquad b\lesssim_{\rm pol}N_{0}\rho.
The condition B_{\rm eff}>\mathfrak{r} ensures both A\geq 2 and the
strengthened richness inequality Ab>C\rho^{1-2\epsilon}\#P.
Every chosen witness is an actual point of the same ambient set P.
Therefore the parent-richness hypothesis is valid even though the
chosen witness subset varies from tube to tube. For each of the
O(n^{2}) possible classes the lemma yields
\#\mathcal{T}_{A,b}\lesssim_{\epsilon}C\rho^{-c_{0}\epsilon}\frac{(\#P)^{2}b}{(Ab)^{3}}\lesssim_{\rm pol}C\rho^{-c_{0}\epsilon}\frac{N_{0}^{3}\rho}{k^{3}}.
For this application use the dyadic parameter tubes constructed
above, in each fixed parameter grid and bounded-slope chart separately.
Every frozen ancestor and its 6r-neighborhood lie in a tube of
width O(r) about the original line. The angular estimate was imposed
with this fixed enlargement, so it supplies the occupancy hypothesis
for these ancestors. Any fixed factor is included in C before the
richness inequality is checked.
Choose a comparable larger dyadic fine side if necessary and push
P to its fine cells with multiplicity. The 4T convention then
contains the cells of all assigned witnesses. The fine side remains
smaller than D, so all frozen parent counts are preserved.
Parameter separation bounds the number of original tubes represented
by each new parameter cell by a fixed constant. Treat these finitely
many classes separately. No witness points are discarded. The two
bounded-slope charts can be obtained by interchanging coordinate axes.
Summing the logarithmic classes and choosing the fixed tolerances
and \epsilon sufficiently small yields the following estimate.
In particular, the factor k^{-3} contributes a loss of 3\beta.
Choose this and all other exponent losses to have sum less than \eta. Then
\#\mathcal{T}\lesssim_{\eta}\delta^{-3\mathfrak{r}+B_{\rm eff}-\eta}\quad\text{for }\eta>0.
(23.6)
In the case a<\frac{1}{3} this improves the effective parent-cap exponent
2a by \alpha(1-3a). In the other case it retains the indispensable
fine-resolution floor \zeta.
23.3 The exponent comparison
Write a=a_{c}+(\varpi-\mathfrak{u})\omega, where a_{c}=\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\Delta}{2},
\varpi-\mathfrak{u}\geq-1, and \mathfrak{r}=\frac{1}{2}+f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})\omega with
f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})=\min\{2,2\mathfrak{u}\}, using the global drawing and elementary
parent-covering richness bound specified above. These are leading
exponents, with the finite-scale errors accounted for as above.
Since \frac{1}{3}<\alpha<\frac{1}{2}, comparison with the linear
branch of B_{\rm eff} yields
\displaystyle\Lambda-\{3\mathfrak{r}-\alpha-(2-3\alpha)a\}
\displaystyle=-1+\frac{5}{2}\alpha+(3-6\alpha)a-3f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})\omega
\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{4}+\alpha+(\frac{3}{2}-3\alpha)\Delta-(9-6\alpha)\omega.
Thus this branch is positive if
\omega<\frac{-1+4\alpha+6(1-2\alpha)\Delta}{12(3-2\alpha)}.
(23.7)
The inequality used here is
3f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})-(3-6\alpha)(\varpi-\mathfrak{u})\leq 9-6\alpha,
which follows from f_{0}\leq 2 and \varpi-\mathfrak{u}\geq-1.
For the floor branch use
f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})-\alpha\mathfrak{u}\leq 2-\alpha and \varpi\leq 2.
Its margin is at least
\frac{3\alpha}{4}(1-2\Delta)-(6+3\alpha)\omega, and is positive if
\omega<\frac{\alpha(1-2\Delta)}{4(2+\alpha)}.
A sufficient uniform condition for B_{\rm eff}>\mathfrak{r} is
\omega<\frac{\frac{\alpha}{4}+(1-\frac{3\alpha}{2})\Delta}{4-3\alpha}
combined with the floor richness condition \zeta>\mathfrak{r}.
Indeed its leading margin is
\zeta-\mathfrak{r}=W_{0}-(\varpi-\mathfrak{u}+f_{0}(\mathfrak{u}))\omega\geq W_{0}-3\omega>0, using f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})-\mathfrak{u}\leq 1
and the structural condition W_{0}>6\omega.
The next section verifies these inequalities and the range required
by the certificate iteration.
24 The dimension-dependent pinned improvement
Fix \frac{1}{3}<\alpha<\alpha_{*}. For 0<\kappa<\frac{1}{2}, the certificate implies
\Delta=\frac{4\kappa(1-q)}{1-2\kappa} and
\omega=q-\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\Delta}{2}. The two exponent inequalities are
respectively equivalent to q<Q_{b} and q<Q_{f}, where
\displaystyle Q_{b}(\kappa;\alpha)
\displaystyle=1-\frac{5(1-\alpha)(1-2\kappa)}{6\{3-2\alpha+2(1-2\alpha)\kappa\}},
(24.1)
\displaystyle Q_{f}(\kappa;\alpha)
\displaystyle=1-\frac{1-2\kappa}{4+2\alpha-4\alpha\kappa}.
(24.2)
Write \mathcal{Q}_{\alpha}(\kappa)=\min\{Q_{b}(\kappa;\alpha),Q_{f}(\kappa;\alpha)\}.
For comparison, denote the earlier pinned bound with parameter
\alpha by
D_{0}=10-3\alpha-6\alpha\kappa,\qquad Q_{\mathrm{old}}=1-\frac{(5-3\alpha)(1-2\kappa)}{2D_{0}}.
Thus Q_{\mathrm{old}} is Q(1+\kappa) with \alpha_{*} replaced
by \alpha.
24.1 The required scale inequalities
Write
H_{b}(\Delta)=\frac{\alpha-\frac{1}{4}+(\frac{3}{2}-3\alpha)\Delta}{9-6\alpha},\qquad H_{g}(\Delta)=\frac{\frac{\alpha}{4}+(1-\frac{3\alpha}{2})\Delta}{4-3\alpha}.
Then
H_{g}(\Delta)-H_{b}(\Delta)=\frac{(\alpha-1)(-6\Delta+3\alpha-2)}{6(2\alpha-3)(3\alpha-4)}>0\quad\text{for }\frac{1}{3}<\alpha<\frac{2}{5},\Delta\geq 0.
Thus the first exponent inequality implies the effective parent-spread and
strengthened richness condition. The floor comparison implies the original
structural restriction, since
\frac{1}{24}-\frac{\alpha}{4(2+\alpha)}=\frac{2-5\alpha}{24(2+\alpha)}>0.
We next check the range required by the certificate iteration.
For 0\leq\kappa<\frac{1}{2}, put
H=1-2\kappa,\qquad D_{b}=3-2\alpha+2(1-2\alpha)\kappa,\qquad A=4\alpha-1+6(1-2\alpha)\kappa,\qquad J=2+\alpha-2\alpha\kappa.
At q=Q_{b} the bootstrap parameters are
\Delta_{b}=\frac{10(1-\alpha)\kappa}{3D_{b}},\qquad\omega_{b}=\frac{A}{12D_{b}}>0,
and
Q_{b}-\left(\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)=\frac{HA}{12D_{b}}>0,
\left(\frac{7}{8}+\frac{\kappa}{4}\right)-Q_{b}=\frac{H\{11-14\alpha-6(1-2\alpha)\kappa\}}{24D_{b}}>0.
For the last inequality, the bracket is at least 8(1-\alpha)>0.
At q=Q_{f} one has
\Delta_{f}=\frac{2\kappa}{J},\qquad\omega_{f}=\frac{\alpha H}{4J}>0,
Q_{f}-\left(\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)=\frac{\alpha H^{2}}{4J}>0,
\left(\frac{7}{8}+\frac{\kappa}{4}\right)-Q_{f}=\frac{H\{2+\alpha(2\kappa-1)\}}{8J}>0.
The lower certificate comparisons also give \Delta_{b},\Delta_{f}<\kappa.
Consequently \min\{Q_{b},Q_{f}\} lies in the required bootstrap interval,
with 0<\Delta<\frac{1}{2} for \kappa>0. The strict inequalities persist for
q below and sufficiently close to this minimum. The two main geometric
comparisons then become strict by the positive linear coefficient of 1-q.
24.2 The useful interval and its branch switch
Define
\displaystyle\kappa_{\mathrm{rel}}
\displaystyle=\frac{5-8\alpha-3\alpha^{2}}{6\alpha^{2}-48\alpha+30},
\displaystyle\kappa_{bf}
\displaystyle=\frac{5\alpha^{2}-\alpha-1}{10\alpha^{2}+2\alpha-6},
\displaystyle\kappa_{f}
\displaystyle=\frac{6+10\alpha-\sqrt{(6+10\alpha)^{2}-48\alpha(1+2\alpha)}}{24\alpha}.
For \frac{1}{3}\leq\alpha\leq\frac{3}{8} one has the strict ordering
0<\kappa_{\mathrm{rel}}<\frac{3}{20}<\kappa_{bf}<\frac{3}{16}<\kappa_{f}<\frac{1}{5}.
(24.3)
Here is an elementary verification. The numerator controlling
Q_{b}-Q_{\mathrm{old}} is the increasing linear polynomial
P_{0}(k)=(6\alpha^{2}-48\alpha+30)k+3\alpha^{2}+8\alpha-5.
Its value at zero is negative and
P_{0}(\frac{3}{20})=\frac{39\alpha^{2}+8\alpha-5}{10}>0.
The numerator controlling the branch switch, before multiplication
by 1-2k, is the decreasing linear polynomial
P_{bf}(k)=(10\alpha^{2}+2\alpha-6)k-5\alpha^{2}+\alpha+1.
Its values at \frac{3}{20} and \frac{3}{16} are, respectively,
-\frac{7\alpha^{2}}{2}+\frac{13\alpha}{10}+\frac{1}{10}>0 and
-\frac{25\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{11\alpha}{8}-\frac{1}{8}<0.
Finally, the numerator controlling Q_{f}-(\frac{2}{3}+k) is
P_{f}(k)=1+2\alpha-(6+10\alpha)k+12\alpha k^{2}.
It is strictly decreasing on [0,\frac{1}{5}], and
P_{f}(\frac{3}{16})=\frac{35\alpha}{64}-\frac{1}{8}>0 whereas
P_{f}(\frac{1}{5})=\frac{12\alpha}{25}-\frac{1}{5}<0.
This proves (24.3).
Put D_{b}=3-2\alpha+2(1-2\alpha)\kappa and
J=2+\alpha-2\alpha\kappa. Direct subtraction yields
Q_{b}-Q_{f}=-\frac{(1-2\kappa)P_{bf}(\kappa)}{6JD_{b}}.
Thus Q_{b} is the active branch for \kappa\leq\kappa_{bf} and
Q_{f} for \kappa\geq\kappa_{bf}.
The second branch exceeds \frac{2}{3}+\kappa exactly up to \kappa_{f}
in the interval considered. The numerator of
Q_{b}-(\frac{2}{3}+\kappa) is
P_{b}(\kappa)=1+\alpha-(4+6\alpha)\kappa+12(2\alpha-1)\kappa^{2},
over the positive denominator 6D_{b}.
This polynomial decreases strictly for \kappa\geq 0.
At \kappa_{bf} the two branches agree and exceed \frac{2}{3}+\kappa_{bf},
by (24.3). Therefore the first branch also exceeds
that comparison throughout [\kappa_{\mathrm{rel}},\kappa_{bf}].
24.3 Comparison with the previous bounds
The exact differences are
Q_{b}-Q_{\mathrm{old}}=\frac{(1-2\kappa)P_{0}(\kappa)}{6D_{0}D_{b}},\qquad Q_{f}-Q_{\mathrm{old}}=\frac{\alpha(2-3\alpha)(1-2\kappa)^{2}}{2JD_{0}}>0.
Thus the minimum strictly exceeds the retained-prefix bound
for \kappa_{\mathrm{rel}}<\kappa<\kappa_{f}, and agrees at the lower endpoint.
The bootstrap comparisons above show that both branches also exceed
\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\kappa}{2}.
After 1+\kappa\nearrow\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E, this becomes \frac{1}{4}+\frac{\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E}{2}.
24.4 Closing the dimension argument
Let \kappa\in[\kappa_{\mathrm{rel}}(\alpha),\kappa_{f}(\alpha)] be a strict
source Frostman excess, and take q<\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha}(\kappa) sufficiently
close to this minimum. The bootstrap range and both geometric comparisons
are then strict. The main comparison implies the effective parent-spread
and strengthened richness condition.
The floor comparison implies the original
profile restriction. If the bad pin set at this threshold had dimension
greater than one, the common finite construction, the retained
certificate, and the reverse angular restriction would produce the
configuration above. Its tube lower exponent strictly exceeds the
refined upper exponent, a contradiction for small recurrent \delta.
Now take admissible \alpha\nearrow\alpha_{*}, source exponents
1+\kappa\nearrow\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E, and a countable sequence of thresholds approaching
Q_{\rm rel}(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E). For an analytic source one can first pass to compact
subsets with dimensions approaching \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E.
Bad pins for E are bad for
each such subset. The lower endpoint S_{\mathrm{rel}} also follows from the
previous retained-prefix estimate, with which the new formula agrees.
The upper endpoint follows from strict source exponents and continuity.
The countable union of threshold exceptional sets has Hausdorff dimension
at most one. This proves Theorem 1.6, including the
stated strict comparisons and endpoint values.
25 Exceptional sets above the universal threshold
The preceding proof used the pin measure through the ancestor bounds
of the plateau and through the Frostman bound in
(17.16), where the exponent 1+\varkappa was
replaced by 1. Keeping \varkappa in the Furstenberg estimate
yields explicit bounds for the exceptional set above q_{*}.
Combined
with the certificate level \lambda_{*} of Section 8.5
it proves part (b) of the theorem.
Proposition 25.1 (The pin exponent in the comparison).
In the setting of Section 21, suppose that
the pin measure has Frostman exponent 1+\varkappa with
0\leq\varkappa<\frac{5}{2}-6\alpha, and that
\frac{3\alpha}{4}(1-2\Delta)+\frac{\varkappa}{8}(3-2\Delta)-\Bigl(10-3\alpha-\frac{\varkappa}{2}\Bigr)\omega>0.
(25.1)
Then the finite configuration is impossible for all sufficiently
small \delta, once the loss parameters are small compared with the
left side of (25.1), with (1-2\Delta)\frac{40\alpha-11}{96},
and with W_{0}-6\omega.
Proof.
By (17.16), P^{\prime} is a
(\rho,1+\varkappa,\delta^{-o(1)})-set. Choose the direction grid
before \xi and \xi_{\mathrm{cut}}, and let \eta_{*}>0 be the
minimum tolerance of Theorem 18.3 over this grid at
t=1+\varkappa and \epsilon=\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}. Take
\alpha\max\{\xi,\xi_{\mathrm{cut}}\}<\min\{\frac{\vartheta}{4},\frac{\eta_{*}}{4}\}.
The theorem then applies with t=1+\varkappa. Since s^{\prime}<1\leq t, the
exponent is \min\{\frac{s^{\prime}+t}{2},1\}, and (18.4)
becomes \#\mathcal{T}\gtrsim\delta^{-\Lambda^{\prime}} with
\Lambda^{\prime}=\min\Bigl\{\frac{(1+3s_{\mathrm{act}}+\varkappa)\zeta}{2},(1+s_{\mathrm{act}})\zeta\Bigr\}-3\vartheta-\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}.
Everything else is unchanged, and (21.9) holds. We
show that both entries of the minimum exceed U_{1} by the stated
margins.
For the first entry, the derivation of (21.10), with the
additional term
\frac{\varkappa\zeta}{2}\geq\frac{\varkappa}{2}\bigl(\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\Delta}{2}-\varpi\omega+\mathfrak{h}-\xi_{\mathrm{par}}-\xi\bigr),
gives
\displaystyle\frac{(1+3s_{\mathrm{act}}+\varkappa)\zeta}{2}\geq L^{\prime}-3\tau,\qquad c=c_{\alpha}+\frac{\varkappa}{2}<\frac{1}{4},
\displaystyle L^{\prime}=\frac{3}{4}(1+\alpha)+\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3\alpha}{2}\Bigr)\Delta+\frac{\varkappa}{8}(3-2\Delta)-c\varpi\omega+c\mathfrak{h},
the last inequality being \varkappa<\frac{5}{2}-6\alpha. The proof
of (21.11) used only 0<c_{\alpha}<\frac{1}{4}, so
with c in place of c_{\alpha} it yields
L^{\prime}-U_{1}\geq\frac{3\alpha}{4}(1-2\Delta)+\frac{\varkappa}{8}(3-2\Delta)-(9-c)\omega,
which is the left side of (25.1).
For the second entry, \zeta\geq 1-a-\xi and
a=a_{c}+\varpi\omega+\xi_{\mathrm{par}}-\mathfrak{h} give
(1+s_{\mathrm{act}})\zeta=(1+\alpha)\zeta+(1-\alpha)a\geq 1+\frac{\alpha}{2}-\alpha\Delta-2\alpha\varpi\omega+2\alpha\mathfrak{h}-3\tau.
With \mathfrak{u}=\frac{\mathfrak{h}}{\omega} as in
Section 21, we have
f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})\leq\min\{2,2\mathfrak{u}\} in both branches,
3f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})-2\alpha\mathfrak{u}\leq 6-2\alpha, and (8.13)
implies
6+2\varsigma+\chi-2\mathfrak{e}+2\alpha(\varpi-1)\leq 9-\frac{u_{0}}{4}+2\alpha\min\{1,u_{0}\}\leq\frac{35}{4}+2\alpha.
Hence
(1+s_{\mathrm{act}})\zeta-U_{1}\geq\Bigl(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\Bigr)(1-2\Delta)-\Bigl(\frac{35}{4}+2\alpha\Bigr)\omega-3\tau\geq(1-2\Delta)\frac{40\alpha-11}{96}-3\tau,
using \omega<\frac{1-2\Delta}{24}.
The leading term is positive
because \alpha>\frac{1}{3}. The conditions (21.8)
are as before. Choosing \beta,\tau,\sigma,\vartheta,\epsilon_{\mathrm{RW}}
small compared with the three margins, the argument of
Proposition 21.1 leads to a contradiction.
∎
Proof of Theorem 1.7.
Fix \frac{1}{3}<\alpha<\alpha_{*} with its row length.
For part (a), put \Delta=0 and let
q_{\alpha}=\frac{3}{4}+r_{\alpha}=\frac{15-3\alpha}{20-6\alpha}\leq q<\frac{19}{24},\qquad\omega=q-\frac{3}{4}<\frac{1}{24}.
Define
\varkappa_{\alpha}(q)=20-6\alpha-\frac{15-3\alpha}{q}=\frac{(80-24\alpha)\omega-6\alpha}{3+4\omega}.
(25.2)
It is nonnegative for q\geq q_{\alpha}, increasing in q, and at most
\frac{20-42\alpha}{19}, which is less than \frac{5}{2}-6\alpha
because \alpha<\frac{55}{144}. Let K be compact with
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}K>1, and suppose that its bad-pin set B_{q} has
dimension greater than 1+\varkappa_{\alpha}(q). Fix \varkappa with
\varkappa_{\alpha}(q)<\varkappa<\min\Bigl\{\frac{5}{2}-6\alpha,\dim_{\mathrm{H}}B_{q}-1\Bigr\},
and apply Lemma 20.4 with a pin
measure of Frostman exponent 1+\varkappa. With \Delta=0, the
left side of (25.1) is
\frac{3\alpha}{4}+\frac{3\varkappa}{8}-(10-3\alpha-\frac{\varkappa}{2})\omega,
which is positive exactly when \varkappa>\varkappa_{\alpha}(q), and W_{0}-6\omega=\frac{1}{4}-6\omega>0. Proposition 25.1
and the proof of Theorem 1.1 give a
contradiction. Hence \dim_{\mathrm{H}}B_{q}\leq 1+\varkappa_{\alpha}(q),
which is the asserted bound for q<\frac{19}{24}. The bad-pin set
at \frac{19}{24} is the union of the bad-pin sets at thresholds
q_{j}\nearrow\frac{19}{24}, so countable stability and the
continuity of the bound in q give the endpoint. The passage to
analytic E is as before. Finally, the right side of (a) decreases
as \alpha increases, its derivative in \alpha being
-6+\frac{3}{q}<0.
Letting \alpha\nearrow\alpha_{*} through fixed
row lengths proves the bound with \alpha_{*}, and q_{\alpha}\nearrow q_{*}.
For part (b), let 0<k\leq\frac{1}{6} and put
\mathcal{D}=\frac{k(4-k)}{1-3k+k^{2}},\qquad B=1-b_{\mathrm{sym}}(k)(1-k)=\frac{(1+k)(3-k)}{4-k},\qquad c=c_{\alpha}+\frac{k}{2}<\frac{1}{8}+\frac{1}{12}<\frac{1}{4},
(25.3)
and, for q=\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon, \Delta=\mathcal{D}(1-q),
\omega=\varepsilon-\frac{\Delta}{2}: these are the level and the
margin supplied by Lemma 8.8 with b=b_{\mathrm{sym}}(k)
and (8.17). With the pin exponent \varkappa=k, the
left side of (25.1) is affine in q, with slope
-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\mathcal{D}(10-6\alpha-k)+20-6\alpha-k\bigr)<0, and it
vanishes at
P_{\alpha}(1+k)=\frac{\frac{15-3\alpha}{2}+\bigl(5-3\alpha-\frac{k}{2}\bigr)\mathcal{D}}{10-3\alpha-\frac{k}{2}+\bigl(5-3\alpha-\frac{k}{2}\bigr)\mathcal{D}}=\frac{k^{3}+(1+3\alpha)k^{2}-(5+15\alpha)k+15-3\alpha}{9k^{2}-(21+6\alpha)k+20-6\alpha};
(25.4)
so the margin is positive exactly when q<P_{\alpha}(1+k). Both
denominators are positive for 0\leq k\leq\frac{1}{6}, and P_{\alpha}(t)
is the function P(t) of the theorem with \alpha in place of
\alpha_{*}.
If P_{\alpha}(1+k)\leq B, the conclusion follows from
Theorem 1.5 with p=1+k<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E, because
B(1+k,\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E)\geq B(1+k,1+k)=B, using that
B(p,t) is nondecreasing in its source parameter t. So let P_{\alpha}(1+k)>B and let
B<q<P_{\alpha}(1+k) be close to P_{\alpha}(1+k). We check the remaining
hypotheses at q=P_{\alpha}(1+k).
They are strict, so they persist.
The range (8.16) for b=b_{\mathrm{sym}}(k) is
B\leq q<\frac{1+B}{2}, and
\frac{1+B}{2}-P_{\alpha}(1+k)=\frac{(1-3k+k^{2})\bigl(20+3k-7k^{2}-18\alpha+12\alpha k\bigr)}{2(4-k)\bigl(20-6\alpha-(21+6\alpha)k+9k^{2}\bigr)}>0.
Since q>B and \mathcal{D}(1-B)=k, we have 0<\Delta<k\leq\frac{1}{6}.
The margin \omega increases with q and vanishes at
q_{0}=\frac{3+2\mathcal{D}}{4+2\mathcal{D}}, where \Delta=\frac{\mathcal{D}}{4+2\mathcal{D}}<\frac{1}{2}
and the left side of (25.1) equals
\frac{3\alpha}{4}(1-2\Delta)+\frac{k}{8}(3-2\Delta)>0.
Hence
q_{0}<P_{\alpha}(1+k) and \omega>0. Finally,
\omega<\frac{1-2\Delta}{24} is equivalent to
q<\frac{19+10\mathcal{D}}{24+10\mathcal{D}}, and
\displaystyle\frac{19+10\mathcal{D}}{24+10\mathcal{D}}-P_{\alpha}(1+k)=\frac{(1-3k+k^{2})G_{\alpha}(k)}{2(12-16k+7k^{2})\bigl(20-6\alpha-(21+6\alpha)k+9k^{2}\bigr)},
\displaystyle G_{\alpha}(k)=20-79k+57k^{2}-14k^{3}-42\alpha(1-3k+k^{2}),
where G_{\alpha} decreases in \alpha and in k on
[0,\frac{1}{6}] for \alpha\leq\frac{3}{8}, since
\partial_{\alpha}G_{\alpha}=-42(1-3k+k^{2})<0 and
\partial_{k}G_{\alpha}=-79+114k-42k^{2}+42\alpha(3-2k)<0 there.
So
G_{\alpha}(k)\geq G_{\frac{3}{8}}(\frac{1}{6})=\frac{17}{432}>0. Hence
\omega<\frac{1-2\Delta}{24}, so that Section 8
and all the conditions of Section 21 apply, and
\varkappa=k<\frac{5}{2}-6\alpha.
Let K be compact with \dim_{\mathrm{H}}K>1+k, and let
q<P_{\alpha}(1+k), q>B, be so close to P_{\alpha}(1+k) that the
checks above hold. Apply Lemma 20.4
with a source measure of Frostman exponent 1+k.
Then every
limiting source profile satisfies f(u)\geq(1+k)u. If the bad-pin
set B_{q} of K had dimension greater than 1+k, the lemma would
also supply a pin measure of Frostman exponent 1+k, and every pin of
G would have \dim^{A}x\geq 1+k.
Corollary 6.2, applied to the pairs of G with
1<p\leq s<1+k and then p,s\nearrow 1+k, yields the prefix estimate
(8.15) with b=b_{\mathrm{sym}}(k) for every
limiting profile.
Lemma 8.8 supplies the certificate at
level \lambda=1+\Delta along low-distance precisions, and Proposition 25.1 with the argument of
Theorem 1.1 would give a contradiction. Hence
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}B_{q}\leq 1+k.
By inclusion this holds for every
q<P_{\alpha}(1+k), and by countable stability at q=P_{\alpha}(1+k).
The first expression in (25.4) increases with
\mathcal{D} and with k, its partial derivatives having the positive
numerators (5-3\alpha-k)(10-6\alpha-k) and 15-3\alpha+(5-3\alpha)\mathcal{D}, and P_{\alpha}(1+k) increases with \alpha, the numerator of its
\alpha-derivative being 3(1-3k+k^{2})(10-29k+11k^{2})>0. Letting
\alpha\nearrow\alpha_{*} and using countable stability once more
proves the bound with P(1+k). For analytic E with
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1+k, choose a compact K\subset E with
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}K>1+k.
Since \Delta_{x}K\subset\Delta_{x}E, the bad-pin
set of E is contained in that of K.
For the last assertion of the theorem, P is increasing on
[1,\frac{7}{6}], because \mathcal{D} increases with k and the
first expression in (25.4) increases with \mathcal{D} and
with k.
So for 1<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\leq\frac{7}{6} and q<P(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E) there is
k<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E-1 with q<P(1+k), and the set of x\in E with
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E<q has dimension at most 1+k<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E. Its
complement in E therefore has dimension \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E.
∎
The comparison P(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E)>Q(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E) for 1<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\leq\frac{7}{6} follows from the
same monotonicity: writing
Q_{\alpha}(1+k)=\frac{3}{4}+E_{r_{\alpha}}(k) for the earlier pinned
bound with parameter \alpha, this is the
first expression in (25.4) with \frac{4k}{1-2k} in
place of \mathcal{D} and 0 in place of the pin exponent k, and
\mathcal{D}-\frac{4k}{1-2k}=\frac{k^{2}(3-2k)}{(1-2k)(1-3k+k^{2})}>0.
26 Uniform finite-precision estimates and selection
The lower-box proof uses a new pair at each terminal precision.
We therefore need uniform estimates for such changing pairs.
This section supplies that uniformity, together with the
selection and covering arguments; an asymptotic statement for
one fixed pair would not suffice.
Lemma 26.1 (Limits of finite generic pairs).
Fix an oracle A, 1<\sigma\leq 2, C>0, and a bounded region.
Suppose n_{j}\to\infty and pairs x_{j},y_{j} in that region have
|x_{j}-y_{j}|\geq n_{j}^{-C}. For each n=n_{j}, write x=x_{j},y=y_{j},
e=\frac{y-x}{|y-x|}, and suppose for every 1\leq m\leq 2n that
\displaystyle K_{m}^{A}(x),K_{m}^{A}(y)
\displaystyle\geq\sigma m-C\log_{2}(n+2),
(26.1)
\displaystyle K_{m}^{A,x}(e),K_{m}^{A,y}(e)
\displaystyle\geq m-C\log_{2}(n+2),
(26.2)
\displaystyle K_{m}^{A,x}(y)
\displaystyle\geq K_{m}^{A}(y)-C\log_{2}(n+2),
(26.3)
\displaystyle K_{m}^{A,y}(x)
\displaystyle\geq K_{m}^{A}(x)-C\log_{2}(n+2).
(26.4)
Every joint subsequential limit of the normalized endpoint and
conditioned distance profiles satisfies (10.1)
in both orientations, with f(u),g(u)\geq\sigma u.
The conditional-parent transfer of
Lemma 14.1 also holds in the
finite approximation, with arbitrarily small fixed linear error.
26.1 Uniform estimates for changing pairs
Proof of Lemma 26.1.
We first explain precisely why the retained information estimates survive
when (x,y) varies with n. Set
\ell_{n}=\lceil(\log_{2}(n+2))^{2}\rceil,\qquad k_{n}=\lceil n^{\frac{1}{4}}\rceil,\qquad h_{n}=\lceil\sqrt{n}\rceil,
and fix 1<d<\min\{\sigma,\frac{5}{4}\}. For all large n,
K_{m}^{A}(x),K_{m}^{A}(y)\geq dm\qquad(\ell_{n}\leq m\leq 2n).
(26.5)
Thus the point-dependent initial cutoff supplied by effective dimension
is replaced by an explicit logarithmic-squared cutoff. On a local
projection piece with right endpoint b\geq k_{n}, a deficiency
C\log(n+2) is at most 5C\log(b+2) for all large n.
The local projection estimates of Stull [33, Lemmas 14–15] are
finite statements. Their tolerances, a logarithmic directional
deficiency, and a bound on the norm of the point control the threshold.
The remark following Lemma 14 states this uniformity explicitly, and
the proof of Lemma 15 has the same dependence. Their oracle simulations
are uniform. In particular, no threshold depending on an unrecorded
property of the selected direction is used here.
For the closed projection estimate, the proof of Lemma 3.2 retains
K_{R}^{A}(x\mid p_{v}x,v)\leq\max\{K_{R}^{A}(x)-R,\frac{K_{R}^{A}(x)-u}{2}\}+o(n)
(26.6)
when R is a fixed positive fraction of n and \frac{u}{R} is bounded below.
In fact, for fixed c_{0},\tau,\xi>0, the error is at most \xi R
uniformly for c_{0}h_{n}\leq R\leq n and \tau R\leq u\leq R, once n
is large enough. Its partition has a bounded number of pieces once
\tau is fixed. Discard projection pieces below k_{n}, at cost
O(k_{n})=o(R) uniformly in this range. Every remaining piece has a
common directional-deficiency constant, and k_{n}\gg\ell_{n} ensures
that (26.5) supplies the lower dimension cutoff throughout
the remaining argument. Sum the bounded number of local estimates
with a fixed sufficiently small tolerance. This proves the required
uniform version of (26.6), including the smaller
projection precisions used inside the prefix partition.
The circle geometry has a second possible dependence on the pair. If
|x-z|=|x-y| and v=\frac{y-z}{|y-z|}, then
\left|e\cdot v\right|=\frac{|y-z|}{2|x-y|}.
(26.7)
Since |x-y|\geq n^{-C}, approximating v by a perpendicular to e
loses at most O_{C}(\log n) bits of precision. The normalization of
y-z and the perpendicular-bisector formula have the same uniform
logarithmic costs on bounded sets. The tangent transition also widens
by only O_{C}(\log n). The distance-transfer search and the
complexity-lowering oracle in Fiedler–Stull [7, Lemmas 14–15]
are uniform finite algorithms; their parameter descriptions cost
O_{C}(\log n) here. Supplying a bounded number of additional precision
indices does not introduce a point-dependent constant.
These observations account for the pair-dependent quantities, namely the
initial lower-complexity cutoff, the directional deficiency, the
separation of the endpoints, and the finite parameter descriptions.
We next carry out the limit argument using these bounds.
26.2 Limits of a sequence of changing pairs
Consider a sequence n_{j}\to\infty and pairs (x_{j},y_{j}) satisfying the hypotheses of Lemma 26.1. Write \rho_{j}=|x_{j}-y_{j}|.
Take a common uniformly convergent subsequence of the normalized
complexity functions
\begin{array}[]{ll}f_{j}(u)=n_{j}^{-1}K_{\lfloor un_{j}\rfloor}^{A}(y_{j}),&g_{j}(u)=n_{j}^{-1}K_{\lfloor un_{j}\rfloor}^{A}(x_{j}),\\[2.0pt]
d_{x,j}(u)=n_{j}^{-1}K_{\lfloor un_{j}\rfloor}^{A,x_{j}}(\rho_{j}),&d_{y,j}(u)=n_{j}^{-1}K_{\lfloor un_{j}\rfloor}^{A,y_{j}}(\rho_{j}).\end{array}
Set their values at zero to zero and interpolate between integer
precisions. Standard precision-increment estimates are uniform for
bounded points and for all oracles, so this extraction does not require
the points to be fixed. The limits satisfy
\sigma u\leq f(u),g(u)\leq 2u,
(26.8)
with f,g nondecreasing and 2-Lipschitz, and d_{x},d_{y} nondecreasing
and 1-Lipschitz, vanishing at zero. Every limiting upper bound on the terminal distance complexities is inherited by d_{x}(1) and d_{y}(1).
26.3 The transfers remain valid
Recall the closed transfer quantities
p_{g}(u;b)=\min\{b-u,\frac{g(b-u)+u}{2}\},\qquad H_{f,g}(a,b)=\min\{f(b),\inf_{a\leq u\leq b}(f(u)+p_{g}(u;b))\}.
Fix 0<a<b\leq 1 and a cap c<H_{f,g}(a,b) with c>f(a).
For a circle competitor at separation |y_{j}-z|=2^{-un_{j}}, the
uniform collision estimate, after the source complexity is capped
at cn_{j}, reads
K_{bn_{j}}^{A,G_{j}}(z)\geq n_{j}\min\{c,f(u)\}+n_{j}p_{g}(u;b)-o(n_{j}).
(26.9)
Integer subscripts are rounded. For u<\frac{b}{2} this follows from
(26.6) and the bisector identity. For u\geq\frac{b}{2}, the
tangent estimate has p_{g}(u;b)=b-u; the logarithmic transition is
absorbed by the cap’s fixed strict margin.
The lowering oracle preserves information conditional on the source.
Consequently (26.3)–(26.4) preserve the positive pin
term, while the negative projection residual can only decrease. This
uses the original directional randomness, without requiring new
randomness relative to G_{j}. The strict gap between c and H
verifies the competitor hypothesis of the finite distance-transfer
lemma. After the chain rule, removal of G_{j}, and letting the local
tolerance tend to zero, we obtain
d_{x}(b)-d_{x}(a)\geq[H_{f,g}(a,b)-f(a)]_{+}.
(26.10)
The same reasoning applies with the endpoints exchanged.
We also need the full partition transfer. Put h_{g}(u)=g(u)-u. In a
yellow interval, h_{g} has its minimum at the left endpoint; in a teal
interval it has its minimum at the right endpoint. Define
\mathcal{L}_{u}(g;R)=\inf_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{[v,w]\in\mathcal{P}\,\mathrm{yellow}}(h_{g}(w)-h_{g}(v)),\qquad P_{g}(u;R)=g(R)-\mathcal{L}_{u}(g;R),
where the infimum is over finite labelled yellow–teal partitions of
[0,R] with piece lengths at most u. Set
\widehat{p}_{g}(u;b)=\max\{p_{g}(u;b),P_{g}(u;b-u)\},\quad\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,b)=\min\{f(b),\inf_{a\leq u\leq b}(f(u)+\widehat{p}_{g}(u;b))\}.
For a cap below \widehat{H}, Lemma 7.1 supplies finitely many templates, a
bounded piece count, and a common strict margin for all
u\in[a,\frac{b}{2}]. The local Stull estimates apply to their approximate
yellow and teal conditions. Discard [0,\kappa n_{j}], charge its full
complexity, and encode the rounded endpoints of each template in
O(\log n_{j}) bits. Every remaining local estimate is at a
macroscopic precision and has uniform constants. First let j\to\infty
and then \kappa\downarrow 0. Substituting these gains into
(26.9) proves
d_{x}(b)-d_{x}(a)\geq[\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,b)-f(a)]_{+},
(26.11)
and its reverse orientation. In particular, the number of witnesses
does not increase with j or with the competitor.
26.4 The prefixes remain valid
Appendix A proves its prefix bound using yellow blocks
and selected teal blocks. We check how its errors depend on the terminal precision. Use the fixed d chosen above. For a selected teal block
[a,b] with b\gg h_{n}, its root identities and (26.5) give
\frac{a}{b}\geq\frac{d}{5-d}-o(1)>0,\qquad a\leq\frac{b}{2},
and, for a\leq u\leq b,
F(u)\geq F(b)-\frac{d}{2}b+\frac{d-1}{2}u,\qquad F(u)\geq F(b)-(b-u),
where F(m)=K_{m}^{A}(y), with interpolation. The closed collision gain
is at least
\min\{b-u,\frac{d}{2}b-\frac{d-1}{2}u\}-o(b).
Each branch, added to F(u), is at least F(b)-o(b).
The lowering and transfer lemmas therefore pass the full teal source
increment to the distance. The source increment per unit length is at
least
c_{d}=\min\{1,\frac{d(3-d)}{5-2d}\}\geq\frac{2}{3}.
On a yellow block, the distance increment is at least its length,
up to the local error, while the source increment is at most twice
its length. The at-most-doubling yellow subpartitions use the same
uniform finite local estimates.
Every selected teal block at least halves the right endpoint; two
outer yellow blocks are not consecutive. Consequently the sum of
right endpoints is O(n) and there are O(\log n) relevant blocks.
Stop when the right endpoint is a fixed multiple of h_{n}, so the
discarded range costs O(h_{n}). For fixed local tolerance \xi, the
total error is
O_{d}(\xi n)+O_{C,d,\xi}((\log n)^{2})+O_{d}(\sqrt{n}).
(26.12)
The precision ratios ensure that all needed lower bounds remain above
the discarded cutoff. No estimate dividing by 2-D at D=2 is used.
If L_{n} is the total yellow length, the two sums are therefore
K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\geq\frac{2n+L_{n}}{3}-o(n),\qquad K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\geq K_{n}^{A}(y)-L_{n}-o(n).
Weighting them by \frac{3}{4} and \frac{1}{4}, and applying the same argument at
each fixed positive proportion of n_{j}, yields
d_{x}(u)\geq\frac{u}{2}+\frac{f(u)}{4},\qquad d_{y}(u)\geq\frac{u}{2}+\frac{g(u)}{4}.
(26.13)
In (26.12), n\to\infty precedes \xi\downarrow 0.
The same strict-cap argument with a source parent supplied as
finite side information proves the last assertion. In
Lemma 14.1, the parent precision
is a fixed positive fraction of n. Describing the parent precision costs O(\log n); the parent
itself is supplied as side information, and the projection, tangent, and complexity-lowering
steps just considered have the same uniform bounds. Thus its error
is at most \varepsilon n for all sufficiently large n, with
\varepsilon>0 fixed before taking the limit.
This proves Lemma 26.1.
∎
27 Selecting generic pairs at a fixed precision
After a fixed similarity, assume E\subset[0,1]^{2}. Fix an oracle A
which computes the dyadic masses of \mu and finite rational
Hausdorff approximations to E. It is fixed before any bad set or
pair is selected.
Lemma 27.1.
Assume the Frostman upper bound \mu(B(z,r))\leq Cr^{s}, s>1, and fix M>0 and 1<\sigma<s.
There is C<\infty such that for all sufficiently large n and
every Borel set B\subset E with \mu(B)>n^{-M}, there exist
x,y\in B satisfying (26.1)–(26.4) for m\leq 2n
and |x-y|\geq n^{-C}.
27.1 Quantitative description tails
Let Q_{m}(z) be the dyadic square of side 2^{-m} containing z.
The Kraft–Chaitin code for the computable masses satisfies, uniformly in
z,
K_{m}^{A}(z)\leq-\log_{2}\mu(Q_{m}(z))+c_{A}\log_{2}(m+2).
(27.1)
For any oracle W, Kraft’s inequality implies
\mu\{z:K^{W}(Q_{m}(z))<-\log_{2}\mu(Q_{m}(z))-h\}\leq 2^{-h}.
Point and square descriptions differ by O(\log(m+2)), uniformly
on the bounded support. Indeed, a precision-m approximation
identifies a bounded list of squares; the appropriate member costs a
bounded index. In the other direction, a bounded descendant index
selects a center within 2^{-m} of the point.
Apply this estimate with W=(A,x) for each fixed x, integrate in
x, and then do the same in the other orientation. The Frostman
upper bound for \mu(Q_{m}(z)) and (27.1) show that,
outside a subset of \mu\times\mu measure at most
O(n^{1-D}),
(27.2)
both independence inequalities and both lower-complexity floors hold
for all m\leq 2n, with a deficiency at most
C_{D}\log(n+2). Here D>0 is arbitrary and C_{D} is fixed once D
is chosen. The estimates are uniform in the real oracle being
conditioned on; no intersection over all pins of unspecified
almost-everywhere sets is taken.
27.2 A radial estimate uniform over separated restrictions
Choose 1<a<s with a<2, choose 2-a<b<1, and choose
1<p<\min\{2-b,\frac{b}{2-a}\},\qquad\tau=1-\frac{1}{p}>0.
All the corresponding energies of \mu are finite. The quantitative
estimate in Orponen’s proof [25, Section 3, equation (3.6)] has
the following consequence. If \nu,\eta\leq\mu have disjoint compact
supports, then
\int\|\pi_{x}\eta\|_{L^{p}(S^{1})}^{p}\,d\nu(x)\leq C_{\mu},\qquad\pi_{x}(y)=\frac{y-x}{|y-x|}.
(27.3)
The constant is uniform over these restrictions. To see the dependence,
use the weighted source |x-y|^{-1}\,d\eta(y) in Orponen’s estimate;
its bound uses only fixed powers of the a-energy of \eta and the
b-energy of \nu. Both are bounded by those of \mu. On the fixed
bounded support, the unweighted radial measure is dominated by a
constant times this weighted one. The support separation is used for
the approximation argument, and its reciprocal does not enter this
energy bound. Taking strict inequalities in the choice of p avoids
the endpoint in the angular energy estimate.
For every fixed pin x, the directions whose precision-m
complexity relative to (A,x) is below m-D\log_{2}(n+2) lie in at
most 2^{m}(n+2)^{-D} arcs of length O(2^{-m}). The union over
m\leq 2n has arclength at most O(n^{1-D}). By Hölder and
(27.3), the measure of pairs in \operatorname{supp}\nu\times\operatorname{supp}\eta
failing this directional condition is at most
O_{\mu}(n^{-(D-1)\tau}).
(27.4)
This remains true for pin-dependent unions of arcs. Interchanging
\nu and \eta gives the other orientation. Reversing a unit vector
costs only O(1) bits.
27.3 Two separated portions of the bad set
Choose a fixed integer J with Js>M+1. Partition the unit square
into a grid of side length h=\frac{1}{\lceil n^{J}\rceil}. There are
O(n^{2J}) cells. One cell Q has
\mu(B\cap Q)\gtrsim n^{-(M+2J)}.
The bounded collection of cells within distance 2h of Q is
contained in a ball of radius O(h), so its mass is
O(n^{-Js})<\frac{\mu(B)}{2} for large n. Among the remaining cells
there is an R with
\mu(B\cap R)\gtrsim n^{-(M+2J)},\qquad\operatorname{dist}(Q,R)\geq h.
Consequently
(\mu\times\mu)((B\cap Q)\times(B\cap R))\gtrsim n^{-(2M+4J)}.
(27.5)
Use (27.3) with \nu=\mu|_{Q} and \eta=\mu|_{R}.
Their supports are contained in separated closed cells; the cells’
boundaries are null because s>1. Choose D so large that
D-1>2M+4J+2,\qquad(D-1)\tau>2M+4J+2.
The sum of the description and angular exceptional measures is then
smaller than (27.5). A remaining pair belongs to
B\times B and has all the required finite-precision properties.
Since h\gtrsim n^{-J}, increasing the fixed constant C also gives
|x-y|\geq n^{-C}. This proves Lemma 27.1.
Remark 27.2.
Neither B nor its restricted measures are added to the oracle.
The mass code always uses the original \mu. This avoids a possible
circularity between choosing a bad set and selecting incompressible
points from it. Shmerkin’s lower box argument [30, Section 6.3]
uses the same broad strategy of selecting a pair from a set of mass
at least a negative power of the precision. The grid and Kraft
estimates above give the version needed for the present complexity
hypotheses.
27.4 Coding a small pinned cover
Lemma 27.3.
For the fixed oracle A above, there is a constant c such that
for every x\in E, r\in\Delta_{x}E, and n\geq 1,
K_{n}^{A,x}(r)\leq\log_{2}N_{2^{-n}}(\Delta_{x}E)+c\log_{2}(n+2).
(27.6)
Proof.
Using A and the oracle x, compute a finite rational approximation
to \Delta_{x}E with Hausdorff error at most \frac{2^{-n}}{100}. Round its
members to a grid of mesh \frac{2^{-n}}{4} and remove repetitions. The
resulting list is a 2^{-n}-net for \Delta_{x}E. It has at most a fixed
constant times N_{2^{-n}}(\Delta_{x}E) members, since an interval of radius
2^{-n}, enlarged by the approximation error, meets only a bounded
number of the grid cells. The index of a suitable list member,
together with n, has length at most the right side of
(27.6). The algorithm is the same for every pin.
∎
The cover bound is an upper bound for the description of every
distance at that pin. That is why two bad pins force the common
distance to be short in both orientations.
28 Matching endpoints at a fixed precision
Fix an oracle A encoding the original Frostman measure and the
compact set, as in Section 27. Given n, let
m_{n}=\lfloor\sqrt{\log_{2}(n+2)}\rfloor
(increase small values to one). For each point z, record the
m_{n} values
\left\lfloor\frac{m_{n}}{n}K^{A}_{\lfloor j\frac{n}{m_{n}}\rfloor}(z)\right\rfloor,\qquad j=1,\ldots,m_{n}.
Each value has O(m_{n}) possibilities on the fixed bounded support.
Thus the number of possible records is
(O(m_{n}))^{m_{n}}=2^{O(\sqrt{\log n}\log\log n)}=n^{o(1)}.
(28.1)
If two points have the same record, the uniform precision-increment
bound gives
\sup_{1\leq r\leq n}|K_{r}^{A}(x)-K_{r}^{A}(y)|\leq O(\frac{n}{m_{n}})+O(\log n)=o(n).
(28.2)
Indeed, the discrepancy at a recorded precision is at most \frac{n}{m_{n}};
between records, increasing precision costs at most twice the gap,
plus a logarithmic error. The initial interval is handled by
0\leq K_{r}^{A}(z)\leq 2r+O(\log n).
Let B\subset E be Borel with \mu(B)>n^{-M}. Partition B by these
records. For large n, one class has mass at least
n^{-(M+1)}. Apply Lemma 27.1 inside that class. We obtain x_{n},y_{n}\in B, separated by
at least n^{-C}, such that for r\leq 2n both points have the
prescribed dimension floor, both directional deficiencies are
O(\log n), and both full-oracle independence deficiencies are
O(\log n). In addition, (28.2) holds.
No record or selected class is put into the oracle. The selection
lemma works inside an arbitrary Borel set of the stated mass.
Thus all its constants remain uniform after replacing M by
M+1. Along every convergent subsequence of the selected
normalized profiles, (28.2) forces f=g.
This finite-scale matching does not assert that there is one fixed
pair with matching profiles at every scale. The endpoints may
change with n, which is permitted in the lower-box proof.
28.1 The lower-box bound and its exceptional sets
The dimension-dependent part of Theorem 1.3.
Fix q<L(s) and M>0. Choose
1<\sigma<\min\{s,\frac{9}{8}\} with q<\frac{3\sigma+1}{5}.
Suppose that along infinitely many n the set
B_{n}=\{x\in E:N_{2^{-n}}(\Delta_{x}E)<2^{qn}\}
has mass greater than n^{-M}. Select generic matching pairs from
B_{n} by the preceding construction, using lower complexity floor
\sigma. Lemma 26.1 supplies limiting
profiles satisfying the full scalar system. They have f=g and
f(u)\geq\sigma u.
Lemma 27.3 implies
K_{n}^{A,x_{n}}(|x_{n}-y_{n}|),\ K_{n}^{A,y_{n}}(|x_{n}-y_{n}|)\leq qn+O(\log n).
Both terminal distance profiles are at most q.
Proposition 12.1 instead makes each of them at least
\frac{3\sigma+1}{5}>q. This contradiction proves (1.4).
Take M=2, apply Borel–Cantelli, and then let q increase to
L(s) through a countable sequence to obtain the bound L(s) in (1.3). The universal part is proved in Section 30.
∎
The spectral assertions in Corollary 1.4.
First assume E is compact. The map x\mapsto\Delta_{x}E is
continuous for the Hausdorff metric; the covering conditions above
and hence the lower-box sublevel sets are Borel.
For \frac{4}{5}\leq q<\frac{7}{8}, put p=\frac{5q-1}{3}.
If the Borel set F=\{x\in E:\underline{\dim}_{\mathrm{B}}\Delta_{x}E\leq q\} had Hausdorff
dimension greater than p, choose an r-Frostman probability
measure carried by F, with p<r<\dim_{\mathrm{H}}F. Since L(r)>q,
Theorem 1.3 is a contradiction.
At q=\frac{7}{8}, the same reasoning applies to the set with
\underline{\dim}_{\mathrm{B}}\Delta_{x}E<\frac{7}{8}, using any exponent r>\frac{9}{8} below its alleged
dimension. This proves the endpoint bound.
For a bounded set E, apply these results to its compact closure.
The identity \Delta_{x}\overline{E}=\overline{\Delta_{x}E} and invariance of
lower box dimension under closure prove the last assertion.
∎
The proof does not give an exceptional-set bound of one for
\underline{\dim}_{\mathrm{B}}\Delta_{x}E<L(\dim_{\mathrm{H}}E). The Frostman measure on a putative
exceptional set can have exponent smaller than \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E.
The statements of Corollary 1.4 give the precise
conclusions with the correct quantifiers.
29 The geometric contradiction near the endpoint
The rigid plateau has a concrete geometric meaning. The source
parent at exponent \frac{3}{10} admits very little mass in any
one annulus, while the pin measure is spread over the scales
between \frac{1}{5} and \frac{3}{5}. We show that these two
facts are incompatible with only \delta^{-\frac{4}{5}} radii at each pin.
The finite statement below records the hypotheses separately
from the one-sided plateau parameters used earlier.
Proposition 29.1 (Finite configurations near the rigid plateau).
There is an absolute \varepsilon_{*}>0 with the following property.
Fix C>0 and a pin Frostman exponent 1+\varkappa>1.
Let 0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*} and \delta=2^{-n}.
Suppose a common source set, a set of actual pins, and assigned
radial incidences satisfy (F1)–(F4), with
the following replacements for their parameters.
q=\frac{4}{5}+\varepsilon,\qquad b=\delta^{1-\varepsilon},\qquad\eta\asymp\delta^{\frac{3}{10}},\qquad F=\frac{1}{2}.
The source-parent mass bound is \delta^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}.
No branch-specific source bound is assumed. For the pin ancestors,
replace T_{\theta} by
T(u)=\max\left\{0,\frac{1}{5}-\operatorname{dist}\left(u,\left[\frac{1}{5},\frac{3}{5}\right]\right)\right\}
and replace \tau_{0} by \varepsilon.
Use a fixed mesh of candidate pair-separation exponents from
\frac{1}{5}-3\varepsilon to \frac{3}{10}, with consecutive
gaps at most \varepsilon, and corresponding pin ancestor mass
classes. Assume p\geq n^{-C}.
Polynomial factors in n are allowed in the angular bounds and
in the fixed geometric constants, and the source and pin supports
may have separation at least n^{-C}.
Then no such configuration exists for all sufficiently large n.
The threshold may depend on the fixed measures, C, \varkappa
and \varepsilon.
Proof.
We first check that the common thinning and the global circle
drawing remain applicable under these hypotheses.
Retain each source vertex with probability a fixed small multiple
of p\frac{m}{b}, as in Lemma 17.1. This is at most
one because m\leq b. The expected occupancy of a source parent
in a radial bin is polynomially bounded, so a Chernoff estimate
and a union bound make every such occupancy polynomially bounded.
The total source count is at most b^{-1} up to polynomial
factors, and the retained incidence count is at least
(b\upsilon_{f})^{-1} up to polynomial factors.
For angular intervals of length at least
\rho_{\min}=\delta^{\frac{4}{5}+4\varepsilon}, the additive
logarithmic sampling error is absorbed by \frac{|J|}{b}, since
\frac{\rho_{\min}}{b}=\delta^{-\frac{1}{5}+5\varepsilon}
tends to infinity by a fixed power. The source-parent occupancy
estimate, if needed, has the positive margin
1-\varepsilon-(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{2}.
Thus the thinning uses the same source vertices at every pin.
The proof of Proposition 17.3 now
applies to this thinned set. Its two vanishing ratios are
b\delta^{-q}=\delta^{\frac{1}{5}-2\varepsilon},\qquad\frac{\delta^{2q}}{b}=\delta^{\frac{3}{5}+3\varepsilon}.
These are the only numerical uses of the earlier plateau
parameters in the global drawing argument. It produces rows
whose pairs have at least
k_{1}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\frac{\delta^{2q}}{b\upsilon_{f}}
witnessing pins. The argument of
Proposition 17.5 selects rows at a common
pair-separation scale. Writing that scale as \delta^{a} and the
tube width as \rho=\delta^{\zeta}, we have
\frac{1}{5}-O(\varepsilon)\leq a\leq\frac{3}{10},\qquad\zeta=1-a+O(\varepsilon).
Only the global richness is used; no second pruning or
branch-specific source estimate is required.
Let \upsilon_{\zeta}=\delta^{\gamma} be the common pin ancestor
mass. The sampling proof of Lemma 17.7
retains one pin per \rho-cell and preserves every witness set.
Indeed, its expected witness count is at least
\frac{k_{1}\upsilon_{f}}{\upsilon_{\zeta}}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\delta^{-\gamma+\frac{3}{5}+3\varepsilon}.
The Frostman bound implies \gamma\geq\zeta-o(1), and
\zeta\geq\frac{7}{10}-O(\varepsilon), leaving a strict
margin of \frac{1}{10}-O(\varepsilon). Chernoff’s estimate
therefore applies simultaneously to all source pairs.
The resulting witness set P and generating subset P^{\prime} satisfy
\#P\leq\delta^{-\gamma},\qquad\#P^{\prime}\gtrsim_{\mathrm{pol}}\delta^{-\gamma},\qquad k_{\mathrm{rich}}\geq\delta^{-\gamma+\frac{3}{5}+O(\varepsilon)+o(1)}.
(29.1)
The normalized ball counts of P^{\prime} have exponent one, with
polynomial constants, by the original Frostman bound.
The terminal mass \upsilon_{f} has canceled.
The direction selection of Lemma 18.2 uses only the
row count, its angular upper bounds and its pair-separation
range. Those properties hold here. Use the Katz–Tardos
five-entry exponent \alpha=\frac{7}{19}. With the fixed
direction-selection tolerances included in O(\varepsilon),
the direction exponent satisfies
m=\sigma\zeta=\alpha+(1-2\alpha)a+O(\varepsilon).
Choose the exponent in the Ren–Wang theorem slightly below
\sigma, from a fixed finite grid, exactly as in
Proposition 18.4. Since 0<\sigma<1,
the pin exponent one and the middle branch of that theorem imply
\#\mathcal{T}\geq\delta^{-\Lambda+O(\varepsilon)+o(1)},\qquad\Lambda=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\alpha}{2}+(1-3\alpha)a.
(29.2)
Each tube retains the witnesses counted in
(29.1). The finite grid makes the
Ren–Wang tolerances uniform in the possible direction exponents.
For the upper count take R=\delta^{\frac{1}{5}} and
h=\delta^{\frac{3}{5}}. The pin ancestor bound implies
\#(P\cap B(z,r))\leq\delta^{-\gamma+\frac{1}{5}-O(\varepsilon)-o(1)}r\quad(h\leq r\leq 3R).
Summing over dyadic annuli and the ball of radius h proves
the reciprocal-occupancy estimate of
Lemma 19.2 with
H\leq\delta^{-\gamma+\frac{4}{5}-O(\varepsilon)-o(1)}.
The diagonal is covered as well, since the ancestor at exponent
\frac{3}{5} has mass at most \delta^{\frac{4}{5}-\varepsilon}
and contains the occupied \rho-cell.
At zero error the three strict sampling conditions have margins
\frac{3}{5}+\frac{1}{5}-\frac{3}{5}=\frac{1}{5},\qquad\zeta+\frac{1}{5}-\frac{1}{5}-\frac{3}{5}\geq\frac{1}{10},\qquad\zeta-\frac{3}{5}\geq\frac{1}{10}.
They remain strict when all tolerances are sufficiently small.
Substitution in (19.4) yields
\#\mathcal{T}\leq\delta^{-U-O(\varepsilon)-o(1)},\qquad U=\max\left\{3\frac{3}{5}-\frac{2}{5}-\frac{2}{5},2\frac{3}{5}-\frac{1}{5}\right\}=1.
(29.3)
Because 1-3\alpha<0, the least lower exponent occurs at
a=\frac{3}{10}. Consequently
\Lambda-U\geq\frac{3\alpha}{5}-\frac{1}{5}=\frac{2}{95}>0.
(29.4)
Fix all exponent tolerances so that their total cost is less than
half this gap. Then take n large enough to absorb the
polynomial factors. The lower and upper tube counts contradict
each other.
If the support separation is only n^{-C}, angular rounding
and the circle-to-bisector identities introduce fixed powers
of n. Enlarging the fine scale by such a factor changes its
precision by O(\log n). The mass caps and exponent inequalities
above change by o(1) only. This proves the stated uniformity.
∎
29.1 Realizing the finite hypotheses on a common relation
Fix a Frostman measure, an oracle encoding it and its compact
support, a lower dimension exponent greater than one, and a
polynomial relation-mass loss n^{-M}.
The description tails in Section 27
can be imposed on a relation of this size. Choose their
deficiency constant large enough that the discarded product
mass is o(n^{-M}). Simultaneously at both endpoints and for
all m\leq 2n, the retained pairs satisfy
K_{m}^{A}(z)=-\log_{2}\mu(Q_{m}(z))+O(\log n).
The finite generic conditions of Lemma 26.1
are imposed in the same way. Two fixed restrictions of the
original measure may be used for pins and sources.
Let F_{x} be the density of the radial pushforward of the
source restriction. The radial L^{p} estimate
(27.3) implies
\int\int_{\{F_{x}>n^{D}\}}F_{x}(\theta)\,d\theta\,d\nu(x)\leq n^{-D(p-1)}\int\|F_{x}\|_{p}^{p}\,d\nu(x).
For sufficiently large fixed D, removing these pairs costs
o(n^{-M}). Every retained fiber has angular mass at most
n^{D}|I| on an arc I, and this property survives all further
restrictions.
Record both endpoint profiles and both distance profiles on
the mesh of (28.1), with the same rounded
heights. There are n^{o(1)} joint records, so one class retains
polynomial pair mass. Its profiles differ from their
representatives by o(1) uniformly on [0,1].
The two endpoint records need not agree.
Bin the finitely many source, parent and pin-ancestor masses
needed at the candidate scales. These restrictions cost further
polynomial factors. The oracle remains the original one.
We check the annular cap carefully. At parent exponent
a_{\mathrm{par}}=\frac{3}{10} let
C=Q_{\lfloor a_{\mathrm{par}}n\rfloor}(y).
The stable tail certificate and the conditional transfer in
Lemma 26.1 imply
K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|\mid C)\geq(1-\varepsilon)n-K_{\lfloor a_{\mathrm{par}}n\rfloor}^{A}(y).
For an occupied radial bin J, the conditional mass code from
Proposition 14.2 bounds the same
quantity from above by
-\log_{2}\frac{\mu(C\cap\{z:|x-z|\in J\})}{\mu(C)}+O(\log n),
allowing a bounded enlargement of J.
The base mass code at the parent cancels \mu(C). Thus the
original source measure satisfies
\mu(C\cap\{z:|x-z|\in J\})\leq\delta^{1-O(\varepsilon)}.
The source floor at the parent is
f(\frac{3}{10})\geq\frac{1}{2}-O(\varepsilon).
The stable plateau, its Lipschitz extension, and the base floor
produce the pin-ancestor bound with the function T in
Proposition 29.1.
Lemma 20.1 therefore
supplies one common source set and actual pins with assigned
radial labels satisfying that proposition, after slightly
enlarging the fixed error tolerance.
The source law has not been normalized separately at different pins.
Choose the stability tolerance small enough for
Proposition 29.1, and then choose
0<\eta_{0}<\min\left\{\eta(\varepsilon),\varepsilon,\frac{3}{40}\right\}
with any further fixed reduction needed to absorb the errors
above. Lemma 13.3 and compactness imply that
finite generic profiles with both terminal values at most
\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0} satisfy those stable inequalities once
n is sufficiently large. Otherwise a sequence violating them
would converge, by Lemma 26.1, to a scalar
system contradicting the stability lemma.
The resulting finite configurations are impossible.
The constant \eta_{0} is absolute. The geometric gap and the
scalar stability modulus are independent of the measure.
Frostman constants, radial energies, oracle constants and
polynomial relation losses affect only the starting precision.
They remain fixed while n tends to infinity.
30 The universal unpinned and lower-box bounds
30.1 General unpinned Hausdorff dimension
Suppose a compact E with \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E>1 satisfies \dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta E<\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0}. Choose q strictly between these two numbers.
Choose a Frostman measure of exponent greater than one and restrict
it to separated source and pin portions, denoted by \mu and \nu. Encode the measure and
the compact set using a base oracle A as in Section 5.
The effectively compact point-to-set principle implies
\liminf_{n}\frac{K_{n}^{A}(|x-y|)}{n}<q
for every pair of points in these portions. Work with a slightly
smaller intermediate threshold so that logarithmic coding costs
can be absorbed in qn.
On the fixed positive-measure good-pair relation G, both
directional hypotheses, full-oracle independence estimates,
angular-density restrictions, and base mass codes hold. Let R_{n}
retain pairs with low unconditioned distance complexity.
Each pair belongs to infinitely many such R_{n}. The Tonelli argument in Lemma 20.4 gives (\nu\times\mu)(R_{n})\geq n^{-2} at infinitely
many scales. Low unconditioned complexity implies low complexity
in both orientations:
K_{n}^{A,x}(|x-y|),\ K_{n}^{A,y}(|x-y|)\leq qn+O(\log n).
Bin the two endpoint profiles and the distance profiles jointly;
there are n^{o(1)} records on the usual slowly growing scale mesh.
A common retained relation still has polynomial mass.
The resulting bilateral profiles and common finite configurations are excluded by Section 29, since q<\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0}. This proves Theorem 1.2 for compact sets.
For analytic sets, first choose a compact subset with Hausdorff
dimension greater than one; its distance set is contained in the
original distance set.
A recurrent relation of low
distance complexity need not contain many pairs in a single profile
class; a dense relation can connect two different classes. Therefore
finite matching alone does not justify the Hausdorff conclusion. Proposition 13.1 removes precisely that
restriction. It does not improve the abstract scalar four-fifths
bound by itself. The geometric contradiction is still essential.
30.2 Internal pinned lower box dimension
For B_{n}=\{x\in E:N_{2^{-n}}(\Delta_{x}E)<2^{qn}\} we prove
\mu(B_{n})\leq n^{-M}
for every fixed M>0 at all sufficiently large n, whenever
q<\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0}. We use Lemmas 26.1, 27.1, and 27.3. If this conclusion fails, choose two
polynomially separated portions of B_{n} and retain a polynomial-mass
product relation. Both endpoints are bad pins at the same original
precision, so both conditioned distance complexities are low.
Joint binning of arbitrary endpoint profiles suffices, because Proposition 13.1 allows f\neq g. The finite
geometric contradiction above then proves the same quantitative
tail bound. Borel–Cantelli proves the almost-everywhere lower-box
claim. A Frostman measure on a hypothetical internal exceptional
set of dimension greater than one contradicts this conclusion; closure
extends that conclusion from compact to arbitrary bounded E,
since lower box dimension is unchanged by closure.
Together with Section 28, this proves
Theorem 1.3. For q<L_{*}(s), either q<L(s) or
q<\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0}, so one of the two tail estimates applies.
The same exceptional-set argument proves (1.5).
31 The gains and the remaining limitations
The universal unpinned estimate improves \frac{3}{4} by
\frac{1}{20}+\eta_{0}. The gain has two parts. Reciprocal
partition estimates account for \frac{1}{20}; endpoint rigidity
and the common-source geometry account for \eta_{0}.
The universal external pinned gain is
q_{*}-\frac{3}{4}=0.03131\ldots.
The distinctions between these arguments matter when considering
how far the methods can be pushed.
31.1 What can be improved, and the limits of the methods
There is one proved obstruction to a uniform pinned improvement
using only the pointwise inequalities of this paper.
Proposition 7.3 constructs abstract
profiles satisfying both prefix estimates and both transfer
estimates strengthened by the full partition functional, with
f(u),g(u)\geq(1+\theta)u but
d_{x}(1)=\frac{3}{4}+\theta and d_{y}(1)=1 for every sufficiently
small \theta>0. Consequently, further optimization of those
same constraints cannot force a fixed positive improvement over
\frac{3}{4} at a prescribed pin. The common-source mass bounds
supply an additional constraint, and it is this additional
information that permits our pinned improvement. The obstruction
concerns the stated scalar inequalities. We do not prove that
its profiles come from a pair of points satisfying all the
geometric hypotheses, or that every possible pointwise argument
has the same limitation.
31.2 The scalar obstruction at four fifths
The reciprocal argument reaches \frac{4}{5}. The next proposition
shows that the scalar inequalities used in that argument cannot force
a larger universal constant. In the construction, the excess
f(u)-u first rises, stays constant for an interval of length
\frac{t}{5}, and then falls. A partition piece of length at most
\frac{t}{5} cannot join any of that rise to the later fall. We use
this separation to compute the partition loss directly.
Proposition 31.1 (The terminal-flat profiles).
For 1<t\leq\frac{5}{4}, put, on [0,1],
f(u)=g(u)=\min\{2u,u+\frac{2t}{5},t\},\qquad d_{x}(u)=d_{y}(u)=\min\{u,\frac{4t}{5}\}.
The functions f,g are nondecreasing and 2-Lipschitz, and
d_{x},d_{y} are nondecreasing and 1-Lipschitz. All four vanish at
zero. These profiles satisfy f(u),g(u)\geq tu, the ordinary prefix
inequalities, and both full transfer inequalities
d_{x}(b)-d_{x}(a)\geq[\widehat{H}_{f,g}(a,b)-f(a)]_{+},\qquad d_{y}(b)-d_{y}(a)\geq[\widehat{H}_{g,f}(a,b)-g(a)]_{+}
for 0<a\leq b\leq 1. They also satisfy every strengthened prefix
d_{x}(u),d_{y}(u)\geq(1-2b(p,s))u+b(p,s)f(u),\qquad 1\leq p\leq s\leq t,
where
c(p,s)=\frac{p(2-s)+s}{5-2s},\qquad b(p,s)=\frac{1-c(p,s)}{2-c(p,s)}.
Consequently, these scalar inequalities cannot force a universal
unpinned constant larger than \frac{4}{5}, even when the lower
dimensions of both endpoint profiles are strictly greater than one.
Proof.
The slopes of f=g are 2,1,0, in that order. The slopes of
d_{x}=d_{y} are 1,0. These observations prove the monotonicity
and Lipschitz assertions. Also 2u\geq tu, t\geq tu, and
u+\frac{2t}{5}\geq tu for 0\leq u\leq 1 and 1<t\leq\frac{5}{4}.
Write h=f-u. It increases with slope one up to \frac{2t}{5}, is
constant on [\frac{2t}{5},\frac{3t}{5}], and decreases with slope minus one
afterwards. Fix 0<v\leq\frac{t}{5}. An interval of length at most v
cannot meet both the increasing and decreasing parts of h in
positive length. Thus every admissible yellow–teal partition of
[0,R] pays for the entire increase of h on that interval.
Conversely, partitioning at the two corners and subdividing the
monotone pieces realizes precisely that cost. Therefore
\mathcal{L}_{v}(f;R)=\min\{R,\frac{2t}{5}\},\qquad P_{f}(v;R)=\min\{R,\frac{3t}{5}\}.
The closed gain is also at most \frac{3t}{5}: for R\leq\frac{3t}{5} use its
bound by R, and for R\geq\frac{3t}{5} use
\frac{f(R)+v}{2}=\frac{t+v}{2}\leq\frac{3t}{5}. It follows that
\widehat{p}_{f}(v;b)\leq\frac{3t}{5}\qquad(0<v\leq\frac{t}{5}).
(31.1)
We now check the transfer. If b\leq\frac{4t}{5}, the gain cap
\widehat{p}_{f}(a;b)\leq b-a gives the assertion immediately.
If a\geq\frac{4t}{5}, then f(a)=f(b)=t and the right hand side is
zero. Suppose, then, that a<\frac{4t}{5}<b. When a\geq\frac{t}{5}, we have
h(a)\geq\frac{t}{5}, and hence
f(a)+\frac{4t}{5}-a=h(a)+\frac{4t}{5}\geq t\geq\widehat{H}_{f,f}(a,b).
When a<\frac{t}{5}, choose the starting scale a in the infimum defining
\widehat{H} and use (31.1). Consequently
\widehat{H}_{f,f}(a,b)-f(a)\leq\frac{3t}{5}\leq\frac{4t}{5}-a.
The two orientations agree because f=g.
It remains to check the prefixes. The function c(p,s) increases
in both variables on the stated range, and b decreases as a
function of c. Since f(u)\leq 2u, the strongest displayed
prefix occurs at p=s=t. With k=t-1, its coefficient is
b(t,t)=\frac{1-3k+k^{2}}{(1-k)(4-k)}.
For u\leq\frac{4t}{5}, the prefix follows again from f(u)\leq 2u.
For u\geq\frac{4t}{5}, we have f(u)=t. The right side of
the prefix inequality increases with u, so it suffices to check
u=1. The difference is
\frac{4t}{5}-\{1-b(t,t)(2-t)\}=\frac{t^{2}}{5(5-t)}>0.
The ordinary prefix corresponds to coefficient \frac{1}{4}, which is
no stronger. Finally, d_{x}(1)=d_{y}(1)=\frac{4t}{5} and t may decrease
to one. This proves the asserted scalar obstruction. It does not
assert that these profiles are realized by a geometric configuration.
∎
The proposition and Proposition 10.2 identify
\frac{4}{5} as the exact universal constant for the stated full
scalar system. This does not establish geometric sharpness. We have
not realized these profiles by a Euclidean pair with all the required
independence properties and a common source measure.
31.3 The two geometric comparisons
The first geometric upper count has a sharper local limitation.
For a fixed admissible restricted-sum exponent \alpha, the
comparison in (21.11) is
L-U_{1}\geq\frac{3\alpha}{4}(1-2\Delta)-(10-3\alpha)\omega.
The coefficient 10-3\alpha is attained in the abstract
parameter range of Proposition 21.1 by
\varsigma=2, \chi=-1, u_{0}=\mathfrak{e}=0, \varpi=1, and
\mathfrak{u}=2. At transfer level one, where \Delta=0 and
q=\frac{3}{4}+\omega, this uniform comparison therefore requires
q<\frac{3}{4}+\frac{3\alpha}{40-12\alpha}.
Letting \alpha\nearrow\alpha_{*} yields q_{*}. Optimizing the
same coefficient over the same parameter range cannot improve
this bound. It could improve if a stronger restricted-sum
estimate supplied a larger admissible \alpha, if one of the
geometric counts improved, or if additional geometric information
excluded the parameters attaining the coefficient. We make no
claim that those parameters are realized by the configurations
arising from a common source measure. Nor do we claim that
\alpha_{*} is the optimal restricted-sum exponent. Substituting
a stronger input would also require checking the other scale
and richness inequalities in the proof.
The second upper count uses angular information from both
endpoints. Its effective parent exponent is
B_{\mathrm{eff}}=\min\{\zeta,\alpha+(2-3\alpha)a\},
and Section 23 compares the lower count
with both entries. The resulting sufficient conditions are
\omega<\frac{-1+4\alpha+6(1-2\alpha)\Delta}{12(3-2\alpha)},\qquad\omega<\frac{\alpha(1-2\Delta)}{4(2+\alpha)}.
After the certificate iteration these become q<Q_{b} and
q<Q_{f}. Their minimum is the bound proved in
Section 24.
The controlling branch changes
at S_{\mathrm{switch}} after \alpha\nearrow\alpha_{*}. At a
fixed source dimension, improving only the larger branch leaves
the minimum unchanged. Improving the smaller branch requires a
better count, a stronger angular bound, or a further restriction
on the allowable row and parent scales. The verified branch
switch is exact for the two displayed formulas, but the paper
does not prove that these formulas describe the best possible
use of the common relation. In particular, the present second
comparison does not raise the universal pinned constant as the
source dimension decreases to one. Proposition E.1
in Appendix E also shows that
combining the two displayed counts before optimizing does not
improve their threshold within the stated parameter set. Two
explicit choices of parameters suffice to prove this limitation.
They need not arise from a common source measure.
The constant continuation Y(t)=\frac{7}{8} for t\geq S_{0}
comes from the range \Delta<\frac{1}{8} of the reciprocal
estimate. In the source-dimension iteration, the limiting value
of \Delta reaches \frac{1}{8} at t=S_{0}.
The matching-profile argument reaches the same boundary when
the source floor is \frac{9}{8}.
Extending these calculations requires further control at and
beyond this transfer level. The value \frac{7}{8} is not an
upper bound for the other methods of the paper; R(t) exceeds it
for larger t.
The fixed gain \eta_{0} uses a strict geometric exponent gap of
\frac{2}{95} at the limiting configuration. This number is
not the value of \eta_{0}. A numerical dimension gain would
require quantitative control of the stability lemma and all
subsequent tolerances. The qualitative argument suffices for
the stated theorem.
Several losses affect the scale at which a contradiction occurs
but not its limiting exponent. Fixed multiplicative constants,
dyadic rounding, and polynomial factors in n change the
logarithms of the counts by at most O(\log n). Coding errors
have the same order. These contributions vanish after division
by n. The fixed exponent tolerances are chosen arbitrarily
small after a positive leading margin has been established.
Removing these losses alone cannot turn a zero leading margin
into a positive one or raise the constants above. Likewise, the
exceptional-set threshold one uses radial projection input with
source and pin exponents greater than one. Extending that part
of the proof to smaller pin exponents needs an appropriate
replacement for that input, not only a numerical reoptimization
of the final count.
Finally, every distance set is a subset of \mathbb{R}, so its
Hausdorff dimension is at most one. The conclusions giving full
pinned dimension when \dim_{\mathrm{H}}E\geq\frac{3}{2} have
already reached this absolute ceiling. Below that range, the
paper proves the obstructions and limits of individual
comparisons described above.
It does not determine the best bound
obtainable by combining all the information in a common-source
configuration. The optimal unpinned constant and the optimal use of the two
pinned geometric counts remain open.
Question 31.2.
Can the stability statement in Lemma 13.3 be made
quantitative enough to give a useful numerical value of \eta_{0}?
Question 31.3.
Can the two-sided angular information in the common relation raise
the universal pinned constant q_{*}, rather than only the bound at
larger source dimensions?
Question 31.4.
What additional compatibility across scales would turn the finite
rich-bisector counts into a Hausdorff-dimension estimate for one
fixed limiting family of rich bisectors?
Appendix A The two prefix bounds with source information retained
References to equations and lemmas of Stull [33] and
Fiedler–Stull [7] in this appendix use arXiv:2207.12501v2
and arXiv:2309.11701v1, respectively.
We give the partition calculation behind Lemma 3.1,
including its endpoint at planar packing dimension two. The inputs
are the local yellow-interval estimate of [7, Lemma 27], the
circle-collision inequality of [7, Lemma 30], and the
distance-transfer lemma of [7, Lemma 15]. The latter two are
used in Section 4. We retain the source complexity
through the summation instead of replacing it by a dimension bound.
Fix
1<d<\min\{\dim^{A}x,\dim^{A}y,\frac{5}{4}\}.
The upper cutoff \frac{5}{4} is a convenient choice for this
auxiliary parameter because it keeps the rate
\frac{d(3-d)}{5-2d} used below between \frac{2}{3} and one,
with positive denominators. Such a d always exists by (H1),
so this choice imposes no upper bound on either endpoint dimension.
The final inequalities in Proposition A.2
are independent of d.
Let F be the piecewise linear function on [1,\infty) with
F(m)=K_{m}^{A}(y) at every positive integer m, as in
[7, §4.3].
Logarithmic interpolation and rounding errors
will be included in the errors below. Thus
ds-o(s)\leq F(s)\leq 2s+O(\log(s+2)). Write
\Delta_{y}[a,b]=K_{b}^{A,x}(y)-K_{a}^{A,x}(y),\qquad\Delta_{d}[a,b]=K_{b}^{A,x}(|x-y|)-K_{a}^{A,x}(|x-y|).
Finite-precision chain rules identify these differences with the
corresponding conditional increments up to O(\log b).
Lemma A.1 (The selected teal intervals).
For every sufficiently large terminal precision r, the partition
of [7, §4.3], with parameters d and D=2, has the following
properties, up to errors that sum to o(r).
On a yellow block [a,b],
\Delta_{d}[a,b]\geq b-a-o(b),\qquad\Delta_{y}[a,b]\leq 2(b-a)+O(\log b).
On a selected teal block [a,b],
\Delta_{d}[a,b]\geq\Delta_{y}[a,b]-o(b),\qquad\Delta_{y}[a,b]\geq c_{d}(b-a)-o(b),\qquad c_{d}=\min\{1,\frac{d(3-d)}{5-2d}\}\geq\frac{2}{3}.
The same assertions hold with the two endpoints exchanged.
Proof.
Here is the construction and the information it preserves. Starting
from a right endpoint b, first take the maximal terminal block
that is a union of yellow intervals [v,w] with w\leq 2v.
The local yellow estimate, summed on its greedy at-most-doubling
partition, gives \Delta_{d}[a,b]\geq b-a-o(b).
The source upper bound follows from the planar precision-increment
bound. If there is no such terminal block, let t<b be the largest
solution of
F(t)=F(b)-\frac{d}{2}b+\frac{d-1}{2}t,
and let t^{\prime}<b be the largest solution of
F(t^{\prime})=F(b)-(b-t^{\prime}). Set a=\max\{t,t^{\prime}\}.
These are equations (10)–(11) of [7] with D=2.
The lower bound F(b)>db-o(b) and continuity provide the first
root.
The absence of a terminal yellow interval ensures the teal
alternative. Above the last roots we have
F(s)\geq F(b)-\frac{d}{2}b+\frac{d-1}{2}s,\qquad F(s)\geq F(b)-(b-s),\qquad a\leq s\leq b.
(A.1)
The block is teal. The halving argument in
[7, Lemma 25] implies a\leq\frac{b}{2}.
The first inequality in (A.1), together
with F(a)\leq 2a+o(b) and F(b)\geq db-o(b), yields
\frac{a}{b}\geq\frac{d}{5-d}-o(1).
In particular, the block endpoints have a bounded positive ratio.
If a=t^{\prime}, its source increment is b-a+o(b).
Otherwise the defining identity at a=t implies
\frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}=\frac{d}{2}+\frac{a}{2(b-a)}\geq\frac{d}{2}+\frac{d}{2(5-2d)}-o(1)=\frac{d(3-d)}{5-2d}-o(1).
Independence (H3) at the two endpoints transfers this increment
bound to \Delta_{y}. The coefficient is at least \frac{2}{3},
because
\frac{d(3-d)}{5-2d}-\frac{2}{3}=\frac{(d-1)(10-3d)}{3(5-2d)}\geq 0.
It remains to justify the transfer of the whole teal source
increment. Cap the source at m=K_{b}^{A}(y)-\theta b, where
\theta>0 is fixed and small. For a circle competitor at separation
2^{-s}, a\leq s\leq\frac{b}{2}, the capped collision inequality
of Section 4 gives
K_{b}^{A,G}(z)\geq\min\{m,F(s)\}+\min\left\{b-s,\frac{d}{2}b-\frac{d-1}{2}s\right\}-o(b).
Both alternatives in the second minimum, added to F(s), are at
least F(b) by (A.1). If the source term
is capped, the remaining gain is at least \frac{b}{2}.
For s\geq\frac{b}{2}, the tangent bound instead yields
K_{b}^{A,G}(z)\geq\min\{m,F(s)\}+b-s-o(b).
The teal inequality in (A.1) handles
the uncapped case.
The capped case has the required terminal
allowance b-s. Choose the local error tolerance sufficiently
smaller than \theta. These estimates verify the competitor
hypothesis of [7, Lemma 15], in exactly the form used in
Lemma 4.1. Lemma 14(iii) of [7] and (H3)
preserve the capped source increment relative to the pin. Removing
the cap and then letting \theta decrease to zero proves
\Delta_{d}[a,b]\geq\Delta_{y}[a,b]-o(b).
Finally, these local errors can be summed. Two outer yellow blocks
cannot be consecutive, and every teal block at least halves the
precision. Hence the sum of their right endpoints is O(r) and
there are O(\log r) blocks above a fixed initial cutoff. The
greedy subpartitions of yellow blocks have the same geometric
summability property. For a fixed local tolerance \xi, their
total error is O(\xi r)+O((\log r)^{2}).
The discarded bounded
initial range contributes O(1). Take r\to\infty first and
then \xi\searrow 0. This proves the asserted o(r) error.
∎
Proposition A.2 (The retained prefix inequalities).
Let L_{r} be the total length of the yellow blocks in the preceding
partition. Under (H1)–(H4),
K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\geq\frac{2r+L_{r}}{3}-o(r),\qquad K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\geq K_{r}^{A}(y)-L_{r}-o(r).
Proof.
Summing the yellow distance increments and the teal lower bounds
from Lemma A.1 results in
K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|)\geq L_{r}+c_{d}(r-L_{r})-o(r)\geq\frac{2r+L_{r}}{3}-o(r).
For the second inequality, keep the full source complexity.
The sums indexed by Y and T run over the yellow and selected
teal blocks, respectively, with each increment evaluated at that
block’s endpoints:
