\displaystyle K_{r}^{A,x}(y)
\displaystyle=\sum_{Y}\Delta_{y}+\sum_{T}\Delta_{y}+o(r)
\displaystyle\leq 2L_{r}+\sum_{T}\Delta_{d}+o(r)
\displaystyle\leq L_{r}+K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|)+o(r).
The only step removing the pin from this source quantity is (H3):
K_{r}^{A}(y)-O(\log r)\leq K_{r}^{A,x}(y).
Combining proves the second inequality. Multiplying the first by
\frac{3}{4} and the second by \frac{1}{4} cancels L_{r} and
proves Lemma 3.1.
∎
The endpoint calculation involves three places in the cited
argument. The first ratio bound of [7, Lemma 26] survives at
D=2.
Its weaker bound proportional to 2-D is replaced by
\frac{a}{b}\geq\frac{d}{5-d}-o(1). Equations (22)–(23) in the
proof of its Lemma 31 use that weaker bound only to justify the
projection theorem. Here Lemma 3.2 applies because
\frac{s}{b-s} is bounded below. Finally, the bound
S<\frac{2}{d(2-D)} on the number of red–green–blue sequences
belongs to the proof of [7, Theorem 16].
We
use the bounded-ratio partition count proved in
Lemma 3.2 instead. The root identities, halving
argument, source-increment calculation, and summation above do not
divide by 2-D. Thus no limiting assertion in D is needed.
Appendix B The first scalar improvement
This appendix and Appendix C record preliminary
scalar estimates superseded by Proposition 10.1.
The first proof uses a single exchange of the endpoint estimates.
Suppose both
descriptions of the distance are short. With y as the pin, we
deduce that x must have a certain amount of complexity at every
late scale. We then use x as the pin, where this lower bound
improves the projection estimate. The resulting contradiction proves the bound
\frac{13}{17}.
Proposition B.1 (Scalar consequence).
Let f,g,d_{x},d_{y} be scalar profiles as in
Section 2.4, satisfying (2.3),
(4.2), and Lemma 4.1 for both
choices of pin. Then
\max\{d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1)\}\geq\frac{13}{17}.
(B.1)
Proof.
Set q=\max\{d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1)\}. The baseline implies q\geq\frac{3}{4}. If q\geq\frac{7}{8} there is nothing to prove, so assume \frac{3}{4}\leq q<\frac{7}{8}.
For every v\in[\frac{1}{2},1], the tangent range and g(s)\geq s imply
H_{g,f}(v,1)\geq 1.
Combining the prefix estimate and tail transfer with y as the pin yields
q\geq\frac{v}{2}+\frac{g(v)}{4}+\left[1-g(v)\right]_{+}\geq 1+\frac{v}{2}-\frac{3g(v)}{4}.
Consequently
g(v)\geq\frac{4(1-q)+2v}{3}\quad\text{for }\frac{1}{2}\leq v\leq 1.
(B.2)
For s\leq\frac{1}{2}, substitute v=1-s into (B.2) to obtain
p_{g}(s;1)\geq\min\left\{1-s,1-\frac{2q}{3}+\frac{s}{6}\right\}.
(B.3)
Choose the unique a>0 such that
f(a)+\frac{a}{6}=\frac{2q}{3}.
(B.4)
Existence and uniqueness follow because f(u)+\frac{u}{6} is continuous and strictly increasing. Since f(\frac{1}{2})\geq\frac{1}{2} and q<\frac{7}{8}, we have a<\frac{1}{2}. Since f(a)\leq 2a,
a\geq\frac{4q}{13}>0.
(B.5)
For a\leq s\leq\frac{1}{2}, monotonicity implies f(s)+\frac{s}{6}\geq\frac{2q}{3}. Equations (B.3) and f(s)\geq s therefore imply f(s)+p_{g}(s;1)\geq 1. For s\geq\frac{1}{2}, the same conclusion follows from p_{g}(s;1)=1-s. Since f(1)\geq 1, we conclude that
H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq 1.
Now use the prefix estimate and tail transfer with x as the pin:
\displaystyle q
\displaystyle\geq\frac{a}{2}+\frac{f(a)}{4}+\left[1-f(a)\right]_{+}
\displaystyle\geq 1+\frac{a}{2}-\frac{3f(a)}{4}
\displaystyle=1-\frac{q}{2}+\frac{5a}{8}\geq 1-\frac{q}{2}+\frac{5}{8}\frac{4q}{13}=1-\frac{4q}{13}.
Thus 17q\geq 13, proving (B.1).
∎
The envelope (B.2) says that if the distance has a
short description given y, then x must be fairly complicated at
every scale in [\frac{1}{2},1]. Otherwise the transfer with y as
the pin would force a longer description of the distance. We now
regard x as the pin. Its additional complexity improves the
projection gain p_{g} and hence the transfer estimate for d_{x}.
This is the interaction between the two choices of pin. An upper
bound for d_{x}(1) alone does not provide the upper bound for d_{y}(1)
used at the start.
Section 7 makes the
resulting limitation precise.
Appendix C An elementary iteration of the scalar bounds
The first argument used only a lower bound on the tail of each
profile. The interval estimates also give information at intermediate
scales. We use that information twice, and then return to the tail.
This produces an affine update for the cutoff from which the full
tail transfers. Its fixed point determines the exponent \frac{2725}{3521}.
The calculation below shows how iterating the cutoff improves the
initial bound. The reciprocal argument in
Section 10 develops this idea further.
Proposition C.1 (Two intermediate envelopes).
Under the scalar hypotheses of Proposition B.1,
\max\{d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1)\}\geq\frac{2725}{3521}=0.7739278614\ldots.
Proof.
Write q=\max\{d_{x}(1),d_{y}(1)\} and k=1-q.
We will repeatedly move a common tail cutoff to the left. Every
valid cutoff must remain at least k, whereas the update has a
fixed point below k. This will give the contradiction.
By Proposition B.1, we may assume
\frac{13}{17}\leq q<\frac{2725}{3521}<\frac{31}{40},\qquad\frac{796}{3521}<k\leq\frac{4}{17}.
(C.1)
In particular k>\frac{9}{40}. We retain the sharper bound
k>\frac{796}{3521} for the final cutoff update. Set
L(u)=\frac{4k+2u}{3},\qquad A_{0}=\frac{4-12k}{5}.
We will decrease a common tail cutoff A, while preserving the following conditions for both choices of pin.
\displaystyle k\leq A\leq A_{0}\leq\frac{13}{50},
\displaystyle H_{f,g}(A,1),H_{g,f}(A,1)\geq 1,
(C.2)
\displaystyle\beta=\frac{4}{3}(A-k),
\displaystyle f(u),g(u)\geq\begin{cases}2u-\beta,&0\leq u\leq A,\\
L(u),&A\leq u\leq 1.\end{cases}
(C.3)
To initialize, apply the proof of Proposition B.1 first with x as the pin and then with y as the pin. The two resulting cutoffs are each at most A_{0}=\frac{12q-8}{5}. At those cutoffs the respective thresholds H_{f,g} and H_{g,f} are at least one. Hence both thresholds remain at least one at A_{0}. Prefix plus tail then gives f(u),g(u)\geq L(u) for u\geq A_{0}. The functions 2u-f(u) and 2u-g(u) are nondecreasing, so their values at A_{0} supply the early bounds in (C.3). Finally, A_{0}\geq k follows from q\geq\frac{13}{17}.
We first improve the profiles on an intermediate interval.
For
\frac{8}{15}\leq b\leq 4k,\qquad c_{1}=\frac{4k+2b+6\beta}{13}=\frac{8A-4k+2b}{13},
we claim
H_{f,g}(c_{1},b)\geq L(b).
(C.4)
Here b\geq 2A and 0<c_{1}\leq A: the latter upper bound is equivalent to b\leq 2k+\frac{5A}{2}, which follows from b\leq 4k and A\geq k.
For s\geq\frac{b}{2}, the projection gain is b-s, and
f(s)+b-s\geq L(s)+b-s\geq L(b).
For c_{1}\leq s\leq\frac{b}{2}, the pin argument b-s is at least A. If s\leq A, the second projection branch satisfies
f(s)+\frac{g(b-s)+s}{2}\geq\frac{2k}{3}+\frac{b}{3}+\frac{13s}{6}-\beta\geq L(b).
If s\geq A, that branch is instead at least 2k+\frac{b}{3}+\frac{5s}{6}, which is at least L(b) because
4k-2b+5s\geq k>0.
The competing tangent branch for s\leq A is at least b+c_{1}-\beta. The required inequality b+c_{1}-\beta\geq L(b) is equivalent to
19b\geq 40k+21\beta=28A+12k.
It follows from
19b\geq\frac{152}{15},\qquad 28A+12k\leq 28A_{0}+12k=\frac{112-276k}{5}\leq\frac{499}{50}<\frac{152}{15}.
For s\geq A, the tangent branch was already checked, and f(b)\geq L(b). This proves (C.4).
Prefix plus transfer, using f(c_{1})\leq 2c_{1}, yields
d_{x}(b)\geq L(b)-c_{1}.
Since b\geq A, the tail at b has height at least one. Consequently
f(b),g(b)\geq M_{A}(b):=\frac{103k+20b-24A}{39}\quad\text{for }\frac{8}{15}\leq b\leq 4k.
(C.5)
Indeed, q\geq d_{x}(b)+1-f(b) implies f(b)\geq k+L(b)-c_{1}=M_{A}(b).
The calculation with y as the pin proves the same conclusion for g.
We apply the new envelope once more.
For
\frac{37}{50}\leq b\leq 4k,\qquad c_{2}=\frac{128A-103k+32b}{175},
we claim
H_{f,g}(c_{2},b)\geq L(b).
(C.6)
The previous certificate covers s\geq c_{1}, so it suffices to check c_{2}\leq s\leq c_{1}. All pin arguments on this new interval lie in the range of (C.5). More explicitly, c_{2}>0, and
c_{1}-c_{2}=\frac{3(213k-88A-22b)}{2275}>0,
since b\leq 4k, k\geq\frac{9}{40}, and A\leq\frac{13}{50} imply
213k-88A-22b\geq 125k-88A>0. Also
b-c_{1}=\frac{11b+4k-8A}{13}\geq\frac{174}{325}>\frac{8}{15}.
Thus b-s\in[\frac{8}{15},4k] whenever c_{2}\leq s\leq c_{1}, while s\leq c_{1}\leq A. By (C.3) and (C.5),
f(s)+\frac{g(b-s)+s}{2}\geq 2s-\beta+\frac{M_{A}(b-s)+s}{2}\geq L(b).
The last expression increases with s and equals L(b) at s=c_{2}, by the defining formula for c_{2}. The competing tangent branch is at least b+c_{2}-\beta.
The condition that this reaches L(b) is
271b\geq 316A+309k.
It follows from b\geq\frac{37}{50}, A\leq\frac{13}{50}, and
k\leq\frac{1}{4}. This proves (C.6).
Applying prefix and terminal tail exactly as before gives
f(b),g(b)\geq N_{A}(b):=\frac{1534k+254b-384A}{525}\quad\text{for }\frac{37}{50}\leq b\leq 4k.
(C.7)
The two intermediate estimates now improve the full tail.
Set
\gamma=\frac{271}{1050},\qquad T=\frac{796-1534k+384A}{1050},
and let a solve f(a)+\gamma a=T. The solution exists uniquely in (0,A]. Indeed, T>0 follows from A\geq k and k\leq\frac{4}{17}, and
f(A)+\gamma A-T\geq\frac{2934k+587A-796}{1050}\geq\frac{3521k-796}{1050}>0
by the sharper lower bound k>\frac{796}{3521} in (C.1).
This is the one place where k>\frac{9}{40} would not suffice. Also f(a)\leq 2a implies
a\geq\frac{796-1534k+384A}{2371}\geq\frac{796-1150k}{2371}\geq 1-4k;
the last step only requires 8334k\geq 1575, which follows from k\geq\frac{9}{40}.
For a\leq s\leq A, therefore, 1-s\in[\frac{37}{50},4k]. From the new envelope (C.7) we obtain
f(s)+\frac{g(1-s)+s}{2}\geq f(s)+\frac{1534k+254-384A+271s}{1050}\geq 1.
The competing branch f(s)+1-s is also at least one. For s\geq A, invariant (C.2) already supplies the required inequality. Thus H_{f,g}(a,1)\geq 1.
Prefix plus this full tail implies
q\geq 1+\frac{a}{2}-\frac{3f(a)}{4}=1-\frac{3T}{4}+\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\gamma}{4}\right)a,
and hence
a\leq A^{\prime}:=\frac{796-2934k+384A}{971}.
(C.8)
The calculation with y as the pin yields its own full-tail cutoff, bounded by the same A^{\prime}. Every such cutoff is at least k, since prefix plus tail and f(a)\leq 2a give q\geq 1-a. If A^{\prime}<k we have a contradiction immediately.
Otherwise both tails at A^{\prime} have height at least one. Prefix plus tail implies f(u),g(u)\geq L(u) for u\geq A^{\prime}, and monotonicity of 2u-f(u) and 2u-g(u) supplies the early bounds with \beta^{\prime}=\frac{4(A^{\prime}-k)}{3}. Moreover,
A^{\prime}-A=\frac{796-2934k-587A}{971}\leq\frac{796-3521k}{971}<0.
Thus (C.2)–(C.3) are preserved until a contradiction occurs.
The affine update (C.8) is a contraction, with fixed point
A_{*}=\frac{796-2934k}{587}<k.
Starting at A_{0}, finitely many iterations must therefore give A_{n}<k, a contradiction. This proves the proposition.
∎
Remark C.2 (Finiteness and the role of the transfer lemma).
For every fixed q<\frac{2725}{3521}, only finitely many updates are needed. Within each update all interval endpoints are fixed positive proportions of the terminal precision, and Lemma 4.1 holds for every such pair of endpoints. The proof does not sum a precision-dependent number of error terms. Proposition C.1 improves the scalar consequence of the same analytic inputs.
Its conversion to the distance-set statement uses the same fixed-pair and classical arguments as the \frac{13}{17} bound.
Appendix D Pinned consequences of matching profiles
Finite-scale matching allowed us to choose a different pair at each
precision in the lower-box argument. A common dyadic mass law
ensures matching for almost every fixed pair. We can then use
the same scalar estimates to obtain a pinned Hausdorff bound.
The geometric argument supplies the fixed gain near dimension one.
Proposition D.1 (A matched-profile pinned consequence).
Suppose x\neq y satisfy (H1)–(H4) relative to A, and
\sup_{1\leq m\leq r}|K_{m}^{A}(x)-K_{m}^{A}(y)|=o(r).
(D.1)
If \dim^{A}x,\dim^{A}y>1+\kappa with 0<\kappa<\frac{1}{8}, then
\liminf_{r\to\infty}\frac{K_{r}^{A,x}(|x-y|)}{r}\geq\frac{4}{5}+\frac{3\kappa}{5}.
Proof.
Take any sequence of terminal precisions and extract uniformly
convergent endpoint profiles and the distance profile d_{x}.
Condition (D.1) implies f=g.
The prefix and both transfer expressions therefore agree in the
two orientations. Hence (f,f,d_{x},d_{x}) is a scalar system, with
f(u)\geq(1+\kappa)u. Proposition 12.1
proves the lower bound at the terminal scale.
The initial sequence was arbitrary, which proves the assertion.
The two actual conditioned distance profiles need not agree.
∎
Theorem D.2 (Pinned distances under a common dyadic mass law).
Let \mu be a compactly supported planar s-Frostman probability
measure, with s>1, and put E=\operatorname{supp}\mu.
Suppose there are real numbers H(n) such that
-\log_{2}\mu(Q_{n}(z))=H(n)+o_{z}(n)\quad\text{for $\mu$-almost every }z.
Then \dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E\geq L_{*}(s) for \mu-almost every x.
The quotient \frac{H(n)}{n} need not converge.
The mass law says that the linear-scale part of the information in
a dyadic cell is described by one function H(n) at almost every
point. It permits the rate of information growth to vary with the scale.
Proof.
Choose an oracle A encoding E and all dyadic masses of \mu.
Lemma 2.2 implies
K_{n}^{A}(z)=H(n)+o_{z}(n) almost everywhere. For almost every pair,
the difference of the two complexities is o(n). This implies
(D.1) as well. Given a positive tolerance,
the errors at all sufficiently large m\leq r are at most that
tolerance times r; the finitely many smaller errors are bounded
independently of r.
The same coding lemma with the other endpoint as an additional
oracle, followed by Fubini, proves full-oracle independence for
almost every pair. The Frostman bound ensures effective dimension
at least s. To obtain the directional hypotheses, fix 1<s^{\prime}<s
and apply Orponen’s theorem [25, Theorem 1.11] to countably
many source restrictions in balls separated from the pin.
The exceptional centers have dimension at most 2-s^{\prime}<1 and
are \mu-null. Directions failing the logarithmic complexity
bound form an arclength-null set. Absolute continuity and Fubini
therefore supply both directional estimates.
For almost every x, Proposition D.1
applies to almost every y, for each
0<\kappa<\min\{s-1,\frac{1}{8}\}.
Letting \kappa increase to this upper endpoint proves
\dim^{A,x}|x-y|\geq L(s). The pinned image is effectively
compact relative to (A,x), so the effectively compact
point-to-set principle proves the Hausdorff bound L(s).
It remains to prove the fixed gain. Suppose a positive measure
set of pins has pinned Hausdorff dimension below some
q<\frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0}. By a countable restriction, choose
separated source and pin portions of positive measure. The
common mass law and the preceding generic properties can be
made uniform on a positive product-measure relation. Restrictions
to positive-measure sets preserve the law, since the dyadic density
theorem makes their cell-mass ratio tend to a positive constant
at almost every retained point.
Lemma 20.4 supplies infinitely
many precisions with a relation of polynomial mass and low
conditioned distance complexity in the prescribed orientation.
The endpoint profiles agree uniformly in the limit. The distance
profile in that orientation can therefore be duplicated to form
(f,f,d_{x},d_{x}). Lemma 13.3 now forces the
plateau and parent certificate used in
Proposition 29.1.
That proposition needs the small radial cover in only the actual
prescribed orientation. The duplicated scalar profile is used
solely to obtain its mass and plateau hypotheses.
Its contradiction proves the bound \frac{4}{5}+\eta_{0}.
Taking the maximum with L(s) completes the proof.
∎
Corollary D.3 (Uniform branching).
Start with a dyadic square. At level n, retain
m_{n}\in\{1,2,3,4\} children of every retained square, using
the same number for all parents at that level. Their locations
may vary with the parent. Let E be the limiting compact set
and let \mu give equal mass to the retained cells. If
s_{0}=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log_{2}\prod_{k=1}^{n}m_{k}>1,
then \dim_{\mathrm{H}}\Delta_{x}E\geq L_{*}(s_{0}) for
\mu-almost every x.
Proof.
Put H(n)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log_{2}m_{k}. Each retained cell has
mass 2^{-H(n)}. Dyadic boundary lines are null. A bounded
vertical or horizontal segment meets at most C2^{n} cells, whose
total mass is at most C2^{n-H(n)}\to 0. A ball of radius
2^{-n} meets a bounded number of cells, so \mu is
t-Frostman for some t>1. Apply the preceding theorem at every Frostman exponent below s_{0}
and use continuity of L_{*}. The dyadic mass identity is exact.
∎
Alternating two-child and four-child blocks of lengths 2^{j}, j\geq 1,
gives \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{H(n)}{n}=\frac{4}{3} and
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{H(n)}{n}=\frac{5}{3}. The ratio is monotone
within each block. This illustrates why the hypothesis concerns an entire
information profile rather than a single limit of the information per scale.
For the Hausdorff conclusion, the common law supplies one fixed
pair along all sequences of precisions. The lower-box proof needed
only the finite-scale matching of Section 28.
Appendix E Joint optimization of the two geometric counts
Both upper counts apply to the same family of rich bisectors. One
may therefore compare their minimum with the lower count before
optimizing over the parameters. We show that this does not improve
the threshold under the constraints used in the paper. The assertion
concerns these inequalities only. A further restriction imposed by
the common source measure could change the conclusion.
Fix \frac{1}{3}<\alpha<\alpha_{*} and 0\leq\Delta<\frac{1}{2}. Recall that
a_{c}=\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\Delta}{2} and W_{0}=\frac{1-2\Delta}{4}, and put
\displaystyle\omega_{o}
\displaystyle=\frac{3\alpha(1-2\Delta)}{4(10-3\alpha)},
\displaystyle\omega_{b}
\displaystyle=\frac{-1+4\alpha+6(1-2\alpha)\Delta}{12(3-2\alpha)},
\displaystyle\omega_{f}
\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha(1-2\Delta)}{4(2+\alpha)}.
Fix also 0<\omega<\frac{1-2\Delta}{24}. We work with the leading
exponents, with the arbitrarily small tolerances set equal to zero.
The precise parameter set used here is
-1\leq\chi\leq 2,\qquad\varsigma\geq\max\{0,-2\chi\},\qquad 3\varsigma+2\chi\leq 4,\qquad 0\leq\mathfrak{e}\leq\varsigma.
Set
u_{0}=\varsigma+2\chi,\qquad\varpi=\min\{2,1+u_{0}\},\qquad e_{0}=\mathfrak{e}+[u_{0}-1]_{+},
and take 0\leq\mathfrak{u}\leq\varpi+1. In branch 1,
\mathfrak{e}\leq\frac{1}{8} and
f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})=\min\{2,2\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{u}+e_{0}\}.
In branch 2, \mathfrak{e}\geq\frac{1}{8} and
f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})=\min\{2,2\mathfrak{u}\}. Thus we keep the
additional source bound in branch 1. Put
\mathfrak{r}=\frac{1}{2}+f_{0}(\mathfrak{u})\omega,\qquad t=a_{c}+\chi\omega,\qquad W=W_{0}-(\varsigma+\chi-\mathfrak{e})\omega,
and retain
a=a_{c}+(\varpi-\mathfrak{u})\omega,\qquad L=\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}+(1-3\alpha)a.
The first upper exponent is U_{1}=3\mathfrak{r}-t-2W. The second
is the larger of
U_{b}=3\mathfrak{r}-\alpha-(2-3\alpha)a,\qquad U_{f}=3\mathfrak{r}-(1-a).
Thus the combined comparison is contradictory at a template
precisely when
L>U_{1}\quad\hbox{or}\quad(L>U_{b}\ \hbox{and}\ L>U_{f}).
Proposition E.1 (The joint comparison of the displayed exponents).
The assertion that at least one of the two strict leading-exponent
comparisons succeeds for every parameter choice just specified is
equivalent to
\omega<\max\{\omega_{o},\min\{\omega_{b},\omega_{f}\}\}.
The quantifier is uniform. Above this threshold there is an
admissible parameter choice at which both comparisons fail. Thus
joint optimization over this parameter set has the same threshold
as the better of the two separate optimizations.
Proof.
The sufficiency follows from the separate strict comparisons in
Proposition 21.1 and
Section 23. The latter remains valid with
the improved branch 1 richness, since decreasing \mathfrak{r}
can only decrease both upper exponents.
For the converse, only two templates are needed. Both are in branch
one, and both have \mathfrak{r}=\frac{1}{2}+2\omega, including the improved
source bound used in that branch.
First take
(\varsigma,\chi,\mathfrak{e},\varpi,\mathfrak{u})=(2,-1,0,1,2).
Here u_{0}=e_{0}=0, a=a_{c}-\omega, t=a_{c}-\omega, and
W=W_{0}-\omega. Direct substitution yields
\displaystyle L-U_{1}
\displaystyle=(10-3\alpha)(\omega_{o}-\omega),
\displaystyle L-U_{b}
\displaystyle=3(3-2\alpha)(\omega_{b}-\omega).
Consequently this template defeats the combined comparison whenever
\omega\geq\max\{\omega_{o},\omega_{b}\}.
Next take
(\varsigma,\chi,\mathfrak{e},\varpi,\mathfrak{u})=(1,\frac{1}{2},0,2,1).
Now u_{0}=2, e_{0}=1, and a=a_{c}+\omega. The branch-one
minimum is still \min\{2,2\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{u}+e_{0}\}=2.
For this template,
\displaystyle L-U_{f}
\displaystyle=3(2+\alpha)(\omega_{f}-\omega),
\displaystyle L-U_{1}
\displaystyle=\frac{3\alpha}{4}(1-2\Delta)-(\frac{15}{2}+3\alpha)\omega.
At \omega=\omega_{f}, the latter expression equals
-\frac{3\alpha(1-2\Delta)}{8(2+\alpha)}<0,
and it decreases thereafter. This template therefore defeats the
combined comparison for \omega\geq\omega_{f}.
Since \omega_{f}>\omega_{o}, the two obstruction ranges start at
\min\{\max\{\omega_{o},\omega_{b}\},\omega_{f}\}=\max\{\omega_{o},\min\{\omega_{b},\omega_{f}\}\}.
All these thresholds lie in the stated structural range whenever
they control the maximum, since \alpha<\alpha_{*}<\frac{2}{5} implies
\omega_{f}<\frac{1-2\Delta}{24}.
All the displayed template constraints, including
3\varsigma+2\chi\leq 4, \varsigma\geq\max\{0,-2\chi\}, and
0\leq\mathfrak{e}\leq\varsigma, hold for the two choices.
For both choices one may take u_{-}=W and u_{+}=W+t, so the
remaining plateau-location inequalities hold as well. This
completes the calculation.
∎
